# HAProxy — technique cards (179)

Each card is exactly 7 lines. Fetch one with: grep -A 6 "^### haproxy-NNN " haproxy.md

### haproxy-001 — Replace repeated linear scans with an index (tree or hash)
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy · Also: Varnish
Principle: Anything that is looked up repeatedly by key (names, IDs, patterns, records) belongs in an ordered tree or hash index, not in a list that is walked from the start every time.
Why it is faster: A list walk costs O(n) comparisons per lookup, and each comparison is usually a cache miss on a separately allocated node. A tree or hash index makes it O(log n) or O(1). The crossover is low: HAProxy measured large wins at 100 variables and at a few hundred DNS records, not only at millions. Keep the list only where insertion order matters (dumps), or use a tree whose duplicate keys keep insertion order so iteration and round-robin behaviour are unchanged. Splitting one tree into a few roots selected by low hash bits makes each tree shallower for almost no memory.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: a `Map` instead of `array.find()` in a hot JS path; radix or prefix trees for URL routing and IP allow-lists. • Data / databases: add an index on the column the application filters on; a hash index for dedup in ETL jobs. • Embedded: a sorted table with binary search for a command or route table instead of a linear `strcmp` chain. • Native / systems: intrusive trees (the node lives inside the object) so indexing costs no extra allocation.
Watch out: an index has to be maintained on every insert and delete, and costs memory per entry. For a handful of entries a list in one cache line can win; measure the crossover rather than assuming it.
Seen in HAProxy: c4262961f8 2010-05-10 ACL exact-string matches moved into a tree: 52,000 entries cost about 2% CPU at 42k req/s, against 100% CPU at 6.9k req/s with the linear match. b337b532de did the same for network prefixes (52k networks for +3% CPU), and b1dd9bf308 added longest-match for string beginnings. • f79d950163 2014-03-15 Proxies looked up by name and ID through trees (cfd837fb6c), and stick-tables by name (991610d33f, later 1b8e68e89a). • da29fe2360 2019-06-13 The global server-state file is parsed once into a tree instead of being re-read for every backend; d7cee71e77 stores tcp-check rulesets in a tree. • 7893ae117f 2021-10-21 The DNS answer list became an eb32 tree with key 0 (behaves like a list), then dcb696cd31 keyed it by `XXH32(record)` and used an exact lookup: CPU fell from 60-70% to 4% at about 2k DNS req/s with 300 servers. • 4bcfe30414 2023-12-04 `server_find_by_name()` uses the name tree instead of a `strcmp` walk (835263047e); 0844bed7d3 and 5fea59754b give map/ACL `pat_ref` an index over its elements, and 745d1a269b adds a back-pointer so an update visits only its own nodes. • 47ec7c681e 2024-09-15 Variables moved from a per-request list to a compact tree keyed by name hash (a0205f9de4 imports cebtree, 2 pointers per node like a list): 85k to 117k req/s (+37%) with 100 vars, 18x faster at 1000 vars; `var_to_smp` had been 56% of CPU. 5d350d1e50 then used 4 roots selected by low hash bits: +21% over one tree and +67% over the list. • 5e78ab33cd 2025-07-10 `findserver()` uses the existing tree instead of walking the server list, which matters for large configs. • Also seen in Varnish (varnish-001): 91a16493fa 2018-10-31 Added a micro-benchmark (`xyzzy_priv_perf`, ns per lookup) for dynamic priv lookup before changing it. • 25349b1ac7 2018-11-05 Dynamic VMOD privs moved from a `VTAILQ` to a red/black tree keyed by `vmod_id`: "O(n) does not scale ... trade some workspace for O(lg n)". c1d5934b7d 2018-11-05 follow-up. • 596c0a4d26 2021-03-24 ACL entries in the VCL compiler moved from a sorted list with linear insertion (O(n²) for n entries) to a red/black tree iterated in order for code emission. • 074d164ea0 2021-03-23 Generate only the tree operations actually used (`VRBT_GENERATE_INSERT/FIND/REMOVE`) instead of the whole set. 784756e02f 2021-03-23 made the unused augmentation hook an empty macro: it had walked from the node to the root on every insert and remove. bb401ab31d 2021-11-10 removed augmentation entirely. • 3efb24ea66 2024-08-19 Backend connection pools moved from a list scan to a red/black tree keyed by `memcmp` of the pool identity, because heavy dynamic-backend use made the scan expensive. fa5e46fb62 2024-08-19 generates only FIND/INSERT/REMOVE.

### haproxy-002 — Hunt down hidden O(n²) work before it meets real scale
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy · Also: curl, Netty, Seastar, NATS
Principle: Nested loops over "all items × all items" (checks, reference counting, per-thread × per-thread allocation, prepend-then-reverse) are fine in tests and fatal at production scale; accumulate during a single pass or use an existing index instead.
Why it is faster: Quadratic cost grows 10,000x when the input grows 100x, so it hides until a large user arrives. The fix is usually to compute the aggregate at the moment each item is resolved (one pass), or to look up only the neighbours that can collide via an index. Startup and configuration code is the usual hiding place, because it is rarely profiled.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Kubernetes controllers or config generators that validate every object against every other; ORM metadata loading. • Data / databases: use a unique index for dedup during imports instead of a self-join; maintain aggregate columns incrementally. • Native / systems: compilers and bundlers building reverse-reference counts in one parse; per-pair (thread × thread) resources replaced by per-thread ones.
Watch out: test with inputs 100x larger than the typical one; quadratic code passes every small test. Avoid "fixes" that keep the quadratic shape but shrink the constant.
Seen in HAProxy: 3f78357066 2017-01-12 The per-backend sum of referencing frontends' maxconn is accumulated when each reference is resolved, instead of a nested loop over all proxies: loading 10,000 backends went from "several minutes to few seconds". • f91ac19299 2020-02-27 Unique pattern-reference ID allocation became O(n log n): startup with 50k pat_refs went from 77.5 s to 0.17 s (20k: 12.3 s to 0.07 s). e4d7c9dd65 enables protocols on the first thread only, saving about 0.1 s for 150k listeners. • 8d49253588 2022-12-21 QUIC allocated an Rx buffer per listener per thread across all threads; with per-thread shards that was N² = 2304 buffers (576 MB) on 48 threads, now one per bound thread. • 029d75df1e 2024-09-20 Duplicate server-name detection walks only same-key neighbours (`ebpt_prev_dup`) in the existing name tree instead of all pairs: config check time divided by 4 for 2k servers per backend. • 869163e10e 2026-07-22 After converting proxies and servers to doubly linked lists (b6bb1bd741, 81fc220335), items are appended at the tail and the post-parse O(n) reversal disappears (029add93ae). • Also seen in curl (curl-008): a0984eae14 2018-02-07 fnmatch: runs of `*` and `?` are regrouped (`*?*?*` is `??*`), `?` consumes a character immediately, and the star suffix is tried once per position instead of exponentially. 2a1b2b4ef5 and 404c8850da cap the number of star sections. • a15c4a7f8c 2026-09-09 `curl_easy_nextheader()` caches name, origin, index and count from the previous call, so full enumeration is linear; it falls back to a scan only if the call pattern breaks. • dfdae2ee34 2026-10-26 AWS SigV4 canonical header sorting: bubble sort replaced by `qsort`. • ded4c00884 2026-10-14 Trailing `/` and `\` removal walks back once instead of calling `strrchr` after each removed character. • Also seen in Netty (netty-009): 4021eadab2 2026-09-10 DNS search-domain deduplication uses a `LinkedHashSet` instead of `List.contains`: 4,096 domains went from 12,196 to 147 µs. • 10539f4dc7 2018-11-03 `CompositeByteBuf` bulk insert became O(m+n) instead of O(mn). • 5b59250657 2016-04-22 Replaced a `TreeMap` double lookup with a single `get`, and c6a13e28b9 2016-08-02 made `ConstantPool.valueOf` do one `get` plus `putIfAbsent`. • Also seen in Seastar (seastar-009): 7f7a550f98 2014-09-22 Packets extend their fragment array by doubling: "Avoid quadratic behavior". • 28a143aee2 2017-02-12 The metrics export removed a quadratic `rep->_content += s` that was "showing up in latency profiles". 2b2d9de29b 2017-05-08 reserves the protobuf before adding, to avoid quadratic growth. • 95fd824685 2022-05-22 RPC unmarshalling of N arguments by recursive `tuple_cat` was O(N²) work with O(N) instantiations. It became a comma fold into `tuple<optional<T>...>`, which is linear and keeps evaluation order. • Also seen in NATS (nats-015): a0dc9ea3e3 2020-11-24 `validateLeafNode`: "Reducing complexity of lookup" with an `accNames` set instead of a nested loop. • 417839172b 2026-02-24 `checkStreamCfg` checks `SubjectsCollide` only over `cfg.Subjects[i+1:]`.

### haproxy-003 — Don't restart a search from the beginning: seek once, then iterate or resume from a hint
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Positioning in an ordered structure is expensive and moving to the next element is cheap, so seek once per batch and iterate; when repeatedly searching for "the first free X", remember where the last search ended.
Why it is faster: A tree lookup is O(log n) with a cache miss per level, while `next()` is amortized O(1) and usually touches nodes that are already cached. For "lowest unused ID", scanning from 1 makes N insertions O(N²); a hint that starts after the last allocation and is lowered on deletion keeps the result exactly the same (still the lowest free ID) at near-constant cost.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: in a B-tree or LSM range scan, seek once and advance the cursor; keyset pagination instead of OFFSET loops. • Native / systems: fd, PID and slot allocators with a next-fit pointer; inode bitmaps. • Embedded: a free-slot hint in a fixed object table so allocation does not rescan from slot 0.
Watch out: the hint must be corrected on every operation that can invalidate it (a delete below it, a concurrent insert), or the "lowest" guarantee silently breaks.
Seen in HAProxy: 0b25d5e99f 2018-10-10 The run-queue `eb32sc_lookup_ge()` moved before the batch loops, so later tasks are reached with `next`: 5.5x fewer lookups and a shorter run-queue lock hold time. • f858386f63 2026-08-26 Adding servers at runtime keeps a `first_unused_id` hint, lowered on delete: 20k servers in 66 ms instead of 14.4 s (218x), 50k in 408 ms.

### haproxy-004 — Augment tree nodes with a subtree summary so filtered lookups prune whole branches
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy · Also: Pebble
Principle: When each lookup only wants entries matching an attribute, store in every internal node the OR (or min/max) of that attribute over its subtree and descend only into branches that can contain a match.
Why it is faster: Without the summary, a filtered "first matching entry" has to skip non-matching nodes one by one, which degenerates to O(n) when most entries belong to someone else. With it, a mismatched subtree is rejected in one test and the lookup is O(log n) again. The summary is maintained along the insertion path at no extra asymptotic cost.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: multi-tenant schedulers with per-subtree tenant masks; interval and segment trees. • Data / databases: R-tree bounding boxes and zone maps (min/max per block) let a scan skip whole pages. • Web / backend: a priority queue of jobs where each worker may only take certain job types.
Watch out: the summary must be updated on delete as well as insert; if the filter is rarely selective, or the data can be partitioned instead (one structure per owner), partitioning is simpler and faster.
Seen in HAProxy: 5d19fd479a 2017-11-05 `eb32sc` trees OR each node's thread scope into its ancestors on insertion; d1d55ac619 makes lookup/first/next descend only into branches with `node_s & scope`. • 8d38805d3d 2017-11-05 The shared run queue switched to eb32sc with the thread bit as scope. Before, 200,000 lookups needed 430M skips. With 2 threads: 15.9k to 447k req/s (x28), spinlock time from 85.2 s to 0.46 s, lookup from 13 µs to 40 ns. • 319d136ff9 2022-06-16 Once each thread got its own run queue, the scope was no longer needed and the queues went back to plain eb32 trees with cheaper insert and lookup (570k to 585k RPS): the augmentation is worth it only while the filter is selective. • Also seen in Pebble (pebble-002): 176637822e 2020-10-22 The manifest B-tree got an `Annotator`, which caches compensated level size and the "next elision-only candidate" per node. ManySSTablesNewVersion took 73.3 ms before and 0.1 ms after with 1M sstables (-99.85%), and 6.01 to 0.08 ms with 100k. The low-priority bottom-level elision compaction from a2588dc339 2020-08-13 became a cached lookup through this annotator. • a6d295204a 2024-08-14 Range annotations aggregate over a key range in O(log n): 4,015 ns/op compared with 513,519 ns/op for the `version.Overlaps` scan. 7b047cd675 2024-08-27 uses them in `EstimateDiskUsage`. • 28840262eb 2024-08-14 An annotator also picks the best tombstone-dense compaction candidate without a scan. • ae18416e71 2024-10-08 Annotations made safe for concurrent use, so expensive annotation work (which can include S3 requests) runs without the DB mutex. Occasional duplicate computation is accepted. • 5e7485cfab 2025-08-26 Annotation storage moved from an RWMutex plus a dynamic slice to a fixed-index array of atomic values, so lazy computation is lock-free. 414d942e73 2025-08-26 stops computing annotations under `DB.mu`.

### haproxy-005 — Pick by sampling two candidates (power of two choices), not by scanning all of them
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy
Principle: To place work on the least-loaded of N servers or threads, draw two candidates (one from a rotating index is fine) and take the less loaded; this gets most of the benefit of a full scan at O(1) cost and with no shared rotating state.
Why it is faster: Scanning all N candidates costs O(N) and needs a consistent global view; pure random placement has poor tail balance. Two random choices reduce the maximum load exponentially compared with one choice. Refinements matter: if the second draw can return the first pick it degrades to a single draw, ties between idle candidates should be broken by a smoothed rate, and mixing in the local thread keeps some locality.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: client-side load balancing (gRPC/Envoy P2C least-request), picking a DB replica in a proxy. • Native / systems: thread or core selection for new connections; work-stealing victim selection. • Data / databases: shard placement by post-placement load; cuckoo hashing uses the same two-choice idea.
Watch out: the load signal must be cheap and reasonably fresh; with stale or tied signals the choice degenerates to random. Make sure the two draws are distinct.
Seen in HAProxy: 760e81d356 2018-05-03 `balance random` reuses consistent hashing on a random key with a bounded-load factor, so load transitions do not hammer new servers. • 1efafce61f 2019-01-27 Accepted connections go to the less loaded of two threads (connection count and queue length) via per-thread rings (e0e9c48ab2); fc630bd373 takes one candidate from a rotating index, beating pure P2C by more than 5% on H2 and TLS resumption; 364f25a688 reuses the rotating start for idle-connection takeover. • 8adffaa899 2023-04-19 Selection also compares the local thread so some connections stay local (visible on 224 threads at 200G); 77e33509c8 re-validates the shared index before the heavy comparison; 84fe1f479b adds a round-robin "fair" mode for short connections; 9d360604bd spans thread groups. • 93cc18ac42 2025-09-04 The default `balance` changed from roundrobin to random (two draws), which adapts to load with no shared rotating state. • e6ed42090a 2026-11-13 The second draw now excludes the first pick: with 1 slow and 3 fast servers the slow one's share fell from 8525 to 3572 requests and throughput rose 33% (11.1k to 14.6k/s). b6bdb2553b and 747ff09818 break ties with the per-group session rate: the spread across 1600 servers went from 300..780 to 580..628.

### haproxy-006 — Keep weighted selection in trees keyed by "next position", with bounded probing
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Implement weighted round-robin, least-connections and consistent hashing as ordered trees keyed by each server's next due position or current load, so a weight or load change is one reposition instead of a table rebuild, and cap any probe walk.
Why it is faster: Table-based weighted round-robin must be recomputed when a weight changes; a tree reposition is O(log n). Accounting for the load after placement (+1) makes least-connections choose correctly at low load. Walking a hash ring to skip ineligible nodes can be O(ring) with extreme weights, so exit early for the single-server case and cap the number of nodes visited. A cheaper static algorithm is still worth keeping when weights never change.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: service-mesh load balancers with dynamic weights; ketama clients for sharded caches (cap the probe length). • Native / systems: weighted fair job schedulers; timer wheels and priority buckets. • Data / databases: token-ring replica selection (Cassandra-style) with bounded walks.
Watch out: under heavy concurrency the tree itself becomes a contention point; repositions may need to be deferred or batched. Degenerate inputs (one server, extreme weights) must be special-cased.
Seen in HAProxy: b625a085d8 2007-11-26 Fast Weighted Round Robin with a tree: weight changes take effect immediately, no table recomputation. • 6b2e11be1e 2009-10-01 Consistent hashing via closest-key tree lookup; 9757a38feb exposes `static-rr` as the CPU-cheaper option with unlimited servers. • 1eb6c55808 2018-12-14 leastconn queues servers by (conns+1)/weight, the load after placement. • 9fe72bba3c 2025-03-26 leastconn tree nodes hold several lock-free lists of equal-key servers, and a minimum of empty nodes is kept so moving a server between existing keys needs no tree mutation: 830k to 1.55M rps with 500 servers on 64 cores. • d9550ce4f3 2026-11-13 Consistent-hash picks exit immediately with one server, give up after 3 consecutive nodes of the same server, and cap distinct-server changes at 2N (bb782e2f48), removing bursts of up to about 4096 node visits per pick.

### haproxy-007 — Engineer hash tables: measured multipliers, short probes, per-key probe steps
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Choose hash constants and the bits you extract by benchmarking on real keys, and resolve collisions with probes that stay short (adjacent slots for tiny tables, a key-derived odd step for loaded ones).
Why it is faster: A multiplicative hash is only as good as its constant and the output bits taken; poor low bits create collisions that lengthen every probe. Adjacent linear probes are nearly free because the slots are already in the cache line or prefetched. A fixed probe step clusters entries; deriving the step from the unreduced hash (forced odd) spreads them and keeps probe counts low even at high fill.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: open-addressing maps (SwissTable, Robin Hood), Fibonacci hashing for power-of-two tables, profiler symbol caches. • Data / databases: shard-key hashing in distributed caches; hash-join tables. • Embedded: small fixed lookup tables where a few adjacent probes beat chaining with allocations.
Watch out: a constant tuned on one key distribution can be poor on another; keep the benchmark. Linear probing degrades quickly above high fill factors, which is where per-key steps help.
Seen in HAProxy: 64435aaa85 2022-09-07 The scheduler's caller/callee activity hash scans up to 16 adjacent slots on a collision: "pretty fast since close and likely prefetched". • f0d188f6ed 2023-08-12 About 7.5% of 32-bit primes were tested over 128 arrangements of 40k real pool pointers; `ptr_hash` got 0xacd1be85 (4-bit uniqueness 38.0 to 42.2%). 58946d44f8 centres the extracted bits where mixing is best: +15% unique entries at 10 bits, +24% at 11 bits. • fb7e5e1696 2026-03-11 Memory-profiling bins use double hashing with an odd per-key step: retries per lookup went from 1.17 to 0.6 in one test, and from 2.01 to 1.44 in another while table usage rose from 89.8% to 94.3%.

### haproxy-008 — Use a seeded 64-bit hash as the identity instead of a registry or string compare
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy · Also: Varnish
Principle: Store and compare a 64-bit hash of a name rather than the name itself or an index into a global registry; collisions are negligible and a random seed defeats attackers.
Why it is faster: A global grow-only name table needs a lock and keeps memory forever; a string compare touches every byte and a separate allocation. One 64-bit integer compare replaces both, and the string may not need to be stored at all. The trade-off is a tiny, bounded probability of collision, which a per-process random seed makes impossible to force.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: composite cache keys and ETags compared by hash; feature hashing for high-cardinality fields. • Native / systems: symbol interning via hashes in interpreters; hash-based field IDs in serialization formats. • Data / databases: hash-first prefilters in joins and dedup.
Watch out: where a collision would be a security or correctness bug (authentication, unique constraints), confirm with a full compare after the hash matches.
Seen in HAProxy: 3a4bedccc6 2021-08-31 Variable names are hashed with seeded XXH3/XXH64 (2c897d9d1b) instead of being registered in a central, locked, grow-only name index. • d993e1eeae 2025-12-05 The TLS SNI hash is stored in the connection and in the cached session (9794585204, 92f77cb3e6, 28654f3c9b); matching compares hashes, and be998b590e stops storing the SNI string in the cache at all. • Also seen in Varnish (varnish-003): 877851aad2 2008-12-03 The critbit hasher stores a SHA256 digest in each objhead. • eefeafeb2a 2008-11-25 Endianness fast path in `vsha256.c`: skip per-word byte swaps only with credible big-endian detection. • 574c23bdb0 2009-01-12 SHA256 became standard in `hash_classic.c` too; 29a160053e 2009-01-12 deleted the segment-by-segment `HSH_Compare()` and compares the digest with `memcmp()`. • ed03c78f5c 2009-12-16 The digest is computed once into `struct sess` right after `vcl_hash` and reused. • fb70da351b 2019-10-03 Connection-pool IDs became an integer; ec70dbc750 2019-10-08 derives them from the first 64 bits of a SHA256 over protocol, IPv4, IPv6 and UDS path, deleting the per-pool compare method; 685a578691 2019-10-30 asserts the addresses match on a hit to guard against collisions.

### haproxy-009 — Look up fixed key sets with a perfect hash or a length-bucketed table
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy · Also: curl, Netty, PostgreSQL, Varnish
Principle: For a small, static set of keys (status codes, keywords, well-known header names), generate a collision-free hash into a tiny table, or pack the keys by length so a lookup compares only same-length candidates.
Why it is faster: A sparse `switch` compiles to a big jump table or a branch chain that pollutes the branch predictor and grows the code. A brute-forced multiply/shift perfect hash into a power-of-two table gives a branch-free O(1) lookup in a few bytes. A length-sorted byte stream with a per-length start index rejects almost everything on the length alone and keeps the whole table in a few cache lines.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: keyword and opcode dispatch in lexers (gperf-style). • Web / backend: HTTP method/status maps and known-header interning in JS or Go servers. • Embedded: command decoders in CLIs with tight flash and RAM.
Watch out: the table must be regenerated whenever the key set changes, and unknown inputs must still be verified against the stored key.
Seen in HAProxy: 2c3139489c 2018-12-10 The HPACK encoder packs all 54 static header names into a 666-byte stream sorted by length (then frequency) with a 32-entry per-length index: slightly faster, and it now finds all names instead of 10, saving 25-30 bytes per encoded message. • 19def65228 2024-01-10 `http_get_status_idx()` uses a perfect hash into a 32-entry `uchar` table instead of a sparse switch, saving more than 800 bytes of code and data; 3e31e2927c adds the generator and 41f28b3c53 regenerates it for new codes. • Also seen in curl (curl-011): db8866fad9 2020-05-11 The `protocols[]` table reordered by survey popularity (HTTPS, HTTP, FTP...); the author notes the gain is not measurable but the least likely matches now come last. • b2d8f3f7f9 2023-11-17 Scheme lookup becomes a perfect hash generated by d6447b1bd2 (`scripts/schemetable.c`), one scheme per slot. • 522ea5420f 2024-03-18 "save cpu cycles": `http_header()` checks length with `sizeof` before compare and switches on `hd[0]` instead of a long if/else chain. 93e91e965e 2025-10-08 HTTP/2 `on_header()` checks name length before `strncmp`. • 2687751471 2026-08-24 Scheme lookup: generated mod-59 hash plus confirm replaced by length dispatch and character compares, 31% faster; 23d2782bee 2026-11-17 finishes with a per-length `MKPROT(a,b,c,d)` switch. • 283db12e05 2026-04-08 Time-zone names: linear search to bsearch over 69 entries; e7f76c782b 2026-09-17 weekday, month and zone names found "without loops", dateparser test "almost twice as fast"; 2c4d103bac 2026-11-03 removes the tables and bsearch for a `switch` on `MKNAME3` packed values. • d9ab46a9ce 2026-11-09 Cookie attribute names matched by `switch(nlen)` first, then at most one or two `strncasecmp`; cookierecv test 10-13% faster. • Also seen in Netty (netty-006): 2ac79dcb74 2010-04-16 `HttpClientCodec` switches on `method.getName().charAt(0)` ('H' for HEAD, 'C' for CONNECT) before calling `equals`. a979433f91 2009-06-15 uses `switch (line.charAt(0))` instead of `startsWith`, and f059f91ff4 2009-06-15 restructures the header loop the same way. • 93b144b7b4 2017-11-11 `HttpMethod.valueOf` uses a perfect hash (`hashCode() >>> 6` masked to 16 slots): about 10% faster (known methods 29.2 to 31.8 ops/µs, mixed 21.1 to 25.6). b7ceeb1797 2019-01-30 added an `==` fast path in `equals`. 664f20315c 2025-04-02 compares against interned "GET"/"POST" first. • 2e433889b2 2019-02-04 `DateFormatter.tryParseMonth` dispatches on characters directly instead of trying 12 `regionMatches` calls: `parseHttpDate` 3-15% faster (Dec: 3.78M to 4.34M ops/s). • 2ed95c906a 2022-10-03 `HpackStaticTable` uses two perfect-hash tables (name, and name+value): lookupHttp went from 16.0 to 10.5 ns and nameOnlyMatch from 13.7 to 3.2 ns. cce3d5b636 2024-01-31 then dispatches pseudo-headers with `switch(length)` plus a reference compare against the interned constants. • 613cfcb600 2020-11-30 The QPACK static table maps each name to the list of its positions: findFieldNameRefForSingleMatch went from 4.86M to 36.3M ops/s. 85ec20ecbd 2020-12-21 makes the HPACK lookup return on the first name mismatch, because equal names are adjacent. • eec0b5d514 2025-05-21, 0a11fc1fdb 2025-07-04 and 54b8663619 2026-02-05 replaced small resident `Map`s and factory objects (TLS groups, key material, `HttpContentCompressor` encodings) with a string `switch`, which is faster and keeps no map in memory. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-008): c64d0cd5ce2 2019-01-09 SQL keyword lookup uses a generated perfect hash; raw parsing time dropped about 20%. • 80f8eb79e24 2020-10-11 Unicode normalization quick check via perfect hash: 30% faster for NFC and 50% for NFKC. • 783f0cc64dc 2020-10-23 Unicode composition and decomposition via perfect hash in the backend: 30-40x for NFC/NFKC recomposition at 35 kB of binary; frontend libpq keeps binary search to stay small. • 27bdec06841 2025-03-15 Case mapping uses generated nested range branches yielding a 16-bit offset into one shared table; the author measured it against radix trees and perfect hashing and found it faster and smaller. • Also seen in Varnish (varnish-009): f4dffe593b 2021-05-10 `http_hdr_flags()` uses gperf-derived `http_asso_values[256]` indexed by the 4th character plus length into a 39-slot table, replacing per-header loops over `tbl/http_headers.h` in `http_isfiltered()` and `http_DoConnection()`; removed `HTTP_Init()`. • e40007dfc2 2022-11-08 Grew the perfect hash to every entry of `tbl/http_headers.h`, not only those with filter flags (also closing a correctness hole).

### haproxy-010 — Right-size the container: compact trees, bitmasks for tiny sets, lists for duplicates
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Match the container to the shape of the data: a bitmask for a small bounded set, a 2-pointer compact tree for large or cold indexes, and plain duplicate lists when one key carries many entries.
Why it is faster: Node size multiplies by the number of entries; going from a 40-56 byte node to 16 bytes saves hundreds of megabytes on millions of keys, and writing less memory can even speed up loading. A 64-bit mask beats any tree for 64 or fewer members in both memory and lookup. When a tree almost always holds a single key with many duplicates, list-based duplicate handling is as fast or faster than a subtree, and walking duplicates with "next dup" skips a key compare per step.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `EnumSet`/bitflags for small enum sets; SmallVec for usually-tiny arrays. • Data / databases: posting lists for low-cardinality secondary indexes; prefix-compressed or succinct indexes for cold data; null bitmaps in rows. • Embedded: bitmasks instead of linked sets on RAM-constrained MCUs.
Watch out: compact structures can be slower on some key shapes (long strings here); choose them where memory dominates, and keep fast structures on hot, small indexes.
Seen in HAProxy: e36b3b60b3 2025-01-12 Pattern references moved to compact trees: 280 MB saved for 17.7M IPs, startup 19.2 s to 18.2 s, with long strings 9% slower, which was accepted. Names, GUIDs and IDs followed (91258fb9d8 GUID 56 to 32 B, 413e903a22, d0d60a007d, d18d972b1f). • ceaf8c1220 2025-09-12 Idle-connection trees ("almost always a single key and many duplicates") use compact trees with list duplicates: node 56 to 32 B, per-thread server struct 96 to 72 B, as fast or faster. • d0d60a007d 2025-07-15 `proxy_find_by_name()` walks with `cebis_next_dup()`, removing a `strcmp` per `use_backend`: 3.09M to 3.18M rps (static) and 2.47M to 2.59M (dynamic) with 100k backends. • 6a92b14cc1 2025-08-29 Proxy log-steps moved from a tree to a 64-bit mask, removing a significant memory cost in defaults sections.

### haproxy-011 — After an exact lookup, iterate only the matching key's duplicates
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy
Principle: In an index that allows duplicate keys, iterate with "next duplicate" after an exact match rather than "next", so a collision never turns into a walk over the rest of the structure.
Why it is faster: "Next" continues into unrelated keys and must re-compare each one; on crafted inputs with thousands of colliding records, the per-record walk becomes quadratic. "Next duplicate" stops at the key boundary, bounding the worst case to the number of true matches.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: range scans that stop at the key boundary; multimap `equal_range`. • Native / systems: hash chains that stop at the first key mismatch. • Web / backend: any lookup over attacker-controlled keys (DoS hardening).
Watch out: this requires duplicates to be stored contiguously or linked; a structure that scatters equal keys needs a different bound.
Seen in HAProxy: dc537b91a6 2026-09-29 DNS response validation changed `eb32_next()` to `eb32_next_dup()`, bounding the worst case for responses with about 4000 colliding records. • 029d75df1e 2024-09-20 Duplicate-name checks walk `ebpt_prev_dup` neighbours only (config check time divided by 4 for 2k servers per backend).

### haproxy-012 — Iterate only the items that need work: keep explicit active lists and dense survivors
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy · Also: curl
Principle: Maintain a list of the items that are actually blocked, ready or dirty, and compact survivors densely after a pass, so periodic handlers cost O(active) instead of O(total).
Why it is faster: A handler that loops over every stream or every file descriptor to find the few that need attention pays for the idle majority on every call. Enqueueing items when their state changes moves that cost to the (rare) transition. Writing survivors back densely after a batch makes the follow-up pass shorter and branch-free, which matters most when it runs under a lock.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: dirty lists in UI renderers instead of diffing the whole tree; ready lists in connection managers. • Native / systems: event loops that track active connections instead of scanning a fixed fd table; GC and entity cleanup that compact live objects in place. • Data / databases: dirty-page lists for checkpointing instead of scanning the buffer pool.
Watch out: every state transition must update the list, or items are lost; the list adds a membership field to each item.
Seen in HAProxy: 9d4b56b88e 2017-11-06 After running a batch, surviving tasks are written back densely (`local_tasks[final_tasks_count++] = t`) and the locked requeue loop iterates only those: 644k to 657k req/s with 2 threads. • 4b42dd4ae0 2024-12-05 The QUIC mux keeps a `recv_list` of streams blocked on demux instead of looping over all streams, and e296585ae9 a `purg_list` of completed streams instead of walking the stream tree. • 75b335abc7 2024-07-15 A thread-local `fd_highest` bounds re-registration loops instead of scanning `maxsock` entries: on an 80-core machine with 2M maxconn, CLI readiness went from 1580 ms to 10 ms. • Also seen in curl (curl-004): 4d53dc5d80 2010-08-09 `curl_multi_info_read` reads a `msglist` filled at completion instead of looping over all handles: "We MUST make this function as fast as possible as it will be polled frequently... fully O(1)." • 3c8c873252 2014-09-02 A `multi->pending` list holds handles waiting for a connection slot, which "avoids a rather hefty slow-down especially notable if you add many handles". • 843b3baa3e 2023-03-26 PENDING and MSGSENT handles leave the main list: "the main loop does not need to iterate over these handles. A performance improvement." • 0d1e43af8d 2025-03-07 QUIC backends stop iterating `multi->process` (646ffb591a, 469c037fcf for quiche and OpenSSL-QUIC). 73c0180cb6 2026-09-02 later replaces the completion list with a `msgsent` bitset (see the ID-and-bitset entry).

### haproxy-013 — Keep indexes minimal: coalesce ranges, and drop structures that only reorder
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Merge overlapping or adjacent intervals on insert so the index holds unique keys, and remove intermediate structures whose only job is to reorder data once the destination can absorb disorder.
Why it is faster: Overlapping intervals stored separately inflate the tree, force duplicate-key handling and complicate accounting. Coalescing keeps the structure small and lookups and frees cheap. A temporary sort tree in front of a buffer costs an insert and a removal per item; a gap-tolerant buffer that writes at the right offset makes it pure overhead.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: TCP SACK scoreboards; free-space maps in allocators and filesystems. • Data / databases: merged time ranges in TSDB or calendar indexes; write-at-offset files instead of sort-then-write. • Embedded: bitmap reassembly buffers for fragmented packets.
Watch out: coalescing loses the identity of individual ranges; only do it when nothing downstream needs them separately.
Seen in HAProxy: ae3e768d32 2024-10-02 QUIC stream ACK ranges are merged on insert and the tree switches to unique keys, which also enabled out-of-order ACK accounting in the mux window (f6599cf5a6) and restored lost throughput. • f50425c021 2025-10-20 Received CRYPTO frames go straight into a non-contiguous buffer that tolerates gaps, and the temporary ordering tree was removed (-72/+14 lines).

### haproxy-014 — Use the O(1) operation your list allows: splice with known tails, append instead of reverse
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy · Also: curl
Principle: When a chain's head and tail are known, move it between lists in constant time; when a list has a tail pointer, append directly instead of prepending and reversing.
Why it is faster: Moving a chain element by element is O(n) and touches every node; a splice rewrites four pointers. Prepend-then-reverse is a singly-linked-list idiom; with a doubly linked list the reversal pass is pure overhead.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `list_splice` in intrusive lists; merging free-list chunks in allocators. • Data / databases: moving page chains between LRU lists in buffer managers. • Web / backend: accumulator-plus-reverse in functional code replaced by a deque.
Watch out: a splice is only correct if the cached tail pointer is always kept accurate.
Seen in HAProxy: eccb97f60e 2023-11-16 The shared cache moves a row between its hot and available lists using the existing `last_reserved` pointer instead of iterating blocks; 610b67fd8b flattened the two-level reserve loop. • 869163e10e 2026-07-22 Proxies and servers are appended at the tail and the post-parse reversal is dropped (029add93ae). • Also seen in curl (curl-007): ee8016b3de 2017-11-04 The curl tool keeps `url_get`, `url_out` and `url_ul` cursors instead of rescanning the URL list for each `-o` or `-T`: speedup of "several magnitudes on 100K URLs". • 09b9fc9009 2013-08-02 The multi's handle list gains a tail pointer `easylp`. • 55ecaa52b7 2026-09-12 The pause writer appends at the tail and consumes from the head; its flush had been O(N²). • 2cb089c698 2026-10-11 `Curl_slist_duplicate` and `cookie_list()` build with a tail pointer; 2910040880 2026-10-10 tool header buffering switches to a list with a cached tail; 3341da7dc2 2026-09-09 happy eyeballs gets a tail pointer.

### haproxy-015 — Replace an iterated update with a closed-form approximation
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When applying the same incremental update N times (a moving average, a decay), derive a single formula for N steps at once and accept a small, measured error.
Why it is faster: The loop costs O(N) per batch; the closed form costs O(1) regardless of N. A first-order approximation is often accurate to well under a percent, which is irrelevant for statistics used in heuristics.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: batched updates of decaying rate meters in metrics libraries. • Native / systems: smoothing with variable time steps in game physics. • Data / databases: adaptive load estimators in query planners.
Watch out: check the error bound over the full range of N you will see; a linearization that is fine for small N can diverge for large ones.
Seen in HAProxy: 627505d36a 2018-10-17 `swrate_add_scaled()` updates a sliding-window average for N samples at once with `*sum + v*s - (*sum + n)*s/n`; the authors measured under 0.2% error.

### haproxy-016 — Mark the end in-band and let the iterator skip structural filler
Lens: algorithms · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Put an "end of message" flag on the last element so scans stop without peeking ahead, and make the iterator itself skip unused or placeholder entries so callers do not test for them.
Why it is faster: Without an in-band marker, loops either peek at the next element or consult a separate message-level flag on every step. Centralising the skip of unused slots in the iterator removes a test from every caller loop and the code that goes with it.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: NIC descriptor rings with an end-of-packet bit. • Data / databases: tombstone-skipping iterators in storage engines. • Embedded: framed buffers with a last-fragment flag.
Watch out: every producer must set the flag consistently, including on error and truncation paths, or consumers run past the end.
Seen in HAProxy: 3a8bc71e46 2026-07-08 HTX blocks gained an end-of-message flag (a771d82f35); 3288fa10b3 stops the FCGI payload-size loop at that block. • fb968bb018 2026-07-08 `htx_get_next/prev/first_blk()` skip unused blocks themselves (83024657c8), deleting 74 lines of caller tests.

### haproxy-017 — Give each thread its own run queue and timer queue; share only what must be shared
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Most tasks are only ever run by one thread, so give every thread private run and wait queues that need no lock, and handle the rare cross-thread case through per-thread inboxes or by messaging the owner.
Why it is faster: One global queue with one lock is a contention point that grows with the thread count, and its cache line bounces on every insert. Private queues need no lock, keep their trees smaller (lookup depth drops by about log(threads)) and keep emptiness checks local. When some sharing is unavoidable, one inbox per thread with its own lock turns one hot lock into many cold ones; the final step is to make the first thread that queues a timer its owner and let others only send it a wake flag.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: per-core run queues with work stealing (Go, Tokio, ForkJoin); per-CPU hrtimers and timer wheels. • Web / backend: per-worker timer wheels in an event-loop server, with messaging for cross-worker timers. • Data / databases: per-core task queues in thread-per-core engines (Seastar/ScyllaDB style).
Watch out: private queues can leave one thread overloaded while others idle; keep a cheap rebalancing path (sampling a few other threads) and make cross-thread wakeups explicit.
Seen in HAProxy: f6e6dc12cd 2018-05-18 Per-thread run queue `rqueue_local[]`: tasks bound to one thread go there with no lock, and the global queue is used only for multi-thread tasks. • b20aa9eef3 2018-10-15 Per-thread timer wait queues; the global tree and its lock are used only for tasks that can run on several threads. On 12 threads, time in the wait-queue lock fell from 4.7 s to 11 ms and throughput rose 1.5%. 4815c8cbfe did the same for the fd cache. • 6f78038d72 2022-06-16 One shared (inbox) run queue per thread with its own lock (b17dd6cc19) instead of a global `rq_lock`; c958c70ec8 drops a global mask because a local emptiness test suffices; 319d136ff9 switches back to plain trees: 570k to 585k RPS. b0e7712fb2 gives shared wait queues one instance per thread group, because "the WQ lock uses a huge amount of CPU alone". • 0988b9c773 2026-04-28 Shared timers get an owner thread (encoded in the tid field) that keeps them in its private tree; other threads only set a wake flag. aaee6c463c removes the wait-queue lock and per-group wait queues entirely; b9aa1c0e64 redispatches an expired task to one of 3 random threads whose run queue is less than half as long; 78cc7dacbb skips that when single-threaded.

### haproxy-018 — Keep timers and run queues in ordered intrusive trees
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Store pending timers and runnable tasks in an ordered tree whose node is embedded in the task, so insertion is O(log n) with no allocation and "next to expire" is the leftmost node.
Why it is faster: Linear timer lists cost O(n) per insertion; a general-purpose balanced tree can still burn CPU on rebalancing and allocation. An intrusive radix-style tree costs no allocation, no rebalancing and gives the earliest deadline in O(1) from the left edge. Keeping non-expiring tasks out of the tree and using one relative-time tree instead of several further reduces work.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: timer heaps or wheels in event loops; intrusive trees and lists in kernels. • Embedded: RTOS timer lists replaced by an ordered structure with no allocation. • Data / databases: lock-wait and lease-expiry queues.
Watch out: an ordered tree still costs O(log n) per change; for millions of timers that are mostly extended, combine it with lazy requeueing (next entry) or a timer wheel.
Seen in HAProxy: 5e698ef509 2006-05-02 Non-expiring tasks go into a dedicated queue instead of the timer structure. • 964c936b04 2007-01-07 A red-black tree for the wait queue, replaced by 96bcfd75aa because the rbtree "consumes a lot of CPU", then by an ebtree in 9789f7bd68 (-939 lines); 58b458d8ba makes the run queue an ebtree too. • e35c94a748 2009-03-21 One relative timer tree instead of 4: +0.5%.

### haproxy-019 — Requeue timers lazily: treat the tree key as a lower bound
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy · Also: Tokio
Principle: Timeouts are mostly extended and rarely fire, so leave a timer at its old, earlier position and fix it only if the expiry scan reaches it before its real deadline; never requeue on every I/O event.
Why it is faster: Moving a timer on every activity costs a delete plus an insert, typically several times per request. With the key as a lower bound, most timers are never moved: they are either cancelled or bounced once during the scan. Separate tree nodes for the run queue and the wait queue mean waking a task does not unlink its timer. The cost is occasional spurious expiry checks, which is far cheaper than systematic requeueing. Keeping the front of the queue exact still matters, or the poller wakes up just to reposition entries.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: idle timeouts for millions of keep-alive connections; cache TTL refresh without reheaping. • Native / systems: lazy deletion in priority queues; tickless kernels. • Embedded: watchdog or protocol timers that are re-armed on every packet.
Watch out: the expiry handler must re-check the real deadline before acting, and the earliest key must stay accurate or the poller wakes up too early.
Seen in HAProxy: ce44f12c1e 2008-07-05 A timer with the same expiry as the last queued one (common at ms resolution) is inserted next to it as a duplicate: +1%. • 4136522527 2009-03-08 Tasks are requeued only when the new timeout is earlier; misplaced nodes are bounced during the expiry scan. 4726f53794 adds a second tree node per task, saving 1-3 delete/insert cycles per session. • 98c6121ee5 2011-09-10 Skip scanning an empty run queue. • c49ba52524 2019-12-11 Expired timers are woken right after poll returns, removing a systematic second `poll()` per timer; 77015abe0b cleans up the front of the wait queue so the next expiry is exact (about 15% fewer wakeups with many health checks); 2ae84e445d passes a "wake" flag to the poller instead of computing zero timeouts. • d7111e7ace 2023-02-14 The stream task is no longer requeued after every I/O, because the rare spurious expiry is cheaper than the systematic requeue. • Also seen in Tokio (tokio-105): ae67851f11 2020-11-23 "Cached vs true timeouts": `extend_expiration` does a `compare_exchange_weak` that succeeds only if the deadline grew. Otherwise `reregister` takes the lock. The deadline word uses `STATE_DEREGISTERED = u64::MAX` and `STATE_PENDING_FIRE = MAX-1`, and `mark_pending` CASes from a timestamp, refusing to fire early. • d96bbf0465 2023-02-09 The deadline was stored in both `Sleep` and `TimerEntry`. It is now kept only in `TimerEntry`. 61fcc3bc0b 2023-11-05 The driver reads `wheel.elapsed()` instead of keeping its own cache. • df77063485 2024-05-15 Removed `TimerShared.true_when`, one `AtomicU64` per timer, once the state word could carry the needed information.

### haproxy-020 — Use a lightweight FIFO lane for callbacks that need no priority or timer
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy
Principle: I/O completions and handoffs do not need ordering or timeouts, so push them on a plain per-thread list instead of the ordered, locked run queue; resumed work that was already ordered once can skip the queue too.
Why it is faster: Inserting into an ordered tree under a lock costs O(log n) plus contention; appending to a list costs a few pointer writes. A cross-thread handoff becomes a lock-free list append. The trade-off is fairness: work that bypasses the ordered queue can jump ahead, so the bypass is reverted once regular wakeups became cheap.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: the microtask vs macrotask split in JS runtimes. • Native / systems: softirq/tasklet vs timer work in kernels; separate ready and delayed queues in async runtimes; Go's `runnext` slot. • Embedded: deferred-procedure queues for ISR follow-up work.
Watch out: a fast lane with no ordering can starve the ordered work; give it its own budget (next entry).
Seen in HAProxy: b0bdae7b88 2018-05-18 Tasklets run ASAP from a per-thread list and share their first fields with tasks; mux and stream I/O moved to tasklet subscriptions (7505f94f90, a1411e62e4). • 2bd65a781e 2019-09-24 The accept-queue rings use tasklets: +4% accept rate. eb8c2c69fa adds `task_kill()`, which inserts into the shared urgent tasklet list instead of a locked wakeup (09e0d9ecbc): 319k to 328k req/s with about 130k takeovers/s. • a4e39890f3 2022-04-22 `task_instant_wakeup()` sends a resumed task straight to its owner's lock-free list; for server queues (27fab1dcbc) 4k connections on 16 threads went from 40-80k to 570k req/s. 3fdacdddaf reverted to the normal wakeup for fairness once per-thread queues made it cheap (580k RPS).

### haproxy-021 — Split runnable work into classes with CPU budgets; isolate heavy and self-requeuing work
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Run urgent, normal, bulk and heavy work from separate queues with budgeted shares, demote work that keeps re-waking itself, allow at most one heavy item per loop, and let unused budget flow to the next class.
Why it is faster: One FIFO lets a single 700 µs task (a TLS handshake) or a self-rearming bulk transfer add milliseconds to every cheap task behind it. Budgeted classes give latency isolation without starving throughput, and batching bulk work together also improves cache hit ratio. Shares are tuned from measurements; voluntary yielders must not be punished, and a woken task must not inherit the current low-priority class.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: separate executors for interactive and batch work; offloading CPU-heavy jobs from a Node or async-Rust event loop. • Data / databases: query admission classes; separate queues for expensive queries. • Embedded: a single RT slot for audio or control callbacks ahead of best-effort work.
Watch out: fixed shares tuned on one workload can starve another; keep them configurable, and watch for priority inheritance through callbacks.
Seen in HAProxy: a62917b890 2020-01-30 Three tasklet classes; a17664d829 auto-demotes self-waking tasklets to bulk, keeping the CLI responsive at 50 Gbps with 40k connections. c633607c06: mixed traffic went from 11 kcps + 36 Gbps with a 400 ms CLI to 17 kcps + 35 Gbps with a 35 ms CLI. 1dfc9bbdc6 and 3ef7a190b0 retune shares; e7723bddd7 adds `tune.sched.low-latency`, halving latency with very deep run queues. • 74dea8caea 2021-02-26 `TASK_HEAVY` cut SSL-stress response time from 14 ms to 3.0 ms; 401135cee6 adds a heavy class with about 1% bandwidth; 9205ab31d2 marks handshakes heavy; 76390dac06 runs at most one heavy task per loop: 2.75 to 1.38 ms, 99.9th percentile 58.1 to 2.9 ms. • ba4c7a1597 2023-02-16 The heavy budget adapts: 1 in low-latency mode, otherwise 1 + 1 per 1024 queued tasks (1 per 128 when idle), staying under 1 ms end-to-end on 56 cores. • 282b9b7d16 2026-03-21 Cooperative yielders are no longer demoted to bulk (about 10% better average latency); 9852d5be26 stops wakeups inheriting the current class; ed6a4bc807 rebalances to 40% normal and 3% bulk. • 40ad8a129c 2026-06-24 A real-time lane (e37a20d330) serves exactly one RT tasklet ahead of all classes per pass: the wakeup latency of a 10 ms periodic task under 10 Gbps load dropped from 220 µs to 1.8 µs (about 550 ns in low-latency mode).

### haproxy-022 — Bound the batch run per loop iteration, and re-derive the bound when the architecture changes
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Cap how many tasks run between two polls so I/O and other queues are serviced regularly, run each task at most once per pass, and recompute the cap when the scheduler's structure changes.
Why it is faster: Large batches amortize per-iteration overhead; small batches flush I/O sooner and shorten lock hold times. The optimum moved with the design: with one shared scheduler it shrank as threads grew (about 280/√threads), but with per-thread schedulers that rule made 256-thread batches only 14 tasks long, and overhead dominated. Tasks that wake each other can ping-pong forever inside one pass; a per-task loop stamp stops that.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: capping microtasks per tick in a JS runtime; Kafka `max.poll.records`. • Native / systems: epoll `maxevents` and NAPI budgets; re-tune batch sizes when moving from a global queue to per-core queues. • Embedded: cooperative schedulers that yield after N jobs.
Watch out: a batch size is a property of the whole design, not a constant; re-measure after structural changes.
Seen in HAProxy: 8a48f67526 2017-11-14 At most 200 tasks per call; b4a4d9aed4 applies the limit to applets; 1599b80360 makes it `tune.runqueue-depth`; 673867c357 merges the applet scheduler into the task scheduler so applets share nice values and lose their big lock. • 4327d0ac00 2021-02-19 Depth 200 to 40: up to +18% (613k to 722k at 16 threads), RTT 1.62 to 1.40 ms. 060a761248 defaults to 280/sqrt(nbthread); 1691ba3693 splits a queue between 1x and 2x the budget into two equal halves. • 7d40b3134a 2026-03-21 Each task carries the low 16 bits of the loop counter in a struct hole; meeting an already-stamped task ends that queue for the pass, so ping-ponging tasks no longer loop. • d8c2cb4bf6 2026-11-12 With per-thread schedulers, the default depth is a flat 280 again.

### haproxy-023 — Give long operations a per-call budget and make them yield
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy · Also: memcached
Principle: Any loop whose length depends on input or configuration (rule lists, purges, peer updates, frames in a buffer, interpreted code) must process a bounded quantum, save its position and requeue itself.
Why it is faster: An unbounded loop holds the CPU and often a lock, inflating everyone else's latency and tripping watchdogs. A quantum caps the damage per call; the work still completes, just interleaved. Budgets can be scaled to the situation (smaller under a global lock with many threads) and can carry a separate allowance for suspicious input, which demotes abusers.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: chunking long JS tasks with `scheduler.yield()`; gRPC servers limiting messages per read. • Data / databases: batched deletes with LIMIT loops; time-sliced vacuum and compaction; executors yielding every N rows. • Native / systems: incremental GC slices; Redis-style incremental rehash.
Watch out: yielding requires that the saved position stay valid across the yield; objects can be deleted in between.
Seen in HAProxy: 3cfaa8d1e0 2020-10-16 Expired tasks picked from the global wait queue are limited per pass; dfe79251da makes the stick-table purge visit at most twice the number of nodes to delete (128x faster in the worst case); 86c6a9221a bounds a shared-cache lock loop. • fa7b4f6691 2022-01-19 The CLI yields after each pipelined command, so mass deletions no longer trip the watchdog; 8bd146d8af limits peers to 200 updates per call. • 231d3d32be 2024-05-15 Under the global Lua lock, the 10k-instruction yield budget is divided by the thread count, with a floor of 500. • 0846638f7f 2025-01-30 `tune.max-rules-at-once` (default 50) yields during long rule evaluation and resumes at the saved rule. • 5d0f5f8168 2026-03-21 H2 caps frames per wakeup and has a separate RST budget; under an RST flood, SSL response time went from 95 ms to 1.6 ms and the connection rate held at 28.0k instead of 11.8k. 53b7150357 caps synchronous sends per wakeup at 2. • 71a914e0ff 2026-10-01 Server purges under thread isolation yield every 50 servers (2e056f3c42). • Also seen in memcached (memcached-101): 7f09e20ba5 2009-01-27 Hash expansion moves a tunable `hash_bulk_move` number of buckets per lock hold. • e31a591210 2014-04-17 Caps the items the crawler inspects per run (`lru_crawler_tocrawl`). b689a627ea 2015-01-07 "cap aggressiveness of LRU maintainer", and 9d51fdd2de 2017-01-02 cuts the juggle loop from 1000 to 500 iterations. • 367385c2fd 2016-12-30 Lets crawler runs be limited in length. e01bc5cca5 2017-06-04 limits internal crawls to the largest class size so a crawl ends under constant sets. 37bfa412de 2018-02-12 limits a metadump to the item count at start.

### haproxy-024 — Run newly woken follow-up work in the same pass, right after its producer
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O, Tokio
Principle: When a task wakes another during this loop iteration, run it before polling again, and insert it right after the current task so it uses the data while it is still in cache.
Why it is faster: If a woken task waits for the next poll round, every I/O event costs two loop turns and the data goes cold. Draining runnable work while budget remains halves poll calls. Inserting at the head of the local list, in order, keeps producer and consumer back to back, cutting cache misses and wakeup latency.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: microtasks before the next macrotask in JS; coroutine schedulers resuming ready coroutines immediately. • Native / systems: Go's `runnext` and Tokio's LIFO slot; run-to-completion pipelines in DPDK. • Embedded: direct task handoff in an RTOS.
Watch out: combined with no budget this starves I/O; always bound the extra work per pass.
Seen in HAProxy: 5c8be272c7 2020-06-19 `process_runnable_tasks()` keeps going while budget remains: half as many calls under the poll loop and to `poll()`, 2-3% faster on some workloads. • ad548b54a7 2022-06-29 `tasklet_wakeup_after()` inserts at the head of the thread's list in order; the QUIC datagram handler (1b0707f3e7) uses it to "drastically reduce the latency" and Rx-buffer overruns. • fcab647613 2024-10-12 The H2 mux wakes stream consumers right after itself: average wakeup latency 1900 to 51 µs, 21% fewer L2 misses, 2-3% more throughput. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-095): cab2e1bcc3 2014-09-04 The timeout list is detached before firing, so only entries added before the run are invoked. • c2a52a44dc 2014-09-08 `zero_timeout` moved into the socket loop and is drained before poll: `while (run_pending(loop) + h2o_timeout_run_all(...) != 0);`. • c92b314f2c 2015-04-06 "process pending callbacks ASAP (for every timeout callback)". • Also seen in Tokio (tokio-010): ed5a94eb2d 2019-10-19 `Worker { next: Cell<Option<Task>> }`: `push` moves the previous occupant into the ring, `local_pop` takes `next` first, and `push_yield` bypasses the slot so yielding tasks go to the back. • caa7e180e4 2020-03-28 `Core.lifo_slot` is checked before the ring. LIFO tasks run inside one `coop::budget` without resetting it, and once the budget is exhausted the task goes to `run_queue.push_back`. • 70364b7079 2023-05-15 charged one budget unit per LIFO dispatch. 9eb3f5b556 2023-05-23 replaced that with `MAX_LIFO_POLLS_PER_TICK = 3` and a per-core `lifo_enabled` flag, because 128 units was too generous for tasks with 5-10 µs polls. b023522a37 2022-08-24 added a `disable_lifo_slot` builder option. • 873cb8ae2f 2024-05-30 `block_in_place` moves the LIFO task into the stealable run queue before handing the core off. • eeb55c733b 2026-04-03 tried making the slot itself stealable (`AtomicNotified` in the queue, `push_lifo` via `swap`), and 967f5715a7 2026-05-04 reverted it. 8a2408edcf 2026-10-23 records each LIFO poll separately in the poll-time histogram so tuning data stays accurate.

### haproxy-025 — Summarize "who has work" in per-thread or per-queue bits
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Keep one bit per thread or per queue, set on enqueue and cleared when drained, so "any work for me?" and "which queue?" are a single load and test; drop the summary if it is more expensive to maintain than to derive.
Why it is faster: A global "work pending" counter makes every thread believe it has work and skip sleeping; checking N queues costs N loads. A bit per thread lets a thread test only its own bit (often with no lock) and sleep in the poller when idle, and skip a lock entirely when its bit in the global queue mask is clear. Maintaining exact masks across threads costs atomic writes, so once queues became per-thread the masks were replaced by direct tests.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: the Linux O(1) scheduler priority bitmap; ready masks in RTOS kernels with ffs/ctz. • Web / backend: per-worker ready bits in a work-stealing pool. • Data / databases: per-shard dirty bits for a background flusher.
Watch out: a shared mask is itself one cache line written by all threads; prefer per-thread flags when writers are many.
Seen in HAProxy: 3911ee85df 2017-11-14 `active_tasks_mask` replaces the run-queue count in the polling decision (8a48f67526); 69553fe62c does the same for the fd cache. • 1ee55fddea 2018-12-14 The global run queue is scanned only if the thread's bit is set in `global_tasks_mask`, saving a lock/unlock per scheduler call. • cfbb3e6560 2019-05-29 `active_tasks_mask` was removed as "costly to maintain" in favour of `thread_has_tasks()`; 49f90bf148 adds `tl_class_mask`, one bit per non-empty tasklet class.

### haproxy-026 — Wake exactly the thread that has work, and let one waker do the broadcasting
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When handing work to a sleeping thread, signal only that thread through its own wake channel, and when many producers would wake the same subscribers, let one thread at a time own the wake-up duty.
Why it is faster: Without a targeted wakeup, handed-off work waits for the target's poll timeout; with a broadcast, every thread wakes to find nothing. A per-thread pipe or flag fixes latency without the herd. Per-thread sleeping and notified flags avoid a shared mask written by everyone. When every producer walks the subscriber list, contention grows with producers × readers; a single waker guarded by an exchange flag, plus a pending counter, keeps it linear without losing events.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: an eventfd per event-loop thread (libuv `uv_async_send`); signalling one worker's condition variable instead of broadcasting. • Web / backend: a single flusher goroutine for pub/sub fan-out. • Embedded: per-task notification bits instead of a shared event group.
Watch out: with a single waker, events arriving during the walk must be recorded (pending counter) and re-checked, or wakeups are lost.
Seen in HAProxy: fd83f0bfa4 2018-03-19 A stopgap forced wakeup after dequeued connections owned by sleeping threads showed a performance drop; 79321b95a8 adds a per-thread pipe to wake a thread sleeping in the poller. • e7475c8e79 2022-06-20 A per-thread `TH_FL_SLEEPING` flag replaces the shared sleeping-thread mask, and `wake_thread()` does a single atomic fetch-or; dce4ad755f remembers notifications per thread so remote threads no longer modify a shared mask. • c7bd7a68e4 2024-03-02 Ring writers no longer all walk the reader list: one waker at a time, guarded by an exchange flag with a pending counter, eliminated watchdog triggers on a 24-core EPYC.

### haproxy-027 — Make wakeups readiness-driven, not speculative
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Instead of waking a consumer "just in case", let it subscribe: if the resource is already ready, schedule it immediately, otherwise arm the event; skip wakeups for consumers that declared they will not consume or that are already ready.
Why it is faster: Every speculative wakeup costs a scheduler round trip, cache refills and often a failed I/O attempt. A smart subscribe gives the same latency when data is ready and nothing at all when it is not, and removes the need for blind self-wakeups throughout the stack.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: promise APIs that resolve synchronously when a value is ready; skipping `notify()` when no waiter is registered. • Native / systems: condition variables with predicate checks; Netty/libuv write paths that only arm interest on EAGAIN. • Embedded: enabling an interrupt only when the FIFO is actually empty.
Watch out: removing a "just in case" wakeup can expose a missing event somewhere else; HAProxy reverted one such optimization (stuck streams), so test edge cases such as shutdowns and errors.
Seen in HAProxy: 908d26fd03 2018-10-25 Redundant send attempts and tasklet wakeups removed (33a09a5f2a); 0dfccb20f5 stops `stream_int_notify()` waking the tasklet. • 7e59c0a5e1 2020-02-28 `conn_subscribe()` schedules immediately when already ready: in close mode epoll_ctl went from 399,464 to 205,739 and failed recvfrom from 100,000 to 2,651. 493d9dc6ba and 2c1f37d353 convert mux-h1 wakeups to subscribe: tasklet wakeups per request 3 to 1, many per-request call counts roughly halved. • 04f03e15c3 2022-06-01 No applet wakeup for send when it declared it will not consume. • c76e072e43 2025-11-06 The QUIC backend start wakes the I/O callback only if the connection is not already ready (with 0-RTT it always is); 25559e7055 defers transport start until after mux creation.

### haproxy-028 — Spread periodic and background work across threads and time; desynchronize it
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy · Also: NATS
Principle: Avoid having all threads (or all instances) act at the same moment or on the same target: keep periodic tasks bound but migrate them toward a randomly sampled less-loaded thread, randomize start points, and jitter mass events over a window.
Why it is faster: Unbound timers in a global queue make every thread lock it to compute its sleep time and wake together (thundering herd). Comparing load with one random peer needs no global view. Identical start points make threads collide on the same locks and lines. Closing every idle connection at once causes a reconnection storm; a random factor per deadline smooths the peak.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: jittered cache TTLs and client retries; staggered cron jobs across replicas; rolling restarts with randomized drain. • Native / systems: worker pools that avoid a hot first worker; random victim selection in work stealing. • Data / databases: staggered TTL scans and compactions across nodes.
Watch out: randomness makes behaviour harder to reproduce; keep a deterministic mode for tests, and never jitter work that has a hard deadline.
Seen in HAProxy: d114f4a68f 2022-10-12 Health checks stay thread-bound but migrate if a random other thread has less than half the load: CPU -0.5-1%, 99.99th-percentile TTFB 32.96 ms to 4.9 ms, no more herd wakeups. 34e4085f8a places peer applets on the least-loaded thread; 555c192d14 wakes a random owner thread instead of the first. • b5d968d9b2 2022-04-08 `close-spread-time` spreads idle-connection closes and per-request close decisions randomly during soft-stop. • c454296f07 2024-07-17 Log-forwarding applets pick a random thread, then the least loaded of 3 (2513bd257f), and are recycled after N messages to rebalance (237849c911). • f87cf8b76e 2025-09-09 Eviction starts from a random shard; 658eaa4086 makes each thread group insert servers in a different order; ded2110ec6 assigns peer-sync threads backwards so they avoid the stick-table threads. • Also seen in NATS (nats-113): c5fbb63614 2021-12-29 Up to 1 s of random jitter on the ephemeral-consumer delete threshold, so mass timeouts don't fire together. • 607858f213 2022-03-29 Stream and consumer compaction tickers run at `compactInterval + rand(1 min)`. • f396d6d6ba 2024-04-27 The interest-state check after a snapshot is delayed by a random 5-15 s. • 886e9ee1bb 2024-10-31 The first file-store sync is randomized, to avoid "many of them trying to all sync at once, causing blips and contending dios". 4a6717d6a4 2024-11-14 narrows it to [interval/2, interval]. 455808b5c3 2024-12-05 adds up to 30 s of jitter to the stream state flush loop. • 24f709833d 2026-05-12 `updateLeafNodesEx` walks `acc.lleafs[(start+i)%n]` from `rand.Intn(n)`, avoiding lock convoys.

### haproxy-029 — Give each periodic maintenance task an owner thread instead of a run-anywhere janitor
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Bind each instance of a periodic task (expiry, purge, sync) to one thread and rotate the assignment across instances, or run one janitor per thread over that thread's own data.
Why it is faster: A run-anywhere task requeued from many threads contends on the global wait queue and on its own data lock, and a single global janitor must take every thread's locks in turn. A per-thread or per-shard owner needs neither, and halves cross-thread wakeups.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: per-partition maintenance threads; per-shard active expiry in caches. • Native / systems: per-arena GC sweeps; per-core timers in thread-per-core runtimes. • Web / backend: per-worker connection-pool reapers instead of one global reaper.
Watch out: pinned maintenance can overload its thread if one instance is much larger than the others; rotate or shard the work.
Seen in HAProxy: e05afda249 2025-09-10 Stick-table expiry tasks pinned to one thread with rotation: half the multi-thread wakeups, +12% request rate with 16 peers. ded2110ec6 does the same for peer sync (+1-3%) and 2ce5e0edcc for resolvers (CPU 11% to 7%); 8bc8a21b25 runs one expiry task per shard on different threads. • 3c923d075c 2026-05-28 Idle-connection purging moved to a per-thread task over a per-thread server tree (6f8dab2583 adds the back-pointer), with no global lock.

### haproxy-030 — Admit expensive operations through a bounded, load-aware queue
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Limit how many CPU-heavy operations run concurrently per thread, queue the rest in order, and shrink the concurrency you offer to new clients when a smoothed load metric exceeds a target.
Why it is faster: Starting too many heavy operations at once makes all of them miss their deadlines together and slows unrelated traffic. A per-thread limit with a FIFO keeps each operation finishing in time. A cheaply maintained peak-averaged run-queue length is a good overload signal; reducing advertised concurrency sheds new work at the protocol level before it costs CPU.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: semaphore-limited bcrypt or image jobs; adaptive concurrency limits; bulkheads. • Data / databases: connection admission queues (PgBouncer); throttling by queue depth. • Embedded: limiting concurrent flash erases.
Watch out: queued work still ages; combine with deadlines so the queue does not just delay inevitable timeouts.
Seen in HAProxy: 844a3bc25b 2023-08-31 `tune.max-checks-per-thread` queues health checks in FIFO order (slow OpenSSL 3 handshakes); cfc0bceeb5 and 016e189ea3 migrate checks off overloaded threads to one of 3 random threads; 3df6a60113 limits concurrent QUIC handshakes per listener. • b3a84800b4 2026-03-19 A 512-sample peak-averaged run-queue length per thread; c238965b27 shrinks the advertised H2 max-concurrent-streams quadratically above a target, cutting attack load from about 100000 to 1500; 932d77e287 adds a floor.

### haproxy-031 — Sleep until the moment work becomes possible, instead of polling or re-waking
Lens: scheduling · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O, curl
Principle: Compute when a limit lifts, a pacing credit refills or a retry is likely to succeed, and arm a timer for that moment; drop periodic wakeups whose reason is gone.
Why it is faster: Polling every millisecond, or re-waking a task to check a pacer, burns CPU and power for nothing. A precise timer wakes once. Burst credit sized to cover the expected scheduling delay keeps throughput without spinning. Most deferred operations finish quickly, so a short first retry followed by a slow steady cadence gives low latency without busy retries.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: token-bucket limiters that compute the next permit time; job-status polling with a quick first poll. • Embedded: tickless idle and sleep-until timers in firmware; radio TX scheduling with sleep plus burst. • Native / systems: timer coalescing; heartbeat intervals that grow on idle clusters.
Watch out: coarse timers need enough burst credit, or throughput falls; the timer must be re-armed when conditions change early.
Seen in HAProxy: efcbc6e66d 2009-03-06 `maintain_proxies` wakes exactly when a rate-limited frontend becomes ready instead of every millisecond. • 4f59d38616 2020-12-22 The maximum forced idle wakeup grew from 1 s to 60 s once its original reason (clock-drift detection) no longer applied. • 8098be1fdc 2025-01-22 QUIC pacing sleeps on a timer with burst credit instead of re-waking a tasklet, "drastically" reducing CPU; cb91ccd8a8 sizes the credit from the measured loop time. • 098503ad02 2026-10-22 CLI purge retries after 50 ms first, then at the old 1 s cadence: most purges finish in 0.05 s instead of 1 s. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-102): 75452d34ef 2017-09-27 `h2o_timerwheel_get_wake_at` feeds the poller's maximum wait. • 409f520764 2018-12-06 The socket pool re-arms its expiry timer for the time until the oldest entry expires (at least 1000 ms), and only while entries remain. Before, a constant 1000 ms timer ran forever. • 214161e489 2022-04-07 The response throttler computes `ceil(-bytes_left*1000/bytes_per_sec)` and links the timer for exactly that delay, instead of a `HUNDRED_MS` tick. • 65e5c2702b 2022-06-13 The loop waits `INT32_MAX` when all memory recyclers are empty, otherwise 1000 ms. d4f00918dd 2022-11-01 centralizes this in `h2o_cleanup_thread`. • 38ca911748 2023-02-16 The file cache is cleared every iteration, and the recyclers are trimmed at most once per second (thread-local `next_gc_at`). The function returns the maximum wait. • Also seen in curl (curl-032): dfe1884c25 2006-06-22 `CURLOPT_MAX_SEND/RECV_SPEED_LARGE` and `CURLM_STATE_TOOFAST`, which "will not select". • 7de44e0a42 2010-03-26 Sub-second pauses computed from buffer size and target rate, nudged by 1/64 with shifts; 9124bfba45 2010-08-10 TOOFAST arms a timer for that time. • a9e46749b2 2015-04-06 Removes a bogus `* 8` that made curl wait "8 times too long, and then transfers very quickly"; 4b86113f5e 2016-08-16 measures from reference points that reset after each limit-size of data. • 24badd29f5 2025-09-02 Break out of the receive loop only after `max_recv_speed/4` bytes, since "too small receives will just cost cpu unnecessarily". 221b7dda38 2025-09-25 and 52ac8104e1 2025-12-29 fix busy loops with tiny limits and in blocking mode.

### haproxy-032 — Make unused features cost nothing: one cheap test at the call site
Lens: laziness · Source: HAProxy · Also: Envoy, jemalloc
Principle: When an optional subsystem (filters, tracing, quotas, reuse, TLS options) is not configured, skip every call, argument evaluation and counter update for it behind a single predictable flag test.
Why it is faster: Calling into a function that loops over an empty list, evaluating trace arguments for a disabled trace, or maintaining a counter for a limit nobody set all cost cycles (and atomics on shared lines) on every request. A flag test at the call site is one predictable branch. An inline function still evaluates its arguments; a macro that tests the enabled state first does not.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: guard metrics and tracing hooks behind an `enabled` flag; skip quota counters when no quota is set; supplier lambdas or `isDebugEnabled()` before building log arguments. • Native / systems: static keys / jump labels in Linux; Rust `log!` macros that skip formatting. • Data / databases: skip per-row memory accounting when no memory limit is configured. • Embedded: compile out or flag-gate debug hooks in release firmware.
Watch out: the flag must be kept exactly in sync with configuration changes at runtime, or a feature silently stops working when enabled later.
Seen in HAProxy: 396d2c6782 2007-11-04 Cache and cookie layer-7 checks are skipped when not needed; 1e5dfdad77 rewrites the server-name header only when the server changes. • 3e34429515 2015-11-24 A `HAS_FILTERS(strm)` flag guards all filter callbacks, because the cost of empty filter calls was "huge" on chunked messages; 7c39ab4ac2 runs the Lua GC only when cosockets are used. • f52170d2f4 2019-03-08 With no data filter, body data switches to infinite forwarding and bypasses HTTP analysis. • 3b78f2aa5d 2021-09-08 Removed a variables-size counter that always equalled another and cost up to 38% CPU: 770k to 1.06M req/s; 55f8a830dc guards each remaining atomic add with "is a limit set": 1.06M to 1.24M req/s. • ad14c2681b 2021-04-10 `trace()` became a macro that tests the trace state before evaluating arguments: +2.5% HTTP/1 req/s. • 926712ab2d 2021-10-20 No connection-hash computation for TCP proxies, which can never reuse; 6bfabfdc77 extends this to all backends that cannot reuse (it had become a regression), and 52866349a1, ef07d3511a, eb3d4eb59f and fee3bd48b4 skip SNI/ALPN setup on non-SSL connections. • 33c461314c 2024-11-19 Latency timestamps are not updated when profiling is off. • 33c8270903 2026-05-20 A global stream-accounting counter is touched only when stream elasticity (off by default) is enabled, removing atomics from every stream open and close. • Also seen in Envoy (envoy-022): 6591604546 2016-10-05 Added `generate_request_id` (skips UUID generation) and router `dynamic_stats` (skips building canary and virtual-cluster stat names), and wrapped header mutation in `if (config.isTracing())`. • 037064f27e 2021-09-20 Custom stat namespaces do their `find_first_of` and registry lookup only if any namespace is registered. • a9d652cb22 2021-10-07 EDS keeps its copy of the load assignment only when LEDS is configured. • b38f7ea508 2022-01-12 The gRPC access logger no longer always builds a retry config. 4b05b1e605 2022-06-30 skips creating an async file manager thread when both directions are bypassed. 7f85f5fab8 2022-12-01 skips parsing the bootstrap bind config when the cluster has its own. • 8cbbf66dd3 2022-03-16 Setting the local interface name needs `getifaddrs()` per upstream connection, so it was made an option that defaults to off "due to performance concerns". • 313677b773 2022-11-18 Zone-aware LB latches `locality_weighted_balancing_` and runs locality selection only when it is set. • 068fc61960 2022-11-22 RDS builds the virtual-host map and rebuilds VHDS state only when `has_vhds()`, so updates no longer copy every vhost into a `std::map`. • b3151d0178 2022-12-01 The catch-all virtual cluster, and its stats, are created only when a vhost has virtual clusters. 57666993be 2023-01-04 likewise creates the vcluster stats scope only when needed. • ee7991d50e 2024-08-06 ext_proc buffers the body for retry only if the gRPC service has a retry policy. This was found in load-test flame graphs. • b7c429f534 2024-12-10 Clusters drop the default upstream codec filter factory when no upstream filters are configured. 59047d5fdc 2025-07-25 RBAC creates its protobuf arena and CEL expression builder only if a policy uses CEL. • 20df9b51bd 2025-11-25 c-ares returns immediately when unroutable-family filtering is off, instead of listing interfaces on every resolution.
