One user's DNS-filter CPU dropped from a 12-core peak, with dropped queries, to 0.015 cores. • Also seen in jemalloc (jemalloc-014): 7013d10a9e 2010-05-11 Avoid `isalloc()` when profiling is enabled but deactivated "so that profiling overhead under such conditions is negligible"; a3b3386ddd 2012-11-13 avoid profiling-accumulation locking when `prof_interval` is 0. • fe0e399385 2018-04-19 Hooks: `if (likely(atomic_load_u(&nhooks, RELAXED) == 0)) return;` at every invoke site. • 5aa46f027d 2017-04-20 c834912aa9 2018-05-29 Automatic arenas (which can never be reset) skip full-slab and large-list tracking and the `large_mtx` on flush. • d22e150320 2018-05-24 Skip the muzzy pool (two mutex operations) when muzzy decay is disabled; 04cb7d4d6b 2019-11-06 bail out of muzzy decay before taking its mutex under default settings. • d0f187ad3b 2019-12-04 0f552ed673 2020-01-13 Gate decay work on the interval and do not purge huge extents when decay is off; 655a096343 2020-03-11 103f5feda5 2020-03-11 move the background-thread inactivity check out of the purge inner loop. • 63677dde63 2020-12-05 Detect statically whether `pages_huge` can succeed instead of trying at runtime.

### haproxy-033 — Re-run only what changed: gate re-evaluation on change flags
Lens: laziness · Source: HAProxy
Principle: On each wakeup, check cheaply what actually changed (event cause, buffer flags, relevant inputs) and re-run only the stages whose inputs changed; keep built results until something invalidates them.
Why it is faster: A "loop until converged" state machine, or a handler that re-analyses everything on any wakeup, wastes whole passes when the change is irrelevant. Masking on the flags the next stage cares about turns most passes into an early return. A timer-only wakeup on an idle connection only needs its expiry refreshed. A work list that is still valid after an interrupted send does not need rebuilding.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: React memo / shallow-compare only relevant props; incremental builds. • Data / databases: change-data-capture triggers; cached query plans until the schema changes. • Embedded: dirty bits in control loops so only changed outputs are recomputed.
Watch out: a missing change flag means a stage never reruns when it should; prefer over-invalidating to under-invalidating, and test wake causes individually.
Seen in HAProxy: 2bea3a1155 2008-08-28 Calls per HTTP session 5 to 3; 0be0ef9604 stops re-evaluating the request when only the response changed; b67a9b8ca8 checks timeouts only when they have just fired. • 2f976e18b8 2010-11-11 Analysers rerun only if buffer flags changed (for example `http_sync_req_state` 10 to 8 calls per session). • 798f4325fa 2012-11-08 A timer-only wakeup just refreshes the expiry, saving about 10k wakeups/s for 100k connections with 10 s timeouts. • 1ec9bb5b62 2019-06-06 `process_stream()` stops looping on shutdown-request flags: 30% fewer `si_sync_send()` calls; 3c5c066d66 applies the analyser flag mask; 829bd4710f removes a `goto redo` loop. • 14710b5e6b 2024-12-12 The QUIC mux keeps its built frame list across interrupted sends and refreshes it only on new data, reset or window increase.

### haproxy-034 — Test for "nothing to do" before calling the expensive routine
Lens: laziness · Source: HAProxy · Also: Aeron, Varnish, libuv, PostgreSQL
Principle: Before calling cleanup, send, GC, formatting or encoding code, test cheaply whether there is anything for it to do (empty list, nothing queued, nothing freed, no room for output) and return early.
Why it is faster: Routines that take locks, walk structures or issue syscalls are pure cost when their input is empty. An inline emptiness test is a load and a branch. Checking output room before formatting avoids producing text that is then thrown away; calling a heap trim only after something was actually freed avoids thousands of useless `madvise` calls.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: skip `clear()` on empty per-request maps; return before serializing an empty payload; check backpressure before rendering more HTML. • Data / databases: skip a rollback when no writes happened; run VACUUM only when there are free pages. • Native / systems: skip `fsync` or flush on an empty buffer; trim heaps only after large frees.
Watch out: a racy emptiness check is only safe when a stale answer is harmless (the work is picked up later); otherwise it can skip work that just arrived.
Seen in HAProxy: 4e33d8677a 2009-10-11 Stats dumping checks `buffer_almost_full()` before printing: 10% less CPU for HTML stats. • cda7f3f5c2 2018-10-28 `vars_prune()` is called only if the variable list is not empty: +1.5-5% multithreaded throughput. • 6e1fe253b7 2023-03-28 `pool_gc()` runs only if the stick-table purge freed something; strace had shown about 29.7k `madvise` calls during soft-stop. • 4be5435014 2023-01-26 H3 trailers sending exits early when there is no trailer block, skipping a realignment and QPACK encoding into a copy. • d1b573059a 2022-11-23 No `h1_send()` when no error message was produced; c09ef0c5fc skips QUIC sending with no frames. • 34b31d85cb 2024-04-10 `qel_need_sending()` gates `qc_send()`, and fully sent encryption levels leave the send list (c6e3d60fc1). • Also seen in Aeron (aeron-015): 14473f817a 2017-09-30 Shortcut out of `updatePublishersLimit` when the IPC publication is not active. • 6eca09d237 2018-03-13 Channel, registration-id and source-identity work for a new image happens only when matching subscriptions exist. d860d2770a 2018-07-30 creates the date formatter and lambda only when the error log has errors. • 111013c058 2019-07-31 `Image.controlledPeek` returns immediately when `initialPosition >= limitPosition`. d5c637d438 2019-10-07 checks the clock tick only when `workCount <= 0`. • f73887cac6 2020-01-03 Padding frames skip the checksum staging copy and are written straight from the term buffer. 2f9317a9e5 2020-12-07 The C++ `offer(buffer, offset, length)` passes NULL for the reserved-value callback instead of routing through a default supplier. • 1a21d900b3 2021-04-01 IPC publisher-limit update scans subscriber positions only when `length > 0`. • 5f13e6478e 2024-02-07 `RetransmitHandler` keeps `activeRetransmits`. ### haproxy-035 — Compute only what the consumer will use, when it uses it
Lens: laziness · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Do not fetch, convert or decode data on the critical path that only some outputs, debug modes or rare fallbacks need; obtain it at the moment of first use.
Why it is faster: Per-request work done "in case" is paid by every request but used by few. A syscall such as `getsockname()`, a calendar conversion for a raw timestamp, or inflating a compressed fallback table at every start are all avoidable when the result is not read.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: compute an ETag only if the client sent If-None-Match; skip reverse DNS unless logging needs it; lazy request properties in frameworks. • Embedded: decompress embedded assets on demand. • Data / databases: project only the needed columns instead of `SELECT *`.
Watch out: lazily computed values that depend on mutable state must be captured before that state changes (an address after the socket is closed, for example).
Seen in HAProxy: 20814ff1fc 2017-09-15 ALPN is retrieved only inside the debug-output block, not on every accept; ee1bdd5a03 stops scanning for and adding "Connection: close" in Lua applet responses. • 543d4507ca 2019-04-10 `get_gmtime()` is skipped for the raw `%Ts` log format, where the broken-down time is unused. • 34a3eb4c42 2021-11-05 The client destination address is fetched only when needed, usually saving a `getsockname()` per connection; 8e358af8a3 drops an unneeded memset on tree removal. • c5d909b5c4 2026-09-15 The embedded compressed symbol table is inflated only when the binary's own `.symtab` is unreadable, saving about 1.5 MB of decompression per object at every start.

### haproxy-036 — Derive values when read instead of maintaining them on every write
Lens: laziness · Source: HAProxy · Also: memcached, PostgreSQL
Principle: If a value is written constantly but read rarely, or can be computed from state that exists anyway, store the raw inputs and compute the value on read.
Why it is faster: Rotating a sliding-window counter on every event, or keeping a size counter that is only used as "is it empty?", costs work (often atomic) on the hot write path. Computing the counter's age at read time, or testing the tree root pointer, moves the cost to the rare reader. Shared summaries maintained by all threads are especially costly.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: token buckets refilled lazily on request (Redis/NGINX style); exponential-decay scores computed on read. • Data / databases: approximate row counts derived from page statistics instead of a maintained counter. • Native / systems: `empty()` via head pointer instead of a size field updated on every push.
Watch out: if reads become frequent, the computation moves onto the hot path; re-check the read/write ratio.
Seen in HAProxy: 3d8c5531d8 2009-03-06 `read_freq_ctr()` uses the counter's age without rotating it, and the code shrank by a third; 79584225e5 computes the remaining-events budget once before the accept loop. • cfbb3e6560 2019-05-29 The "costly to maintain" active-tasks mask is replaced by testing the queues directly. • 2c41d77ebc 2021-02-24 `rqueue_size`, used only as a boolean, is no longer maintained; the tree root pointer is tested instead. • Also seen in memcached (memcached-012): 67d7cfc135 2012-08-31 Removed `STATS_LOCK(); stats.evictions++ ...` from `do_item_alloc`. `do_item_stats_totals()` sums per-class `itemstats[]` on read, removing "a handful of instructions from the alloc hot path, which is always worth doing". • 5d7dc88da3 2015-02-06 Once the global cache lock was gone, the hash-expansion trigger counter `hash_items` needed its own mutex on every insert and delete. • 5c0face158 2018-02-12 Removed `hash_items` and its lock. `assoc_start_expand(curr_items)` runs from the once-per-second `clock_handler` using an existing total. This also fixed a bug where a burst of sets could stop expansion from restarting. • c630eeb7f4 2019-07-26 Dropped `mem_requested` bookkeeping from `slabs_alloc`/`slabs_free`. The LRU maintainer sums `sizes_bytes[]` at the start of each batch; this "actually speeds up some hot paths as a number of branches and operations are removed completely". • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-056): 3c2313f4815 2008-11-03 The statistics collector writes its file only when a backend requests it, throttled to 500 ms, instead of on a fixed cycle. • 54b6cd589ac 2017-09-19 Multibyte-aware truncation of the reported query text moved from every activity report to the reader; it had been a bottleneck for fast queries with long text. • 31c002aa897 2026-08-21 Outer-join reduction stopped collecting "safe quals" eagerly at every level and finds them on demand, removing quadratic space growth with nested inner joins.

### haproxy-037 — Order checks by cost: cheap rejection before expensive work
Lens: laziness · Source: HAProxy · Also: Varnish
Principle: Run the inexpensive test that usually decides the outcome (length, a precomputed bound, a literal parse, a collision check) before the costly operation (string compare, copy, DNS, object construction).
Why it is faster: Most candidates fail a cheap filter, so the expensive step runs only for the few that pass. An integer length compare filters almost all header names before `strcasecmp`; a load-time maximum length rejects long headers before any copy; an IPv6 literal can never be a hostname, so trying the literal parsers first avoids a blocking DNS round trip; checking for a collision before building a connection object avoids building it only to throw it away.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: validate and authenticate before allocating a session; length or Bloom prefilters before regex matching in routers. • Data / databases: check unique constraints before a heavy row build; Bloom-filter checks before disk reads; cheap selective predicates first. • Native / systems: check for a numeric string before `getaddrinfo`.
Watch out: the cheap test must be a correct necessary condition; a prefilter that wrongly rejects is a bug, not an optimization.
Seen in HAProxy: a329463655 2017-08-23 Lua applet headers test `name_len == 10` before `strcasecmp("connection")` and use memcpy instead of a format string: calls divided by 3, applet 5% faster. • 5a8728d03a 2025-11-10 The QUIC CID insert and collision check happen before `qc_new_conn()`, so colliding connections are never built. • 35560e3ad3 2026-07-22 IP patterns try the literal parsers before the resolver: a 20k-entry IPv6 ACL file went from 14 s to about 1 s in `haproxy -c`. • 076ec9443c 2026-02-14 DeviceAtlas computes the maximum length of any matching header at load time and skips longer headers before any copy or compare. • Also seen in Varnish (varnish-020): 5d8edbab3f 2007-10-29 "If we find no ESI elements of an object, don't penalize it with ESI's chunked delivery". • 8e30779950 2008-07-24 Skip ESI parsing of objects that do not look like XML: the first non-whitespace character must be `<`. • 9f15b0123e 2011-04-20 "Only do ESI processing if we find some ESI in the object" (`esi_found`). • dce01d1ec4 2011-09-05 varnishncsa: "don't process header when inactive", moving `strchr` after the `lp->active` test. • fa4f00e1d 2015-06-26 Log cursors test for `VSL_ENDMARKER` before the shared-memory overrun and validity checks. • 0eb5bae284 2025-01-22 / 92bf39a746 2025-01-22 `ban_any_variant`: after N ban checks on non-matching variants, only ban-check variants that already matched Vary; 52b0d2594c 2025-07-09 lowered the default to 0.

### haproxy-038 — Skip the search when state already proves the answer
Lens: laziness · Source: HAProxy
Principle: If existing state guarantees the result of a search (all candidates saturated, a scan just failed and nothing changed), return that result directly or back off instead of searching again.
Why it is faster: An O(N) selection whose outcome is known costs O(N) for nothing, and repeating a failed full scan for every object multiplies it. A non-empty backend queue can only exist after every server was tried, so a new request can go straight to the queue. A failed lookup that cannot succeed until time passes can be rate-limited like a negative cache entry.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: connection-pool acquire returning "exhausted" immediately when waiters exist; negative caching of failed lookups (DNS NXDOMAIN TTL). • Native / systems: allocator "no free blocks" flags that skip the free-list walk. • Data / databases: skip a lookup when a count or Bloom filter says the set is empty.
Watch out: the shortcut must be invalidated when the proving state changes (a server frees a slot), or work waits needlessly.
Seen in HAProxy: 82cd5c13a5 2020-09-29 If the backend already has pending requests, server selection returns "full" immediately for leastconn/first/roundrobin/static-rr: 200 saturated servers on 8 threads went from 110k to 148k RPS and the reposition function left perf top. b88ae18021 queues random-LB requests on the backend so the fastest server picks them up. • cf2562cddf 2025-08-29 After a failed shared-memory stats object reuse, retries are delayed instead of rescanning the whole area for every object, speeding up startup for large configs.

### haproxy-039 — Give the common or trivial case its own minimal path
Lens: laziness · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Detect inputs that need much less than the general algorithm (count only, full and contiguous, never-emitted variants) and run a tight specialized loop, falling back to the generic code for the rest.
Why it is faster: A generic loop pays for every case it supports on every iteration: wrap-around handling, partial input, extra delimiter checks. A specialized path for the dominant case drops those tests entirely, and can reserve output once instead of per item.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: `COUNT(*)` paths that skip row decoding. • Web / backend: a "whole frame already in buffer" fast path in HTTP/2 or protobuf decoders; HEAD-only response paths. • Native / systems: a single `reserve()` before bulk pushes; parsers for your own wire format that reject unused syntax.
Watch out: two paths mean two implementations to keep consistent; test the fallback on inputs that straddle the fast-path conditions.
Seen in HAProxy: 14389e7036 2011-07-10 halog dropped tab-delimiter support, which haproxy never emits; checking for it cost 4% CPU. • e1a908c369 2012-01-03 halog's line-count mode runs a separate loop with no per-line parsing: 2.5x faster, 2 GB/s of logs on a 2.4 GHz Xeon. • bdcefe58b7 2021-05-21 `h1_parse_full_contig_chunks()` handles many small contiguous chunks (2700 one-byte chunks can fit in 16 kB) and falls back to the generic parser; e071f0e6a4 reserves one maximum-size DATA block up front; f7c2044f8f copies wrapping input in one call instead of two parser runs.

### haproxy-040 — Abandon doomed work early and skip preparation for objects about to die
Lens: laziness · Source: HAProxy · Also: Varnish
Principle: Check for cancellation or errors before starting a costly operation, and when an object is taken over only to be destroyed, skip the steps that would make it reusable.
Why it is faster: Connecting to a backend for a client that already left, or rehydrating a connection that will be closed immediately, spends real resources on results nobody will use.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: check `AbortSignal` or `context.Done()` before database calls; drop queued jobs whose requester is gone. • Data / databases: close pooled connections without reset when they are being evicted. • Native / systems: move-for-delete APIs with a "drop" flag.
Watch out: some cleanup is still mandatory even for doomed objects (releasing locks, counters); only skip the reuse preparation.
Seen in HAProxy: 4f48990c1a 2013-12-04 The stream interface returns directly on a connection error flag. • 6cd8d13c05 2016-04-07 With `abortonclose`, a failed client read is checked before connecting to the server. • 21f7974e05 2025-08-08 Strict-maxconn idle-connection kills call the takeover with `release=1`, skipping allocations that would prepare the connection for reuse. • Also seen in Varnish (varnish-026): f6c4379a85 2013-02-15 On return from the waiting list, `poll()` the socket and release the session early if the client has closed. • ff8ae1fada 2023-10-18 Adds a transport `poll` method; 05a686983d 2023-10-18 `h2_poll()` reads `r2->error` without a critical section; 6ad7be6fe8 2023-10-18 `req_poll()` returns `VCL_RET_FAIL` at each VCL step and counts `req_reset`.

### haproxy-041 — Carry resolved references and built objects forward instead of recomputing them
Lens: laziness · Source: HAProxy · Also: Aeron, memcached
Principle: When an earlier phase already found or built something (a resolved section, a server pointer, an event payload), keep and reuse it rather than looking it up or building it again.
Why it is faster: Re-looking up an object by name repeats a search whose answer is already in hand; rebuilding an event that another path just built repeats allocation and formatting. Carrying the pointer is free, and can also make it possible to drop thread-safety from a structure that is then only used at boot.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: build an event once and fan it out to subscribers; keep resolved handles in ORM sessions. • Native / systems: resolve symbols once at link or load time. • Data / databases: resolve a query plan once and reuse it.
Watch out: a carried pointer must not outlive the object; with runtime deletion, carry a reference count or a generation check.
Seen in HAProxy: c249f6d964 2023-04-19 Server UP/DOWN events reuse the STATE event's data instead of building another event. • 2ca7601c2d 2026-03-18 The http-errors section is resolved once at check time and reused at init. • 592e53fbe0 2026-10-09 The purge task uses an in-use test or a connection count instead of re-looking-up the server or proxy (1aae07f01f); be1974801a restricts the all-servers list to boot so it can be a plain list instead of a multi-thread one. • Also seen in Aeron (aeron-019): e3ef070201 2017-03-15 Passes the already-read `rawTail`/`termOffset` into the appender so it does not re-read and re-mask ("Reduce duplicate work"). • 0211cb858f 2018-02-19 "Single lookup of receiver id in the message". 35b19bc95c 2018-05-28 replay uses the known `stopPosition` instead of reading `image.position`. 311dc63493 2018-06-19 passes the `ClientSession` to the service callback "to save repeated lookup". 33df946f85 2018-11-12 reuses `alignedFrameLength` instead of recomputing the alignment. • d035e1a565 2019-04-25 and 04a9659bd4 2019-04-26 (C) Use the length returned by the formatter, and constant channel lengths, instead of `strlen`. • 00a9f0ca1f 2019-12-06 `Header.position()` reads frame length and term id directly and uses the known offset, instead of composing `termOffset()`, `frameLength()` and `termId()`. • 9a6c50832a 2021-10-19 "Avoid double syscall getting tmp dir" (`getenv` once). 89e583efb4 2021-11-02 uses the already-loaded `limit` in the C++ fragment assembler. • Also seen in memcached (memcached-010): 217dcce072 2006-11-26 "Pass key lengths along with the keys in all API functions... to avoid needing to call strlen() repeatedly". `assoc_find` checks `nkey == it->nkey && memcmp(...)`. • 01fa48f0bf 2008-03-03 Stopped recalculating the slab class id: an eviction "would crawl the list of slab classes three times". • 8d3ac82666 2008-03-03 `slabs_free` reads the stored `it->slabs_clsid`: "slightly reduce user CPU time, especially during heavy evictions". • 3bdfd46 2008-10-19 "removed some wasteful function calls": uses sprintf's return as `vlen` instead of `strlen(val)`. • 0fd0bc18df 2009-08-19 The CAS suffix length comes from `snprintf`'s return value, replacing `sprintf` followed by `strlen`. • bab9acd115 2011-10-02 The item and hash-table functions gained a `const uint32_t hv` parameter. Callers hash once and pass the result through get, link, unlink and store. • c3d22fef64 2013-12-19 The slab mover reuses an already computed `hv` instead of rehashing.

### haproxy-042 — Pay once at ingest: index, canonicalize and parse a single time
Lens: amortization · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Do the work every later consumer would otherwise repeat (locate fields, lowercase names, validate) once, when the data enters the system, and store the result so later operations are cheap byte compares or direct jumps.
Why it is faster: Case-insensitive compares per lookup cost a `tolower` per character plus a call; a single lowercase pass on input lets every later compare be a vectorized `memcmp` or a case-sensitive test. Recording each header's offset and length during the single parse lets rules jump straight to a header without re-parsing. The index itself is small (4 bytes per header here) and can be allocated only when something needs it.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: lowercase HTTP header maps at parse time (Go's `CanonicalHeaderKey`); lazily indexed JSON tapes. • Data / databases: a casefolded email column with an index; normalized identifiers at index time in search engines. • Native / systems: intern normalized identifiers in compilers; field-offset arrays for CSV or log rows.
Watch out: if the original form must be preserved (for display or forwarding), store both or make canonicalization reversible; ingest-time work is wasted on data that is never read.
Seen in HAProxy: e5f20dcea8 2006-12-03 `hdr_idx` records each header's position at 4 bytes per header (about 2.4% memory); 0637fa0671 adds the end-of-line pointer; bf2886274c allocates the index only when an ACL needs HTTP parsing. • ed00e345e2 2018-12-07 HTX always stores header names lowercased, using a 256-byte table (d6735d611e); the H2 encoder no longer lowercases them again (c2a10d4b4c), and later code uses case-sensitive compares (b045bb221a). • 75cc65356f 2021-10-15 DNS labels are stored lowercase and compared with `memcmp()`: 22% less CPU (the case-insensitive compare and `tolower` were about 18% before).

### haproxy-043 — Size batches for the cache, not for the maximum
Lens: amortization · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Batching amortizes overhead, but past the point where a batch's working set leaves the cache, each item is revisited cold and the batch gets slower; measure and pick the size that keeps the batch hot.
Why it is faster: Accepting 100 connections in a row means each one's state is evicted before the next stage touches it again. A smaller batch keeps all of them in L2/L3 between stages. With many threads competing for the same queue, smaller batches also spread work more evenly.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `recvmmsg`/`epoll_wait` maxevents; GPU tile sizes. • Data / databases: bulk-insert and group-commit batch sizes. • Web / backend: consumer `max.poll.records` and worker prefetch counts.
Watch out: the optimum depends on cache size, thread count and item size; it moved by more than 10x across HAProxy's history, so re-measure on new hardware.
Seen in HAProxy: a0250ba38d 2008-01-06 `maxaccept` made tunable and limited to 8 in multi-process mode. • 4827fd2a7e 2011-07-22 maxaccept 100 to 32: connection rate 67 to 94 kcps and forwarding 30k to 39.5k on a Core i5 with 4 MB L3. • 66161326fd 2021-02-19 MAX_ACCEPT 64 to 4, the best accept rate at every thread count (16 threads: 274k to 336k). The run-queue batch (see loop budget) went through the same re-tuning.

### haproxy-044 — Do more per wakeup: receive and demux several rounds before notifying consumers
Lens: amortization · Source: HAProxy · Also: curl, memcached, Kafka
Principle: While downstream buffers are free, keep receiving and parsing a bounded number of extra rounds, and make one upward call per pass instead of one per frame.
Why it is faster: Waking a handler after each partial frame produces many tiny runs with poor locality and a high instruction count per byte. Batching within one wakeup cuts calls, instructions and cache misses. The cap bounds latency: HAProxy measured that a higher limit was slightly faster but raised peak latency by a third.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Netty `maxMessagesPerRead`, NGINX `multi_accept`; draining all available WebSocket frames before dispatch. • Native / systems: NAPI poll budgets in NIC drivers. • Data / databases: draining fetches in batches in stream consumers.
Watch out: always cap the extra rounds and stop when downstream buffers run out, or one connection monopolizes the thread.
Seen in HAProxy: 2a761dcf0d 2018-02-26 The H2 demuxer calls the data layer once per demux pass instead of once per frame: POST 58k to 60k req/s, half as many receive callbacks. • 1b0707f3e7 2022-06-30 The QUIC datagram handler reads up to `maxpollevents` datagrams per wakeup. • 04ce6536e1 2024-10-12 `h2_process()` does up to 12 extra receive rounds while shared Rx buffers are free: 549 to 660 MB/s (+20%), `h2_io_cb` calls 2.6M to 300k, 17% fewer instructions, 31% fewer L2 and 39% fewer L3 misses. • Also seen in curl (curl-042): c788efffd4 2007-09-29 Bundled c-ares reads UDP packets in a loop until none remain, "to avoid having to run the entire event loop once per packet". • 86f266b004 2014-02-14 axTLS non-blocking connect calls `ssl_read` up to 5 times per call: "can save ~25% of time on SSL handshakes" on a 20 ms latency link; 8749bbe7fd 2014-02-18 documents why. • Also seen in memcached (memcached-015): 32f382b605 2003-05-27 `conn_read` tries `try_read_command` on already-buffered bytes before reading the socket or returning to the loop. • 89deba77a0 2003-09-05 "accept all connections in same event": loop on `accept()` until EAGAIN. • f1351f9bba 2008-04-02 "Syscall optimizations": `reset_cmd_handler` jumps to `conn_parse_cmd` when `rbytes > 0`, so binary pipelines use as few syscalls as text. • c25d0bd68a 2021-07-29 Worker notifications moved to eventfd: one `read` returns `ev_count` and the handler pops that many queue items. Pipe mode reads up to 32 bytes at once. "Syscalls can be expensive enough that handling a few of them once here can save both throughput and overall latency." • c0f01efa3a 2025-01-21 After a bare `get`, the state goes to `conn_new_cmd` rather than `conn_mwrite`, so buffered pipelined commands are parsed and their responses and IO batched together (other commands already did this). • 3df5460a78 2020-04-11 Incomplete requests (< 2 bytes, or a SET short of data) made memcached "hard loop while waiting". The response object is now started only when a response will be sent. • Also seen in Kafka (kafka-040): 84a14fec29 2017-03-29 `StreamThread.runLoop` processes all queued records between `poll()` calls: over 200K records/s more with 100-byte records, close to raw-consumer efficiency.

### haproxy-045 — Defer contended updates and let one owner apply them in batches
Lens: amortization · Source: HAProxy · Also: Tokio
Principle: When many threads must update a shared ordered structure, try the lock once; on failure, or always, push the update to a cheap list and let a single task apply all pending updates under one lock acquisition.
Why it is faster: N threads each taking a write lock for one update pay N acquisitions plus contention; a single owner draining a batch pays one. Several updates to the same item merge into one. The structure is briefly approximate, which is acceptable for ordering heuristics such as least-connections or expiry.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: change buffers for secondary indexes; group commit. • Web / backend: leaderboard or LRU repositioning by a background sweep; write-combining loggers. • Native / systems: actor mailboxes drained by a single owner.
Watch out: readers see a slightly stale structure; do not use this where exact ordering is a correctness requirement, and bound the pending list.
Seen in HAProxy: b6a8318cc2 2025-02-11 leastconn repositioning uses a write trylock and, on failure, wakes a per-server requeue tasklet (627280e15f): 24-core EPYC 382k to 610k rps (reposition share 43% to 2.5%); 64-core Altra 311k to 1.21M rps, latency 3.2 to 0.84 ms. • 388539faa3 2025-05-02 Stick-table updates go to per-thread-group lock-free lists that a tasklet inserts into the tree in batches; 8bc8a21b25 batches expiry reinsertion per shard; d5562e31bd then removes the table lock entirely. • Also seen in Tokio (tokio-029): 19500f7df8 2018-03-28 Timer entries: `AtomicStack::push` sets `queued` via `fetch_or(true)` and returns early if it was already set. The driver's `process_queue` takes the whole stack and applies the changes to its wheel. d822b721b4 2018-08-20 factored this into `timer/atomic_stack.rs`. • 73d733a341 2025-11-27 Per-worker timer wheels: cancellations from other threads go to a `cancellation_queue` (an intrusive list behind `Arc<Mutex>`) that the owning worker drains with `mem::take`. Remote registrations go through `push_from_remote`, and local registrations use an unsynchronized queue.

### haproxy-046 — Do expensive bookkeeping once per interval or per batch
Lens: amortization · Source: HAProxy · Also: curl, PostgreSQL, H2O
Principle: Throttle housekeeping that would run on every access to at most once per time interval, and share one expensive measurement (a timestamp) across all the work in a scheduling pass.
Why it is faster: A maintenance walk on every access multiplies its cost by the access rate; a timestamp check bounds it to one run per interval. Clock reads can be syscalls and give inconsistent values within one task; one timestamp taken by the scheduler for tasks that ask for it is cheaper and consistent.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: request start time captured once in middleware; debounced stats recomputation; lazy TTL purges. • Native / systems: per-frame timestamps in game loops; cached coarse clocks. • Data / databases: statistics refresh at most every N seconds per table.
Watch out: throttled cleanup lets garbage accumulate between runs; size the interval so the backlog stays bounded.
Seen in HAProxy: 73be796462 2020-12-10 A per-entry timestamp makes the cache's expired-duplicates cleanup run at most once per second per Vary list. • c5052bad8a 2024-11-19 Tasks flagged `TASK_F_WANTS_TIME` get one timestamp taken by the scheduler; QUIC pacing uses it instead of its own CPU-time read (22bd92a87f). • Also seen in curl (curl-041): 072a8b2955 2007-03-19 User progress callback frequency separated from the once-a-second speed calculation; 8e719e3ef5 2007-04-18 the internal meter updates at most once per second; 01c2b397aa 2010-06-17 removes a duplicate update in TOOFAST. • 1691a31cab 2013-07-31 The tool progress bar redraws at most once per second; the old logic could do "hundreds of updates per second that would make a significant impact in transfer performance". • 01368d395c 2014-08-11 Dead idle connections are pruned, but only if at least 1,000 ms have passed since the last prune. • 0b84d0cf1b 2023-02-26 The Windows send-buffer tuning call runs at most once per second; it had run hundreds to 100,000 times per second and was "the highest capture count" in the profile. 5691a6cf36 2024-02-09 moves that throttle from per-transfer to per-connection. • 2f1e94eaed 2026-06-25 Connection liveness checks are skipped if the last one was under 1 s ago; ef6b17cf12 2026-10-15 restores the cheap pending-input check on every reuse of idle non-multiplexed connections (stray bytes caused shifted responses) and keeps the 1 s limit for the rest. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-064): 957d08c81f9 2007-04-30 Backend statistics messages are rate-limited, piggybacking on the commit timestamp to avoid an extra clock call; 45cbdaa038d lets autovacuum keep statistics data for one second. • 51004c7172b 2019-09-22 The NOTIFY sender advances the queue tail every 4 pages instead of after every batch, reducing exclusive-lock use. • 8242752f9c1 2022-11-29 Hot-standby KnownAssignedXids compaction triggers at 2x live entries, at most once per 128 commit records (chosen by benchmark), opportunistically when about to wait for WAL, and never when already compact. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-030): f7f738c2a8 2015-07-21 A cleanup thread calls `SSL_CTX_flush_sessions` every lifetime/4 "instead of relying on OpenSSL to clear them every 255 handshake attempts". • 40e132838d 2021-08-09 fixed the memory-recycler trimming so it runs at most once per second from the loop's idle path. • 38ca911748 2023-02-16 `h2o_cleanup_thread()` clears the file cache every iteration, trims buffer, socket and mempool recyclers at most once per second (thread-local `next_gc_at`), and returns the maximum wait: `INT32_MAX` when recyclers are empty, otherwise 1000 ms.

### haproxy-047 — Process a batch in one context and flush only at the end
Lens: amortization · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When a stateful transformation (compression, encoding) has a per-flush cost, feed it everything available and flush once per batch instead of once per chunk.
Why it is faster: Each compressor flush emits a sync marker, closes the Huffman block and, in a stateless compressor, loses back-references. Compressing per small chunk is slower and compresses worse; one flush per batch fixes both.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: gzip middleware that does not flush on every write; WebSocket permessage-deflate. • Data / databases: log compression in batch shippers; block compression per page rather than per row.
Watch out: waiting to flush adds latency for interactive streams; flush when the input queue is empty, not on a fixed size.
Seen in HAProxy: b77c5c2693 2015-12-07 Chunked responses are buffered and compressed at forwarding time instead of per chunk, which is faster. • fcee2555fc 2026-11-01 All available chunks are queued and flushed once after the last: a 1 MB HTML response sent in 256-byte chunks shrank from 405057 to 402030 bytes at level 1.

### haproxy-048 — Coalesce small pieces whenever you copy them anyway
Lens: amortization · Source: HAProxy
Principle: If a message made of many tiny blocks is being copied or defragmented anyway, merge adjacent blocks during that copy so every later pass pays per-block overhead fewer times.
Why it is faster: Every later analysis pass costs something per block. Merging during a copy that must happen anyway costs almost nothing extra and reduces all future per-block work.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: coalescing iovecs before `writev`; merging small rope nodes. • Data / databases: compacting small LSM runs during compaction. • Web / backend: combining small writes in user space before the socket.
Watch out: merging must not cross boundaries that consumers rely on (message or frame limits).
Seen in HAProxy: 783db96ccb 2026-02-03 Adjacent HTX DATA blocks are merged during transfer and defragmentation (0c6f2207fc); f559c202fb defragments unaligned messages when waiting for the payload.

### haproxy-049 — Put the fields used together on the hot path in the same cache line
Lens: memory-layout · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Order a record's fields by how often and how close together they are accessed. Fields read on the fast path go in the first line, and rarely used or very large members go to the tail or to a side structure.
Why it is faster: The CPU fetches memory in 64-byte lines. When the fields an operation needs are scattered over three lines it pays up to three misses, and when they share one line it pays one. Putting big members (a 128-byte `sockaddr_storage`, a name string) at the end also keeps hot offsets small, which shortens instruction encodings and shrinks code. A lookup structure follows the same rule: the field read right after a tree or hash hit should sit next to the node, not behind a large payload. The cost is reduced readability, and the layout tends to drift as fields are added, so re-check it with a tool such as `pahole`.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in Go or Rust request/connection structs, keep the state machine, buffers and deadlines up front and move rarely read metadata (peer certificate, debug names) into a lazily allocated side struct. • Embedded: control blocks read in an ISR should have their hot fields in the first line or word group. • Native / systems: hash map entries should start with the hash, key length and next pointer. ECS and struct-of-arrays layouts in game engines apply the same idea. • Data / databases: keep hot columns or tuple-header fields together and move wide, rarely read attributes out of line.
Watch out: the gains are small (about 1-3%) and only show on genuinely hot structures. Measure with cache-miss counters, not intuition, and remember that "hot" differs between workloads.
Seen in HAProxy: ac68c5d92c 2009-10-04 Counters moved next to the hot members of their struct. • 8d5d77efc3 2009-10-18 Rare fields split out of `fdtab` into a separate `fdinfo` array: +0.5%. • 520bbb2b85 2010-01-10 `http_txn` reordered: +3% on pipelined requests. • 7b81563443 2011-10-21 The 128-byte `sockaddr_storage` moved to the struct tail, and code shrank by 2 kB. • 0a8535fec8 2012-10-13 109e95a1b4 2012-10-13 Channel reordering gave +2% and session reordering about +1%. • fd5efb5936 2017-11-26 The 33-byte cache id moved to the end because it "was creating a hole and forcing the hot remaining part to be split across two cache lines". • cb92f5cae4 2018-04-26 `polled_mask` moved out of `struct fdtab` into its own array so fdtab fits in one 64-byte line again. • 4ed84c96cf 2019-02-03 `proto_list` and `name` moved to the end of `struct listener`, so the fields read by `accept()` and `default_target()` land in the first line. • 9628c42284 2021-10-21 The tree node `link` moved above the large address union in `resolv_answer_item`: DNS response validation CPU dropped by 4 points. • 6a28a30efa 2022-09-07 `cpu_time`/`lat_time` moved from `struct task` to `struct stream`, their only user, bringing the task back to 120 bytes.

### haproxy-050 — Remove padding holes and narrow over-wide fields
Lens: memory-layout · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Reorder members by size so that alignment wastes no bytes. Use the narrowest type that holds the real range, and put new small fields into existing holes instead of growing the object.
Why it is faster: On 64-bit targets a 4-byte or 1-byte field between two pointers leaves 4-7 dead bytes. Removing holes is free (no logic changes) and raises cache density, so more objects fit per line and per page. Narrowing enums to 8 bits, locks to 32 bits or a timestamp to its low 32 bits often fills a hole that already exists, so the struct shrinks by a whole line. The trade-off is that a narrowed field must still cover the real value range, and wrap-around must be handled explicitly.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: run `pahole` on C/C++/Rust binaries. In Rust, avoid `#[repr(C)]` where the compiler could otherwise reorder fields for you. • Web / backend: Go does not reorder fields. The `fieldalignment` linter finds wasted bytes in structs that are allocated per request. • Data / databases: declare Postgres columns from widest to narrowest alignment so tuples carry no padding. Order fields in protobuf/flatbuffer schemas the same way. • Embedded: pack structs on RAM-constrained MCUs, but avoid unaligned access on cores that trap on it.
Watch out: `packed` attributes can produce slow or faulting unaligned accesses. Prefer reordering. Fields written by different threads should not be packed into the same line: that is the false-sharing problem, which pulls in the opposite direction.
Seen in HAProxy: 378e041797 2012-10-13 `struct target` packed: -8 bytes each in connection and session. • 939478d04d 2013-12-06 2518db4bfa 2013-12-06 05efc0f33a 2013-12-07 c8987b3664 2013-12-06 The DIET series packed the `obj_type` enum to 8 bits and took 8 bytes each off session, task and http_txn. • f82eb02830 2013-12-07 Packed `eb_node`: `eb32_node` went from 48 to 40 bytes, saving 16 bytes per task and 20 per stick-table entry. • 4edd6836fc 2014-08-28 The proxy struct lost 48 bytes. • d7e33bbe2f 2017-11-26 a51108443e 2017-11-26 08eaa78739 2017-11-26 Struct server -40 bytes, proxy -16, check -16. • 4952985b71 2018-03-19 `use_ssl` moved into a hole. 319f078d6f 2018-10-21 `eb32sc_node` shrank from 64 to 56 bytes by moving the key after `eb_node`. • 8f1f177ed0 2020-10-18 `lock_t` narrowed from long to unsigned int to fill holes. • 768c2c5678 2022-09-06 The tasklet keeps only the low 32 bits of `call_date` in an existing hole, which makes it 48 bytes, or exactly 64 with debugging on. • 8df81b6fcc 2025-08-24 Struct server: 40 bytes of holes removed, one cache line less (3840 bytes). f9a6ae727c 2025-09-04 fd012b6c59 2025-11-03 Further 4-byte holes plugged. • 1ad74d634a 2026-06-16 `fcgi_conn` saved 8 bytes. 7d40b3134a 2026-03-21 `last_run` placed in the `TASK_COMMON` hole. c73d05326e 2026-08-11 An 8-bit hole reused for `debug_count`.

### haproxy-051 — Size storage to what is actually used: unions, real bounds, no worst-case arrays
Lens: memory-layout · Source: HAProxy · Also: jemalloc, libuv
Principle: Fields that are never live at the same time should share storage. Arrays and private areas should be sized to the real protocol or configuration bound rather than a compile-time worst case, and never per (N × N) when per N suffices.
Why it is faster: Every instance pays for the largest variant it could ever need. A union for mutually exclusive members (config text versus compiled form, frontend-only versus backend-only fields, IPv4 versus IPv6 address) and arrays sized from configuration at boot make the common object smaller. Smaller objects mean less RAM, fewer cache misses and less to scan when iterating. The cost is a discriminant flag and a little code to manage it.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: use Rust enums or TypeScript discriminated unions for parse-then-compile states, and drop the source text once the compiled form exists. • Embedded: use unions for protocol states in connection blocks. Size pools from a config value instead of `#define MAX_x`. • Native / systems: look for any per-thread-pair or per-listener-per-thread allocation. They grow quadratically as core counts rise. • Data / databases: cap peer-controlled lists (ranges, options). This saves memory and also hardens against DoS.
Watch out: a union with a wrong discriminant is a memory-safety bug. Caps need a well-defined behaviour when they are reached, such as rejecting the input or degrading gracefully.
Seen in HAProxy: 0cac26cd88 2019-01-14 The load-balancing parameters went into a transparent union: `struct lbprm` 392 to 372 bytes, `struct proxy` 6624 to 6480 bytes. • 38d41996c1 2020-11-03 The pattern chain became singly linked, because it is only deleted from the head: 32 bytes saved per pattern. • 99cca08ecc 2021-05-03 `session_list` moved into a union with members that only frontends use. • 8d49253588 2022-12-21 QUIC had allocated an rxbuf per listener per thread across all threads. With 48 threads that was 2304 buffers (576 MB). It is now one per bound thread. • 6c0117168e 2023-01-06 The per-session and per-stream stick-counter arrays now come from a pool sized at boot by `tune.stick-counters`, which can be 0. • 9ded834adc 2023-01-06 The RFC 7239 option's `expr`/`expr_s` fields share a union with a mode flag, and the strings are freed right after compilation. • 0ed94032b2 2023-04-17 a5c1a3b774 2023-06-22 QUIC ACK ranges were capped at 32, and the maximum TLS secret was set to 384 bits (-16 bytes per secret). • 03816ccfa9 2024-03-03 Splitting out ring storage saves a `struct buffer` per in-memory ring. eeaeb412dc 2024-12-12 The QUIC congestion-control private area went from 160 to 144 `uint32_t`. • Also seen in jemalloc (jemalloc-041): 7393f44ff0 2010-10-01 Omitted page-map entries for the chunk's own header pages and computed `map_bias` iteratively, which "avoids barely spilling over into an extra chunk header page". c2fc8c8b3a 2010-10-01 sized dynamic structures with `offsetof()`. • 4f37ef693e 2014-01-16 Shrank profiling static buffers when profiling is disabled, saving about 65 KiB. • dc553d52d8 2016-10-28 Allocated radix-tree child nodes with the correct per-level fanout, so leaves were no longer oversized (2^16 elements instead of 2^4). • 421b351779 2026-09-03 With a runtime page size, heap-allocated `edata` covers only the bitmap needed for the booted page size, where the static maximum was about 9x larger. • 8a2aa363aa 2026-08-31 Derived `TCACHE_NBINS_MAX` with every term at the maximum page size, after mixing limits had overshot. 41b6bc3b0d 2026-10-29 bounded `PSSET_NALLOC_BINS` by the real page-size-class count up to HUGEPAGE instead of 64, which shrank the heap array and its bitmap scans. • Also seen in libuv (libuv-022): 890d443558 2012-05-23 "shave about 100 bytes off uv_udp_send_t": the destination is stored as `sockaddr_in6` rather than `sockaddr_storage`. • 3e5d261440 2020-04-13 `make_program_env()` replaces `alloca(n * sizeof(DWORD*))` with `DWORD required_vars_value_len[ARRAY_SIZE(required_vars)]`, which also fixed an element-size bug. • 78c65d0f10 2020-05-12 `uv_udp_set_source_membership()` replaces two `sockaddr_storage` locals with `union uv__sockaddr`.

### haproxy-052 — Use small fixed-size codes in place of strings and general containers
Lens: memory-layout · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Convert values that come from a known set to integers or enums once, represent small bounded sets as bitmasks, and keep a hash next to (or instead of) a string that is only ever compared for equality.
Why it is faster: Comparing two integers takes one instruction, while `strcmp` walks memory and branches. An enum also removes a heap allocation, and a 64-bit mask replaces a whole tree (nodes, pointers, allocations) for a set of at most 64 members. Comparing stored hashes turns a variable-length compare into a fixed one and can let you drop the stored string entirely. The trade-off is that the escape hatch ("other" values, hash collisions) needs a correct slow path.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: use HTTP methods and statuses as enums, feature or permission sets as bitflags (`EnumSet`, Rust `bitflags`), and interned symbols in interpreters. • Data / databases: use dictionary encoding for low-cardinality columns, null bitmaps, and a hash-first equality prefilter in joins. • Embedded: use status bytes instead of strings in command protocols, and a bitmask for a small set of active channels.
Watch out: a hash used as identity needs a large width, and a seed if attackers can choose the inputs. A bitmask caps the set size permanently.
Seen in HAProxy: 19979e176e 2012-04-04 Stats admin statuses converted from repeated `memcmp`/`strcmp` to numeric codes. • d02210cd30 2017-07-24 Method variables store the enum and allocate a string only for `HTTP_METH_OTHER`. • 6a92b14cc1 2025-08-29 Proxy log-steps moved from an ebtree to a 64-bit mask. The tree had a significant memory cost, especially in defaults sections. • d993e1eeae 2025-12-05 9794585204 2025-12-05 92f77cb3e6 2025-12-05 28654f3c9b 2025-12-05 The SNI hash is stored in the connection and the cached TLS session, and matching compares hashes. be998b590e 2025-12-05 then stopped storing the SNI string in the cache.

### haproxy-053 — Represent time as one wrapping integer, not a multi-field struct
Lens: memory-layout · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Store internal timestamps and deadlines as a single integer counter (ms or ns) rather than a {seconds, microseconds} pair.
Why it is faster: With a two-field struct, every compare, add or subtract needs carry and borrow logic and touches two words. A single integer makes each of these one instruction, halves the storage and frees one value (0) to mean "not set". A 64-bit ns counter wraps after 585 years. A 32-bit ms tick wraps after 49 days and therefore needs wrap-safe comparisons.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: use epoch-ms numbers rather than `Date` objects in hot JS paths, and `Instant`/integer durations in Rust and C++. • Embedded: use a monotonic systick counter for all RTOS timeouts, compared with wrap-safe signed subtraction. • Data / databases: store timestamp columns and index keys as int64 epoch-ns.
Watch out: narrow ticks wrap. Always compare with `(int)(a - b) < 0`, never with `a < b`.
Seen in HAProxy: 0c303eec87 2008-07-07 All expiration timers converted to integer ticks: "simpler and faster code" and about 64 bytes saved per session. • 76d343d3d3 2023-04-27 `tv_ms_elapsed()` replaced by a linear ns subtraction. 69530f59ae 2023-04-28 The internal `now` timeval replaced by a u64 `now_ns`, which simplified the clock update code.

### haproxy-054 — Shrink lookup tables until they fit in L1
Lens: memory-layout · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Merge parallel lookup tables into one bit-per-property table, and store only the columns that really vary. Columns with very few distinct values can be computed with a couple of compares instead.
Why it is faster: A table that misses L1 costs more than the computation it replaces, and large tables evict the program's own hot data. Seven 256-byte class tables cost about 2 kB of cache, while one 256-byte table with a bit per class costs 256 bytes, and testing a bit is as cheap as testing a byte. Likewise, computing a code length with one to three branches beats loading it from a table twice the size.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: lexer character classes, URL-encoding tables and Huffman/inflate tables in codecs. • Embedded: keep tables `const` and minimal so they live in flash and fit small caches. Replace sparse tables with arithmetic. • Web / backend: use a `Uint8Array` bitmask class table in JS/WASM tokenizers rather than several regex tests.
Watch out: computing a column instead of storing it only pays off when the computation is a branch or two that predicts well. Benchmark on the target CPU.
Seen in HAProxy: 8d5d7f20b9 2007-01-21 The original HTTP parser came with per-class lookup tables. • 2235b261b6 2016-11-05 Seven HTTP character-class tables (about 2 kB) merged into `http_char_classes[256]` with `HTTP_FLG_*` bits: binary 4 kB smaller, about +1% on small objects (222k req/s). • 9f4f6b038c 2022-09-20 The HPACK Huffman decode tables changed from `{code,len}` structs (4×512 B + 64 B) to `uint8_t` code-only tables, with the length derived in code and one small table merged into another. That gave 4×256 B, +1.6% maximum H2 request rate, and 1088 B less data.

### haproxy-055 — Embed small children and keys inline instead of pointing to them
Lens: memory-layout · Source: HAProxy · Also: jemalloc, Varnish
Principle: When a child object or key is small and always present, store it inside the parent (a flexible array member, an embedded struct) instead of allocating it separately and storing a pointer.
Why it is faster: A separate allocation costs a pointer (plus back-pointers), allocator overhead and a dependent load, which is often a cache miss, on every access. In a tree, a pointed-to key costs a second miss at every level. Inlining makes it one allocation and one line per comparison. This is the counterpart of lazy sub-allocation, and the break-even depends on how small the child is and how many parents need it. HAProxy moved the same `conn_hash_node` out in 2021 and back in 2025 once compact trees had shrunk it.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: intrusive lists and trees (`list_head`, Boost.Intrusive), `SmallVec`/small-string optimisation, and flexible array members for keys in hash tables. • Data / databases: store keys inline in B-tree pages, and denormalise a hot 1:1 side table back into its parent. • Web / backend: embed always-present sub-structs by value in Go instead of using `*T` fields that each cost an allocation and GC pressure.
Watch out: embedding an optional or large child makes every parent pay for it. When only a minority of instances need the child, allocating it lazily wins instead (see allocation below).
Seen in HAProxy: 3ac9912837 2023-11-26 `pat_ref_elt` switched from a pointer node plus `strdup()` to a node with the key in a flexible array member `pattern[0]`. set-map went from 173 to 182 kreq/s/core. With 16M IPs, memory fell from 4.21 GB to 3.68 GB (32 bytes per entry) and load time fell about 20%. • 75e480d107 2025-07-22 The shared frontend/backend counters merged into the parent counter struct, removing one indirection per stat update and recovering most of a ~20% regression caused by sharding. • 2d6b5c7a60 2025-09-12 `conn_hash_node` moved back into `struct connection` (the compact tree node is 16 bytes versus 40): at least 24 bytes saved per backend connection and 2.08 to 2.13 Mrps (+2.4%) on 64 cores. • Also seen in jemalloc (jemalloc-032): fde3e20cc0 2017-03-27 Embedded the automatic thread cache inside TSD and initialized it with TSD, replacing lazy-creation checks with `tcache_available()`. • ce41ab0c57 2017-03-22 `rtree_t` holds a statically sized `root[]` array instead of an `atomic_p_t root`, which "avoids one atomic operation per tree access". • 9ed3bdc848 2018-10-09 Moved `bytes_until_sample` from the lazily created `prof_tdata_t` into TSD, so the allocation fast path "does not need to load tdata now, avoiding several branches". • ce0b7ab6c8 2023-03-28 Stored the thread name inline in `prof_tdata_t`, which avoids internal alloc/dalloc during `prof_sample` and fixes a dump/update race. • Also seen in Varnish (varnish-042): 884f8ebdde 2008-11-19 "Save a malloc per objhead by putting a few fields into the objhead for the hash_slingers to use". 877851aad2 2008-12-03 made crit-bit branch nodes part of the objhead, which "saves malloc operations big time". 3982b7a7b7 2009-02-16 overloaded those fields to shrink the objhead. • fddbc27a1b 2010-05-26 Counterpoint: split the crit-bit "Y" node back out, because node and objhead lifetimes differed and too many objheads sat on the cooling list. • 4571689fdc 2011-09-18 Embedded the sockaddrs directly in `struct sess`. • 197f744d65 2010-11-29 Moved the waiting list into its own struct to save a pointer per objhead. 5c8268062b 2016-01-06 later reversed this and put the list head back in the objhead, because the saving did not pay for the extra allocation and misses. • 763cc6ef0e 2021-11-17 Socket-option table entries hold a `union sock_arg` (linger, timeval or int) inline instead of a `calloc`'d `void *`, and temporaries use a stack union.

### haproxy-056 — Keep queue and ring entries small: pass pointers, trim per-message metadata
Lens: memory-layout · Source: HAProxy
Principle: In high-volume queues and rings, enqueue a pointer or descriptor rather than a copy of the record, and keep the metadata that travels with each message to the minimum.
Why it is faster: A record copied through a queue is written twice and pollutes both cores' caches. Small entries let the same memory hold many more slots, which absorbs bursts without growing. When the messages themselves are tiny (about 50-byte datagrams), a generic 128-byte address container per message dominates ring capacity and bandwidth.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: pass IDs or `Box`/pointers through actor mailboxes and job queues instead of serialised payloads. • Native / systems: use descriptor rings (as io_uring and NIC rings do), and avoid `sockaddr_storage` in per-packet structs. • Data / databases: keep event headers in telemetry and WAL pipelines compact, using a variant type rather than a struct sized for the largest case.
Watch out: passing pointers across threads transfers ownership and lifetime responsibility. The consumer must free the object, and the object must not be freed while it is queued.
Seen in HAProxy: 83efc320aa 2020-10-14 The connection is allocated before it is queued to the target thread. Entries shrank from 64 to 8 bytes, the ring grew from 256 to 1024 slots in half the memory, and the connection rate rose 1%. • cc231f3468 2026-04-21 Two `sockaddr_storage` fields per datagram were replaced by a `union sockaddr_in46`, saving 200 bytes per datagram in the MPSC rings.

### haproxy-057 — Align buffers and pooled objects at the allocator, not in the type
Lens: memory-layout · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Have the allocator or pool return cache-line-aligned memory for hot objects and I/O buffers, while leaving the struct types themselves unpadded.
Why it is faster: `memcpy` and kernel copy routines run fastest on aligned addresses, with full-width vector moves and no split loads. Hot objects at arbitrary 16-byte offsets can straddle lines, or share a line with a neighbour that another thread writes. Aligning at the pool fixes both and makes benchmarks reproducible. Aligning the type instead would make the compiler pad every embedded or arrayed instance.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: use `posix_memalign` or `aligned_alloc` for I/O buffers (4096 is required for O_DIRECT) and aligned slab caches (`SLAB_HWCACHE_ALIGN`). • Data / databases: align page buffers to 64 or 4096 so checksums and copies vectorise. SIMD parsers want aligned, padded input. • Embedded: align DMA buffers to the cache line so cache maintenance never touches neighbouring data.
Watch out: 64-byte alignment wastes up to 63 bytes per object. Reserve it for hot, frequently copied or cross-thread objects, not for every small allocation.
Seen in HAProxy: eb075d15f6 2025-08-05 ef915e672a 2025-08-05 Aligned-pool infrastructure was added. c471de7964 2025-08-11 c2687f587e 2025-08-11 d6095fcfe6 2025-08-11 The task/tasklet, connection and pendconn pools were aligned to 64 bytes. The types themselves were deliberately not aligned, "as we don't want the compiler to expand them". • 1bb9754648 2025-08-11 Per-thread server arrays aligned: 1.72M to 1.75M rps, mostly better reproducibility. • 77335f52fc 2025-08-11 The buffer pool became `create_aligned_pool(..., 64, ...)`. Buffers had previously been randomly aligned to 32 or 64.

### haproxy-058 — Store bookkeeping inside the free space it describes
Lens: memory-layout · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When a buffer contains gaps (for example out-of-order data with holes), write each gap's size into the gap itself instead of keeping a side table.
Why it is faster: The memory overhead becomes zero beyond the buffer itself, there is no separate allocation to manage, and the metadata sits next to the data it describes, so it is already in cache when that region is touched. The catch is gaps too small to hold a header, which need a special "reduced gap" encoding.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: boundary-tag allocators, and free lists threaded through free slots. • Data / databases: free-space chains inside DB pages. • Embedded: TCP reassembly in small stacks, where a side table per segment is unaffordable.
Watch out: this only works for gaps at least as large as a header, so minimum-size and edge cases need careful handling and fuzzing.
Seen in HAProxy: edeb0a61a2 2022-05-04 The last gap may be shorter than a header (`NCB_BK_F_FIN`), so in-order inserts that only partly cover the buffer end are not rejected. • 1b5f77fc18 2022-05-09 `ncbuf` introduced for QUIC out-of-order reassembly: block and gap sizes are stored in the gaps, with a reserved header before the head.

### haproxy-059 — Typed free-list pools, with pools of similar size merged
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy · Also: memcached
Principle: Serve fixed-size objects from per-type LIFO free lists whose link lives inside the freed object. Merge pools whose object sizes are close so they share memory and hot objects.
Why it is faster: A pool allocation is a couple of pointer moves: pop the head and store the next pointer. There are no size-class searches, no headers and no locks inside libc. Objects return warm and memory stays dense. Many small pools fragment memory and keep many cold free lists, so merging pools that are equal after rounding, or within about 1% or 16 bytes of the average, cuts the pool count and footprint. Refilling several objects at once amortises the slow path. The trade-off is that pools hold memory after a burst (see trimming below), and merging mixes types, which makes use-after-free bugs harder to attribute.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: slab caches per object type (as in kernels), with merging of near-equal size classes as Linux SLUB does. • Web / backend: Go `sync.Pool` per message type, Netty pooled buffers, and .NET `ArrayPool<T>` bucketed by size. • Embedded: fixed-block allocators per message type, with no heap fragmentation and deterministic timing. • Data / databases: per-type memory contexts or arenas for tuples and plan nodes.
Watch out: pools hide leaks and use-after-free from ASan-style tools unless you have a debug mode that bypasses or poisons them. Keep an exact-size escape hatch for objects whose size has external meaning, such as TLS record buffers.
Seen in HAProxy: 50e608d721 2007-05-13 Pools v2: `pool_alloc2` pops `*(void**)free_list` and `pool_free2` pushes. • 7dcd46d471 2007-05-14 Pools sorted by size and merged via `MEM_F_SHARED`, with 16-byte rounding and a GC on out-of-memory. • 982b6e37e4 2009-01-25 2b50247695 2012-10-30 Specialised pools for pre-created pipes and zlib state. • a885f6dc65 2014-12-03 Refill N entries at once, calling the GC only once. 0262241e26 2014-12-08 The allocator split into 3 layers so large buffers skip poisoning. • 581bf81d34 2016-01-25 `MEM_F_EXACT` stops buffers being rounded past 16 kB, which had caused short SSL writes. • 8280ea97a0 2019-07-18 appctx got its own pool instead of sharing the connection pool, whose size had diverged. 2bdcc70fa7 2020-05-19 The HPACK table moved from malloc/free to a pool. • 93c2ea0ec3 2023-09-12 Size rounding refined: 93 pools merged into 36 (43 with tagging). • 6b17310757 2025-03-20 Merging by the pool's average size (within 1% or 16 bytes, exact matches always respected): 48 to 36 pools and about 6% less memory (4.12 to 3.88 MB). • Also seen in memcached (memcached-036): 10698bae63 2011-12-18 The slab free list changed from a `void **slots` array grown by `realloc` to a doubly linked list through `it->next/prev`, which lets the page mover unlink a specific free chunk. • e52734366c 2021-07-28 `cache.c` dropped constructor/destructor support ("wasted branches") and its doubling `ptr[]` array, replacing them with an `STAILQ` threaded through free objects. • 894e4c16b5 2022-01-12 `STAILQ_INSERT_TAIL` became `STAILQ_INSERT_HEAD`: "was originally LIFO … This is friendlier for cache."

### haproxy-060 — A per-thread hot-object cache in front of the shared pool, sized to L2
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy · Also: jemalloc, Netty
Principle: Serve allocations first from a small per-thread cache of recently freed objects and fall back to the shared pool only on a miss. Cap the cache at roughly L2 size and make the cap tunable.
Why it is faster: A freed object is likely still in the freeing core's cache, and handing it back to the same thread avoids both a miss and any atomic or lock on the shared list. A cache larger than L2 holds objects that are no longer hot and costs memory on every thread. HAProxy measured that 512 kB was faster than 1 MB once shared refills became cheap, while allocation-heavy QUIC workloads wanted 3 MB. Storing the cache head inside the pool object removes an index computation and covers every pool.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: tcmalloc/jemalloc thread caches and per-CPU slab magazines. Size them to L2 and to the burst a protocol produces. • Web / backend: Netty `PooledByteBufAllocator` thread caches, Go per-P `sync.Pool`, and per-worker object caches in game servers. • Data / databases: per-connection caches of plan or tuple objects, and per-core mbuf caches in DPDK-based engines.
Watch out: memory use scales with the thread count, and objects freed on another thread drift between caches. Expose the size as a tunable and measure. A cache that is too large is slower, not just wasteful.
Seen in HAProxy: e18db9e984 2018-10-16 About 512 kB of freed objects per thread, in per-pool lists plus an LRU: +7.5% throughput on 24 threads and cache misses down from 19.2% to 17.7%. "It seems pointless to keep more than an L2 cache." • 0a93b6413f 2018-10-16 The first 32 pools come from a static array, so the pool's index is cheap pointer arithmetic. 7f0165e399 2018-11-26 The cache became an array initialised at boot, removing init checks and 2.5 kB of code from the alloc/free paths. • 160287b676 2020-01-29 A per-thread pipe cache removed a 5-6% locking cost at high bitrates. • ed891fda52 2020-06-01 Local caches enabled for locked pools too: about +10% on 8 threads. d79422a0ff 2020-06-28 Buffers now come from the local cache: +10% HTTP/1 on 16 threads. • f587003fe9 2021-03-02 Cache doubled to 1 MB to absorb a burst of 32×16 kB H2 buffers. 9f3129e583 2021-04-17 The cache head moved into the pool header, removing the 64-pool array and the index computation. • f5e94b2f47 2022-01-02 Back to 512 kB once batched refills existed: up to 10% faster on multiplexed H2 (16-core ARM). 284cfc67b8 2022-12-19 Size made runtime-tunable (`tune.memory-hot-size`) for large-L2 CPUs. • 0bb686a72d 2026-02-19 The haterm benchmark tool sets the hot size to 3 MB. Pools had been taking about 12% CPU with QUIC, and it now reaches about 11M req/s on 80 cores. • Also seen in jemalloc (jemalloc-046): 289053c95b 2009-06-22 Magazines: "allocate/deallocate objects without any locking when the cache is in the steady state". • 84cbbcb90a 2009-12-29 Converted magazines into per-class `tcache_bin_t` slot arrays. 3ee7a5c5b0 2009-12-29 removed contention-driven arena rebalancing, since the cache made it pointless. • dafde14e08 2010-03-17 Extended the cache to large objects when medium classes were removed, so that range kept a lock-free path. • 143e9c4a2f 2022-10-21 Threads that only free (reclamation threads) stayed in a minimal state with no cache and suffered contention. After 128 slow fetches such a thread is promoted to the full state with a cache and batched flushes. • Also seen in Netty (netty-049): 8429ecfcc4 2014-03-01 `PoolThreadCache`, modelled on jemalloc's tcache, with tiny/small/normal tiers, only for known event-loop threads. 14600167d6 2013-11-30 No lock when freeing huge unpooled chunks. • 48f1398869 2014-09-02 Buffers record their allocating thread; only a free on that thread goes into its cache. • 81fee66c78 2015-05-19 Cross-thread frees go back to the owner's cache through a JCTools MPSC array queue (the commit notes the same-thread case got slightly slower). • 2b8fd8d43b 2016-11-22 `useCacheForAllThreads=false` limits caches to event-loop threads. 24b39bc287 2016-12-01 No cleanup watcher when caches are disabled. 0be53f296f 2015-08-07 Cache entry objects are actually recycled. • 9681842d54 2019-03-22 `cacheTrimIntervalMillis` and `trimCurrentThreadCache()`. 78c4abbfb7 2019-04-01 Caching for all threads off by default. • 05b7af7ae4 2020-04-06 The arena passes the already-fetched thread cache down the call chain instead of looking it up again: 57.1 to 50.7 ns per get-and-release. • 28fe6a9b91 2024-05-06 In the adaptive allocator, event-loop threads get a private magazine, saving two atomic operations (lock and unlock) per allocation. 7b4fe3d25c 2024-11-11 Their magazines use a thread-local pool and only shared ones pay for MPMC queues. accd981104 2026-01-06 Unguarded recyclers and a local LIFO list cut more atomics. • ee2cb981ce 2026-06-05 Size-classed caches purge by epoch every 16/128 polls; the thread-local cache is a ring split into non-empty and empty zones for an O(1) capacity scan, removing malloc/free churn on 2-core containers.

### haproxy-061 — Recycle memory while it is still hot: LIFO reuse, evict the coldest, free early
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy · Also: Netty
Principle: Always hand out the most recently freed object or buffer and trim the oldest ones. Order work so that buffers are released (sent, consumed) as soon as possible, so the same few buffers cycle through the cache.
Why it is faster: Reusing a buffer while it is still in L1/L2 avoids misses on both the free and the next write. Evicting the newest object from a full cache throws away exactly the memory that is hot. Sending output before reading new input, or sending inline instead of deferring to a later loop pass, frees buffers sooner. A workload that would otherwise pin thousands of buffers then runs on a couple of dozen, which cuts RAM and cache pressure at the same time.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in Node streams, write immediately and queue only on backpressure. Connection pools should hand out the most recently returned connection. • Native / systems: process TX completions before RX in NIC drivers, and use LIFO free lists with FIFO trimming in object pools. • Data / databases: buffer-pool free lists that reuse the most recently freed frame first.
Watch out: LIFO reuse concentrates wear on the same objects, so use-after-free bugs show up quickly. That helps debugging but hurts in production. Sending inline must still respect fairness between streams.
Seen in HAProxy: 57ec32fb99 2017-04-11 The connection handler calls `send()` before `recv()`: slightly less memory and a small performance gain. • 87212036a1 2021-04-19 fa19d20ac4 2021-04-19 Freed objects always go into the local cache, and the oldest are evicted until the cache is at most 75% full. • d5ec100661 2022-01-02 Fixed eviction that used `LIST_NEXT` and so evicted the newest objects: +0.5-1% on 16 threads. • e16762f8a8 2023-11-04 `h2_snd_buf()` sends directly instead of waking a tasklet. At 64 connections × 100 streams the buffer count fell from 10400 to 22-24 (160 MB saved) and throughput rose from 4.4 to 5.9 Gbps, with the same number of syscalls. • Also seen in Netty (netty-051): 3e42292d8b 2015-03-20 Thread-cache regions switched from FIFO to LIFO (index `index-1 & (len-1)`): wrk 4,449,510 to 4,508,150 req/s. • 0d3b8362ee 2024-09-13 The Recycler's local pool uses `pollLast()` instead of `pollFirst()`; benchmarks showed higher throughput and lower GC time. • accd981104 2026-01-06 A local LIFO free list on the adaptive allocator's thread-local path.

### haproxy-062 — Move objects between local and shared pools in clusters
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When a per-thread cache spills to or refills from a shared free list, link N objects together and move the whole cluster with one atomic operation or lock acquisition.
Why it is faster: One atomic operation per object on a shared list puts a contended cache line on every allocation. Clustering divides shared-list traffic and contention by the cluster size, and it is what made a smaller local cache viable. The cost is an extra link field (a `down` pointer) per free object and slightly coarser memory return.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: tcmalloc/jemalloc transfer caches and batched `free` in slab allocators. • Web / backend: return pooled buffers to a global pool in batches from worker threads. • Data / databases: move free pages between per-core and global free lists in batches.
Watch out: large clusters move memory between threads in lumps, which can starve another thread briefly. Keep clusters small (about 8).
Seen in HAProxy: a885f6dc65 2014-12-03 An early form: refill N entries at once with a single GC call. • afe2c4a1fc 2021-12-30 Refill goes through the local cache. 361e31e3fe 2022-01-02 The evictable count is computed once per pool rather than per object. • 148160b027 2022-01-02 A `down` pointer chains clusters. 43937e920f 2022-01-02 Eviction is batched, 8 per round. 1513c5479a 2022-01-02 Clusters of `CONFIG_HAP_POOL_CLUSTER_SIZE` are pushed with one call: "accesses to the shared pools are reduced by CONFIG_HAP_POOL_CLUSTER_SIZE".

### haproxy-063 — Drop your own global pool layer when the system allocator already caches per thread
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy
Principle: If the underlying allocator already has per-thread caches, keep only your thread-local layer and fall straight through to `malloc` instead of maintaining a process-wide free list.
Why it is faster: A process-wide free list is a single contended cache line that every core hits. Modern allocators (glibc ≥ 2.26 tcache, jemalloc, tcmalloc, mimalloc) already scale per thread, so a duplicate global layer only adds a bottleneck.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: remove custom global free lists when linking jemalloc or mimalloc. Benchmark both ways at high core counts. • Web / backend: in Go, prefer per-P `sync.Pool` over a mutex-guarded channel pool. • Data / databases: hand small, short-lived allocations to a thread-caching allocator rather than a global arena.
Watch out: older or minimal libcs (some musl versions, embedded libcs) do not cache per thread, so keep the global layer as a build option.
Seen in HAProxy: 0bae075928 2021-03-02 `CONFIG_HAP_NO_GLOBAL_POOLS` became the default with threads and glibc ≥ 2.26. On 64 cores, performance had been divided by 8 between 32 and 64 H2 connections because of global pool contention.

### haproxy-064 — Allocate optional parts lazily, only for the objects and phases that use them
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Do not allocate helpers, sub-states or buffers at construction time. Turn parts that only some instances or phases need into pointers that are filled on first use, and allocate on the branch that needs them, not before it.
Why it is faster: Peak memory then scales with active work, not with configured or idle objects. HAProxy saved 80 MB for 5000 servers, about 256 bytes per connection, and 380 MB for 100k backends. Construction gets faster, and objects get smaller and denser in cache. The cost is a NULL check on access and possible allocation failure at an awkward moment. Objects that are torn down should likewise skip any preparation for reuse.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: create per-connection TLS, compression and auth contexts lazily. In JS, memoise heavy objects behind a getter. In Rust/Java, use `OnceCell` or the holder idiom. • Data / databases: keep optional row extensions out of line (TOAST-style), and build indexes and stats lazily. • Embedded: allocate per-session buffers only when a session actually starts transferring. • Native / systems: parse into `string_view`/slices and copy only on the branch that must normalise.
Watch out: a lazy allocation on the hot path can fail under memory pressure where an eager one would have failed safely at startup. When the object is almost always needed, embed it or create it at boot instead.
Seen in HAProxy: c9f825f060 2010-03-17 Check buffers only for checked servers: 80 MB saved with 5000 servers. • 32e3c6a607 2013-10-11 Outgoing connection allocated on first use. 0a23bcb8be 2013-12-01 Dynamic applet context: stream_interface down from 144 to 64 bytes. • 32b5ab2a28 2014-04-21 Temporary compression buffer allocated only when needed. f3764b7993 2016-03-31 Error-capture buffers (up to 32 kB per proxy) allocated on demand. • 1ef4cbc693 2019-07-17 e71fca81dd 2019-07-17 The inline `conn->addr.{from,to}` became pool-allocated `src`/`dst` pointers: about 256 bytes saved per end-to-end connection. 5a0b25d31c 2019-07-18 Lua cosockets no longer allocate a remote connection at all. • 8990b010a0 2021-02-19 The connection's hash node became a separate allocation used only by server-side connections. (It moved back inline in 2025, once it had become small; see the memory-layout section.) • 1c25b18e17 2022-05-24 93fba32430 2022-05-24 9f9263ed13 2022-09-13 QUIC: the stream endpoint is allocated at attach, no send descriptor exists for receive-only streams, and the CRYPTO reassembly buffer is used only for out-of-order data. • 6635aa6a0a 2023-06-20 3097be92f1 2023-06-22 90a63ae4fa 2023-07-04 276697438d 2023-08-07 QUIC packet-number spaces, encryption levels, the negotiated TLS ctx and the CID tree root became pool pointers, and only Initial is allocated in `qc_new_conn()`. a57ab0fabe 2023-04-26 433af7fad9 2023-03-07 No Tx buffer for a FIN-only frame, and useless TLS ctx allocations removed. • 49a619acae 2025-07-11 The default-server entry is allocated only on first need: RSS for 100k backends went from 800 MB to 420 MB. e6064c5616 2025-11-20 20dc8e4ec2 2025-02-27 Stream endpoint at attach, and no rxbuf for a FIN-only frame. • 21f7974e05 2025-08-08 An idle connection taken over only to be killed passes `release=1`, skipping the allocations that prepare it for reuse. • 9bc6c4d18c 2026-08-03 `str2net()` duplicates the string only when a '/' is present: load time 5% lower for IPv4 lists and 8% lower for IPv6.

### haproxy-065 — Release buffers and state as soon as they go idle
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O
Principle: Return transient buffers to the pool right after the I/O that used them, shrink objects that enter a terminal state into a small replacement, and delete objects that can no longer be reached.
Why it is faster: A buffer pinned to every idle connection multiplies memory by the connection count. With pools, freeing and reallocating is cheap, so holding memory "just in case" buys nothing. Shorter-lived buffers also tend to stay cache-hot. A closing connection that keeps a 1 kB+ struct for its whole drain period wastes the same memory, and copying the few fields it still needs into a stub frees the rest.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: WebSocket and long-poll servers should keep no read buffer while idle (the "bufferless idle" pattern in Go and Netty), and should release Netty `ByteBuf`s right after the write. • Native / systems: TCP TIME_WAIT minisockets replace full sockets during close. • Data / databases: LSM tombstones replace full rows. Garbage-collect superseded MVCC versions and shadowed config objects.
Watch out: freeing and reallocating on every operation only pays off when the allocator is a cheap pool. With a slow allocator, keep a small per-thread reserve instead.
