Seen in HAProxy: 10fc09e872 2014-11-25 Session buffers allocated only during processing or I/O and released afterwards. • 35dbd5d719 2017-09-22 The H2 demux buffer is allocated on Rx and "released if it's still empty after recv()". • 0c7679dd86 2022-02-24 5b68986d77 2022-08-08 The QUIC stream Tx buffer is freed after ACK, and the connection Tx buffer is released on every send completion. • 9f7cfb0a56 2023-08-07 b930ff03d6 2023-08-08 A closing QUIC connection is swapped for a small `quic_cc_conn` that shares a common prefix and has its own I/O callback and timer. dc9b8e1f27 2023-08-04 CONNECTION_CLOSE datagrams sit in a small (≥128 B) pool buffer. • ccd1ecba1d 2024-09-20 Shadowed duplicate defaults sections are dropped: with 300k sections, about 1.9 GB of RAM saved and 25% faster startup. • b248b1c021 2026-02-02 Removed two dead per-thread compression buffers left over from removed code. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-051): a19adac114 2017-12-07 Brotli `shrink_buf(1)` frees `bufs.entries[1..]` at the start of each compress call. • a01c722bc2 2019-02-27 The file handler closes the fd in `do_pread` once the last range is read, not after the final send completes. • 9081308e4 2022-04-01 HTTP/2 streams with no request body close as soon as the write side completes, instead of lingering until connection close. • a03b0f1fb 2022-04-17 "recycle memory earlier", and 818b75bd6 2022-04-20: `_write_buf.flattened` is freed as soon as all cleartext has become TLS records.

### haproxy-066 — Do not zero or poison memory that is about to be overwritten
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Use plain allocation plus explicit initialisation of the fields that are read before being written, instead of `calloc`/`zalloc`/`= {}` or a `memset` before each use.
Why it is faster: Zeroing writes every byte, which costs memory bandwidth and evicts useful cache lines, often for a struct that is fully set a few lines later or a buffer that is bounded by the received length anyway. Debug poisoning on every free has the same cost. Reused scratch contexts only need the fields that matter reset. The risk is reading uninitialised data, so every field must be accounted for.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `Buffer.allocUnsafe` in Node for buffers that are filled immediately, and `Vec::with_capacity` plus push instead of `vec![0; n]`. • Native / systems: `MaybeUninit` in Rust. Audit `memset` before `recv` in network code. • Embedded: skip clearing DMA buffers that the peripheral fully overwrites.
Watch out: uninitialised memory can leak old data to peers. Never skip zeroing for anything that is sent over the wire or exposed across a security boundary, unless every byte is provably written first.
Seen in HAProxy: 0262241e26 2014-12-08 Large buffers bypass the poisoning layer. • af580f659c 2022-02-23 Redundant poisoning on `pool_free()` removed. ba19acd822 2022-08-08 QUIC fake datagrams use `pool_malloc()` and set only the two fields that are read. • ddc616933c 2023-06-15 9f9bd5f84d 2023-06-15 30254d5e75 2023-06-15 `pool_zalloc()` replaced by allocation plus explicit init for `quic_conn`, its SSL ctx and rx packets. 4cf784f38e 2023-11-06 `quic_frame` locals are no longer designated-initialised (which zeroed the whole struct). • ccc4341258 2024-05-02 The log-build context became thread-local with no full `memset` per line. • e8a0dbff93 2024-11-07 `_pat_ref_new()` uses `malloc` instead of `calloc`. • 259465498f 2026-09-01 0e36267aac 2026-03-26 The DNS response buffer is no longer cleared before each receive, since parsing is bounded by the received length. Another useless memset removed.

### haproxy-067 — Reuse per-thread scratch space instead of allocating per call
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy · Also: Aeron, Kafka, Seastar
Principle: Allocate a bounded workspace (temporary string, match data, formatting context) once per thread and reuse it on every call. Keep large per-call structs off the stack, and prefer one-shot library APIs that do not build a context per call.
Why it is faster: A per-call `malloc`/`free` or `strdup` pays allocator costs every time. Large stack frames get pushed (and often zeroed) on every call and push the stack past hot lines. A thread-local workspace needs no lock and stays cache-hot. Generic library APIs that allocate and configure a context per operation hide the same cost. Length-aware APIs avoid the temporary copy altogether.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: a per-goroutine `bytes.Buffer` via `sync.Pool`, a thread-local `StringBuilder`/`MessageDigest` in Java, and one-shot `HMAC()`/`EVP_Digest()` calls. • Embedded: static scratch buffers instead of large locals on small task stacks. • Native / systems: per-thread regex match data, compression and decompression contexts, and `string_view`/`&str` APIs that need no NUL-terminated copy.
Watch out: a thread-local scratch buffer is not reentrant. A nested call that uses the same buffer corrupts the outer one. Bound the size, because unbounded inputs cannot use a fixed workspace.
Seen in HAProxy: 6adad11283 2017-04-21 The "trash" scratch chunks became thread-local. • 338c670745 2020-02-14 `make_arg_list()` copies into the trash buffer instead of `strndup()`/`free()` per argument. 21f3695126 2020-03-19 The length-based `regex_exec2()` avoids NUL-terminating copies. fdea1b6319 2020-08-06 A copy dropped because Lua strings are already NUL-terminated. • 728b5aa835 2024-04-30 bf3b4001ce 2024-05-02 In `sess_build_logline()`, a read-only buffer became a global and a 256-byte buffer in a thread-local context replaced stack temporaries. • fda6dc9597 2025-10-13 PCRE2 match data was created and freed on every match. It is now a thread-local allocation (matches are capped): +2.5%. • 20686bf5fd 2026-10-14 The generic `EVP_PKEY` HKDF (a context per call) was replaced by native one-shot HKDF APIs: +11% H3 handshakes on OpenSSL 3.5.4 and +4% on AWS-LC. An earlier attempt to reuse per-thread contexts (4e0af590e8 2026-05-28) was reverted (54633f078c 2026-05-28) because it broke on AWS-LC. • Also seen in Aeron (aeron-049): cf59f51b71 2014-09-13 Gave each UDP transport a single direct receive buffer with header flyweights wrapped over it once, and separated sender and receiver transports for footprint. eea521ee7a 2014-10-04 removed a layer of method-reference indirection. • 775e73c19d 2015-05-01 "Use a direct buffer for writing to the client", and the event buffer too (9940f6175e 2015-05-01). • d9aea57cda 2016-05-15 "Pull out receive buffers and flyweights from channel endpoints to transport pollers so that buffers can be reused to reduce memory footprint and cache misses". Catalog recovery reuses it (7668f58437 2020-04-23). • 4e65d5ebb5 2021-07-02 One `ExpandableArrayBuffer` is shared across all counter allocations during cluster-backup setup. • Also seen in Kafka (kafka-056): c060c48285 2017-05-31 KAFKA-5150 adds `BufferSupplier`, a cache keyed by size, used by the consumer's `Fetcher`. LZ4 now works on ByteBuffers directly. Reported: up to 40x faster LZ4 decompression for small batches, and at least 2x for batches under 10 messages. 1c786c589a 2017-06-03 applies the same reuse in the log cleaner. • 0e46dd4a1d 2020-04-16 The cleaner's `buildOffsetMap` iterates with `streamingIterator(decompressionBufferSupplier)`, consistent with `filterTo`. • 6b005b2b4e 2021-05-30 `RequestLocal` carries a per-handler-thread caching `BufferSupplier` through `Partition.appendRecordsToLeader`, `LogValidator` and `GroupCoordinator`. `ZstdFactory` uses it as zstd's `BufferPool`. Producer perf test with 5000 partitions and lz4: 346K to 432K records/s (+25%), p99.9 1514 to 662 ms. • fe6a827e20 2023-06-05 `ChunkedBytesStream` takes its per-codec intermediate buffer (16 KB gzip/zstd, 2 KB snappy/lz4) from the same lock-free supplier, replacing `BufferedInputStream` and per-call skip arrays. Measured: 20-70% higher broker skip-iteration throughput. • b46505c8de 2023-12-05 `CoordinatorPartitionWriter` uses a thread-local `GrowableBufferSupplier` instead of `ByteBuffer.allocate(min(16384, maxBatchSize))` per append. d585a494a4 2024-05-24 sets the builder's write limit to `maxBatchSize`, so that the reused 16 KB initial buffer no longer caps how large a batch can get. • Also seen in Seastar (seastar-055): 81de5c3d05 2019-02-18 LZ4 compression uses a per-thread `reusable_buffer` (grow-only, never value-initialized) for input and output larger than the 128 KB chunk. • b263a9e0a8 2020-09-29 Logging replaced `fmt::print(os, ...)`, removing "half-a-dozen unnecessary allocations" per `do_log()`. 28b412d8 adds `log_buf`, and 1c9efcdb adds a static 8 KB buffer plus a `log_writer` interface (not `std::function`, which could allocate), so callers can format with zero allocations. • 0e4139ed6e 2023-01-14 The cached log timestamp is formatted into a 32-byte fixed buffer that falls back to the heap only on overflow, with a test asserting zero allocations. • c37f30929e 2025-10-23 The Prometheus exporter writes into a hand-rolled growable char buffer, appends literals directly, and calls the formatter only for numbers. Allocations went from 4 per metric to 0. 6e2f629c earlier formatted straight to the stream to avoid a string copy.

### haproxy-068 — Move data by swapping buffer ownership, not by copying
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O, TigerBeetle
Principle: When the destination buffer is empty and the whole source is being moved, swap the two buffer descriptors (pointer, size, head) instead of copying the bytes. Shape reads so this case happens as often as possible.
Why it is faster: A swap is O(1) regardless of payload size, while a copy of 16 kB costs thousands of cycles and pollutes the cache. In a proxy, data typically passes through 2-3 layers, so turning each hand-off into a swap gives "true zero-copy end to end". HAProxy saw 140 to 202 kH/s on one transfer path. Swaps require equal-size buffers and an empty destination, so reads should be sized so the buffer is empty when the next layer wants it.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `std::swap`/`std::move` of vectors between pipeline stages, and page flipping or `vmsplice`. • Embedded: ping-pong DMA buffers, where the driver swaps the filled and empty buffer pointers. • Web / backend: pass `Buffer`/`ByteBuf` references through stream stages instead of concatenating, and use transferable ArrayBuffers to workers in JS. • Data / databases: swap page frames between the read buffer and the cache instead of copying.
Watch out: the fast case is narrow (empty destination, whole payload, same size), so design the read path to produce it. Otherwise you only add a branch.
Seen in HAProxy: b034b2598d 2014-12-08 `bi_swpbuf()` swaps the caller's buffer with the channel's when the channel's buffer is empty. • 7999bfbfd3 2018-07-20 `b_xfer()` swaps the `struct buffer`s when the destination is empty and the whole source is wanted: 140 to 202 kH/s. • 3815b227fe 2018-12-11 H1 output swaps areas when the HTX holds exactly one DATA block of the requested size: "true zero-copy … from end-to-end", reaching 97% of legacy mode. 98de12a5d1 2018-12-12 H2 does the same: +3% on H2 over TLS. • 2f6edc84a8 2019-05-15 `h2_rcv_buf` swaps the stream rxbuf with the channel buffer. 0f6d6a9ab6 2019-05-23 A fully drained HTX message is reset in O(1) instead of being walked. • 88718955f4 2023-03-17 The first H1 read is capped at `bufsize - maxrewrite` so the buffer can be released or swapped. strace showed 3 reads (16320+16+182) replaced by 2 (15360+1158), and less RAM. • 8d46a2c973 2023-10-27 f8e095b058 2023-12-08 H3 and hq-interop map the response onto the HTX buffer and write the frame header in the space freed by the HTX header, then return the old area to the caller. • fae478dae5 2026-01-28 36282ae348 2026-02-03 8b27dfdfb0 2026-02-03 Once multiple buffer sizes exist, swaps are restricted to buffers of equal size. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-052): eb41e5f9c9 2016-01-04 "zero-copy for fast path": the mruby chunked sender reads directly from the HTTP client's socket buffer (`h2o_mruby_http_peek_content`), removing a `receiving` buffer and its memcpy. On the final chunk the socket buffer is stolen into `shortcut.remaining` and replaced with an empty prototype-backed buffer. • Also seen in TigerBeetle (tigerbeetle-061): a587316de2 2021-12-08 Compaction needs "only ... 2 buffers each for index, filter, and data blocks, which are swapped between on each write". • d8078c49b9 2022-12-19 "Separate cache blocks from cache entries". On write completion, `std.mem.swap(BlockPtr, cache_block, completed_write.block)` replaces `copy_disjoint`. `write_block` "will consume `block` and replace it with a fresh block".

### haproxy-069 — Reserve headroom and align reads so data lands where its final container expects it
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy
Principle: If a payload will later need a header in front of it, receive it at an offset that leaves exactly that room. Size each read to what fits in one hop of the next layer, and reset an empty ring buffer to offset 0.
Why it is faster: Data received at the "right" place can be reinterpreted in place or swapped, and the header written into the headroom, so the copy disappears. Any copies that remain are aligned. Filling a buffer to raw capacity forces a split into two transfers with unaligned copies when the next container has fixed overhead, so under-reporting the free room by that overhead avoids the split. An empty ring reset to the start offers the largest contiguous free region, with no wrap-around split.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `skb_reserve`/`skb_push` headroom in Linux, and DPDK mbuf headroom for prepending headers. • Web / backend: reserve length-prefix space in a protobuf or length-delimited encoder and backfill it. Reset `bufio`/Netty read indices when drained (`discardReadBytes`). • Embedded: reserve header space in RX buffers for in-place forwarding, and size DMA reads to frame boundaries. • Data / databases: read pages minus the page header so payloads align with in-memory structures.
Watch out: headroom and offset logic is easy to break when a protocol's header size varies. Assert the invariants, and fall back to copying when they do not hold.
Seen in HAProxy: 78f548f49e 2018-12-05 An empty H1 input buffer is prepared as if it held an HTX block, so `recv()` writes at the final position before the swap: +13%, for +50% in total on large transfers. • c5efa33021 2018-12-05 The Tx temp buffer points at the empty output area offset by `sizeof(struct htx)`: +10%, with "no more calls to __memcpy_sse2_unaligned()". • 3906e22f6f 2018-12-05 45f2b89156 2018-12-05 `buf_room_for_htx_data()` under-reports room by the HTX overhead: up to +33% on large objects "due to less recv() calls and unaligned copies". • 2a59e87735 2018-12-12 The H2 Rx head is advanced to align the 9-byte frame header against the 40-byte HTX header. e0f24ee149 2018-12-14 c0960d1185 2018-12-14 Realignment moved into the muxes to simplify this. • 4fc51a73e6 2021-02-04 00d7cde551 2021-02-04 `b_slow_realign_ofs()` sets the H1 input head to a chosen offset "to permit zero-copies". • 82c51b561e 2022-05-17 The QUIC Rx buffer is realigned to the start once it is emptied.

### haproxy-070 — Produce directly into the final destination; remove intermediate buffers
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy · Also: memcached, Varnish, TigerBeetle, simdjson, curl
Principle: Decode, format, hash or forward straight from the source into the consumer's buffer instead of building a temporary and copying it.
Why it is faster: Each staging buffer costs a full read and write of the data and extra cache footprint. HAProxy measured a temporary decode buffer cutting throughput from 220k to 140k req/s. Feeding fields into an incremental hash avoids concatenating them first. Transforming read-only input while copying it into a fresh output is one pass instead of copy-then-modify. In a relay, letting the producer write into the consumer's output buffer removes both a copy and a wake-up.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: write to an `io.Writer`/response stream instead of building strings, use `stream.pipeline` instead of buffering bodies, and call `hasher.update()` per field for composite cache keys. • Native / systems: `sendfile`/`splice` in relays, io_uring provided buffers handed to egress, and image filters that go src to dst rather than copy then in-place. • Data / databases: zero-copy deserialisation (FlatBuffers, Cap'n Proto), and `COPY` straight into table pages. • Embedded: decode directly into DMA or peripheral buffers.
Watch out: writing into someone else's buffer couples the layers (size negotiation, partial writes, late header patching). Keep a generic copying fallback for the cases the fast path cannot handle.
Seen in HAProxy: 937f760e1e 2018-02-26 A temporary decode buffer was measured cutting throughput from 220k to 140k req/s ("this memcpy() will disappear soon"). 539e029cc5 2018-11-19 The H1 `rxbuf` was then removed, and messages decode straight into the channel buffer. • 56460ee52a 2022-11-28 Stick-table hashes are seeded with the table name instead of `memcpy`ing name and key into a trash chunk: +1%. • 242c6f0ded 2023-08-03 1d68bebb70 2023-08-03 799518e63f 2023-08-03 Mux-to-mux fast-forwarding lets the producer mux write directly into the consumer's buffer or a pipe. It was implemented for H2 11c05c516a 2023-08-03, H1 2d80eb5b7a 2023-09-29, PT ec22d3102d 2023-10-06, QUIC 1bcc0f8892 2023-10-27 and the cache applet ebead3c0a1 2023-11-23. 4be0c7c655 2023-11-07 loops to forward at least one full buffer. • 39b6f5b04c 2024-01-23 c061ba30f7 2024-01-23 868205943c 2024-01-25 Applets (cache, stats) write zero-copy into the peer mux. 91b77c1632 2024-01-25 reserves space for unknown-size chunked output and patches the size afterwards. • ea09075f59 2024-01-25 The connection hash is fed field by field with `XXH64_update()` instead of concatenating then hashing. • 107d6d7546 2024-02-20 db1cd8f881 2024-03-18 d26a160133 2024-05-02 Addresses are written straight into the cmsg buffer, `inet_pton()` writes into the destination, and log dates print straight into the output. • 4d8d922e26 2026-10-21 546ddc5faa 2026-10-21 `smp_prepare_out()` lets `lower`/`upper` transform into a fresh output in one pass instead of copying then modifying. • Also seen in memcached (memcached-037): 32f382b605 2003-05-27 `conn_nread`: the item is allocated in `process_command`, leftovers are drained from the read buffer, and then `read(c->sfd, ..., c->rlbytes)` goes into `it->data`. • a85a6e15d3 2008-04-29 The binary protocol leaves header and key in the read buffer and points at them in place (`binary_get_key`). • bb0980fbba 2018-10-23 (code as of this commit) `_get_extstore()` allocates the destination item or chunks before queuing the flash read, so the data lands in its final RAM location. • 585d03f198 2020-12-08 `conn_nread` can also target a temporary malloced blob (`c->item_malloced`), so the proxy gets the same direct read for buffered set values. • Also seen in Varnish (varnish-060): ef73f2f812 2006-04-19 The first fetch code read backend bodies directly into storage chunks. `fetch_straight` does one `STV_alloc(cl)` and `HTC_Read` into `st->ptr`. • c048d79e1a 2015-02-23 `http_TimeHeader` allocates the final size in the workspace and formats the date into its tail, "to avoid some pointless double-buffering". • 5921f91a7b 2016-01-05 `VSB_extend` uses `realloc` for dynamic buffers: "Avoids memcpy/free". • cb82537ddb 2016-08-15 "Avoid a pointless strdup(), just return the vsb already": the compiler output is written with `VSB_len`. • Also seen in TigerBeetle (tigerbeetle-053): eac5b772ea 2023-11-27 Removes the client `demux_buffer`. `decode(event_offset, event_count)` returns a subslice of the reply and adjusts `result.index -= event_offset`. 62c1eb8cc3 2023-12-01 replaces the intrusive demux pool with a simple free list. • This depends on the resumable EWAH codec in dfbb8abc2c 2024-01-17. • 7edff1d712 2024-10-03 Compaction consumes the sorted in-memory table's values in place, one block's worth at a time, instead of copying them into staging blocks. 820da61ccc 2024-10-03 removes the staging block. • Also seen in simdjson (simdjson-035): d6bd35410 2026-10-01 Deserialization assigns directly into destination strings and, for large or non-trivial elements, emplaces an empty element and fills it (removing it on error): recipe_book +24% on Xeon (1042 to 1296 MB/s), +7% on Zen 5. Small elements keep the temporary, because in-place value-initialisation emitted `rep stos` (-17% on citm). • 5e9765757 2026-11-20 `to_json_array` resizes the destination once (`resize_for_direct_write`) and writes directly; an earlier version with try/catch inside the `resize_and_overwrite` callback blocked inlining. • Also seen in curl (curl-063): d301d69fbf 2004-05-12 `snprintf` into a stack buffer followed by `strdup` replaced by a single allocating `aprintf()`. • d8ff0336a5 2007-01-03 Reads had gone through a second "master" buffer for pipelining on every transfer, "a considerable performance difference"; it is now used only when the handle can pipeline. 18a758d907 rewinds chunked leftovers only when pipelining. • ea12c72d12 2011-10-13 `gethostname()` writes into the caller's buffer and is trimmed in place ("avoid a double copy"). • 7e8561e030 2020-09-22 `Curl_dyn_vprintf()` drives the printf engine straight into the caller's dynbuf; `Curl_dyn_addf()` no longer does vaprintf + append + free. • 4cfa5bcc9a 2023-04-11 The URL parser normalizes directly into the dynbuf instead of an intermediate buffer, part of a ~41% faster parse. • 48e196426b 2026-09-01 `curl_easy_escape` counts unreserved runs and writes into a pre-sized allocation instead of dynbuf appends.

### haproxy-071 — Avoid compaction that moves more bytes than it saves
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy · Also: Netty
Principle: Realign or defragment a buffer only when the amount moved is small or the data will then fit. Otherwise copy what fits, split the rest, or wait. Coalesce small fragments only when the data is being copied anyway.
Why it is faster: Compacting 15 kB to make room for a 1 kB append costs far more than it saves. Treating a nearly full buffer as full, so the next call does one large aligned copy or a swap, beats a partial top-up. Conversely, moving a few bytes to the front before a read avoids splitting that read into two syscalls. Merging many tiny data blocks when they are copied anyway (on transfer or defragmentation) removes per-block overhead in later passes at almost no extra cost.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: ring buffers with scatter-gather writes instead of compaction, and coalescing iovecs before `writev`. • Web / backend: rope or chunked strings in editors and template engines. Merge small rope nodes only when rebuilding anyway. • Data / databases: log-structured storage that compacts in the background or by threshold, not on every write.
Watch out: never compacting leads to fragmentation that blocks large appends. Use explicit thresholds (bytes moved, fraction free) rather than "never" or "always".
Seen in HAProxy: 29a22bc0a7 2018-12-04 A nearly empty H1 input buffer is realigned before `rcv_buf`, which is "cheaper … than to do 2 calls to recv()". • 06ae84a8ac 2018-12-12 If an H2 frame does not fit, or is at least 1/4 of the buffer, the mux is marked full instead of calling `b_slow_realign`: a little over +1% H2 bandwidth. • 8ab128c06a 2019-03-21 Larger blocks are copied when the realignment cost is small: 1-10% better downloads. • 455d5681b6 2019-05-24 H2 skips realignment when the data will not fit and refuses to move more than 1 kB: stable bitrate, with realignments about once per 15 s at 60 Gbps. • 0350b90e31 2019-05-28 `htx_add_data()` never defragments and accepts partial copies: 44 to 55 Gbps cached, and 700 Mbps to 30 Gbps uncached with 1 MB buffers. d7884d3449 2019-06-11 Both free areas of an HTX message are tracked so defragmentation is needed less often. • 783db96ccb 2026-02-03 0c6f2207fc 2026-02-03 Adjacent DATA blocks are merged during transfer and defragmentation. f559c202fb 2026-02-10 Unaligned messages are defragmented while waiting for the payload. • Also seen in Netty (netty-067): c2d2f0b254 2012-02-28 "FrameDecoder discards readableBytes only when it has to" and "ReplayingDecoder does not perform memory copy if possible". ea6ed5cb61 2012-02-29 Copies only the part after the checkpoint. • e329cc3524 2012-06-19 The zero-copy frame decoder copies a fragment only when the capacity it pins exceeds a threshold, and copies only the live bytes. • a2aadef4da 2012-08-08 `discardSomeReadBytes()` compacts only when `readerIndex >= capacity >>> 1` or on a full drain. 689c47980b 2012-02-29 Minimum cumulation allocation of 256 bytes. • c8a941d01e 2015-09-25 The decoder discards read bytes every 16 reads to bound memory without compacting on each one.

### haproxy-072 — A small bounded ring of buffers per multiplexed connection
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Give a multiplexed connection a ring of output buffers rather than one, but cap the number in use to the number of active streams.
Why it is faster: With a single buffer, one stream fills it and stalls every other stream, and constant full/empty transitions multiply context switches. A ring smooths the flow. Beyond one buffer per active stream, extra buffering adds no throughput and just pins RAM and pollutes caches, so producers should block at that point.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: per-connection write-queue limits in HTTP/2 and gRPC servers, tied to the number of active streams. • Native / systems: NIC TX rings, and double or triple buffering in graphics and audio. • Embedded: bounded per-socket TX queues in small network stacks.
Watch out: a ring with no cap turns into a memory amplifier under many slow clients. The cap is what makes it safe.
Seen in HAProxy: 9c218e7521 2019-05-26 A 31-buffer ring for mux-h2 (+1 kB per connection): throughput went from a varying 34-95 Gbps to a steady 88-96 Gbps, and buffer-full events dropped 2000-4000×. d9464167fa 2020-01-10 then removed a stop/start workaround that was no longer needed. • 0a7ab7067f 2023-11-09 `h2s_make_data()` blocks when ring buffers exceed `nb_streams`: 10k single-stream connections went from about 180k to about 20k buffers, with better bandwidth. d656ac7e13 2023-11-28 The same cap applies to the zero-copy path, and 16 parallel streams now use half the memory.

### haproxy-073 — Tiered buffer sizes: small for small or waiting payloads, large only on demand
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Offer several buffer size classes. Move small or queued payloads into small buffers, and use large buffers only when a request needs them.
Why it is faster: A single fixed size wastes memory on requests that sit in queues, on headers and on health checks, and blocks bodies that are too large. With send buffers counted against a window, a small frame should not consume a full slot. The cost is conversion between sizes and the rule that zero-copy swaps only work between buffers of equal size.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: sized pooled allocators (Netty's tiny/small/normal size classes), and compact representations for queued requests. • Data / databases: keep queued queries compact and spill large results to separately sized buffers. • Embedded: two mbuf sizes (small for ACK/control, large for data), as in many TCP/IP stacks.
Watch out: every size boundary is a copy. Shrinking a payload that will soon grow again costs more than it saves, so move into small buffers only payloads that are idle or waiting.
Seen in HAProxy: d0d8e57d47 2024-08-13 885e4c5cf8 2024-06-13 A small-buffer pool was added (`tune.bufsize.small`). b355e89bf9 2024-08-14 H3 HEADERS frames use small buffers, so they no longer consume a full in-flight slot. • d89ec33a34 2026-01-30 ce912271db 2026-02-03 5737fc9518 2026-02-03 A large-buffer pool, used only when needed (for example while waiting for a body). • 5acdda4eed 2026-03-09 181cd8ba8a 2026-03-10 c9a9fa813b 2026-03-16 a61ea0f414 2026-03-19 92a24a4e87 2026-03-19 Queued HTTP requests, TCP streams, L7 retries, health checks and small chunks move into small buffers. 0213dd70c9 2026-03-10 adds helpers to transfer between sizes.

### haproxy-074 — Trim pools adaptively, and reclaim only when something was freed
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O
Principle: Track a slowly decaying estimate of how many objects are really needed, free surplus objects directly to the OS, run expensive reclamation only after cleanup actually released memory, and let operators disable unbounded trimming.
Why it is faster: Pools keep their peak size after a spike. Freeing straight to the OS once the pool exceeds about 125% of the average need returns RSS without hurting steady-state reuse. Calls like `malloc_trim` (which issues many `madvise` calls) are expensive even when there is nothing to return, and on a huge heap they can block long enough to trip a watchdog.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: object and connection pools that shrink towards a moving average of demand, and `System.gc()`/compaction triggered only after large frees. • Data / databases: buffer-pool shrink policies, and running VACUUM only when there are free pages. • Embedded: trim RTOS heaps only after large releases.
Watch out: trimming too eagerly causes allocate/free churn at the next burst. Bump the estimate quickly on misses and decay it slowly.
Seen in HAProxy: a1e4f8c27c 2020-05-08 `needed_avg`, a sliding average over 1024 samples. 63a8738724 2020-05-08 Objects are freed directly when `allocated >= 1.25*needed_avg`: after a 40k-connection spike, RSS returned to about 69 MB instead of staying at 938 MB. • 88366c2926 2020-11-03 114d698fde 2020-11-03 `malloc_trim()` is called from `pool_gc()` and on map reload. • c4e56dc58c 2022-03-08 The `no-memory-trimming` option was added after very long `malloc_trim()` calls on reload triggered the watchdog. • 6e1fe253b7 2023-03-28 `pool_gc()` runs only if the stick-table purge freed something. strace had shown about 29.7k `madvise` calls during soft-stop. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-048): 48b9b13772 2021-07-23 `h2o_mem_clear_recycle(full=0)` frees one chunk per bin after each `h2o_evloop_run`. 40e132838d 2021-08-09 throttles it to once per second, and b791173449 2021-11-01 extends it to the TLS buffer recycler. • b75be3ec2 2022-06-08 Replaces the fixed `max_cnt` with a growable vector plus a `low_watermark` updated on each allocation. 635753e89 2022-06-16 frees `(low_watermark+1)/2` per period. Merged as PR #3031 "auto-tuning of memory recycler" (6bc369fcf 2022-06-30). • 65e5c2702 2022-06-13 The loop waits `INT32_MAX` when all recyclers are empty and 1000 ms otherwise. 1f3b5c9b1 2022-10-31 adds the mem-pool recycler, and d4f00918d 2022-11-01 centralises the logic in `h2o_cleanup_thread`. • 38ca911748 2023-02-16 Cheap maintenance (the file cache) runs every iteration. Recycler GC is rate-limited via a thread-local `next_gc_at`, and the function returns the maximum time the loop may sleep.

### haproxy-075 — Give allocations under memory pressure priority classes and a reserve
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When buffers are scarce, classify requests by criticality, serve waiters from per-class queues, refuse less critical requests while more critical ones wait, and keep a small emergency reserve so the most critical work can always progress.
Why it is faster: First-come-first-served allocation under pressure can deadlock (every task holds one buffer and waits for a second) or let bulk traffic starve the work that would free memory. A reserve margin and priority ordering keep the system making forward progress, and its throughput degrades smoothly instead of collapsing.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: kernel `GFP_ATOMIC`-style reserves and mempools for I/O completion paths. • Data / databases: buffer-pool reservations for WAL and recovery that queries cannot consume. • Web / backend: connection-pool priority lanes so health checks and control traffic never queue behind bulk work.
Watch out: priority systems can starve low classes indefinitely, so make sure the lowest class still eventually gets served.
Seen in HAProxy: 656859d478 2014-11-25 f4718e8ec0 2014-12-02 An atomic two-buffer allocator, and a margin so the last buffer is never taken (deadlock avoidance). • 44e973f508 2018-03-01 One buffer wait slot per H2 connection instead of one per direction. • 72d0dcda8e 2024-04-16 A criticality argument was added to `b_alloc()`. a5d6a79986 2024-04-22 Waiters are queued on 4 heads with a bitmap of non-empty queues. d1eb48a12b 2024-04-24 Requests defer to more critical waiters. • 9a27d7aa6f 2024-04-29 a160b3c50c 2024-04-29 Wake-up callbacks do not allocate, so one task can obtain all the buffers it needs. 0ce51dc93b 2024-04-26 fc792694a6 2024-04-29 A small per-thread emergency buffer pool.

### haproxy-076 — Create hot-path resources at startup instead of lazily under a lock
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy
Principle: For resources that the hot path always needs, create them at boot so the fast path needs neither an "initialised yet?" check nor a lock, and cannot fail at runtime.
Why it is faster: Lazy creation on a shared path needs a check, often a lock, and an error path in every caller. Doing it once at startup removes all three and moves failure to a point where it is easy to report. This complements lazy allocation: be lazy for optional per-object parts, and eager for always-needed global infrastructure.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: eager singletons in DI containers for clients and pools that every request uses. • Embedded: static allocation of all pools at boot, so there are no runtime allocation failures. • Native / systems: preallocate per-thread arrays at thread creation instead of using `pthread_once`/lazy TLS on every access.
Watch out: eager creation of resources that are often unused inflates startup time and RSS, so apply it only to resources that are always or almost always needed.
Seen in HAProxy: e548974ca8 2018-11-26 The compression pool is created at startup, which "has performance and reliability impacts" and removes a spinlock. • 7f0165e399 2018-11-26 The pool cache became an array initialised at boot instead of a lazily initialised thread_local: init checks and 2.5 kB of code left the alloc/free paths.

### haproxy-077 — Grow buffers in rounded steps, not by the exact few bytes needed
Lens: allocation · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When growing a buffer, round the requested size up to the allocator's granularity (or grow geometrically) instead of reallocating for each small increment.
Why it is faster: The allocator rounds to 8 or 16 bytes anyway, so asking for the exact size gains nothing and forces another `realloc` at the next small increment. Rounding up is free and removes the repeated calls.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: preallocate arrays and typed arrays in JS, and set `StringBuilder` capacity ahead of time. • Data / databases: grow row and record buffers in page-sized steps. • Native / systems: use geometric growth (1.5× or 2×) for vectors and string builders.
Watch out: geometric growth can overshoot badly for huge buffers. Cap the growth step or use chunked storage beyond a threshold.
Seen in HAProxy: 2c6fe24001 2023-08-21 `ssl_sess_new_srv_cb()` rounds with `len = (len + 7) & -8`, after traces showed repeated realloc(+1/+2) even for health checks.

### haproxy-078 — Resolve static decisions once at load time, not per event
Lens: caching · Source: HAProxy · Also: Envoy, Kafka
Principle: Anything that depends only on configuration or on an object's fixed identity (combined flags, option compatibility, derived masks, hash seeds, length bounds) should be computed once after parsing and stored where the hot path reads it.
Why it is faster: Per-request code then does a load instead of combinatorial logic, shifts, string concatenation or a hash of a constant prefix. Precomputed bounds also let the hot path reject inputs before any copy or compare. The cost is keeping the derived values consistent when configuration changes at runtime (hot reload), so recompute them on those events.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: compile routing tables, middleware chains, templates and regexes at startup. Use seeded/keyed hashing for namespaced keys instead of `prefix + key` strings. • Data / databases: prepared statements, plan caching, and min/max-length prefilters for LIKE or regex predicates. • Embedded: derive masks, shifts and calibration constants at boot into `const` or RAM tables. • Native / systems: store string lengths next to the strings (pointer+length types) so `strlen` is never recomputed.
Watch out: stale derived state after a runtime config change is a classic bug. Tie recomputation to every mutation path, including hot reload.
Seen in HAProxy: 3bc13774e1 2008-12-07 Analyser flags preset on the listener at registration. c1a2167e9d 2009-08-16 Frontend and backend analyser bitmaps precomputed and merged at config time. • 5f4a47b701 2017-10-31 387bd4f69f 2017-11-10 Repeated `1UL<<tid` and `1UL<<(id-1)` expressions replaced by stored `tid_bit` and `pid_bit`. • 36d1565640 2022-10-17 The table name length is cached to avoid repeated `strlen`. 56460ee52a 2022-11-28 `t->hash_seed = XXH64(t->id, ...)` at init, and keys are then hashed with that seed instead of concatenating name and key: +1% with an 80-byte table name. b4b03779d0 2022-03-05 A header name is stored as an `ist` (pointer and length). • 03ca16f38b 2024-04-30 Log-format option bits are resolved per node after parsing instead of in `sess_build_logline()`. 48e0efb00b 2024-04-30 Encoding checks run only for dynamic nodes. • 076ec9443c 2026-02-14 The maximum length of any header that could match is computed at init and on reload, so longer headers are skipped before any copy or compare. • Also seen in Envoy (envoy-078): 23caf7fc51 2016-11-04 Stat prefix `cluster.X.` and the maintenance-mode runtime key are built in the cluster constructor; the router stopped formatting them per request. • 01e800451f 2016-11-12 LB runtime key literals became `static const std::string` so picks no longer allocate. • ea4c1bc3ba 2018-02-02 Cached the URL-encoded PEM certificate used for XFCC. • 43fc77901e 2018-12-04 Response-code stat names precomputed in a `CodeStats` object. • c6627752ac 2020-05-17 HTTP/1 and HTTP/2 codec stats created once per cluster and passed to every connection. • 8ea1ef2f3a 2021-12-07 Router context caches the generic conn-pool factory pointer instead of a registry lookup by name per request. • 24840256cb 2022-02-16 Stopped creating regex program-size stats on every regex compile (symbol-table locking during config processing). • 35f8d59b11 2022-02-21 tcp_proxy builds its tunneling `HeaderParser` once at config time; 7858bd2e23 captures the cache reference in the filter factory. • 96451cb003 2023-11-20 Direct-response content is read once in the factory, not per connection. • 5b0d56db1d 2024-10-15 Proxy-protocol stats built once in the socket factory; sockets hold a reference (5a80c0e360 also replaced `+` concatenation in stats setup). • c6a4a29326 2025-12-30 Lua builds the lower-cased header key once outside the multi-value loop. • 8128c1cf48 2026-04-15 Router precomputes which strict-check headers are enabled as bools instead of string-comparing per header. • e1b1b0e9f8 2026-10-16 mongo_proxy builds its ~23 stats once at config load instead of per connection. • cce7948758 2026-11-13 RBAC builds metadata field names at config load and resolves the per-route config once per request instead of 4-9 times. • 6cff6c2768 2026-11-13 ext_proc builds its gRPC client cache key once per filter config; It runs on `Log.appendAsFollower`. • c2cc8a6b43 2020-11-06 `ConnectionQuotas` caches `interBrokerListenerName` instead of deriving it several times per accept. • 71fa008b45 2023-03-03 `MirrorConnectorConfig` caches the `ReplicationPolicy` instance. ee65ca22b5 2023-12-24 `StringSerializer`/`StringDeserializer` store a resolved `Charset` instead of looking up a name on every call. 133f2b0f31 2024-06-14 makes `SystemTime` a singleton. • 9dfc5a7242 2026-05-16 uses `MetadataRecordSerde.INSTANCE` everywhere. e0f32977ca 2026-06-17 precomputes `VoterSet.voterKeys` instead of a stream plus a new HashSet per call. ed9898d7d3 2026-07-30 `SaslServerAuthenticator` caches the `KafkaPrincipal` per session instead of invoking the principal builder on every `principal()` call. • 4a0e011be3 2015-11-26 `MemoizingConstructorSupplier` caches reflective Snappy/LZ4 stream constructors; producer CPU dropped about 20% in production.

### haproxy-079 — Memoize expensive non-indexable results, but gate the cache on cost
Lens: caching · Source: HAProxy · Also: Envoy, NATS
Principle: Cache the results of costly operations that cannot be indexed (regex or substring matching over large lists, User-Agent detection) in an LRU keyed by a hash of the input. Bypass the cache when the work is cheap (few entries) or the answer is known in advance (empty set).
Why it is faster: A hit replaces thousands of regex evaluations with one hash and one lookup. HAProxy saw 6k/s go back to 70k/s with 10k regexes. The cache has its own fixed cost, though: hashing, LRU maintenance, locking and pre-creating an entry on a miss. Below a break-even point, recomputing is cheaper, and on an empty list the cache is pure overhead. A revision number on each entry invalidates stale results when the pattern list changes, and the rare hash collision is an accepted, bounded risk.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: cache UA parsing, GeoIP lookups and regex routing decisions, but skip Redis or a memo cache for computations cheaper than the round trip. • Data / databases: compiled query plans keyed by normalised SQL. Skip the query entirely when the ID list is empty. • Native / systems: JIT tier-up thresholds are the same idea, where only code hot enough to repay compilation gets compiled.
Watch out: hash-keyed result caches can return wrong answers on collision, so use 64-bit or wider hashes and a seed if attackers control the inputs. Measure the break-even instead of guessing it.
Seen in HAProxy: 69c696c138 2015-04-28 A 64-bit ebtree LRU. f3045d2a06 2015-04-29 A global LRU cache for pattern matching keyed by hash of (input, expression, seed): 10k regexes went from 6k/s back to 70k/s. • 105c8e6368 2015-06-29 The same cache applied to 51Degrees User-Agent detection. • 821fc95146 2023-08-22 Every `pat_match_*()` function checks that the pattern list is non-empty before touching the LRU: about +10% request rate for a single empty-list match on 8 threads. • 555994c968 2024-11-03 The LRU is consulted only when the list has at least 20 entries for simple matchers and at least 5 for regex. • Also seen in Envoy (envoy-089): 17efc83801 2018-07-17 Subset LB looks up the filter metadata map once and skips re-evaluating predicates for hosts already known to match. • 1b699a2b26 2018-11-26 Rejected stats are evicted from scope caches when the stats matcher is applied. • e8b4d0f0bc 2019-03-19 Stats-matcher results cached, including rejected names, "to avoid doing regex matches on hot-path". • dd7bab757c 2021-07-08 When the encoded-prefix fast path decides, rejected names are no longer stored, which cut memory significantly. • 27387070f8 2021-07-22 JWT cache: LRU of verified tokens (100 entries), rechecks only the time constraint on lookup, and skips tokens over 4 KiB. • Also seen in NATS (nats-070): 15ad596019 2012-11-07 "Added RR eviction and size bounds to cache": `defaultCacheMax = 1024`, `RemoveRandom()`. 504726f47d (2012-11-13) appends a new subscription to every cached key it matches, and tokenizes outside the lock. • eb28cf0eda 2018-03-10 Updates copy only the queue-group slices of a cached result, since readers may hold references. • 543d403337 2018-08-25 Literal subscriptions update only their exact cache key. d21ac8de35 (same day) moves the cache to `sync.Map`; one background sweeper, claimed by CAS, evicts down to a low-water mark (`slCacheSweep = 512`). • 03aacecb81 2020-05-19 Back to a plain map under the RWMutex: `sync.Map` gave "only about 2x in the crazy extremes" while a normal map lets batch removal work efficiently. • acc8da8b6e 2020-05-19 `RemoveBatch` sets the cache to nil, removes without cache updates, bumps the generation and starts an empty cache that refills on demand.

### haproxy-080 — A one-entry "last lookup" cache in front of a search
Lens: caching · Source: HAProxy · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: When consecutive lookups usually target the same key, keep the last {key, result} pair and compare against it before searching. Invalidate the pair when that key is removed.
Why it is faster: A single compare replaces an O(log n) tree descent or a hash probe and its cache misses. Real workloads have strong temporal locality (the same peer dictionary entry, the "current generation" of a map), so the hit rate is very high and the memory cost is one pair.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: a last-used-page cache in B-tree cursors, which helps sequential and clustered inserts. • Native / systems: inline caches in interpreters and VMs, and last-hit caches in symbol tables and DNS resolvers. • Web / backend: memoise the current tenant or config object per request instead of resolving it on every access.
Watch out: forgetting to invalidate the pair on delete creates a dangling pointer. Under shared mutation the pair itself needs synchronisation, or it should be thread-local.
Seen in HAProxy: b65717fa55 2019-06-07 Peers keep `prev_lookup` to skip the ebtree lookup when the key repeats. fd827937ed 2019-06-07 The stored dictionary entry length is used instead of `strlen()`. • 545cf59b6f 2025-12-17 Map elements are grouped per generation. 1fdccbe8da 2025-12-22 `pat_ref->cached_gen` caches the current generation: with 10M map entries, commit+clear went from 3.3 s to 2.1 s, insert from about 20.5 s to 18.9 s, and startup from 4.22 s to 3.78 s. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-104): bad5fe9797f 2002-06-15 The compiled-regex cache search starts at the last hit and wraps around. • 299c4420778 2005-05-29 `superuser_arg` keeps the last user ID and result, invalidated by a syscache callback. e6b92542a39 2005-10-06 avoids a redundant cache lookup on success. • 611b4393f22 2008-03-11 `TransactionIdIsInProgress` checks a single-item XID status cache first, saving "a lot of shared-memory searches". • f5ae3ba4828 2015-01-16 and 779fdcdeeeb 2015-01-16 The TID bitmap caches the last page entry, invalidated if the page becomes lossy. • 0e57b4d8bd9 2015-10-09 Text sorts cache the previous strxfrm blob and strcoll result, which "don't seem to hurt even when they don't help". • 2b272734353 2018-03-26 B-tree inserts cache the rightmost leaf. For increasing keys they skip the root-to-leaf descent, using a try-lock and falling back on failure. • 3592e0ff98b 2022-08-02 After the same partition is found 16 times in a row, tuple routing just checks the last partition's bounds instead of binary searching. • a86c61c9eef 2023-12-04 The search_path cache checks its last entry before hashing. 12d3345c0d2 2024-09-03 A last-collation cache. 30df61990c6 2025-12-14 A last-entry check for buffer refcounts with an inline hit path.

### haproxy-081 — Resolve derived positions and pointers once per context and carry them
Lens: caching · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When several consumers need the same field of a record, or every update resolves the same indirection (shard by thread group, field offset in a line), resolve it once and store the final pointer in the context that lives for the whole operation.
Why it is faster: Each resolution costs dependent loads (a TLS read, an array index, a pointer chase) or a rescan from the start of the record. A session never changes thread group and a log line never moves, so the result is stable and caching it is safe. HAProxy recovered most of a ~20% regression this way, and a log analyser got 40% faster.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: resolve the shard or tenant handle once in middleware and attach it to the request, and hoist `ThreadLocal.get()` out of loops. • Data / databases: store the resolved partition or page pointer in the cursor, and memoise column offsets in a row decoder. • Native / systems: compute `split()`/field offsets once per line in log processors.
Watch out: this is only valid while the underlying mapping is stable for the context's lifetime. If objects can migrate (thread, shard, page), the cached pointer must be refreshed.
Seen in HAProxy: 758a6ea46c 2011-07-10 halog caches `time_field` and other common field positions: status counts with an HTTP pre-check are 40% faster. 26deaf51d9 2011-07-10 The line format is checked once and the pointer reused. • c24de077bd 2025-07-24 Sessions and streams store "fast pointers" to their thread group's counters (`fe_tgcounters`, `be_tgcounters`, ...) instead of resolving `shared[tgid]` on each update. Together with 75e480d107 2025-07-22, this restored performance to 3.2 levels.

### haproxy-082 — Cache expensive clock reads and time conversions, refreshed by a cheap key
Lens: caching · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Take one timestamp per scheduling step and share it, use a nearly free coarse clock as the "has time moved?" key for refreshing costly values, and memoise time conversions per thread, because the same second repeats constantly.
Why it is faster: Precise clock and CPU-time reads can be syscalls, and library conversions like glibc `localtime()` take a global lock. On 64 cores, HAProxy's logging alone halved throughput, with 80% of the time spent in a spinlock slow path. A small thread-local ring keyed by input second hits almost always on the first probe when scanned newest-first, and removes both the lock and the computation. A coarse clock (vDSO, tick resolution) costs a few nanoseconds and tells you when a cached value needs refreshing.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: cache `Date.now()` and the formatted HTTP `Date` header per event-loop tick, and cache formatted log timestamps per thread (Java `DateTimeFormatter` output, Node ISO strings). • Native / systems: capture time once per frame or per request, and cache `getpwuid`-style lock-taking libc results per thread. • Embedded: gate RTC-to-calendar conversions on a systick change.
Watch out: a shared per-step timestamp drifts during a long task, so do not use it for precise intervals inside heavy work. Check which libc calls take global locks; it is not documented in their signatures.
Seen in HAProxy: 07cbd8e074 2022-11-25 `now_mono_time_fast()` uses `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_COARSE`. df188f145b 2023-04-04 `now_cpu_time_fast()` keeps a thread-local CPU-time cache that is refreshed only when the coarse time changes. • c5052bad8a 2024-11-19 The scheduler takes one timestamp for tasks flagged `TASK_F_WANTS_TIME`. 22bd92a87f 2024-11-21 QUIC pacing reads it instead of calling `now_cpu_time()`. • 34b9b528e8 2026-07-23 `get_localtime()`/`get_gmtime()` got a 4-entry thread-local ring visited newest-first. `option httplog` had cut throughput from 1.8M to 910k req/s on 64 cores, and it now runs at native speed (12k misses for 38.4M lookups, against 59M with a naive scan order).

### haproxy-083 — Share expensive-to-rebuild state (TLS sessions) across workers
Lens: caching · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Put state whose reconstruction is very costly, such as TLS session resumption data, in a cache that all processes or threads can read. Use cheap locks or read-mostly locks, and skip locking entirely when there is only one worker.
Why it is faster: A full TLS handshake costs orders of magnitude more than a resumption. With per-worker caches, every new worker or thread pays full handshakes until its own cache warms, which grows with the thread count and caused minutes-long rekeying storms after restarts. Fixed-size blocks let large sessions span several slots. Comparing a stored hash (for example of SNI) avoids keeping and comparing strings in the cache.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: shared TLS session caches and ticket keys across workers (as in Nginx's `ssl_session_cache shared`), and in shared memory for pre-fork servers such as PHP-FPM. • Data / databases: share prepared-statement metadata or authentication results across pool workers. • Native / systems: shared JIT or compiled-artifact caches. Elide the lock when running single-threaded.
Watch out: a shared cache is a contention point and a security boundary. Use read-mostly locking, evict entries that fail resumption (HAProxy clears the index with a CAS), and never share secrets across tenants.
Seen in HAProxy: 3e541d1c03 2012-09-03 A shared-memory SSL session cache using futexes and x86 locked instructions. 4b3091e54e 2012-09-24 No shared memory or locks when there is only one process ("locking is much more expensive than just a test"). • af9619da3e 2012-11-28 128-byte blocks so huge client-cert sessions span several blocks. cd1a526a90 2014-05-07 A spinlock replaces process-shared pthread mutexes by default. • 607041dec3 2023-08-21 52b260bae4 2023-08-21 2cb896c4b0 2023-08-21 Per-thread backend session caches gained an rwlock and a global "last good session" index, so a thread with no session resumes from another thread's. This ends the rekeying storms by health checks after a restart. • 28654f3c9b 2025-12-05 be998b590e 2025-12-05 The cached TLS session stores the SNI hash and no longer stores the SNI string.

### haproxy-084 — Inline the tiny fast path, keep big or cold bodies out of line
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Split hot functions into a small inline wrapper that handles the dominant cheap case and an out-of-line function for everything else. Un-inline large bodies that are copied into many call sites.
Why it is faster: Inlining removes call overhead and lets the compiler specialise the code for each caller, but every inlined copy costs i-cache and inlining budget. A wrapper of a few instructions gets the benefit without the bloat. A medium-sized body inlined at hundreds of sites evicts hot code, so moving it out of line often makes the program both smaller and faster. One exception: inlining a whole cluster of helpers that share state (a bit buffer, counters) at once lets that state live in registers across the calls.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `__attribute__((cold, noinline))` or Rust `#[cold]`/`#[inline(never)]` on growth, error and logging paths; keep `Vec::push`-style fast paths tiny. • Web / backend: keep hot JS functions small and monomorphic so V8 inlines them, and move rare branches into separate functions. • Embedded: in size-optimised firmware, inline only one-line accessors and keep driver bodies out of line to fit the flash and i-cache. • Data / databases: a cache `get()` that inlines the hit path and calls out on a miss.
Watch out: inlining decisions flip with compiler versions and call-site counts. Measure binary size and throughput together, and re-check after big refactors.
Seen in HAProxy: dab722b6b7 2006-05-04 Uninlining 6 common functions cut code size by 15% and raised performance by 1-2%. Later trims: 0bc3493d2c (-3 kB), e33aecefa6, c64e5397f6. • 75cf17ee30 2008-08-29 The opposite case: forcing large ebtree functions inline on gcc ≥ 3 made the code both smaller and faster. e1ee956399 inlined tiny lookup wrappers to save space. • 4df8206832 2008-08-29 An inline check in `task_wakeup` skips already-running tasks (more than 16% of calls were redundant). 531cf0cf8d added inline `task_queue` prechecks (+1%). • 3dd0c4e20e 2012-10-26 Inline `hex2i()`: chunk parser +74%. With 24e6d972aa (+41%) and 55a6906125 (+12%, inline `channel_forward` fast path), the parser got "almost 3 times faster". • f4718e8ec0 2014-12-02 `b_alloc_margin` uses the inline `b_alloc_fast` when the pool has margin. • b992ba16ef 2017-11-05 Factoring the unlock and exit paths of `wake_expired_tasks` saved a few tens of bytes, about +1% overall. • 9c6dbf0eea 2021-02-24 `tasklet_wakeup_on()` split into inline fast path and out-of-line slow path: 6 kB smaller, +1%. • b2a853d5f0 2021-04-19 Un-inlining the 251-byte `pool_put_to_cache()` removed 90-112 kB of code; it had cost up to 4% req/s. `pool_get_from_cache()` stayed inline because its result is used immediately. 407ef893e7 removed ~166 kB of inlined lock debugging, f14d19024b ~270 kB. • 15c322c413 2022-01-24 `pool_free()` became a macro doing the NULL test plus a call to out-of-line `__pool_free()`: ~1% smaller executable; d392973dcc did the same for allocation, another ~1%. • 2c1a9c3a43 2024-09-15 Inlining `vars_prune()`, which usually returns at once because most configs have no variables, folds the emptiness test into the caller. • e3c9a9c6b5 2026-08-11 Inlining all three cooperating Huffman and bit-buffer helpers of the decompressor together: ~352 bytes smaller and 13-17% faster decompression.

### haproxy-085 — Lay out branches for the common case, and verify hints by measurement
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy · Also: Aeron, H2O, jemalloc, memcached, simdjson
Principle: Order tests and mark rare conditions so the hot path runs as straight-line fall-through code, and fold several rare checks into one test. Keep a hint only if a benchmark confirms it helps.
Why it is faster: Taken branches and scattered cold code waste fetch bandwidth and i-cache lines. `unlikely()` moves rare code out of line, and testing a whole flag mask at once removes several branches from the fast path. Testing first the operand that usually decides the outcome also avoids loading the other one, which matters when it sits on a contended cache line. A wrong hint, or one inside a small inline helper, pushes common code far away in every caller.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: C++20 `[[likely]]`/`[[unlikely]]` or Rust `#[cold]`, checked with `perf annotate`; profile-guided optimisation automates this. • Web / backend: check the common path first in hot JS loops and keep exception paths out of hot functions so the JIT does not deoptimise. • Embedded: one combined `(flags & (ERR|EOF|CLOSED))` test per loop in ISRs and drivers. • Data / databases: put cheap, selective predicates first in hand-written filter code.
Watch out: hints that help on one compiler version can hurt on the next. Never hint inside small inline functions without measuring every caller.
Seen in HAProxy: 976f1ee561 2006-12-17 The HTTP parser FSM puts the most frequently taken states first. • 70bcfb77a7 2008-01-27 GCC 4 compiled `__builtin_expect(x!=0,1)` into a double test that cost the HTTP parser 10%, so the macros were redefined. • 534c5a2224 2008-09-04 Hints in `tick_is_expired()`; 47d940485a in list macros; 2281b7fd12 frontend errors marked unlikely; 2b028dd828 tunnel mode marked unlikely (+0.2%). • 0161d62d23 2013-04-02 Hints in the chunk-size parser: +15% in the worst case. • 6d2889ba3d 2008-08-17 A few flag checks skip a series of tests. d6e999b127 folded error handling into handshake handling, removing two tests from the connection fast path (under +0.5%). • aa39860aef 2017-11-10 Removing `unlikely()` from `hex2i` stopped letters being placed "far away in the calling function": chunk parsing about 5% faster. • c939835f77 2017-10-08 `likely()` re-enabled for gcc ≥ 5, which "produces better code with likely() than without", unlike gcc 4. • 1526f1942c 2018-12-10 A hand-ordered comparison tree with the short case hinted in the HPACK length encoder: +1% H2 request rate. • fc76bbc0f5 2021-11-08 Reordering the tests in halog's `field_start()` (`!c` first, then `c == ' '`) so the compiler emits fewer jumps: +5%. • 6c54bf7295 2025-06-08 `unlikely()` on the ebtree leaf test: a consistent +0.5%. • a8f50cff7e 2026-02-11 `srv_dynamic_maxconn()` tests `minconn == maxconn` (usually true, on a rarely written line) before `beconn`, which sits on a busy line. • Also seen in Aeron (aeron-074): 684aabd887 2014-10-04 "Avoid branch mis-prediction on frames uncommon comparing to !tsd_fast". • 7160617107 2019-11-15 Adds `unlikely()` to the non-slab and too-large/sampled checks in `free_fastpath` because "previously there were jumps in the common case", and moves the tcache load after them. • Also seen in memcached (memcached-067): c12ebb2b33 2008-02-24 Adds `likely()`/`unlikely()` as `__builtin_expect`, with a no-op fallback for compilers that lack it. • f58de2a4b1 2011-12-15 `do_item_alloc` "reorders branches from most likely to least likely, and removes all redundant tests". It no longer checks refcount or exptime twice on the eviction path. 40b7b4b2b6 2011-12-15 `do_item_get` tests the item for NULL only once ("Far fewer branches now"). • 1c94e12c3d 2012-08-18 The per-call lock-mode check in `item_lock` is wrapped in `likely()`. • Also seen in simdjson (simdjson-054): 326c175dcb 2020-04-15 "Massive performance boost for get<double>": `is_double()` compares `tape[json_index] == tape_double` and `get<double/int64/uint64/bool>` become `if (unlikely(!is_double())) {...}` instead of a switch. • 80d26298a0 2023-09-20 `visit_primitive` tests `'"'` first, then digits or `'-'`, and switches only over `t`, `f`, `n`.

### haproxy-086 — Scan several bytes per instruction with SWAR word tricks
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy · Also: simdjson, Netty
Principle: Load 4 or 8 bytes as one integer and use carry and borrow arithmetic to test all of them at once for "below X", "equals X" or "outside [lo, hi]". Fall back to per-byte code only on a hit or at the tail.
Why it is faster: A byte loop pays one load, one compare and one branch per byte. A word test pays the same for 8 bytes and skips whole words in the common clean case. It needs cheap unaligned loads, and it must not read past the buffer (or the buffer needs slack). The more short inputs there are, the smaller the gain: one commit used 32-bit words for URIs so short ones pay little overhead.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: JSON, CSV and HTTP tokenisers that look for quotes, commas or control bytes; a WASM parser fast path. • Embedded: UART or packet scanners on MCUs without SIMD, gated by an architecture check for unaligned access as HAProxy does. • Data / databases: NUL or newline search in log ingestion; ASCII validation in UTF-8 decoders.
Watch out: unaligned word loads fault on some CPUs, and reading past the end needs a guaranteed margin. Bit tricks are easy to get subtly wrong, so unit-test every byte value at every position.
Seen in HAProxy: 1769a18f62 2010-05-04 halog: a branch-free `has_zero()` in fgets made line counting 30% faster; d2c142c7ee added more 64-bit lookups (about +10%). • 0431f9d476 2016-11-05 Header values skipped 8 or 4 bytes at a time with `(*(long*)ptr - 0x0e0e..) & 0x8080..`, stopping on any byte ≤ 0x0D: 194k to 212k req/s with 1 kB cookies (+9.2%). • 5f10ea30f4 2016-11-05 Same idea for the URI, 32-bit only: 212k to 216k req/s with 100-char URIs. • e6d9c21059 2016-11-05 XOR with 0x2c2c2c2c and 0x22222222 plus the has-zero trick finds a comma or quote: 212k to 217k req/s. • 8f3ce06f14 2019-11-22 `ist_find_ctl()` finds any byte below 0x20 eight at a time (about 0.43 bytes/cycle); 54f53ef7ce uses it to validate H2 header values. • e9f4d67b15 2021-11-08 halog `field_start()` uses `has_zero64()` under `HA_UNALIGNED_LE64`, relying on the buffer margin from fgets2: +35% parse speed. • b9bf16b382 2024-04-24 A fused range check `((x - lo*0x01010101) | (hi*0x01010101 - x)) & 0x80808080` merges two branches into one in the URI fast-skip loop, and fixed a correctness bug in the older two-branch form. • b43f64659c 2026-11-04 `ist_find_outside()` reads one unaligned word at a time; de80a2699c makes header validation skip 0x20..0x7e eight bytes at a time, then checks CR, LF and NUL per byte. • Also seen in simdjson (simdjson-099): 6243b14b07 2026-11-20 Plain-string scan in `internal/direct_parse.h`: 20k strings at 3450 MB/s with SWAR versus 1730 MB/s for a byte loop and 2080 MB/s for the general On-Demand path (M5 Max). • Also seen in Netty (netty-087): 5337d3eeb4 2021-01-15 added `ByteBufUtil.firstIndexOf`/`SWARByteSearch`: an unrolled 0-7 byte prefix, then a loop over `_getLongLE`, used only when `PlatformDependent.isUnaligned()` holds. • 0daaec3ac2 2023-01-27 reused SWAR to find (CR)LF in HTTP/1.1 line and header parsing. • 0a1781c863 2024-03-27 added `SWARUtil.applyPattern` with a "test for any set bit before computing the index" step. a38a85c908 2024-04-09 added `lastIndexOf`, 3bd40777a0 2024-04-04 added `bytesBefore`, and b9af2e686a 2024-04-15 switched call sites to `indexOf`. • ed63318c9a 2024-03-25 made `AsciiStringUtil.toLowerCase/UpperCase` use a SWAR `containsUpperCase` with an unrolled int/short/byte tail. It returns `this` when there is nothing to convert.

### haproxy-087 — Pad buffers with a sentinel so inner loops skip bound checks
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Allocate slack bytes after the data and plant a terminator there, so the scan loop can over-read whole words and stop on the terminator instead of testing the end pointer every iteration.
Why it is faster: It removes one compare and branch per iteration, and it makes word-at-a-time scanning safe without a separate tail loop. The cost is a few bytes per buffer and one store per read.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: zero-padded input for SIMD parsers (simdjson-style); a sentinel at the end of a linear search array. • Embedded: a guard byte at the end of a receive ring so the parser needs no length check per byte. • Data / databases: pad page or row buffers so decoders can read whole words.
Watch out: every producer must keep the padding and the sentinel in place. One code path that fills the buffer to its real end turns the over-read into a memory-safety bug.
Seen in HAProxy: 31a02e9c5b 2011-09-10 halog's `fgets2` grows the buffer by 32 or 68 bytes, writes `*end = '\n'` after each read, and changes the loop bound from `end-32` to `end`: about 4% faster on 32-bit. • e9f4d67b15 2021-11-08 Ten years later the 64-bit SWAR `field_start()` relies on that same guaranteed margin (+35%).

### haproxy-088 — Specialise the hot loop for the common input shape
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Add a tight routine for the dominant case (complete, contiguous, small, no options) and fall back to the general code for everything else. Drop support for input variants your producer never emits.
Why it is faster: General code pays for wrap-around, partial input, rare encodings and options on every item. A specialised path drops those tests and keeps state in registers. Each removed comparison in a tight loop counts: one rarely used delimiter check cost 4% of CPU.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: an HTTP/2 or protobuf decoder fast path when the whole frame or at least N bytes are in the buffer; a single-byte varint fast path. • Data / databases: a `COUNT(*)` path that skips row decoding. • Embedded: a parser for your own wire format that rejects unused syntax instead of tolerating it.
Watch out: two paths must stay behaviourally identical. Fuzz them against each other, and keep the fast path's entry test cheaper than what it saves.
Seen in HAProxy: 14389e7036 2011-07-10 halog dropped tab-delimiter support: haproxy never emits tabs, and the check cost 4% CPU. • e1a908c369 2012-01-03 halog `-c` (count only) gets its own minimal loop: 2.5x faster, 2 GB/s of logs on a 2.4 GHz Xeon. • 19ed92b47d 2018-12-11 HPACK encoder tests the common "length < 127" case first and emits the byte directly (about +0.5% request rate); 30eb809fdb adds a helper for known small method/scheme/status encodings. • bdcefe58b7 2021-05-21 `h1_parse_full_contig_chunks()` handles many small, complete, contiguous HTTP chunks (2700 one-byte chunks fit in 16 kB) and leaves leftovers to the generic parser; e071f0e6a4 reserves one max-size output block up front instead of one per chunk. • f7c2044f8f 2021-02-02 Wrapping input is copied in one call instead of running the parser twice.

### haproxy-089 — Write incremental parsers as resumable state machines with direct jumps
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Express the parser as explicit states joined by direct jumps, with one "advance, or save state and return" step that handles running out of input. Parsing then resumes exactly where it stopped, with no re-scan when data arrives in pieces.
Why it is faster: Re-parsing from the start on each partial packet is quadratic in the number of fragments. Direct `goto` transitions compile to predictable jumps with state held in the program counter, instead of a loop with a big `switch` on a state variable. Switch tables still beat long if/else chains for the remaining dispatch.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: streaming HTTP, Redis or MQTT parsers in Node, Go or Rust that receive data in chunks. • Embedded: protocol decoders on microcontrollers that get bytes one interrupt at a time. • Native / systems: incremental lexers in editors and language servers.
Watch out: goto-heavy state machines are hard to review. Keep the state set small and documented, and fuzz the resume points with every possible split position.
Seen in HAProxy: 58f10d7478 2006-12-04 The HTTP parser was replaced by an RFC 2616 FSM that indexes and parses headers only once. • 8d5d7f20b9 2007-01-21 The "huge rework" introduced `EAT_AND_JUMP_OR_RETURN(good, st)` (`ptr++; if (likely(ptr<end)) goto good; else {state=st; goto ood;}`). A request parses in about 2 µs at 1 GHz. • 230fd0bfdf 2006-12-17 Hand-rolled loops instead of `while()`: about +2%. caabe41a15 adjusted states and direct branches in the forward loops: a few percent. • d655ffe863 2013-04-02 The response-forwarding if/else chain became a `switch`: -20% time in the worst case (1-byte chunks).

### haproxy-090 — Use the hardware's single instructions for bit and checksum work
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O
Principle: Replace portable loops for find-first-set, count-leading-zeros, popcount, byte swap and CRC with the matching CPU instruction or compiler builtin, and with branch-free bit arithmetic where none exists.
Why it is faster: A portable find-first-set or 64-bit byteswap takes dozens of instructions and often a loop; `bsr`, `bswap`, `clz` or a CRC instruction does it in one. Native CRC also removes a 4 kB table from the L1 cache. Where no instruction exists, a parallel formula (multiply-based popcount, a fixed binary search over halves) is branch-free and runs in constant time.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `__builtin_ctzll`, `std::countr_zero` or `u64::trailing_zeros` in bitmap allocators and schedulers; `__builtin_bswap64` in binary codecs. • Web / backend: `Math.clz32` in JS; hardware CRC32C via the platform library for checksums. • Embedded: ARMv8 CRC and CLZ instructions instead of table-driven code in flash-constrained firmware. • Data / databases: popcount and select over bitmaps in roaring bitmaps and column filters.
Watch out: many of these instructions are undefined for zero input or missing on older CPUs. Keep a portable fallback and test it on every target you ship.
Seen in HAProxy: 36eb3a3ac8 2017-09-20 `my_htonll` became a single `bswap` instead of 2 bswaps, 2 shifts and 2 ORs. • 27346b01aa 2018-10-10 x86_64 inline `bsr` for `my_ffsl`: "~30 instructions and ~80 bytes" removed from the fd-cache and task-wakeup inner loop; the zero-input case was declared undefined to drop the test. • cafa56ecd6 2019-02-02 The iterative popcount loop replaced by the multiply method; f3241115e7 adds `mask_find_rank_bit()` (nth set bit) for choosing a thread from a mask. • c7a8a3c7bd 2022-06-21 `one_among_mask()` picks a well-distributed set bit with a 6-step mirror search in about 4 ns (16 cycles), about 2x faster than rank finding, and avoids the bias of always taking the first bit. • 9e274280a4 2021-05-12 No `crc32_fast` table when native CRC is available; 027fdcb168 uses a generic function for the CRC tail bytes (~1 kB less L1 footprint, +0.5%). • 1c46a07460 2023-12-30 Architecture-specific fls with a `__builtin_clz` fallback: 33% faster string trees on ARM. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-069): 47cb049c95 2015-02-10 The HTTP/2 DRR scheduler dequeues using `__builtin_clzll(drr->bits)` over a 64-slot occupancy bitmap. • a2d3f126c4 2016-02-26 The brotli window size is set to `ceil(log2(content_length))` via `__builtin_clzll`. 03ae4520b1 2026-06-25 mirrors this for zstd with `64 - clzll(len-1)`. • ceab5af397 2017-09-25 The timer wheel picks its level with `clz(last_run ^ expire)`. • 657f2ca5d3 2021-07-23 `buffer_size_to_power` maps a buffer size to its power-of-two recycle bin with `__builtin_clzll`. • 4efd53db72 2023-01-04 In the quantile sketch, `ullog2()` replaced the bit smear, de Bruijn multiply and 64-entry table with `63 - __builtin_clzll(x)`. 5ee146b9f7 fixed the edge values.

### haproxy-091 — Move small, fixed-size data with direct loads and stores, not generic calls
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: For copies and compares of a few bytes, or for assembling a multi-byte integer, use plain assignments, one wide load or an open-coded loop instead of calling `memcpy`, `memcmp` or byte-by-byte assembly.
Why it is faster: Library routines are tuned for large vectors and pay call, size dispatch and alignment overhead that dominates on tiny inputs. A single unaligned 32- or 64-bit load (plus a byte swap) replaces four loads, three shifts and three ORs. When profiling shows one size dominates, special-casing it keeps the generic call for the rest.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `DataView.getUint32` or `Buffer.readUInt32BE` instead of manual shifts in JS; avoid `Buffer.copy` for 1-8 byte fields. • Native / systems: fixed-size key copies in hash tables via a single load/store; `memcpy` plus `be32toh` instead of byte assembly. • Embedded: struct assignment instead of `memcpy` for small fixed sizes. • Data / databases: wire-protocol decoders (Postgres, Kafka) reading header fields as whole words.
Watch out: unaligned loads and endianness must be handled explicitly (use `memcpy` into a local, which compilers turn into one load). Profile first: for large or variable sizes the library call wins.
Seen in HAProxy: e11f727c95 2017-05-30 `struct ist` avoids memcmp/memcpy for short strings because the call often "ends up being higher than the few cycles needed"; ed00e345e2 replacing memcpy with `ist2bin_lc()` gave 0.5-1%. • fcce0e1e09 2022-04-01 The Huffman decoder uses `read_n32()` instead of four shifted byte reads: the fast path is 31 bytes shorter on x86. • 04065b87ce 2021-10-28 aarch64 `__ha_cas_dw()` uses assignment instead of `memcpy()`, which some gcc versions did not inline: 8 kB smaller with gcc 5.4. • 23aa79d9a9 2023-02-02 63% of HTX memcpy calls were 8 bytes, so `htx_memcpy()` does `if (likely(len == 8)) write_u64(dst, read_u64(src)); else memcpy()`. • c1abbb11fa 2026-08-21 On little-endian targets the CBOR encoder writes integers with `memcpy` because the bytes are already in order.

### haproxy-092 — Use the platform's tuned primitives for bulk byte work
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy · Also: Kafka, curl, Tokio, Netty
Principle: For long spans, call the libc or library primitive that is already vectorised (`memchr`, `memcpy`, JIT regex, a modern hash) instead of a hand-written byte loop, and give it aligned buffers.
Why it is faster: Modern libc routines use SSE or AVX to process 16-64 bytes per instruction, and regex JITs compile patterns to machine code. Process contiguous runs with one call and handle ring-buffer wrap once, instead of checking wrap per byte. Copies are fastest on aligned addresses with no split loads. Hand-written assembly is worth it only where the compiler and the library both fall short.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `indexOf`/`Buffer.indexOf` in Node, `bytes.IndexByte` in Go, the `memchr` crate in Rust instead of manual loops. • Data / databases: RE2 or Hyperscan for rule engines; XXH3 or wyhash for hash tables; page buffers aligned to 64 or 4096 bytes. • Embedded: the vendor's optimised `memcpy`/`memchr` with aligned DMA buffers.
Watch out: library speed varies by version and platform (HAProxy gates `memchr` on the glibc version). For strings of a few bytes the call overhead loses to an inline loop.
Seen in HAProxy: f9042060c9 2011-09-10 An assembly `field_start()` saved up to 23% of halog time on i386, and the C version was rewritten to mimic it. • 419a598eae 2012-06-12 glibc 2.11 x86_64 `memchr()` was about 40% faster than halog's hand-coded loop. c4710e14fe enables it automatically on x86_64 glibc ≥ 2.15: about 6 GB/s (20M lines/s). • 7adf8f35df 2020-08-13 Stores `pcre2_jit_match` as the match function when JIT compilation succeeds. • 967e7e79af 2020-12-22 XXH64 replaced by `XXH3_64bits_withSeed`; 6f7cc11e6d uses `XXH_INLINE_ALL` so short inputs avoid call overhead. • 11051ed9c7 2024-09-30 `co_getline()` uses `b_contig_data()` + `memchr('\n')` + `memcpy` instead of a byte loop with wrap checks: 1 GB of logs in 1.56 s instead of 2.44 s (-36%). • 77335f52fc 2025-08-11 The buffer pool became 64-byte aligned (buffers had been randomly aligned to 32 or 64) so memcpy and kernel copies can use full vector lines. • Also seen in Kafka (kafka-079): 1552db7d4c 2020-09-12 `System.arraycopy` replaces a manual byte-copy loop. • b125a06c46 2022-02-07 Deletes the 400-line pure-Java `Crc32` (once faster than JNI) and uses the now-intrinsified `java.util.zip.CRC32`. • aecd47b3bc 2022-08-13 `Checksums.update` routes direct ByteBuffers to the intrinsic `Checksum.update(ByteBuffer)` via a cached MethodHandle, adjusting position/limit instead of slicing. CRC32C on 4 KB direct buffers: 0.056 to 4.004 ops/us (about 70x). • 844c1259a9 2021-08-27 `OrderedBytes.upperRange` skips its per-byte comparison loop when a precomputed property (first suffix byte is 0) makes the result known, and does bulk puts. • ce8c5f8497 2026-03-17 `HeadersBytesStore.convertToHeaderFormat` uses a raw `System.arraycopy` for the fixed empty-header layout: 9,287 to 11,636 ops/s. • 9273cdc491 2025-12-12 `Bytes.BYTES_LEXICO_COMPARATOR` delegates to the vectorized `Arrays.compareUnsigned`; it sits under every TreeMap-backed Streams store lookup. • Also seen in curl (curl-096): 4cfa5bcc9a 2023-04-11 urlapi: "more use of memchr() instead of strchr() and less strlen() calls", with junk scanning done once up front.
