### haproxy-093 — Keep values in locals so the compiler can keep them in registers
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy · Also: Aeron, simdjson, TigerBeetle, curl, Seastar
Principle: Load fields that do not change into local variables before a loop, compute into locals instead of through pointers, and group writes to the same object, so the compiler need not assume aliasing and reload memory.
Why it is faster: Every store through a pointer may, as far as the compiler knows, modify any other field, so it reloads them after each write. Locals remove those loads and spills. In pointer-chasing code over trees too large for L3, each avoided load also saves memory bandwidth, and the code usually shrinks.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: hoist `this.field` and `arr.length` into locals in hot JS or Java loops. • Native / systems: `restrict` on C kernel arguments; read the assembly of hot functions to spot reloads. • Embedded: copy volatile or struct fields into locals in ISRs and DSP loops. • Data / databases: keep the probe key in a register during B-tree search.
Watch out: a local copy goes stale if another thread or a callee legitimately changes the field during the loop. Only hoist values that are invariant for the loop's duration.
Seen in HAProxy: e69eada057 2008-01-27 Unsigned HTTP state and offset types: smaller and faster code. • ce3d44a06a 2011-01-04 `ebmb_lookup` moved the return code out of the loop so one register serves `troot` and `node`: -39 bytes and fewer moves. • 6320c3cb46 2012-12-13 A temporary `n` instead of repeated `fdtab[fd].ev |=`, because gcc kept reloading the pointers. 4a29144591 grouped struct updates (-60 bytes of fast-path code); 462c7206bc explicit bit mapping where gcc failed to fold constants. • b238b12e98 2019-03-06 A fused `LIST_DEL_INIT()` keeps the neighbours in registers and writes the self-links once: +3% peak connection rate on 8 threads with visibly less contention; 9570ecf662 applies it to servers. • 5020ffbe49 2021-04-23 32-bit math in `tv_update_date()` avoids 64-bit mul/div and register spills inside a CAS loop (48 bytes smaller). • bf13e53964 2022-11-15 Prefix-tree insert caches `npfx` and `npfx << 1` in locals: loading a huge map went from 8.0 s to 7.5 s; 91d31c9e1c also keeps the new key local (7.45 s). • 447189f286 2024-03-22 `ring_write()` keeps `tail_ptr` and `ring_queue_ptr` in locals: "smaller and slightly faster". • Also seen in Aeron (aeron-076): 265f495c28 2014-04-27 Register-allocated queue buffer refs; The design doc 9dcf5fca5b 2020-09-12 separates persistent state from inline context "intended to be broken up and stored in registers". • 59d9bc9e48 2020-06-06 Stores `uint32_t *next_structural` instead of base plus index; 5f00b37e21 2020-06-06, d178e089a6 2020-06-06 and 8793dd3ceb 2020-06-06 stop caching `idx`, the current char and `len`. • 42c047a927 2026-05-07 The serializer threads a stack-local `writer` struct (pointer, position, capacity) through the inlined call chain. perf had shown `ldr [position]`/`ldr [capacity]` at 15-20% of time; CITM serialization went 4356 to 6776 MB/s (+55.5%). ec49efa5da 2026-05-08 turns the back-pointer into a reference. • 8c1dd8070e 2026-10-26 `skip_child` keeps position and depth in locals because char loads may alias the member fields (part of a 38-57% skip-time reduction with GCC). • Also seen in TigerBeetle (tigerbeetle-081): f7f799000c 2023-03-23 Completion-based compaction. `Compaction.merge`/`copy` copy `values_in`/`values_out` into locals "to ensure a tight loop" over whole blocks. • 67e67c399c 2024-10-22 Compaction refactor: `values_merge(values_out, values_in_a, values_in_b, drop_tombstones)` and `values_copy_drop_tombstones` became free functions. • 16952e1403 2024-12-22 Added the rule to TIGER_STYLE.md: hot loops are standalone functions with primitive arguments, so the compiler does not have to prove fields unaliased. • d0b7c25c9c 2025-08-25 AEGIS `State128L.update`: `var blocks: [8]AesBlock = state.blocks;` "keeps the blocks in registers" with a "single spill at the end". This is the checksum on every message and block. • 8f1223269d 2026-09-01 Extended the idea across blocks: `update_blocks(*[8]AesBlock, ...)` and `absorb_many()`, because "LLVM commits all eight lanes to memory after every iteration" otherwise. Fixed-count init and finalize loops are unrolled with `inline for`. • Also seen in curl (curl-094): 85f9aa08d9 2018-12-20 `Curl_conncache_unlock()` takes the `Curl_easy` directly instead of a connection whose `->data` it had to dereference on the lock path. • ae5fb4188d 2025-10-14 "reduce use of data->conn->": functions with more than two such uses now load a local `conn` (6 files). • e0c8b4b451 2026-09-21 The base64 encoder loads each input triplet into a local `uint32_t` before emitting any output stores. Code comments keep that order from being "cleaned up". • Also seen in Seastar (seastar-081): fe0608e261 2019-05-05 "GCC's alias analysis could not figure out that it could reuse the pointer to the promise", fixed in the future/promise move constructors (c3f42e3316). • 681164c1ad 2020-08-20 Destructor assembly went from reloading `(%rbp)` to a single load. • 8125cfce75 2025-08-10 `noncopyable_function` trivial move through a temporary: eight byte copies became one move.

### haproxy-094 — Take the dominant case out of indirect dispatch
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When one target dominates an indirect call, or a dispatch is usually the identity, test for that case and handle it directly. Choose per-option function pointers once at setup, and generate concrete variants where a pointer sits in an inner loop.
Why it is faster: An indirect call blocks inlining and needs the branch target predictor. A compare against the expected target plus a direct call is predictable and inlinable, and an identity test skips the table load completely. Resolving options to a function pointer once removes per-record if-ladders. Inside tight loops, duplicated specialised code beats one generic routine with a per-element function pointer.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: speculative devirtualisation in C++ hot loops; Rust generics instead of `dyn` in serializers. • Web / backend: keep JS call sites monomorphic; pick the comparator or codec once outside a sort or encode loop. • Data / databases: skip type coercion in expression evaluators when column and target types match.
Watch out: specialised copies grow the binary. Specialise only the inner loop, and keep shared helpers inline so the variants stay in sync.
Seen in HAProxy: 531cf0cf8d 2009-03-08 `if (likely(t->process == process_session)) t = process_session(t); else t = t->process(t);` • a2b39fb5c5 2011-07-10 halog filters became independent functions selected once at startup: +3% and cleaner code. • 3705deea99 2022-04-25 `sample_fetch_as_type()` skips the cast function when it is `c_none`, since the type usually already matches. • 51ade2f1db 2024-09-15 `sample_process_cnv()` skips the 2-D `sample_casts[][]` load when input and output types match: ~2% CPU on converter-heavy configs. • c1abbb11fa 2026-08-21 Function-pointer CBOR encoders were "a lot CPU consuming" and were replaced by concrete `_bin`/`_hex` variants sharing static inline helpers.

### haproxy-095 — Remove branches and dependency steps with arithmetic
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Express wrap-around, clamps and bit tests as arithmetic or conditional moves, express ring maths as offsets rather than pointer comparisons, and let the compiler delete checks that are provably unnecessary for constant arguments.
Why it is faster: A data-dependent branch that mispredicts costs 15-20 cycles. An arithmetic or cmov form costs a couple of cycles every time. Replacing a variable shift followed by a compare with a mask against a precomputed bit also shortens the dependency chain. `__builtin_constant_p` lets one inline helper drop its check at call sites that pass a constant.
Applies elsewhere: Embedded: power-of-two masks instead of if-wrap in UART and audio ring buffers. • Native / systems: `if constexpr` or const generics to drop checks for compile-time arguments. • Data / databases: branch-free binary search and offset wrap in page and log readers. • Web / backend: branchless clamps in hot numeric JS (typed arrays).
Watch out: when the branch is almost always predicted correctly, a branchless form may be slower because it always does both computations. Check the generated code and benchmark.
Seen in HAProxy: bf43927cd7 2013-04-02 `buffer_count`: `count += count<0 ? size : 0` instead of an if, so gcc emits no jump in a function heavily used by chunk parsing. • ab322d4fd4 2018-07-20 `b_contig_space` rewritten with offsets: "removed around 23 bytes from b_putblk() and it removed any conditional jump". • 84340d108b 2024-10-18 `b_peek_ofs()` skips the wrap check when `__builtin_constant_p(ofs) && ofs == 0`: ~320 bytes saved in hot paths, each 16 B roughly one conditional branch fewer. • c9e4adf608 2025-06-07 ebtree lookup tests `y & -(z<<1)` with a precomputed `z=1<<bit` instead of `(y >> bit) >= 2`: eb32 +12.5%, eb64 +10.8% on 1k keys.

### haproxy-096 — Use native-width, unsigned types in hot arithmetic
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy · Also: zstd
Principle: Keep hot indices, offsets and intermediate values in the machine's natural word size and signedness, consistently along the call chain, so the compiler does not insert sign or zero extensions and truncations.
Why it is faster: Mixing `char`, `int` and 64-bit values forces extension and shift-pair instructions at every boundary. Unsigned arithmetic also lets the compiler use cheaper shifts and comparisons. Each removed conversion is small, but they sit in the innermost loops.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `usize` indexing in Rust and `size_t` in C instead of `int` loop counters. • Web / backend: avoid `byte`/`short` arithmetic in Java and C# hot loops (it is promoted to int). • Embedded: use the core's register width (32-bit on Cortex-M) for loop counters even when values fit in 8 bits.
Watch out: unsigned underflow wraps silently. Wider types also cost memory in large arrays, so this applies to locals and arguments, not stored fields.
Seen in HAProxy: 9afe3b59a7 2023-12-17 `flsnz_long()` with fewer conversions; b7068b3152 unsigned `flsnz`, about 2% faster; fc421e5b3d `size_t` offsets in ebtree. • ea1b70900f 2023-04-09 64-bit arguments to `encode24`/`send_huff` in the compressor remove a useless shift pair: +1%. • Also seen in zstd (zstd-091): 3c6b808870 2016-07-30 Switched the decoder's `prevOffset[]` to `U32` with `memcpy`. 761f8dbbd2 and 66f69e58d2 ("restore decompression speed on fizzle") reverted it to `size_t`. • 10bfd0c0d5 2016-10-27 Replaced `while (op<oMatchEnd) *op++=*match++` with an indexed `U32 i` loop. gcc level 1 on silesia went from 904.4 to 932.8 MB/s, and "if it is `U64 i` … the regression returns". • b3c9fc27b4 2019-11-05 `size_t` locals and loop bounds the compiler can reason about in `HUF_readDTableX1_wksp`: about 1-2% faster decompression of 10 KB files.

### haproxy-097 — Classify bytes with small lookup tables, and keep the tables small
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Turn per-character classification or conversion into one load from a 256-entry table, merge several class tables into one bit-per-class table, and shrink or compute away table columns so the whole table stays in L1.
Why it is faster: A table load replaces a chain of compares or a locale-aware function call. One bitmask table tests any class at the cost of a byte test while using 1/N of the cache of N tables. A table that misses L1 loses to arithmetic, so a column with few distinct values is better computed with a couple of compares. Modern compilers can also turn a simple class test into an efficient compare tree, which may be good enough without a table.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: base64, hex and URL-escape tables in encoders; identifier and whitespace classes in tokenisers. • Embedded: a single `const` class table in flash instead of `ctype` calls. • Data / databases: compact Huffman or dictionary-decode tables in storage codecs.
Watch out: tables cost cache space that other hot data needs. Measure against a plain compare loop, and keep tables `const` so they stay in shared read-only pages.
Seen in HAProxy: 2235b261b6 2016-11-05 7 HTTP class tables (~2 kB) merged into `http_char_classes[256]` with `HTTP_FLG_*` bits: binary 4 kB smaller, about +1% (222k req/s). • d6735d611e 2018-12-07 `ist_lc[256]`/`ist_uc[256]` case tables instead of `tolower()`. • 7a835f3cb0 2021-05-21 `hextable[]` replaces `hex2i()` in the contiguous chunk parser. • 9f4f6b038c 2022-09-20 HPACK Huffman decode tables went from `{code, len}` structs (4×512 B + 64 B) to code-only `uint8_t` tables of 4×256 B, with the length derived by compares and one table merged into another: +1.6% max H2 request rate, 1088 bytes less data. • ebab479cdf 2025-05-12 `http_authority_has_forbidden_char()` stays a plain loop with one `unlikely()` range test that compilers turn into a compare tree at ~3.6 cycles/char; hand-tuned register bitmaps were slightly faster but fragile across compilers.

### haproxy-098 — Carry string lengths, compare lengths first, normalise once
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Pass strings as pointer plus length, compare lengths before content, and convert to a canonical form (for example lowercase) once at ingestion so later comparisons are plain byte compares.
Why it is faster: A `{ptr, len}` pair is returned in two registers, avoids `strlen`, and allows NUL in data. An integer length compare rejects almost every non-matching header before any string work. A case-insensitive compare calls `tolower` per character, whereas a lowercased copy can use vectorised `memcmp`. Lengths known at compile time should never be recomputed at run time.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `std::string_view`, Rust `&str`, Go string headers instead of NUL-terminated strings; logging macros that capture `sizeof(literal)-1`. • Web / backend: lowercase HTTP header maps at parse time (as Go's `CanonicalHeaderKey` does); compare `length` before `equalsIgnoreCase` in Java. • Data / databases: store a case-folded column with its own index instead of `LOWER()` per row.
Watch out: normalising at ingestion changes what downstream code sees. Keep the original form if it must be reproduced exactly (logging, signatures).
Seen in HAProxy: e11f727c95 2017-05-30 `struct ist` (pointer + length) introduced, returned in registers on x86-64 and ARMv8. • a329463655 2017-08-23 `name_len == 10 && strcasecmp("connection", …)` plus explicit memcpy instead of `chunk_appendf`: calls divided by 3, the Lua applet 5% faster (41,400 req/s). • ed00e345e2 2018-12-07 HTX always stores lowercased header names; c2a10d4b4c the H2 encoder stops lowercasing them again; b045bb221a later compares are case-sensitive. • 75cc65356f 2021-10-15 DNS labels stored lowercase and `resolv_hostname_cmp()` replaced by `memcmp()`: 22% less CPU (compare plus `tolower` had been ~18%). • 361c227465 2025-09-18 `__trace()` takes an `ist` built at the call site, which removed a runtime `strlen` and greatly reduced `__trace_enabled()` in perf top; 06fa9f717f uses a fixed 3-char length for thread ids.

### haproxy-099 — Format hot output without printf, and parse numbers without per-digit multiplies
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Build short, known-shape output with direct appends and integer-to-ASCII routines (or a pre-sized template filled right to left) instead of format strings. Parse fixed-shape numbers by packing digits into words and multiplying once.
Why it is faster: `printf`-style functions parse the format string at run time and handle many cases you do not need. A template with digits written right to left needs no parsing and no intermediate copy. Numeric parsing that accumulates hundreds, tens and units of several fields in packed words replaces one multiply per digit with one multiply chain at the end. Compilers also fail on some divisions they cannot prove safe, so a hand-written helper can win.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: pre-built HTTP response headers with patched numbers; zap or zerolog style loggers that avoid `fmt`. • Embedded: itoa into a fixed buffer for UART telemetry instead of `printf`, which also saves flash. • Data / databases: fixed-width timestamp and IP parsing in log processors and import tools.
Watch out: hand-written formatters and parsers must handle every edge case (overflow, leading zeros, maximum width) that the library handled. Test them exhaustively against the library.
Seen in HAProxy: d54bbdce87 2009-09-07 "Very fast IP parsing functions" accumulate the digits of all four octets into packed 32-bit words, subtract `'0000'` once and multiply at the end; used for headers such as X-Forwarded-For. • f0d9eecc52 2010-06-20 A hand-written `div64_32`, because gcc cannot prove that the 64/32 division is safe. • 82032f1223 2017-07-27 `chunk_memcpy`/`chunk_memcat` added for binary output instead of format strings. • eb7b2ec83a 2024-03-01 A thread-local `"0000000000 events dropped"` template filled by `ultoa_r()`, re-checking the counter up to 10 times so updates merge: single-digit messages fell by 15-30%. • 4e57516c9a 2026-03-15 `snprintf` in the haterm response builder replaced by `chunk_strcat()` + `ultoa_o()`.

### haproxy-100 — Replace several running counters with one pointer and a precomputed limit
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When many counters track the same position in different units, keep only the moving pointer and compare it against the nearest point where something needs attention. At that checkpoint, settle all the accounting at once and compute the next limit.
Why it is faster: Updating bytes-left, totals, ring indices and batch counters per byte costs about 10 operations and 4 branches. A single pointer compare per byte leaves the loop with one increment and one branch. The checkpoint can also do batch work, such as a CRC, while the data is still in cache.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: output writers that only check for flush at a buffer-limit pointer; interpreters that check a step budget only at backward jumps. • Embedded: TX ring producers that compute the next wrap or flush point once instead of testing per byte. • Data / databases: serializers and page writers that settle checksums and size accounting at page boundaries.
Watch out: every event that must interrupt the loop has to be included in the limit computation. One forgotten event becomes an overrun.
Seen in HAProxy: 271f4f2b5b 2026-08-11 The bundled decompressor keeps only the output pointer and one limit: silesia decompression went from 0.876 s to 0.784 s (-10.6%) in slz form and 1.518 s to 1.388 s (-8.6%) in gzip form; the object shrank from 14078 to 12574 bytes and the match copy went from four phases to two.

### haproxy-101 — Shrink the binary: cold diagnostics, optional subsystems, lean headers
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Move repeated diagnostic text assembly into one cold, data-driven function, make unused subsystems compile out, and keep inline-heavy headers out of files that only need type definitions.
Why it is faster: Code that is compiled in but rarely run still takes i-cache, TLB entries, binary size and build time. Thousands of inlined assertion sites that each build their own message duplicate a lot of code; passing a pointer to a static descriptor to one out-of-line function removes it. Smaller preprocessed output also builds faster, which shortens the measurement loop.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: out-of-line `[[gnu::cold]]` panic and assert handlers; forward declarations or type-only headers; ccache. • Web / backend: `import type` in TypeScript; tree-shaken feature flags in bundles; Cargo features or Go build tags for optional protocols. • Embedded: Kconfig-style feature switches and assert codes resolved on the host to fit flash.
Watch out: every build switch multiplies the configurations to test. Keep a CI job for the minimal and maximal builds.
Seen in HAProxy: b5f271555e 2017-11-20 `pl_barrier`/`pl_cpu_relax` became macros: -O0 builds shrink (11431 to 10967 text bytes) and the helpers no longer pollute profiles. • f11ca5e7a4 2018-10-03 Compiler-flag detection uses `$(CC) -E` instead of `-c`, which was 50% slower for the same answer. • 1db546eecd 2021-10-06 Type files include only the new `ebtree-t.h` (9b7a617a0e): whole code size down ~3.6%, build time down to 6 s; fd21c6c6fd avoids pulling in xxhash for one type. • 9e3093c48a 2021-11-19 `$(info)` replaces `echo` sub-shells that were ~8% of build time; 1d14262a69 orders objects by build time for better parallel make. • d142c7f421 2026-04-14 `USE_TRACE` makes tracing optional: 6-10% smaller binary. 382a495649 `USE_H2` saves about 127 kB, 0bf32cee66 `USE_SPOE` about 92 kB; the 7dfe7d240e "tiny" target is about 80% smaller (13.7 MB to 2.77 MB, BSS 9.87 MB to 85 kB). • 28a09c38ff 2026-08-10 The assertion-message series moved prefix, suffix and records into one shared cold function fed by a static descriptor (1f374058be -70 kB, 30c03d50f6 -193 kB in total); preprocessed output about 7% smaller and builds about 7% faster. 431b6b1cd9 adds a forced tail jump to keep backtraces clean.

### haproxy-102 — Benchmark optimisation flags; higher is not always faster
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Measure compiler optimisation levels and cost models on your own code and compiler versions instead of assuming that -O3 or aggressive vectorisation helps.
Why it is faster: Branchy, scalar-dominated server code rarely benefits from auto-vectorisation, and the extra prologues, epilogues and versioned loops bloat the i-cache. Constraining the vectoriser's cost model keeps the useful optimisations without that bloat.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: benchmark -O2 against -O3 and per-function `optimize` attributes for branchy code; try PGO and LTO. • Web / backend: Rust `opt-level` and `codegen-units` tuning for server binaries. • Embedded: compare -Os and -O2 on the real target; i-cache and flash wait states often favour -Os.
Watch out: results change with compiler releases. Re-run the comparison when you upgrade the toolchain.
Seen in HAProxy: 871c80505c 2025-10-22 `-fvect-cost-model=very-cheap` (the -O2 default) added to the standard flags: at -O3 with gcc 15, 181k vs 172k req/s on one core (+5%) and smaller code; unrestricted vectorisation had even miscompiled code.

### haproxy-103 — Give each thread its own copy; combine only on the rare read
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O, memcached, Envoy
Principle: Keep counters, lists and queues that every thread updates as one instance per thread. Writers touch only their own copy; readers that need the total (stats, dumps, shutdown) sum or walk all copies.
Why it is faster: A shared counter or list head makes its cache line bounce between cores on every update, and an atomic add on it can cost hundreds of cycles under contention. A per-thread copy turns every write into an uncontended local write. The rare read pays O(threads). This works only when the aggregate is read much less often than it is written, and when a slightly stale total is acceptable.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: per-worker request counters summed by the `/metrics` handler; Java `LongAdder` instead of `AtomicLong`. • Native / systems: Linux per-CPU counters; per-core run queues in job schedulers and actor runtimes. • Data / databases: per-connection or per-core buffer-pool hit statistics aggregated on query.
Watch out: memory grows with thread count, and reads become O(threads). If the total is read on every request (for example a global limit check), this moves the cost instead of removing it.
Seen in HAProxy: 4815c8cbfe 2018-01-24 A per-thread `fd_cache_local[]` plus a mostly lockless global list replaced the global `fdcache_lock`. • f6e6dc12cd 2018-05-18 Per-thread run queue `rqueue_local[]`: tasks bound to one thread go there with no lock; the locked global queue is used only for multi-thread tasks. • b20aa9eef3 2018-10-15 Per-thread timer wait queues: on 12 threads, time in the wait-queue lock fell from 4.7 s to 11 ms and throughput rose 1.5%. • a698eb6739 2021-02-24 The global streams list became one list per thread: +7% at 4 threads; 49de68520e then removed the lock entirely (+1.6%). • 9c7b8085f4 2021-02-24 The `tasks_run_queue` counter, "the hottest thread contention point to date", became per thread: +12% req/s on 16 threads; c6ba9a0b9b `rqueue_ticks` per thread (+2%); 955a11ebfa `nb_tasks` too. • d4e78d873c 2021-03-04 `server->actconns` per thread; before, 47 threads adding and 1 deleting could trigger watchdog panics. e8e5091510 the buffer wait queue per thread. • 6f78038d72 2022-06-16 One shared run queue per thread with its own lock (b17dd6cc19); without mixed-thread queues, 319d136ff9 switched from scoped to plain trees (570k to 585k RPS). • e7475c8e79 2022-06-20 A per-thread `TH_FL_SLEEPING` flag replaced the shared `sleeping_thread_mask`, so waking is one fetch-or on the target's own line; dce4ad755f does the same for notifications. • 6be8d09a61 2023-01-12 Byte counters moved into per-thread context and summed by the stats code: `qc_snd_buf()` had used 2.5% CPU at 25 Gbps on 48 threads, all in the atomic add; it vanished from perf. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-085): 809057f56f 2021-06-14 QUIC stats are summed across each thread's `h2o_context_t` by the status handler, through a macro-generated field list (`H2O_QUIC_AGGREGATED_STATS_APPLY`) that keeps the struct, the accumulation and the formatting in sync. • 2cb85960e1 2024-03-04 `h2o_context_t::http3.next_cid` is one per-thread CID counter shared by all QUIC contexts in that thread. CID routing compares node_id and thread_id through it. • Also seen in memcached (memcached-083): 1153bfc498 2007-11-19 Multi-key get accumulates hits and misses in locals and takes one `STATS_LOCK` per request instead of three lock pairs per key. • 1fdfb7e91d 2009-03-04 Moves get/set/hit/miss counters into per-thread stats with `threadlocal_stats_aggregate`. e2da37827d 2009-03-06 does the same for bytes read and written, 25b5189c6a 2009-03-06 splits them per slab class, and 8c3f8d840f 2009-08-18 moves `flush_cmds`. • 943f2e2b24 2009-08-24 Removes the per-get memset of an 804-byte array and a 201-iteration merge loop in favour of direct `slab_stats[id].get_hits++`. • 67d7cfc135 2012-08-31 Drops `STATS_LOCK` eviction counters from `do_item_alloc`. Totals are summed from per-class `itemstats[]` when read ("a handful of instructions from the alloc hot path, which is always worth doing"). • 6ee8daef6a 2015-10-05 The slab mover counts rescues and evictions locally and publishes them once per page pass. c630eeb7f4 2019-07-26 removes `mem_requested` bookkeeping from `slabs_alloc`/`slabs_free`. The LRU maintainer sums existing per-LRU sizes instead, which "speeds up some hot paths". • Also seen in Envoy (envoy-116): 3285c1a6b0 2016-08-10 Counters keep a `pending_increment_` atomic; the flusher latches deltas with `exchange(0)` and skips unused stats. • b8174db216 2017-01-17 The thread-local store gives each worker a per-scope cache in front of the locked central map; scope deletion broadcasts a cache flush. • 9a1e49f6c8 2018-04-23 (reverted in a12d556938 2018-04-26, relanded as ec7d1af2b9 2018-05-03) Per-thread log-linear histograms with two buffers; the main thread asks each worker to swap, then merges the idle buffers into interval and cumulative histograms. • 4364cb5041 2025-10-24 The Peak-EWMA balancer records RTT samples into per-host atomic ring buffers with `fetch_add`, aggregated periodically by the main thread; with one of ten hosts 10x slower, P99 was 18.56 ms vs about 105 ms for round robin.

### haproxy-104 — Split a hot shared structure into hash-selected shards with their own locks
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: Kafka, PostgreSQL
Principle: When data must stay globally reachable, split one structure with one lock into N independent shards, each with its own lock and its own cache line, and pick the shard by hashing the key or object.
Why it is faster: With N shards, two threads collide only when they pick the same shard, so contention falls roughly N-fold and no single lock word bounces between all cores. Hashing the object pointer (not the thread id) keeps alloc and free accounting consistent when an object is freed on another thread. The shard count is a tunable: past a point, more shards add memory and aggregation cost for little gain.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: sharded rate-limit counters and session maps in an API gateway; segment locks as in Java's `ConcurrentHashMap`. • Data / databases: per-bucket latches in a buffer pool or lock manager; striped free lists in slab allocators. • Native / systems: one `SO_REUSEPORT` socket per worker group.
Watch out: operations that span shards (full scans, global limits, ordered iteration) become more expensive and may need all locks. A poor hash (see the multiplier tuning HAProxy did for `ptr_hash`) leaves some shards hot.
Seen in HAProxy: 06885aaea7 2023-08-12 Pool counters split into 8-64 `THREAD_ALIGN(64)` buckets chosen by `ptr_hash()`; 9e5eb586b1 (spreading `used`) measured 812k to 1.5M RPS on 80 threads. • 7bf829ace1 2023-07-24 The shared free list itself was sharded, and a busy head makes the operation skip to another bucket: 368k RPS to 3.5M (8 buckets), 4M (16), 4.7M (32), 5.5M (64). • a9ae094b27 2023-11-08 Jumping to a random bucket instead of the adjacent one breaks thread-pair contention; a57f2a5cfe retries once in another bucket when empty, so idle buckets drain ("show pools" from 10k to about 150 buffers). • e83f937cc1 2023-04-18 QUIC connection IDs in 256 global trees chosen by the first CID byte, each with its own rwlock, replacing thread-encoded IDs so connections can move to less-loaded threads. • 6dfbef4145 2021-10-12 The `shards` bind keyword creates N identical listeners with split thread masks, spreading the kernel's per-socket accept lock. • 1a088da7c2 2024-03-04 Stick tables sharded by `XXH32(key) % CONFIG_HAP_TBL_BUCKETS`: on 80 cores, 150k RPS / 5.4 Gbps became 980k RPS / 36 Gbps. Tuning: 16 shards gave 675k, 32 gave 980k, 64 gave 1008k, 128 gave 1040k. • Also seen in Kafka (kafka-101): adb70da13e 2017-03-04 `ConsumerNetworkClient.send()` no longer takes the global client lock; 022bf12951 2017-03-08 keeps unsent requests in `ConcurrentMap<Node, ConcurrentLinkedQueue>`. • 69e8463c06 2017-10-27 Transaction marker queues use `putIfAbsent` instead of a shared lock; bd216db4c0 2018-06-23 `Pool.getAndMaybePut` uses `computeIfAbsent` (per-bin locking) instead of double-checked locking on one `createLock`. • 459a4dd032 2019-01-03 Purgatory watchers: one global RW lock replaced by 512 `WatcherList` shards picked by `key.hashCode % 512`. • 47841e0bb9 2024-05-11 `FetchSessionCache` split into shards owning disjoint session-id ranges with equal slot shares; new sessions go round-robin, cutting contention on brokers with many fetchers. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-143): 34a84addc7 1999-05-01 the relation-extension lock is taken on page 0 instead of the whole relation. • 5d5087363d 2005-03-04 the global BufMgrLock is split into separate locks for the lookup table and the freelist, plus a spinlock in each buffer header: "substantial improvement in multi-backend situations". ee3b71f6bc 2005-05-19 moves the PGPROC array under its own lock; 349f40b2c2 2006-01-04 turns ShmemIndexLock from a spinlock into an LWLock. • ec0baf949e 2005-12-11 LockMgrLock split into 16 partitions by low hash bits ("only a marginal further improvement over 4"); 51ee9fa157 2006-07-22 generic partitioned dynahash; 10b9ca3d05 2006-07-23 buffer mapping table split into partitions locked in number order; a794fb0681 2006-07-23 lock manager moved onto them. • 3acc10c997 2014-10-02 buffer-mapping partitions raised to 128, "a significant win over the current value and over smaller values such as 64"; fe702a7b3f 2015-11-12 and 6150a1b08a 2015-12-15 give SLRU and buffer locks separate tranches. • 53c2a97a92 2024-02-28 SLRU caches (clog, subtrans, multixact) become configurable and are divided into banks, each with its own LWLock; cross-bank code releases one bank lock before taking the next. • 3e577ff602 2023-02-03 dropping a replication origin locks that origin instead of the whole catalog, so tablesync workers drop origins concurrently. • 404a17c155 2026-04-06 the reverse: lock statistics use one LWLock instead of one per lock type, because the path is cold and finer locks bought nothing.

### haproxy-105 — Skip locks and atomics when ownership proves nobody else can touch the data
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: Netty, Seastar
Principle: Use the information you already have (a single-thread mode, an ownership mask, the scope of an object, a "maybe shared" flag) to decide cheaply that an object is thread-confined, and then access it with plain loads and stores.
Why it is faster: Even an uncontended lock or atomic increment is a locked read-modify-write costing tens of cycles and a cache-line ownership request. If an object can only be touched by its owner, that work buys nothing. A single bit test (`a & (a-1)` is non-zero when two or more bits are set) or a flag set when entering the rare shared state is far cheaper.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `Rc` instead of `Arc` for values that never cross threads; non-atomic refcounts in thread-per-core runtimes (Seastar). • Web / backend: no mutex on request-scoped objects in an event-loop server; a library that switches to non-atomic paths when it detects a single thread. • Data / databases: latch-free access to session-local temp tables, or to pages pinned exclusively by one session.
Watch out: the ownership proof must be airtight: one forgotten path that hands the object to another thread turns a free optimisation into a data race. Document the invariant next to the shortcut.
Seen in HAProxy: 4b3091e54e 2012-09-24 No shared memory or locks in the SSL session cache when only one process runs: "locking is much more expensive than just a test". • f0c531ab55 2017-11-05 With one thread, `process_runnable_tasks` runs a plain loop with no run-queue lock and no batching: about +10% (550k to 606k req/s). • 98d334bd94 2018-10-15 `atleast2(a) { return a & (a - 1); }`; 87d54a9a6d takes the FD lock only if the FD's thread mask has two or more bits: 15% faster on 12 threads. • e388f2fbca 2021-03-02 Muxes set `TASK_F_USR1` only while a connection sits in an idle list where another thread may steal it, so frontend connections never take `idle_conns_lock`. • 61ecf28389 2021-09-08 Variable locks taken only for the process-wide scope, since session/transaction scopes are same-thread: about +5% on 16 threads. • a6920a25d9 2022-08-11 The QUIC RX-packet lock removed because a connection always stays on one thread; 74904a4792 per-thread CID trees "do not require any locking"; 6163e0357e opentracing variable locks only for shared scopes. • 1ea5f410ff 2023-02-02 QUIC packet refcounts made non-atomic: the refcount increment alone had cost 1% CPU at 135 Gbps, and bit rate rose about 1%. • aad6b771dd 2024-09-15 No lock in `vars_prune*` (scopes are single-thread or deinit-only): ~0.5% CPU saved. • Also seen in Netty (netty-108): a462d0e85e 2010-02-19 removed volatile from decoder state "protected by happens-before condition... Executor's internal synchronization". e8c320c6c3 2010-02-19 removed volatile from fields already under `synchronized`, and aa8e48df9f 2010-02-19 from fields guarded by a `BlockingQueue`. 0857f398e0 2010-02-09 made `inWriteNowLoop` non-volatile. • 3b368125b0 2011-12-08 replaced `AtomicReference`/`AtomicBoolean` in `ReplayingDecoder`. 31cebd7ce2 2012-07-03: "No need for AtomicBoolean as we run in the eventloop". • cedcee3f42 2013-02-09 made the leak detector's sampling counter per-instance and non-volatile, and f4e128b807 2013-07-23 made its `disabled` flag non-volatile. 12f1d96914 2013-03-08 read the refcount non-volatilely. That one was reverted in f23d68b42f 2014-02-07, because the refcount is a real cross-thread value. • 0b5e75a614 2016-09-13 made `IdleStateHandler` fields (touched only by the event loop) non-volatile and narrowed its state from int to byte. dcd93e3be0 2016-05-25 made cached Runnables non-volatile: in the worst case they are recreated. 4d8132ff24 2016-07-05 made `DefaultPromise.listeners` non-volatile. • b7b6156505 2019-11-22 dropped the atomic for handler state once pipeline operations became loop-confined. 69575cf5a6 2019-10-13 removed a CAS that ran inside `synchronized`. 361ad99ebd 2022-10-18 uses a plain `state =` store for a thread-local recycler handle. • Also seen in Seastar (seastar-108): 48f570f57c 2014-09-02 Adds its own `shared_ptr`; 87f63f7b90 renames it `lw_shared_ptr`, "efficient, but does not support polymorphic types": intrusive and non-atomic because objects never leave their core. 606368f6f6 adds a debug check for copies on the wrong CPU. • c756a4980c 2019-07-28 The reactor `_polls` counter made non-atomic once the stall detector stopped reading it from another thread. • 7a216214b9 2021-05-15 Cross-thread allocator statistics use `memory_order_relaxed`.

### haproxy-106 — Give per-thread data and contended locks their own cache lines
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: jemalloc, Netty
Principle: Group each thread's hot fields into one struct aligned and padded to a cache line, put contended locks and queue indices on separate lines, and align pooled objects so two threads' objects never share a line.
Why it is faster: Parallel arrays such as `x[MAX_THREADS]` place several threads' entries on one 64-byte line, so independent writes invalidate each other (false sharing). Two hot locks on one line fight even though they are logically independent, and producer and consumer indices on one line slow both sides. Padding costs some memory per instance. Placing a lock next to the data it guards has the opposite, useful effect: acquiring it also brings in that data.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `alignas(64)`, Rust `crossbeam::CachePadded`, aligned slab caches (`SLAB_HWCACHE_ALIGN`). • Web / backend: Java `@Contended` on per-worker counters; Go structs padded with `cpu.CacheLinePad`. • Embedded: SMP firmware ring buffers with head and tail on different lines. • Data / databases: per-shard mutexes padded in sharded hash maps.
Watch out: padding multiplies memory by objects times line size, and some CPUs prefetch adjacent line pairs (128 bytes). Pad only what profiling shows as contended.
Seen in HAProxy: a24d1d0be4 2017-11-26 `rq_lock` and `wq_lock` aligned to 64: about 4.5% more throughput with 2-4 threads; 8b94969054, b6a2f58993 and 1ca1b70cf9 did the same for fd, buffer and pool locks. • 5809052ae1 2017-11-26 The lock moved to the start of `struct fdtab` next to `thread_mask`: about 2% better on fd-heavy loads. • 8d8747abe0 2018-10-15 `struct task_per_thread` aligned to 64 replaced five separate per-thread arrays: cache-miss ratio 19.2% to 18.7%, 3% faster on 12 threads. • 1efafce61f 2019-01-27 Accept-queue entries rounded up to 64 bytes; unaligned entries had cost about 5%. • 4d82bf5c2e 2020-06-28 Three per-thread idle-connection arrays merged into one `THREAD_ALIGNED(64)` struct: +5% HTTP/1 req rate on 16 threads; 20dc3cd4a6 pool LRU heads moved into `thread_info` (+1%); cdb53465f4 aligned the Lua allocator state. • 9c6248560e 2023-08-07 `THREAD_ALIGN(64)` before the stick-table `updt_lock`: 2355k to 4480k RPS on 80 threads, for 64 more bytes per table. • dd8ea5d928 2024-02-28 Ring buffer size, tail and head padded to separate lines: 2.15M to 2.35M msg/s at 48 threads. • c471de7964 2025-08-11 Task and tasklet pools aligned to 64 using the new aligned-pool support (eb075d15f6); also connections (c2687f587e) and per-thread server arrays (1bb9754648, 1.72M to 1.75M rps and better reproducibility). The struct types were deliberately not aligned so the compiler would not expand them. • Also seen in jemalloc (jemalloc-097): 289053c95b 2009-06-22 `base_alloc()` uses `CACHELINE_CEILING(size)` "so that there is no chance of false cache line sharing". 3fa9a2fad8 2010-03-07 cacheline-aligns `tcache_t` allocations. • 3025b021b9 2023-10-23 Cacheline-aligns the arena's bins array and moves the mutex's `locked` spin hint next to `prof_data`, "since it's always modified even with no contention". • Also seen in Netty (netty-119): 32aab3b0b3 2014-06-20 gave `MpscLinkedQueue` the class hierarchy `Pad0`, `HeadRef`, `Pad1`, `TailRef` (16 longs per pad), replacing `AtomicReference`/`PaddedRef` indirection that had "passive false sharing". • cf9dc40990 2022-04-26 removed one 8-byte pad from `InternalThreadLocalMap` after a new field had pushed it from 128 to 136 bytes, which restored a whole number of cache lines. • 36dbdacbed 2024-11-01 uses JCTools' unpadded MPSC queues for the many lightly contended `MemoryRegionCache`s, choosing footprint over padding.

### haproxy-107 — Separate write-hot fields from read-mostly fields
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Keep configuration and other read-mostly data on cache lines that no thread writes, and move frequently written fields (counters, queue lengths, atomics) to their own aligned lines or to a dedicated "read mostly" section.
Why it is faster: Every write to a line invalidates it in all other cores, so readers of a value that never changes still miss if a hot counter sits next to it. When read-mostly lines are never dirtied, they stay in Shared state in every core and reads are L1 hits. Profiles often show this as a surprisingly expensive load of a constant field.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Linux `__read_mostly`; config singletons in their own padded allocation. • Web / backend: split a connection or route object into an immutable config part and a stats part; LMAX Disruptor-style padding. • Embedded: `static const` tables in flash rather than RAM next to mutable state. • Data / databases: keep pin counts and dirty bits away from the immutable page id in buffer descriptors.
Watch out: layouts silently regress when fields are added. Re-check hot structs with `pahole` and `perf c2c` after changes.
Seen in HAProxy: 90e9b8c8b6 2021-03-03 `struct global` reordered after perf showed "a huge access cost on global.mode" caused by neighbouring atomic counters; eef7f7fe68 split `struct server` into config, idle/queue, LB and counter areas. • 401135cee6 2021-02-26 `task_per_thread` laid out over 4 lines, two for local operations and one for atomics: 8 µs lower response time at 1M req/s. • f459640ef6 2021-04-10 A `__read_mostly` attribute with a 64-byte aligned anchor; a1090a5b61 moved fdtab there after perf showed it hot because `ha_used_fds` shared its line; 26d212c744, ff88270ef9 and 56c3b8b4e8 moved more; 9057a0026e made pattern tables `const` (rodata); 9f699958dc marked boot-time pool globals read-mostly. • 4dd33d9c32 2022-12-20 Pool `free_list/used/allocated` moved after a `THREAD_ALIGN(64)`, away from `limit/size/flags`: a QUIC test went from 42 Gbps to 96 Gbps from this reordering alone. a4fb79b4a2 moved the remotely updated `rqueue_ticks` out of the local line; 8e079cdd44 thread flags to the shared area (+3% dequeuing). • fd012b6c59 2025-11-03 Queue and connection counters moved behind `THREAD_ALIGN(64)` at the end of `struct proxy` (perf showed 12.87% on a load of a config field): +1% HTTP/1 on ARM and x86. • a9df6947b4 2026-01-28 `queueslength`/`totpend` moved off the line holding `served/feconn/beconn`: +3.6% RPS on 64 cores; 3ca2a83fc0 the server equivalent (+2%); 868dd3e88b `trace_source` always aligned so linkage cannot place it beside a hot variable.

### haproxy-108 — Read before writing shared memory; write only on a real change
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: PostgreSQL, Seastar
Principle: Before a store, CAS or fetch-op on a shared line, load the current value and skip the write if it would not change anything, or if another thread already holds the flag you wanted to set.
Why it is faster: A store or atomic RMW takes the line exclusive and invalidates every other copy, even if the value is unchanged. A load keeps the line Shared, so many threads can check it in parallel. This is the test-and-test-and-set idea applied generally. On CPUs where fetch-or is emulated with a CAS loop, spinning on a load also avoids write traffic. The extra load costs almost nothing when the line is already cached.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: test-and-test-and-set spinlocks; `if (x != v) x = v;` on shared flags in C++, Go and Rust. • Web / backend: update "last seen" timestamps only when the second changed; skip `setState` when the value is equal. • Data / databases: avoid no-op `UPDATE ... SET col = col`, which still writes WAL; test a dirty bit before setting it.
Watch out: the load-then-write pair is not atomic. Use it only where a racing writer changes nothing important, or re-check under the lock as `fwlc_srv_reposition()` does.
Seen in HAProxy: 5064ab6a98 2021-02-17 `fwlc_srv_reposition()` computes the new leastconn key and returns if unchanged, re-checking under the lock: 40-50% of tree updates avoided, 554k to 714k rps with 4 servers (+29%). • 4d01f3dcdc 2021-04-23 The global date CAS is skipped when another thread already published the same `now_ms` (99.9% of the time at 1M wakeups/s): global writes divided by about 6. 481795de13 writes `now_offset` only in the 0.2% of calls where it changes. • 3238f79d12 2022-11-14 The stick-table expiry requeue returns early when the expiry would not move, so the CAS and task queueing run at most about 1000 times per second per table. • 1f8b14b7be 2024-02-29 Ring readers stop atomically writing the offset back to itself when nothing was read. • 573bbbe127 2024-03-17 The ring queue leader spins on a load of the tail lock bit before `FETCH_OR`: 3.33M to 3.75M msg/s on 24C/48T; 39df8c903d extends this to every architecture except ARMv8.1 LSE. • 4431e3bd26 2025-09-18 `sink_announce_dropped()` loads the counter and attempts a CAS only when the owner bit is clear, instead of a `FETCH_OR` the compiler built as a CAS loop: 110k to 244k req/s with traces on, 128 threads. • e264523112 2026-04-01 `last_sess` changes once per second, so it is stored only if the loaded value differs; eaf42ee886 the backend equivalent. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-151): 3e4818e9dd 2020-01-27 Parallel Hash Join stops setting unused match flags in tuples; the coherence traffic was slowing many-core joins. • 242dfcbafa 2020-05-15 `_bt_killitems` skips items already marked dead, avoiding redundant hint writes and full-page images when checksums are on. • 46846433a0 2021-10-14 `shm_mq` publishes `mq_bytes_written` only after a quarter of the ring is written, cutting SetLatch calls and cache misses. • Also seen in Seastar (seastar-109): c1abe0e573 2015-02-17 `boost::lockfree::spsc_queue::push()` "writes the producer index even when no data is pushed"; the fix guards it with `if (!_completed_fifo.empty())`. • 419e173555 2016-05-01 `process_completions` calls `move_pending()` only if the local pending FIFO is non-empty, because otherwise it "will cause cross-core cache lines to be accessed".

### haproxy-109 — Replace locks with atomic operations on independent fields
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: PostgreSQL
Principle: When a field only needs to be correct on its own (a counter, a refcount, a maximum, a state word whose new value depends only on its old value), update it with an atomic add, OR or CAS loop outside any lock, and keep the lock for real multi-field invariants.
Why it is faster: A lock serialises all the code inside it, so unrelated operations wait in line. An atomic op on one field lets everything else run concurrently and shortens the remaining critical sections. Under heavy contention the atomic line still bounces, which is why HAProxy combined this with sharding.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `AtomicLong`/`AtomicInteger` state machines instead of `synchronized` in Java NIO code; an `Arc`-style refcount in Rust. • Data / databases: CAS-based high-water marks and hit counters outside the page-table latch. • Embedded: atomic ISR counters instead of disabling interrupts.
Watch out: two atomically updated fields are not consistent with each other. If a reader needs both at once, pack them into one word or keep the lock.
Seen in HAProxy: 7ac0e35f23 2018-01-17 FD state changes computed in a CAS loop, with `updt_fd_polling()` called only if the polled bits changed; `fd_may_recv` became one atomic OR: +1% connection rate. 62a627ac19 and 82b37d74d2 removed `poll_lock` entirely; 173d9951e2 maxfd computed with atomics (+1%). • 0d6c75d749 2019-05-25 Frequency counters do one atomic add while the period is current and take the CAS "date lock" only to rotate. • 56c1cfb179 2020-10-21 Queue counters made atomic and moved out of the lock; b927a9d866 dropped the proxy lock around timing stats. • 996f1a5124 2022-10-11 Stick-table entry count updated atomically so `stksess_new()` needs no lock: 2.07M to 2.10M req/s. • 7968fe3889 2023-05-27 Stick-session `ref_cnt` made atomic and moved out of the locks: 415k to 520k rps; a29b073f26 cache entries got an atomic refcount so only the last release takes the write lock. • cec8b42cb3 2023-09-20 The log sampling index updated with a CAS and the log-server spinlock removed: 1.13M to 4.25M logs/s, CPU from 37 to 26 cores. • e3f5ae895a 2022-10-12 A table's next expiry converges to the earliest date through a lock-free CAS loop: 232k to 470k rps on 48 threads. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-145): b64d92f1a5 2014-09-25 portable atomics API; e8fdbd58fe 2017-04-07 64-bit atomics everywhere, with plain loads/stores where single-copy atomicity is guaranteed. • d72731a704 2014-12-25 the clock-sweep hand `nextVictimBuffer` becomes an atomic counter; the spinlock is taken only by the process that wraps around. • 3cda10f41b 2017-08-16 parallel seq scan hands out blocks with `pg_atomic_fetch_add_u64`; 34db06ef9a 2018-03-02 shm_mq read/write byte counts (one writer each) become 8-byte atomics, removing "pretty serious" contention for Gather. • 71e4cc6b8e 2023-07-25 WAL insertion lock's `insertingAt` becomes an atomic updated with exchange, without the wait-list lock: on 64 vCPUs, TPS rose from 132,600 to 360,915 at 256 clients and from 113,440 to 212,566 at 4096. c3a8e2a7cb 2023-12-19 makes `xlblocks` atomic. • ee1cbe806d 2024-04-05 WAL write/flush positions become atomics so readers take no lock; 963d3072af 2024-02-29 unloggedLSN loses its spinlock; bd5132db55 2024-02-29 adds full-barrier atomic read/write helpers; 86db52a506 2024-07-15 lock-free `InjectionPointRun()`. • 74c77052bc 2026-09-04 FastPathStrongRelationLocks, 7344937cbe 2026-09-04 parallel scan start/num blocks and ee97eef695 2026-09-22 shared-sort worker counters become atomics (the increment's full barrier orders each worker's tape write); similar in b1ec662ec1 2026-09-22, cb4a03db9e 2026-09-22 and 67b593861d 2026-09-04. • 943e881733 2026-03-11 `BufferGetLSNAtomic()` skips the header lock when the platform has 8-byte single-copy atomicity; the page LSN becomes one uint64.

### haproxy-110 — Pack related state into one word so one atomic op updates it
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: libuv
Principle: Store flags, small indices, a lock bit or a counter that must change together in the halves or bits of one 32- or 64-bit word, so one atomic load gives a consistent snapshot and one CAS or fetch-op changes everything.
Why it is faster: Fields spread over several words need several atomics, or a lock, to stay consistent. One word means one coherence transaction. A spare bit can even serve as a lock, so the position and the "locked" state are read and written together, and the lock can be dropped as soon as the new position is known.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: packed head/tail in SPSC and MPMC rings; a lock bit in a tagged pointer. • Web / backend: one `AtomicInteger` bitfield instead of several volatiles for connection state. • Data / databases: pin count and dirty bit in one buffer-header word; packed (epoch, counter) timestamps.
Watch out: packed fields limit range (16-bit indices, fewer flag bits). Assert the bounds, and watch for ABA when a packed value can cycle.
Seen in HAProxy: f509065191 2021-04-06 `fdtab[].ev` and `fdtab[].state` merged into one word, with follow-ups such as b41a6e9101 moving more flags in; a199a17d72 computes the ready bits and applies them in one update, with no atomics at all for unshared FDs. • 1673c4a883 2021-04-07 A per-FD bit taken with `BTS` replaced the process-wide `log_lock` around `writev()`. • e6f5ab5afa 2023-04-20 Accept-ring head and tail packed as `head<<16 | tail` in one `uint32_t`, with CAS push and pop; e00470378b log range and index merged into one word to enable a CAS. • eb3d5f464d 2024-02-28 The top bit of the ring tail offset reserved as a lock; 41d3ea521b unlocks as soon as the new tail is known and copies outside the critical section: 5.4M to 6.4M msg/s (3C/6T), 1.54M to 1.88M (24C/48T). 9e99cfbeb6 then removed the remaining ring lock (6.13M to 6.61M). • Also seen in libuv (libuv-062): ea493f1989 2026-05-29 In `src/uv-common.c`, `handle->pending` bit 0 is the pending flag and bits 1+ are the busy counter. `uv_async_send` does `CAS(current, current + 3)`, which sets the bit and adds 2 in one step, then `fetch_add(-2)` when done. Windows and Unix now share the code.

### haproxy-111 — Make each CAS count: small window, good guess, use the returned value
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Do all computation that does not depend on the outcome outside the CAS loop, seed the expected value with the most likely current value, and use the value returned by fetch-add or a failed CAS instead of reloading memory.
Why it is faster: Work inside a retry loop widens the race window and is wasted on every failure. Starting from a usually wrong guess costs one guaranteed failed round trip. A load followed by a CAS costs two coherence transactions (Shared, then upgrade to Exclusive), whereas a CAS with a guess costs one ownership request. Re-reading a variable right after an atomic increment is a second access to a contended line.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: prepare the node fully before the CAS in a lock-free push; spinlock acquire as a plain `cmpxchg(0→1)`. • Web / backend: use `incrementAndGet()`'s result rather than `inc()` then `get()`; Redis `INCR` instead of INCR plus GET. • Data / databases: optimistic updates with `WHERE version = <cached>`, computing the new row before the conditional write.
Watch out: a guess-first CAS writes (or requests ownership) even when it fails, which hurts when most callers only need to read. Choose per call site, as HAProxy does with test-before-CAS elsewhere.
Seen in HAProxy: cc2672f48b 2021-03-03 `curr_used_conns` was re-read after its atomic increment, which showed in perf; 185157201c and 1db427399c added explicit `_FETCH` variants, and a477150fd7 made non-fetch ops return void to force the distinction. • b2475a139e 2021-05-09 The PRNG output computation moved out of the DWCAS loop; 7e4a557f64 global timeval and tick updated in one CAS loop instead of two. • 4d882bd800 2023-04-13 `fd_claim_tgid()` starts with `old = 0` (FD unused, the common case) instead of the desired value. • 87d269707b 2024-07-08 `used = _HA_ATOMIC_FETCH_ADD(&bucket.used,1)` feeds the average directly: 0.5-1% saved where pool accounting had cost ~20% CPU with QUIC on EPYC. • 79ef362d9e 2025-09-18 `ring_write()` presets `tail_ofs=0` and lets the failed CAS return the real value: +2.3% (11.34M msg/s) on 64-core AMD.

### haproxy-112 — Use native atomic instructions instead of CAS retry loops
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: Netty, PostgreSQL
Principle: Use the CPU's direct atomic operations (bit-test-and-set, fetch-add, exchange, ARMv8.1 LSE and CASP) instead of hand-written or compiler-generated compare-and-swap loops.
Why it is faster: A CAS loop can fail repeatedly under contention and is unfair; a native RMW always completes in one instruction and needs much less code. On ARM, LSE atomics scale far better at high thread counts than load-linked/store-conditional loops.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: compile ARM servers with `-march=armv8.1-a` or outline atomics; `fetch_or`/`fetch_add` instead of CAS loops. • Web / backend: `AtomicLong.getAndAdd` rather than a `compareAndSet` loop for counters. • Data / databases: `lock xadd`-style counters for latch statistics.
Watch out: a native fetch-or always writes the line. When most callers would find the bit already set, a load-then-CAS can be cheaper (see the read-before-write entry).
Seen in HAProxy: f8ecc7f667 2019-09-04 CAS loops replaced with BTS/BTR in the FD state functions: more than 1 kB less code. • 92c059c2ac 2021-04-06 Native x86 `BTS/BTR` via asm with flag outputs: fast path 1.6 kB smaller. • 46cca86900 2020-11-30 ARMv8.1 `CASP` for double-word CAS "performs way better on high thread counts"; 6756d95a8e uses gcc outline atomics on aarch64; f8fb4f75f1 register pairs for arm64 DWCAS (~4.5 kB smaller and faster on Graviton2). • Also seen in Netty (netty-112): 83a19d5650 2017-09-25 moved retain and release to `getAndAdd`, about 2x faster under contention (reference counting was about 1% of CPU in gRPC). • fc0a4e15ab 2018-11-28 hardened the scheme: even values mean live and the final release goes to odd. Retain stays XADD. Release is a CAS whose first attempt uses a non-volatile read, with `Thread.yield()` after a failed CAS. ### haproxy-113 — Use the weakest memory ordering that is still correct
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: Aeron, Netty, Tokio
Principle: Re-derive the ordering each atomic access really needs. Prefer relaxed or acquire/release operations over sequentially consistent ones, let one atomic op provide the barrier for several stores, and remove standalone fences.
Why it is faster: A full fence drains the store buffer and stalls the pipeline. Sequentially consistent stores emit full barriers on weakly ordered CPUs such as ARM. On x86 (TSO), load and store barriers need only be compiler barriers. Locked instructions already order memory, so a barrier after them is wasted.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `memory_order_acquire/release` or Rust `Ordering::Relaxed` for statistics and progress flags. • Web / backend: Java `lazySet`/`setRelease` for single-writer publication; `Volatile.Read` rather than full barriers in .NET. • Embedded: coalesce `wmb()` calls in drivers; avoid volatile-plus-fence idioms.
Watch out: ordering bugs appear only on weakly ordered hardware and under load. Test on ARM, and document why each relaxed access is safe.
Seen in HAProxy: 1e56c70cc9 2019-03-07 Reordered stores let one atomic store replace a store plus `__ha_barrier_store()`: about 0.5% on 8 threads. 17fbb4eb3f removed useless barriers after `XCHG` in list macros. • 99198546f6 2021-07-15 On x86 TSO, load/store barriers became compiler barriers (lfence/sfence dropped, mfence kept): +2-2.5% on an 8c/16t Xeon. cb0451146f stopped forcing atomic load/store/xchg to `__ATOMIC_SEQ_CST`. • 0a0a64ef02 2024-03-22 Ring waiter-release stores made relaxed: +3-5% on 80-core ARM (7.25M/s), no change on x86. • a79a67b52f 2026-01-28 A leftover full barrier plus volatile reads accounted for 27% of a function's samples on the `mfence`; atomic loads instead gave +0.5-3%. • Also seen in Aeron (aeron-088): 65d5ce934a 2014-12-15 `AtomicInteger` replaced with a `volatile int` in `Publication`. • 9f7c430b43 2016-01-10 "Ordered write is not necessary because it is only read by the same thread". `f4b6c3f2df` (2016-10-07) `getVolatile()` to `get()` on the writer thread; `b4a2e53f1c`, `1f1a5061f3` (2015-07-25) removed volatile from Sender-owned fields; `c7c70046bf` (2015-07-06) C++ rate counters use `memory_order_relaxed`; `d84fb5098d` (2015-06-02) "a little bit less aggressive fence". • e117c167fb 2018-01-22 C++ atomics: volatile gets use `acquire()` and ordered puts use `release()` instead of `thread_fence()`. • f4bda7024b 2019-01-03 The SPSC queue producer reads its own tail plainly (MPSC consumer in `6083fe29e5`). `a1d4dc2d54` (2019-06-20) removed a `volatile boolean` checked on every flow-control idle "which ... carried too high a cost". • e9418828a0 2020-09-12 Removed blanket `volatile` from C broadcast buffers, queues and ring buffers (15 files), keeping explicit get-volatile/put-ordered macros; ### haproxy-114 — Shrink critical sections: move slow work and side effects out of the lock
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: jemalloc, Kafka, memcached, curl, Netty, PostgreSQL, Tokio
Principle: Hold a lock only around the shared state it protects. Do allocation, freeing, system calls, page faults, wakeups and task queueing before taking the lock or after releasing it, and lock inside the callee around just the sensitive lines.
Why it is faster: Lock hold time multiplies contention: everyone waiting pays for the slowest thing done inside. `malloc`, `munmap` or a first-touch page fault can take microseconds and may sleep. Wakeups and timer requeues take other locks, which also risks lock-order deadlocks. Deciding under the lock and acting after it shortens the hold time without changing semantics.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: build objects before entering `synchronized`; emit logs and metrics after releasing a mutex. • Data / databases: release latches before I/O; collect evicted cache entries and destroy them outside the lock (C++ destructors can be expensive). • Native / systems: prefault mmapped arenas with `MAP_POPULATE` before locking; notify a condition variable after unlocking.
Watch out: work moved out must not depend on state that can change once the lock is dropped. Re-validate after re-acquiring, or carry the decision rather than the state.
Seen in HAProxy: 3e853ea74d 2019-07-04 The pool lock is released during malloc/free; 828675421e touches the new page outside the lock (8x faster debug builds); 85b2cae63c marks the thread harmless during those syscalls. • c7eedf7a5a 2020-10-21 The locked area in `pendconn_add()` shrank to the exact server or proxy section. • 4e9df2737d 2021-02-17 `health_adjust()` took the server lock even for ### haproxy-115 — Prepare outside the lock, publish or detach with one pointer swap
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Build a new object without holding any lock and install it with an atomic exchange. To drain a shared list, swap its head with NULL and process the detached chain privately.
Why it is faster: The critical section shrinks from "copy a big buffer" or "free every element" to one atomic instruction. Readers keep using the old version until they pick up the new pointer, and the detached list belongs to one thread, so it needs no locking at all.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: hot-reload a routing table or config via Go `atomic.Value` or a Rust `ArcSwap`. • Data / databases: swap a materialised-view or statistics snapshot pointer in an in-memory engine. • Native / systems: swap a work queue in O(1) and process it outside the lock.
Watch out: the old object can only be freed when no reader still holds it, which needs refcounts, RCU or a quiescent point.
Seen in HAProxy: c55015ee5b 2018-09-07 Error snapshots are allocated and filled unlocked, then installed with `HA_ATOMIC_XCHG(&proxy->invalid_req, es)`: the lock "is only taken during the pointer swap". It also saves 480 bytes per proxy. • c88914379d 2021-06-10 `pool_flush()` atomically swaps the list head to NULL, then frees without a lock.

### haproxy-116 — Give independent sub-structures their own locks
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: Tokio
Principle: Do not reuse a big object's lock for an unrelated part of it. Give each separately mutated structure (a queue, an update tree, a free list) its own lock, and define a lock order or defer operations so the locks never nest the wrong way.
Why it is faster: One coarse lock makes lookups wait behind updates to a different structure. Separate locks let independent operations proceed in parallel, and each lock is held for less time. The cost is lock-ordering discipline.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: separate locks for a connection pool's free list and its statistics. • Data / databases: separate locks for an LRU list and the hash index; per-table instead of catalog-wide locks. • Native / systems: per-index locks in an in-memory store.
Watch out: more locks mean more ways to deadlock. Document the order, and prefer deferring work over nesting locks.
Seen in HAProxy: 16fbdda3c3 2021-06-18 A dedicated spinlock inside `struct queue` instead of the server and proxy locks: leastconn 362k to 374k req/s, roundrobin +9%. • 85b2fb0358 2021-02-17 leastconn/first `take_conn/drop_conn` no longer take the server lock because the LB lock already protects the tree; 59b0fecfd9 dropping that lock in the stream code took "first" from 432k to 565k rps. • 87e072eea5 2023-05-27 A separate `updt_lock` for the stick-table updates tree: 614k to 1140k/s with peers on 80 threads. • ac9c49b40d 2023-11-16 A cache-tree rwlock separate from the shared-context lock; ed35b9411a narrows the latter to the `avail` list; 48f81ec09d defers entry deletion so the two locks never nest in opposite order. • 9075a7e32f 2024-03-22 The resync task locks one peer at a time instead of all peers. • Also seen in Tokio (tokio-075): 6b3c90cc58 2026-09-07 The multi-thread inject queue had shared the scheduler's `synced` mutex with idle state, a leftover from the removed alternative scheduler. It now has its own mutex inside `Inject`, and the generic `Lock` trait plumbing was deleted. The commit records the cross-structure orderings that remain necessary (a `SeqCst` RMW in `notify_should_wakeup`, and `inject_timers` staying under the idle lock).

### haproxy-117 — Use read locks for lookups and upgrade only when a change is needed
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: Kafka, NATS
Principle: When most accesses only read, use a reader-writer lock: look up under the shared lock and bump refcounts atomically. On a miss or a needed change, upgrade through an intermediate "seek" state or retake a write lock and re-check, because another thread may have changed things in between.
Why it is faster: A spinlock serialises readers that could run together. Read locks let lookups run in parallel, and a three-state (read, seek, write) lock lets one scanner work while readers continue, upgrading only briefly per modification. Losing would-be upgraders back off instead of queueing. Writers need priority, otherwise constant upgrading readers can starve them.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: route and config tables read per request but rarely changed; a `ReentrantReadWriteLock` or `StampedLock` cache with double-checked insert. • Native / systems: parking_lot `upgradable_read` in Rust caches; writer-preferring rwsems. • Data / databases: catalog caches; expiring entries from an LRU while lookups continue.
Watch out: an rwlock read acquire is still an atomic write to the lock word, so a very hot read-only path may need sharding or RCU instead. Never upgrade two readers at once without a seek state, or they deadlock.
Seen in HAProxy: cd10def825 2020-10-17 The load-balancing lock became an rwlock, with read locks in leastconn (58bc9c1ced), first, chash and map selection; ac66d6bafb the proxy lock too. • d48ed6643b 2020-10-16 `wake_expired_tasks()` scans under a seek lock and upgrades per deletion: its CPU went from 2.15% to 1.04% and the check rate rose 3% (2000 servers on 8 threads). • 08b6f96452 2022-02-01 The listener spinlock (10-15% CPU in `listener_accept()` at 300k conn/s on 24 threads) became an rwlock read-locked during accept (fed93d367c); the function left perf top. • 47f229702e 2022-10-11 `stktable_get_entry()`: read lock for the lookup; on a miss, unlock, allocate, take the write lock and retry the insert, switching to an existing entry if one appeared: 455k to 2.07M req/s (4.55x), where the lock had used 25% of CPU. e62885237c uses a lock upgrade where no allocation happens: 363k to 510k (+40%). a7d6a1396e lookups under a read lock with atomic refcounts. • cfeca3a3a3 2023-08-07 `stktable_touch_with_exp()` read-locks, upgrades via seek only when an update must be queued, and falls back to a write lock: 1140k to 2050k RPS. 29982ea769 lets several peers walk updates in parallel; ### haproxy-118 — Decide without the lock whether you need the lock at all
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: Pebble, Varnish, PostgreSQL
Principle: Before taking a lock, check with a plain or atomic read whether there is work to do: an empty list, an unchanged expiry, a disabled feature, an exhausted budget, or a flag saying nobody has observed the data. Lock only on the path that needs it, and re-check under the lock.
Why it is faster: Taking a lock just to find nothing to do costs a cache-line transfer on every call and queues other threads. A racy pre-check is a shared read that many threads can do at once. A per-object "seen" flag set by consumers lets producers skip both lock and work when no consumer cares. The pre-check is only safe when a stale answer is harmless.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: check `if (feature_enabled)` or queue length before `mutex.lock()`; "dirty since last sync" bits for change notification. • Data / databases: update an LRU access time only past a granularity threshold; skip row locks for no-op updates. • Native / systems: work-stealing pools that read the victim's queue length before locking it.
Watch out: the racy read must never dereference memory another thread may be freeing (the reverted HAProxy commit is the cautionary example). Always re-check the condition after acquiring the lock.
Seen in HAProxy: 186e96ece0 2019-05-28 `offer_buffers()` checks the wait list for emptiness before locking. • 1e928c074b 2019-05-28 Three-phase wait-queue check (lockless emptiness test, then read lock to peek, then write lock only if something expired): write locks fell from 1,125,496 to 173, contention down about 4.3x. 151c253a1e skips servers with no idle connections before locking them. • b7ba1d9011 2020-10-21 The caveat: an unlocked test in `pendconn_cond_unlink()` could see a transient NULL during unlink and caused a use-after-free, so 670119955b reverted it two days later. • a7536ef9e1 2022-10-11 `stktable_touch_with_exp()` skips the lock when the expiry is unchanged and no peer sync is needed: 193k to 425k req/s (2.2x) on 48 threads; 8d3c3336f9 checks expiry outside the lock before killing (2.6x). • f5aef027ce 2022-06-14 Once the budget is spent, `wake_expired_tasks()` no longer read-locks the wait queue only to do nothing. • 4c1480f13b 2024-04-02 A `seen` flag set by peer lookups lets `stktable_touch_with_exp()` skip the lock when no peer has looked: connecting a peer used to drop 40 cores from 2.10M to 1.14M RPS, now only to 2.05M (with 864ac31174). • c71ef2969b 2026-02-19 The ALPN-cache read lock is taken on backend connect only if ALPN is configured. • Also seen in Pebble (pebble-119): 42d6858b19 2020-05-18 `Cache.Delete`, which usually misses, checks under `RLock` first: ycsb/D +4.7%, ycsb/A +1.2%. • ca79bd78dd 2025-02-11 The CLOCK-Pro referenced bit is set with `if !e.referenced.Load() { e.referenced.Store(true) }`: CacheGet 20.1 ns to 18.9 ns (-5.8%). • Also seen in Varnish (varnish-107): 3c1612831b 2007-09-23 worker thread: `TAILQ_FIRST(&overflow)` unlocked, locking and re-checking only if it is non-empty. • 96e2a95ee1 2012-02-27 "Avoid taking the saintmode lock if the list empty". • 17323520af 2016-02-23 `HSH_RefBoc`: `if (oc->boc == NULL) return (NULL);` before locking. "This saves a mutex lock/unlock in the common case." • bc13919d58 2018-09-26 The ban lurker skips locking `ban_mtx` to fold counters when both are zero. • bc421898d3 2019-05-06 `if (oc->ban == NULL) return;` before locking in `BAN_DestroyObj`. • 05a686983d 2023-10-18 `h2_poll()` reads `r2->error` without a critical section to detect a reset client, accepting a one-step delay. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-148): b4fbe392f8 2011-07-29 a per-backend `hasMessages` flag lets `SIGetDataEntries` return without taking `SInvalReadLock` or `msgNumLock`; taking them on high-core boxes "can waste a lot of CPU time". • e8d029a30b 2012-06-07 buffer drop/flush loops compare the relation before `LockBufHdr`: "speeds these loops by a factor of 2X to 3X". • be9788e998 2020-09-02 `SyncRepWaitForLSN()` skips `SyncRepLock` at every commit when no synchronous standbys are defined. • 0c8dd518a5 2026-08-18 `deleteLSNWaiter()` checks `inHeap` before acquiring `WaitLSNLock`, which stops a thundering herd from serializing on the lock.

### haproxy-119 — Batch shared-counter updates: count locally, publish once
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: NATS
Principle: Instead of one atomic increment of a shared counter per item inside a loop (often under a lock), accumulate the change in a local variable and publish it once after the loop, outside the lock.
Why it is faster: Each atomic RMW on a shared line is a cache-line transfer, so N items cost N transfers. One update per batch costs one, and taking it out of the lock also shortens the critical section. The counter lags during the batch, which is fine for statistics and budgets.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `metrics.inc(n)` once after processing a Kafka or queue batch. • Data / databases: update a table's row-count statistic once per bulk insert; group-commit counter updates. • Native / systems: batch refcount changes when transferring many references at once.
Watch out: limits enforced from the counter (max connections, quotas) see stale values during the batch, so they can be overshot by up to one batch.
Seen in HAProxy: c309dbdd99 2020-11-30 `process_runnable_tasks` moves its atomic increments (some under the run-queue lock) to after the loop and precomputes the list size as a budget. • 9cef43acab 2021-06-24 `px->served` and the LB `take_conn()` updated once per dequeue loop, outside the lock: leastconn 355k to 362k req/s. 45499c56d3 decrements `grq_total` once after the loop, outside `grq_lock`; c9afbb10f5 stops decrementing then re-incrementing a counter per picked task (~0.5% at 1M req/s). • 29ad61fb00 2023-08-12 Pool `allocated` updates moved into callers that batch them outside loops, in preparation for sharding. • Also seen in NATS (nats-107): d0a9a47ea3 2016-04-03 Inbound message and byte counts are summed during `parse()`, then added atomically to client and server once per read. d603c53f67 2018-06-04 skips the atomics when nothing was counted. • 8d9c57ad44 2022-09-27 Per-delivery `atomic.AddInt64` on account and server out-counters became a local `dlvMsgs` count and one `updateStats()`. This fixed a ~5% large-fan-out regression and came out "even boost a bit". • 24ab7a29c1 2026-11-18 JetStream per-tier usage became `jsaCounters{mem, store atomic.Int64}`, with the lock only for creating a tier (read lock, then write lock and recheck). Multi-stream single-account ingest now matches multi-account throughput.

### haproxy-120 — Combine many writers into one: queue them, let a leader do one operation
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: jemalloc, PostgreSQL
Principle: When many threads would each CAS or lock the same hot location, have them append to a cheap side list instead, and let one leader (the last arrival, or a drain task) perform a single combined operation for the whole batch.
Why it is faster: N threads hammering one tail pointer make the line ping-pong N times. A combining leader turns that into one reservation, while waiters touch other lines. Inside the batch, copying all payloads first with plain loads and then doing a separate pass of release stores lets the CPU prefetch, instead of interleaving stores with loads. Draining per-group side lists into a shared tree from one tasklet amortises the write lock across many inserts.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: group commit in WAL writers; change buffers that batch secondary-index updates. • Web / backend: loggers where request threads enqueue and one writer flushes. • Native / systems: flat combining for concurrent priority queues; completing N I/O-ring entries and then ringing one doorbell.
Watch out: the leader does extra work and adds latency for the batch. Keep one side list per locality domain, or it becomes the new hotspot.
Seen in HAProxy: 1e2311edbc 2024-03-11 `ring_write()`: arriving threads queue on separate wait queues and the last arrival reserves space for all of them: 80-core ARM went from 385k to 4.8M msg/s, 24C/48T EPYC from 1.88M to 3.82M. 6c1b29d06f made the queue count tunable: 6 was best on EPYC, Xeon and Ampere, assigned by `tid % n`. • cabe945876 2024-03-15 Copy all messages first, then a second pass of release stores, instead of a CAS per waiter during the copy: +33% (3.33M to 4.44M) on EPYC, 7.0M/s on ARM. • 388539faa3 2025-05-02 Stick-table updates go to per-thread-group lock-free lists, and a tasklet inserts them into the tree in batches; 8bc8a21b25 one expiry task per shard fed the same way; d5562e31bd then removed the table lock entirely. • Also seen in jemalloc (jemalloc-113): 70c94d7474 2024-01-22 adds a batcher module and fc615739cb 2024-02-09 "Add batching to arena bins", a fast path for frees to non-home bins that "encounter lock contention" (prepared by 6e56848850 2024-02-07). All of it was reverted in 2114349a4e 2025-07-15. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-154): 0e141c0fbb 2015-08-06 `ProcArrayGroupClearXid`: backends CAS themselves onto a list and the leader clears all XIDs under one ProcArrayLock acquisition: "much more efficient at very high client counts". • ccce90b398 2017-03-09 and its re-landed form baaf272ac9 2017-09-01 group CLOG status updates: try CLogControlLock conditionally, else join a group whose leader sets status for all members on the same page (skipped for members with many subxacts). "Very significantly reduces" lock waits on multi-socket pgbench.

### haproxy-121 — Elect a single doer with one atomic bit; everyone else leaves
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: NATS
Principle: For a rare slow-path action (reporting drops, waking readers), keep a bit meaning "a thread is already doing this", in its own word or inside a counter. The thread that sets it does the work; others just record their event and return without entering the slow path.
Why it is faster: The common path becomes one load and no write, instead of a lock taken on every call or every producer walking the subscriber list. A pending counter catches events that arrive during the owner's walk, so nothing is lost. Threads that would only disturb the owner stay out of its cache lines.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: single-flight cache refresh where one goroutine refreshes and the others serve the old value. • Native / systems: a "flush in progress" bit in metrics aggregators; coalesced `notify_all`. • Embedded: ISR-to-task handoff through one atomic word with a busy bit.
Watch out: the owner must re-check for events that arrived while it worked before clearing the bit; otherwise the last event can be stranded.
Seen in HAProxy: c7bd7a68e4 2024-03-02 Ring writers: one thread at a time owns waking the readers via an XCHG flag, with `ring->pending` catching events during the walk. This removed watchdog triggers on 24-core EPYC. • 758cb450a2 2024-03-01 The dropped-message counter shifted left by one so bit 0 elects the reporter; drops add 2.
