This removed a read lock taken on every trace: 11k to 36k RPS with a reader attached (4C/8T). • 08c6bbb542 2025-09-18 `sink_write()` loads the counter once and leaves at once if the owner bit is set: 244k to 262k req/s. • Also seen in NATS (nats-112): a13402cd01 2020-06-18 `fs.expiring` ensures expiry runs one at a time. `indexCacheBuf` double-checks after locking: "Someone else may have filled this in by the time we get here". • 32f1a58b09 2021-04-20 `prunePubPermsCache` starts with `if !atomic.CompareAndSwapInt32(&c.perms.prun, 0, 1) { return }`. • 875f744678 2023-10-24 An atomic swap ensures a single leader loop runs. • 79c6552cca 2025-03-13 Pruning is attempted up to 5 times, each CAS-gated, with `maxPruneAtOnce = 1000`, stopping once under the limit. • e98e7ccec3 2026-04-29 `creatingRaftGroups map[string]chan struct{}` registers in-flight creations under the lock. Concurrent callers wait on the channel and retry while the creator works unlocked.

### haproxy-122 — Trylock, and skip or defer when someone else holds the lock
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: jemalloc, memcached, PostgreSQL, Seastar
Principle: For work that is idempotent, best-effort or can be done later (draining a queue, repositioning in a tree, expiry, logging), try the lock a bounded number of times. On failure, skip because another thread is doing it, move to another resource, or hand the work to an asynchronous task that coalesces requests.
Why it is faster: Blocking on a lock to do work another thread is already doing creates convoys where everyone follows the leader in lockstep. A failed trylock costs one atomic op. Deferring merges many updates into one later acquisition, and background maintenance never competes hard with the request path. The price is temporary inaccuracy (a slightly stale order, a small overshoot), which must be acceptable.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: singleflight cache reloads; best-effort telemetry that drops instead of blocking. • Data / databases: background flushers that skip a busy page; vacuum and TTL sweepers that yield to foreground locks; LRU repositioning done lazily by a sweeper. • Native / systems: work-stealing schedulers that skip a contended victim.
Watch out: something must guarantee the skipped work is eventually done: the winner's loop must re-check, or the deferred task must run. Without that, work is silently lost.
Seen in HAProxy: df187875da 2020-06-11 Log writes use a trylock with up to 200 retries and a CPU relax, then drop the line: about 1000 drops per 10M logs at 480k lines/s on 6 threads, versus 50% drops with a single trylock. • ae0b12ee03 2021-06-24 `process_srv_queue()` trylocks the server queue and the outer loop re-tests the condition: 496k to 575k req/s with one server on 16 threads, queue overhead from ~20% to ~2%. b1adf03df9 threads skip to the next thread's idle list on a busy lock; 9b9f8477f8 the same at high FD counts. • b6a8318cc2 2025-02-11 leastconn repositioning uses a write trylock and on failure wakes a requeue tasklet (627280e15f): 24-core EPYC 382k to 610k rps, 64-core Altra 311k to 1.21M rps (3.6x), CPU use at equal load down 3.55x. • a771b14541 2025-09-09 Table expiry blocks only if nothing was purged yet and aborts on a second contention; f87cf8b76e eviction starts from a random shard and stops after one eviction plus a failed trylock (0.015% overshoot at 350k req/s); b119280f60 allows a temporary size overshoot; 46463d6850 spends the visit budget 4x faster while holding the update lock; b3ad7b6371 peers reschedule when they cannot get the lock. • Also seen in jemalloc (jemalloc-101): 0a9f9a4d51 2015-06-22 `arena_maybe_purge()`: `if (arena->purging) return;` plus a loop instead of recursion. • cf6035e1ee 2017-04-21 `arena_decay_impl`: "If another thread is working on decay, we don't have to wait." f970c497dc 2017-04-21 implements trylock with proper stats. • b693c7868e 2017-03-17 `background_thread_interval_check` uses trylock to avoid adding variance to application threads. • Also seen in memcached (memcached-084): 2db1bf462c 2012-08-17 `do_item_alloc` walks up to 5 tail items, calls `item_trylock(hv)` on each and `continue`s if it is busy. Holding the item lock guarantees nobody raises the refcount at the same time. 0d1f505c15 2014-04-14 the LRU crawler uses the same pattern. • fb26989756 2015-01-03 / a039084732 2015-01-04 `lru_pull_tail` trylocks and skips refcount-held items: "you want them to fall out and cause occasional OOM's, rather than internally work around them". d67d18791f 2017-06-15 lets the slab mover delete items that stay busy. • 8aad5ae070 2024-03-07 `item_flush_expired` trylocks because "we can't block here because the lock order is inverted". 788f592f4a 2024-05-24 hash iterator creation trylocks `maintenance_lock` and returns "ERROR locked try again later". Flash recache opts "to throw away rather than wait on a lock". • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-167): bbb6e559c4 2011-11-07 VACUUM skips pages whose cleanup lock isn't immediately available, waiting only if tuples need freezing; 32896c40ca 2011-02-07 autovacuum workers don't wait for relation locks unless the run is anti-wraparound. • 29a0fb2157 2026-03-11 AIO worker submission conditionally acquires the queue lock; on failure or a full queue, the remaining IOs run synchronously. • Also seen in Seastar (seastar-113): 2185f377e3 2016-04-14 A padded try-lock before the sleep barrier; concurrent barriers had caused "latencies in the tens of milliseconds". c5fcb318e4 moves the trylock into the API so it can be elided when unneeded. • 4fc35ffcd7 2024-03-13 Only one reactor at a time attempts `sys_membarrier` or the madvise barrier; others return false and keep polling, restoring wakeup latency on AWS d3en instances.

### haproxy-123 — Bound the work done per lock acquisition
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Cap how many items one thread processes while holding a shared lock, accept partial progress, and scale the cap down as thread count rises.
Why it is faster: An unbounded loop under a global lock turns into a latency spike for every other thread and can trigger watchdogs. Smaller batches let others in sooner. Below some floor, per-batch overhead dominates, so the cap needs a minimum.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: batch deletes with `LIMIT` per transaction; eviction budgets per pass. • Web / backend: async reapers that process N items per tick. • Native / systems: incremental GC slices sized by core count.
Watch out: if arrivals outpace the bounded drain, backlog grows. Monitor queue length and make the cap adaptive where possible.
Seen in HAProxy: 86c6a9221a 2020-05-01 The shared-context lock loop is bounded. • 3cfaa8d1e0 2020-10-16 Expired tasks picked from the global wait queue are limited to `runqueue_depth` per pass. • dfe79251da 2020-11-03 Stick-table purge visits at most twice the number of nodes to delete: 128x faster in the worst case, seen on NUMA when most entries were still referenced. • 231d3d32be 2024-05-15 Under the global Lua lock, the 10k-instruction yield budget is divided by the thread count, with a minimum of 500.

### haproxy-124 — Back off when spinning: pause, wait for release, grow the delay, randomise
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: PostgreSQL, Seastar
Principle: After a failed CAS or lock attempt, execute a CPU pause, wait with plain reads until the lock looks free, and grow the wait geometrically with random jitter, capped. Yield the CPU if threads may outnumber cores.
Why it is faster: Immediate retries make all waiters hit the line together, and the coherence traffic slows even the owner. A pause stops flooding the interconnect and starving the SMT sibling. Growing, randomised delays desynchronise threads that would otherwise collide in step. The growth rate and cap trade lock latency against throughput, and must be measured. A spinner on an oversubscribed machine steals the CPU from the thread it is waiting for.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `_mm_pause()`, `std::hint::spin_loop()`, test-and-test-and-set with exponential back-off; spin-then-park mutexes. • Web / backend: `Thread.onSpinWait()` in Java; jittered retries against a contended DB row or API. • Embedded: SMP spinlocks in RTOS code with WFE/SEV or bounded delay loops. • Data / databases: latch spin counts and optimistic-transaction retry back-off.
Watch out: too much back-off adds latency and lets the lock sit idle. Tune the curve per platform, and remember that reading before exchanging can also hurt (one HAProxy commit found it "significantly degraded performance" in a list loop).
Seen in HAProxy: 3ea2490b48 2018-07-27 The thread barrier uses `sched_yield()` instead of a relax: with 4 threads on 2 cores, 40-200 conn/s (hundreds of ms) became 7200 conn/s (12 ms). • 168fc5332c 2021-03-01 Lock-free list loops gained `__ha_cpu_relax()`, fixing watchdog panics on 64-core Graviton2; 6f9f2c0857, 6339c19cac relax on failed CASes; d46ed5c26b waits for the rotation lock to drop before retrying (~1 kB smaller). • 688709d814 2022-07-29 Updating the lock library to get exponential back-off: dequeuing 113k to 300k RPS, roundrobin 229k to 1020k RPS at ≥ 48 threads; b13044cc1a adds a switch to disable it so contended functions show in perf. • feeda4132b 2023-07-24 Exponential back-off in pool free-list waits: 1.1M to 2.1M req/s on 56 threads; 544c2f2d9e in `pool_flush()`; 8178a5211c in rwlock writer waits (+33% stick-tables on ARM). • 78fa54863d 2023-08-17 Relax added to the atomic min/max, CAS and XCHG fallback loops, run 8 times per stream stats update. • c618ed5ff4 2023-09-13 Lock-free lists rewritten with back-off: QUIC on 80-core ARM went from 35 to 41 Gbps with CPU from 7800% to 2900%. 4e65fc66f6 grows the wait by `loops*8+7` with an xorshift mask; 98967aa09f lowered the cap after slowdowns on some hardware. • 253fba01a7 2025-02-07 Back-off growth changed from 1.5^N capped at 256k cycles to 1.25^N capped at 128k: max write-lock wait down 4x (about 14 variants measured); b957e2f3ef moved the relax threshold to 90 iterations (10-20% faster lock grab); b74ec1efc2 relax after failed queue CASes. • 658eaa4086 2025-04-17 Each thread group inserts servers in a different order, and f87cf8b76e starts eviction at a random shard, so threads do not all start on the same lock. • Also seen in PostgreSQL (postgresql-157): f8eed65dfb 2003-12-27 x86 does `cmpb $0` before `lock; xchgb` and adds `rep; nop` (PAUSE) in the spin loop. • 9907b9775b 2005-10-11 removes the unlocked pre-test on x86_64: "a big loss on Opterons and at best a wash on EM64T". • bc2a050d40 2012-01-03 (PPC) and b03d196be0 2013-08-29 (x86_64) add a non-locking initial test in `TAS_SPIN`, a win "at sufficiently high contention levels". • Also seen in Seastar (seastar-114): e3915cfcf8 2015-01-15 "call pause instruction in idle loop" (`_mm_pause()` in `reactor::run`). • 3c249686a1 2023-11-15 `spinlock::lock()` gains an inner `while (_busy.load(relaxed)) cpu_relax();` before retrying the exchange.

### haproxy-125 — Tune spin-wait and line-ownership tricks per CPU architecture
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Hide the spin-wait hint and similar cache-coherence tricks behind one primitive whose implementation is chosen and benchmarked per architecture.
Why it is faster: The best pause instruction and spin pattern differ between x86, AMD and ARM. On ARMv8 an `isb` slows a spinner more effectively than `yield`. On x86 a no-op CAS takes a line Exclusive where a load leaves it Shared, which saves an ownership round trip when a write follows soon; on ARM the same trick loses.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: per-target `spin_loop` implementations in Rust and C++ lock libraries. • Data / databases: retune spinlocks when moving a DB engine to Graviton or Ampere. • Embedded: WFE/SEV on ARM cores instead of busy NOP loops.
Watch out: results differ even between x86 vendors. Keep a benchmark per supported CPU family and re-run it on new hardware.
Seen in HAProxy: 1e237d037b 2020-11-28 `pl_cpu_relax` uses `isb` on aarch64: watchdog stalls with 16 threads went away, slightly more than +5%. • c038ca8e8c 2024-03-15 `__ha_cpu_relax_for_read()` for read-polling loops (x86 preferred no pause, aarch64 a short one); 30a659c355 careful relax placement took ARM to 6.0M msg/s on 80 threads. • a727c6eaa5 2025-09-18 The ring queue-owner check uses a no-op CAS on x86 only: +5% at 128 threads (10.56M to 11.08M msg/s); ARM lost about 1% with it and keeps the load. • d25099b359 2025-09-18 A full relax instead of the read variant in ring lock loops: +5% on AMD, no change on Intel or ARM.

### haproxy-126 — Lock-free intrusive lists via XCHG busy markers
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Make a linked list thread-safe without a global lock by atomically exchanging the neighbour pointers with a BUSY sentinel, which locks only the links being changed, then patching and releasing them. Use the same sentinel trick for free-list heads.
Why it is faster: A global list lock serialises every add and remove. Locking individual links lets operations on different parts of the list run in parallel and lets any thread enqueue onto another thread's list. An exchange with a sentinel is a single-width atomic, cheaper than a double-width CAS with a sequence counter, and it also avoids ABA.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: cross-thread wake queues and allocator free lists; lock-free intrusive lists in timer wheels. • Web / backend: `ConcurrentLinkedQueue` for registration lists; atomic "is queued" flags to avoid double enqueue. • Data / databases: bucketed lists inside index nodes so moving an entry between existing keys needs no structural lock.
Watch out: busy markers are short spinlocks, so contended lists still need back-off (HAProxy added it later). Lock-free code is hard to verify; stress-test it on many cores and on ARM.
Seen in HAProxy: 859dc80f94 2019-08-08 `struct mt_list` with add, append and delete built on `_HA_ATOMIC_XCHG(&lh->next, MT_LIST_BUSY)`; ff1e9f39b9 makes tasklet lists mt_lists so any thread can wake any tasklet; ff1d0929b8 moves idle connections without a lock. • 751153e0f1 2021-02-17 `server->actconns` on an mt_list removed the last server-lock use in `sess_change_server()` (+1.8%); dde1b4499a adds `LIST_INLIST_ATOMIC()` to test membership without a lock. • 2a4523f6f4 2021-06-09 The lockless pool free list replaced double-width CAS plus sequence with an XCHG of a `POOL_BUSY` sentinel: 2.6x faster than the locked and no-cache variants on 2 MB buffers; 8715dec6f9 deleted the locked implementation. • 9fe72bba3c 2025-03-26 leastconn tree nodes hold several mt_lists of equal-key servers, chosen at random, and keep spare empty nodes so moves avoid the tree write lock: 830k to 1.55M rps with 500 servers on 64-core AMD.

### haproxy-127 — Protect CAS against ABA with a version counter in spare bits
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: Tokio
Principle: When a CAS compares a value that can return to an earlier state (a free-list head, an index), add an update counter, either in a second word swapped together (double-width CAS) or in unused high bits, so every write is unique. A 64-bit monotonic position cannot wrap in practice.
Why it is faster: It makes lock-free algorithms correct without a lock. Putting the generation in otherwise unused bits keeps it to one single-width CAS. A double-width CAS works anywhere but costs more, so HAProxy later replaced several uses with cheaper schemes.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: tagged-pointer Treiber stacks; Java `AtomicStampedReference`. • Embedded: sequence numbers in lock-free mailboxes between cores. • Data / databases: optimistic-concurrency version columns on rows.
Watch out: small counters (8 bits) can still wrap under extreme rates, and double-width CAS is missing or slow on some CPUs. Check the worst-case wrap time.
Seen in HAProxy: f61f0cb95f 2017-12-21 `__ha_cas_dw` for x86_64, arm and aarch64; cf975d46bc the pool free list as `{free_list, seq}` popped with a double-width CAS, compiling the pool spinlock out. • f69fea64e0 2021-08-03 The double-width CAS on the FD running and thread masks replaced by a single CAS plus a consistency check; it survived 10 billion takeovers at 800k req/s. • ff18504d73 2023-04-20 The listener per-thread index became a `ulong` with an 8-bit (32-bit build) or 40-bit (64-bit build) update counter above the index, so a stale CAS can no longer route a connection to the wrong thread. • 57d8f06215 2026-02-23 The MPSC ring reserves space with a CAS on a 64-bit head, which cannot hit ABA through wraparound. • Also seen in Tokio (tokio-089): c17ecb53e7 2018-07-16 (tree) The sleeping-worker Treiber stack keeps the head index in the low 16 bits and a 48-bit ABA sequence above it (`ABA_GUARD_SHIFT = 16`), with a `pushed` bit so each worker is on the stack at most once. • 78fc94099b 2022-09-27 Work-stealing queue chooses `UnsignedShort/UnsignedLong` by `cfg_has_atomic_u64`, packing 32-bit cursors into a 64-bit atomic: "greatly improves resilience to ABA and has no impact on performance on 64-bit platforms".

### haproxy-128 — Replace an object lock with per-thread "running" bits
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Before touching a shared object, each thread sets its own bit in a running mask; exclusive operations wait until the mask is empty. Change ownership with a CAS on the mask and owner fields, and use the result of the atomic op itself to detect conflicts early.
Why it is faster: Normal operations by the owning thread never contend, because they set and clear their own bit. Only rare migrations (takeovers) pay for a CAS loop. Checking the return value of the atomic op avoids starting an expensive CAS that is bound to fail.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: ownership transfer of sockets between event loops; hazard-style "in use" bits in object caches. • Data / databases: per-core page ownership where other cores must first request a handoff.
Watch out: the mask width limits thread count (HAProxy later added thread groups partly for this). Exclusive waiters can starve if owners re-enter constantly.
Seen in HAProxy: a7bf573520 2020-02-27 The FD `running_mask` replaced the FD spinlock. • 8851664293 2020-03-05 `fd_takeover()` moves an FD to another thread with a double CAS on (running_mask, thread_mask), falling back to an rwlock without DWCAS; 4297363de3 uses the atomic op's return value to see conflicts before the costly CAS; c460c91633 skips the migration lock if another thread already stole the FD.

### haproxy-129 — Hand off work between threads through rings of small, contiguous entries
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Give each consumer thread its own ring. Producers reserve space with a CAS and then publish; entries are small (a pointer, or a size header plus payload that never wraps), and head and tail sit on separate cache lines.
Why it is faster: A shared locked list or per-listener buffer makes every producer and the consumer contend on the same lock and causes head-of-line blocking. A per-consumer ring has one contended word for producers and none for the consumer. Small entries mean more slots in fewer cache lines: build the object first and enqueue only its pointer. Entries that never wrap give the reader one contiguous pointer.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: LMAX Disruptor-style pipelines; packet handoff from RX cores to worker cores in DPDK. • Web / backend: actor mailboxes carrying a pointer rather than a copy; log shipping from app threads to one writer. • Data / databases: WAL buffer reservation by CAS on a 64-bit position.
Watch out: a full ring needs a policy (drop, block, or fall back), and passing pointers means the receiving thread now frees memory allocated elsewhere, which can defeat thread-local allocator caches.
Seen in HAProxy: 1efafce61f 2019-01-27 Per-thread accept rings (256+ entries, 64-byte entries) for handing accepted connections to other threads. • 83efc320aa 2020-10-14 The connection is allocated before queueing it to the target thread: entries go from 64 to 8 bytes, the ring grows from 256 to 1024 slots at half the memory, and connection rate rises 1%. • 57d8f06215 2026-02-23 `mpring.h`: an MPSC byte ring with variable-length, 8-byte aligned entries, padding markers instead of wrap, and head and tail on separate lines; 9b5f11cd3d uses one per QUIC datagram handler, deleting 308 lines and removing list contention and stuck threads.

### haproxy-130 — Pick the cheapest lock primitive that fits, and keep waiting in user space
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: Netty
Principle: Use a plain mutual-exclusion lock where no readers exist, a user-space spinlock or futex where a heavyweight library lock would enter the kernel, and interpose your own lock implementation for third-party code that contends.
Why it is faster: A writer lock from a reader-writer lock must wait for readers to drain, which costs an extra test and code size; a simple lock does not. Process-shared pthread locks and library rwlocks can fall back to futex syscalls under contention, so most CPU goes to the kernel. User-space locks keep waiting in user space and are smaller, which also helps inlining.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `std::mutex` instead of `shared_mutex` when there are no readers; LD_PRELOAD interposition of locks or malloc for contended third-party code. • Web / backend: Go `sync.Mutex` rather than `RWMutex` on write-only paths. • Data / databases: adaptive spin-then-park latches instead of OS mutexes on hot pages.
Watch out: pure spinlocks behave badly when threads outnumber cores or get descheduled while holding the lock. Combine with back-off or yielding.
Seen in HAProxy: 3e541d1c03 2012-09-03 The shared SSL session cache uses futexes and x86 locked instructions instead of pthread mutexes; cd1a526a90 a spinlock replaces pthread process-shared locks by default. • 88ac59be4d 2017-11-06 Spinlocks switched from the write variant to the simple "seek" variant, which "saves one test per spinlock and a few tens of bytes allowing certain functions to be inlined". • 87aff021db 2022-07-10 `USE_PTHREAD_EMULATION=1` redefines `pthread_rwlock_*` on top of the internal lock library, mainly for OpenSSL: lock time fell from 80% CPU (in `futex_wake`) to 12-15% with async engines at 24+ threads. • Also seen in Netty (netty-123): 686ef795f9 2010-02-01 added `NonReentrantLock` and replaced `ReentrantLock` or monitor objects with it in `NioSocketChannel.writeLock` and `SslHandler`, removing the `isHeldByCurrentThread()` checks. • 243264efb0 2008-08-31 uses a `ReentrantReadWriteLock` for the selector guard. 8fb2f7c789 2008-09-28 found "a simple shutdown lock" enough for a single acceptor.

### haproxy-131 — Give each thread its own scratch state for non-reentrant library calls
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: Envoy
Principle: Replace shared scratch buffers and library functions that serialise internally with per-thread buffers, reentrant `_r` variants, or a small per-thread cache of recent results.
Why it is faster: A shared scratch buffer or a non-reentrant call forces a global lock around every use. Some libc functions also take hidden global locks (for example `localtime()` takes a timezone lock). Per-thread state removes that serialisation point, and a tiny per-thread memo keyed by the input avoids the call entirely when inputs repeat (the current second).
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `strtok_r`, `localtime_r`, `rand_r`; per-thread cache of formatted timestamps. • Web / backend: per-thread `SimpleDateFormat`/`MessageDigest` in Java; thread-local regex or compression contexts in Python. • Embedded: per-task copies of RTC-to-calendar conversions.
Watch out: per-thread buffers multiply memory by thread count, and memo caches keyed on time need invalidation when the timezone or clock changes.
Seen in HAProxy: 6adad11283 2017-04-21 Trash chunks became thread-local; 272e252e61 the regex trash buffer; 9a65571781 the time globals. • 943e7ec025 2018-10-29 `crypt_r()` with a thread-local `struct crypt_data` replaced a spinlocked `crypt()`, with the biggest gain "on very slow crypto algorithms". • 34b9b528e8 2026-07-23 A 4-entry thread-local cache in front of `localtime()`/`gmtime()`, scanned newest-first: `option httplog` had halved throughput on 64 cores (1.8M to 910k req/s, 80% in the kernel spinlock slow path); afterwards everything ran at native speed. • Also seen in Envoy (envoy-119): d7badb5f9b 2024-04-17 `static thread_local const int64_t tid` caches the thread id. • 3d18e34f08 2026-11-10 The OpenSSL compat `SSL_get_cipher_by_value` built and freed an `SSL_CTX` and `SSL` per call (once per offered cipher in TLS 1.2 cert selection); a `thread_local` `SSL` cut 188.77 µs to 0.34 µs per call (about 550x).

### haproxy-132 — Use per-thread PRNG streams where statistical randomness is enough
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: jemalloc, NATS, Pebble
Principle: For load balancing, jitter, sampling and IDs that do not need cryptographic strength, give each thread its own fast generator (xorshift or xoshiro family), seeded so the streams never overlap (jump-ahead).
Why it is faster: A shared generator needs a lock or a double-width CAS on every draw, so it becomes a serialisation point as soon as many threads use it. A thread-local state needs no synchronisation, and the generators are a handful of instructions. Jumping the sequence by a huge power of two per thread keeps the streams independent.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `ThreadLocalRandom` in Java, per-P generators in Go's `math/rand/v2`, for LB picks and retry jitter. • Native / systems: per-core RNG in DPDK; parallel Monte-Carlo with jump-ahead streams. • Data / databases: sampling for statistics and tracing in worker threads.
Watch out: never use these generators for keys, tokens that must be unguessable, or anything security-relevant; keep a cryptographic source for those.
Seen in HAProxy: 52bf839394 2020-03-08 xoroshiro128** with a 128-bit CAS replaced OS random APIs, with a 2^96 jump per process. • 06e69b556c 2021-03-02 A thread-local `statistical_prng()`; 1adaddb494 uses it for the "random" LB algorithm, where the shared PRNG was a hotspot; 20f88abad5 for fault injection. • 95f40c698d 2022-11-24 The `rand()` sample fetch switched to it: "locking contention is completely gone"; a840b4a39b for health-check jitter. • 4a9ec66fd8 2026-05-25 The shared xoroshiro128** became a thread-local xoshiro256** jumped by 2^192 × tid: 100 UUIDs per request on 20 threads went from 135k to 1.25M req/s (9x); 26c3b3f41d produces 128 bits per state advance plus a hash, halving PRNG calls. • Also seen in jemalloc (jemalloc-108): d6feed6e66 2017-10-13 `extent_addr_randomize` moves `offset_state` into TSD, seeded with the TSD address; the arena's atomic PRNG is used only without TSD. • 19a51abf33 2019-11-04 Removes the arena's atomic `offset_state` and uses a stack address as seed when TSD is unavailable. bc774a3519 2019-11-04 renames the TSD field `prng_state`. 9e6aa77ab9 2020-08-14 removes atomic PRNG support: "we now expect to only use thread-local randomization for contention-reduction reasons". • Also seen in NATS (nats-116): 24d3ad3e3a 2014-09-18 TODO: "NewSource on Rand do lower lock contention on QueueSubs". • 96d9ce5048 2016-04-03 `c.cache.prand = rand.New(rand.NewSource(...))`: "Global rand has contention with lots of clients". • 6ad8287bbe 2019-11-16 Each account gets its own `prand` for reply-subject generation. • d42553451c 2023-12-19 Added `internal/fastrand` linking `runtime.fastrand`/`fastrandn`. It replaced the account `prand` (used under the account write lock), the per-client `prand` for queue selection, and Raft's rand. • Also seen in Pebble (pebble-127): 1342426ba3 2018-07-15 Each `arenaskl.Skiplist` got its own `rand.Source64` instead of calling the global `rand.Uint32()`. • 7ad569cc28 2020-06-17 `randomHeight` switched from a mutex-guarded PCG source to `runtime.fastrand` via `go:linkname`. abea58adc3 2020-05-17 made the workload generators take a caller-supplied RNG. Those locks were "frequently limiting" ycsb throughput.

### haproxy-133 — Read a thread-local or coarsened copy of a hot global
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: If every thread reads a value that others keep updating (a clock, a date), use a thread-local copy on the fast path and consult the global only near a boundary. If threads must publish it, drop low-order precision so most publishes become no-ops.
Why it is faster: Reading a variable that other cores write costs a cache miss almost every time. A local copy is nearly always equal and costs an L1 hit. When each thread publishes a slightly different high-resolution value, almost every publish invalidates the line everywhere; ignoring the low bits keeps the shared value stable, and precision that nobody needs is lost.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: a shared "now" refreshed once per millisecond for cache TTLs; a per-tick cached `Date.now()` in Node servers. • Data / databases: round "last seen" times before writing them to avoid write amplification. • Native / systems: per-thread coarse clocks instead of a shared high-resolution timestamp.
Watch out: callers that need exact cross-thread ordering of timestamps can no longer rely on the coarse value. Document the granularity.
Seen in HAProxy: 4eaf85f5d9 2022-09-21 The global date is updated only if `(new ^ old) & ~0x1F` (32 µs granularity): wakeups went from 7.3M/s to 66M/s (9x), and with real tasks from 28M to 52M/s. • 58b73f9fa8 2022-09-21 The busy-poll loop updates only the local date and publishes the global one when leaving (with a700420671): 53M to 352M loops/s, where perf had shown 67% in the global date update. • f6a42c3a37 2022-10-11 Frequency counters compare against the thread-local `now_ms` and fall back to the global date (about 0.003% of calls) in an out-of-line slow function: +2-3% at 500k req/s.

### haproxy-134 — Allow lossy updates for estimates
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: For moving averages and similar estimators, try the CAS once and give up if it fails, because another thread has just fed the estimator.
Why it is faster: Retrying a CAS under contention burns CPU and keeps the line bouncing. Losing an occasional sample barely changes an average, so a single attempt bounds the cost to one atomic op per call.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: EWMA latency estimates in client load balancers; approximate hit-rate counters. • Native / systems: sampling profilers and approximate per-core statistics. • Data / databases: planner statistics updated opportunistically.
Watch out: never apply this to counts that must be exact (refcounts, limits, billing). Under heavy contention many samples are lost, which can bias the estimate.
Seen in HAProxy: e327b4a73e 2022-12-19 `swrate_add_opportunistic()` and variants do one CAS with no loop; 2aa14ce5a1 uses them for the pool demand estimators: +1-2% on 48 threads.

### haproxy-135 — Don't maintain shared counters or references nobody needs
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Remove shared accounting that duplicates another value, maintain counters only when the feature that reads them is enabled, and restructure lifetimes so children need not refcount a hot shared parent.
Why it is faster: "Just in case" bookkeeping costs atomics on shared lines on every operation. When no limit is configured, nothing reads the counter. If every child takes and drops a reference on one parent, all cores hammer that refcount.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: skip quota counters when no quota is set; do not copy a `shared_ptr` to a global config per request (use RCU or epochs). • Data / databases: skip per-row memory accounting when no memory limit is configured. • Native / systems: compile out metrics when no exporter is attached.
Watch out: enabling the feature at run time must initialise the counter correctly, and removing a refcount needs a clear proof of lifetime ordering.
Seen in HAProxy: 3b78f2aa5d 2021-09-08 Removed `vars_global_size`, which always equalled the process-scope size and cost up to 38% CPU: 770k to 1.06M req/s. 55f8a830dc guards each accounting atomic with `if (var_*_limit)`: 1.06M to 1.24M req/s. 2c41d77ebc stopped maintaining a size used only as a boolean. • 33c8270903 2026-05-20 The H2 stream-elasticity counter is updated only when that (default off) feature is on, removing atomics from every stream open and close. • c2eef4f4b3 2026-10-15 `stream_free()` no longer takes and drops a reference on the frontend: its pools are flushed earlier, so the frontend is never touched after it could disappear.

### haproxy-136 — Bind work and resources to an owner thread instead of sharing them
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Pin periodic tasks, maintenance jobs, sockets and timers to one owner thread (rotating the assignment across instances), let other threads message the owner instead of touching its data, and stay local when cross-thread sharing is disabled.
Why it is faster: A "run anywhere" task requeued from many threads contends on the global wait-queue lock and on its own data lock. A socket whose replies land on any thread makes receivers fight for the owner's lock. Stealing another thread's connections costs cache-line migration plus kernel FD locking. With one owner, these locks disappear; load balance is restored by spreading owners or sampling a few threads occasionally.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: thread-per-core runtimes (Seastar), per-core timer wheels, per-thread UDP sockets for DNS or QUIC clients. • Web / backend: actor affinity; per-worker DB connection pools that never steal. • Data / databases: per-partition maintenance threads in storage engines.
Watch out: fixed ownership can create hot threads. Rotate assignments and add cheap load sampling to move work when one owner falls behind.
Seen in HAProxy: a826250659 2025-02-25 No killing of other threads' idle connections when pool sharing is disabled: a trylock that took 9% CPU vanished, +12% on 128 threads; 8de8ed4f48 cross-group takeover is off by default. • e05afda249 2025-09-10 Each table-expiry task bound to one thread: half the multi-thread wakeups, +12% req rate with 16 peers. ded2110ec6 peer sync +1-3%; 2ce5e0edcc resolvers CPU 11% to 7%. • d624aceaef 2025-09-10 DNS nameserver sockets bound to the calling thread: CPU 38% to ~10% with 10 nameservers. • 0988b9c773 2026-04-28 The thread that first queues a timer task owns it in its private timer tree; others only send a wake flag. aaee6c463c then removed the wait-queue lock; b9aa1c0e64 samples 3 random threads to redispatch expired tasks for balance. • 3c923d075c 2026-05-28 Idle-connection purging moved to a per-thread task with a per-thread server tree, needing no global lock.

### haproxy-137 — Handle rare global synchronisation with a quiescent-state rendezvous, briefly
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy · Also: memcached
Principle: For rare maintenance, let threads advertise when they are harmless (sleeping in the poller, in a syscall) and have the isolating thread wait only for the others. Keep each isolated section short: yield inside long ones, and replace isolation with a narrower lock wherever one suffices.
Why it is faster: A barrier that wakes every thread costs CPU on idle ones; harmless threads need not respond. Running rare heavy maintenance in isolation can be cheaper overall than making it and every hot path lock-safe. But isolation blocks all traffic, so its duration must be bounded.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: RCU quiescent states; GC safepoints that treat threads blocked in native code as already safe. • Web / backend: config hot-reload that waits only for busy workers. • Data / databases: prefer a table-level lock to a database-wide pause for maintenance.
Watch out: isolation called while holding a lock another thread needs deadlocks. Treat it as a last resort and keep each isolated slice short.
Seen in HAProxy: 60b639ccbe 2018-08-02 `threads_harmless_mask`, `thread_isolate()`, `thread_release()`; being harmless is one atomic OR around poll(). 3ff577e165 made server state changes synchronous with it (about 1960 checks/s, CPU slightly down to ~32%); ca1b15743f isolates once per "show fd" loop instead of per FD. • c0e2ff202b 2020-04-24 `pool_gc()` runs isolated and drops its locks; 26ed183556 `malloc_trim()` runs isolated so it does not compete with allocating threads. • 71a914e0ff 2026-10-01 `proxy_purge_servers()` yields every 50 servers so other threads get a window to run. • 8a4b50b074 2026-08-25 Ring readers attach under the tail lock instead of `thread_isolate()`, which is cheaper and avoids a deadlock when called with a lock held. • Also seen in memcached (memcached-091): 1c94e12c3d 2012-08-18 First design: workers are told over their notify pipe to switch between granular and global locks, and `item_lock` reads a `pthread_getspecific` flag on every call. • 6af7aa0b15 2014-12-27 This becomes `pause_threads()`: a `worker_hang_lock` that workers pass through, plus pausing the rebalancer and crawler. `item_lock` is a plain array index again, trading "the (possibly significant) slowdown during the hash table copy, with a short total hang at the beginning of each expansion". • 41fa179e90 2017-06-21 fixes a long lock pause in hash expansion caused by pause order. 76d0a13a1e 2017-10-16 also pauses extstore compaction during expansion.

### haproxy-138 — Partition threads into groups that match the cache topology, and shard per group
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Split threads into groups aligned with shared-cache domains (CCX, socket, NUMA node). Give each group its own instances of structures that must stay shared (queues, trees, counters, listening sockets), keep work inside the group, and only occasionally cross groups for fairness.
Why it is faster: A structure shared by 128 threads across sockets or CCXs bounces its lines across the interconnect, which is far slower than within one L3. Per-group copies divide the writers per line by the group factor and keep traffic local, while still allowing sharing that per-thread copies would forbid. Smaller groups multiply kernel-side resources such as listeners and spread kernel locks further. Cross-group stealing keeps the load balanced.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: per-socket shards and buffer pools (ScyllaDB/Seastar style); per-NUMA-node lock-manager partitions. • Web / backend: per-CCX worker pools on AMD EPYC with one `SO_REUSEPORT` socket each; NUMA-local connection pools. • Native / systems: work-stealing schedulers that prefer stealing within the same L3 domain.
Watch out: group boundaries must match the hardware; mismatched groups get the costs of partitioning with none of the locality. Cross-group fairness needs an explicit mechanism, or one group can starve while another idles.
Seen in HAProxy: 03f9b35114 2022-06-27 A per-group, cache-line-aligned `ha_tgroup_ctx[]` for the formerly global masks; 35ee710ece FD update list per group; b0e7712fb2 wait queues per group because "the WQ lock uses a huge amount of CPU alone"; 15c5500b6e idle-connection lookup scans only the current group; c21a187ec0 takeover index per group. • 0e875cf291 2023-04-23 `shards by-group` became the default: on a 24-core EPYC with one group per CCX, forwarded connection rate doubled from 112k to 214k/s and kernel locking fell from 71% to 55%. • 26b3e5236f 2025-01-15 One server/proxy queue per group; the dequeuer takes 9 from its own group, then 1 from another, using a bitmask of non-empty groups. This fixed watchdog triggers on AMD. • 3758eab71c 2025-04-17 Weighted round-robin uses one tree and lock per group: 620k to 1.4M rps with 64 servers on 64-core AMD. 16eb0fab31 frontend/backend counters per group; 12c3ffbb48 a local-only rate for max computations so totals are not re-aggregated per update. • 04a9f86a85 2026-02-24 Extra counters stored per group (with b604064980): 4.9M to 5.8M req/s in H1 and 6.0M to 7.6M in H2 with 5 groups of 16 threads; 5495c88441 allocates them only for the actual group count. • 0af603f46f 2026-04-16 Default max threads per group lowered from 64 to 16: 1.58M to 1.71M rps on 64-core Neoverse-N1, and connection:close from 85k to 172k cps thanks to more listeners and less kernel spinlock time.

### haproxy-139 — Partition kernel-side shared resources along the same lines
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When a kernel object shared by all threads becomes the bottleneck (here the process-wide file-descriptor table and its lock), give each thread group its own copy, and partition every resource that carries those handles in the same way.
Why it is faster: All threads of a Linux process share one fd table protected by a kernel lock, so with many threads accept, close and epoll operations contend inside the kernel, out of reach of user-space fixes. Per-group tables make that lock per group. The cost is that fds no longer move freely between groups, so balancing, takeover and pools must respect the boundary.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: multi-process designs (nginx workers, Envoy) get separate fd tables for free; kernel-bypass stacks use per-core queue ownership. • Web / backend: pin socket ownership to event-loop groups in a JVM server. • Data / databases: per-shard processes or file handles instead of one handle shared by all workers.
Watch out: every code path that passes an fd between threads must be audited, since a handle from another group's table is invalid. This is a deep, Linux-specific change.
Seen in HAProxy: defc3d1f05 2026-06-29 One fd table per thread group created with `unshare(CLONE_FILES)`; eef010489a per-group `polled_mask`; 5664f21c36 exposes `tune.fd.tables per-thread-group` for "a nice performance boost" from less kernel fd-table lock contention. • 3430556a88 2026-07-02 No cross-group connection balancing and a2547e15cb no cross-group takeover once tables are split; d6389e6946 a cross-group RPC runs code on the owning group. • c81d794822 2026-07-28 One free-pipe pool per group, capped at maxpipes divided by the group count (734a535200); 02b7685013 removed the global `fdinfo` table and folded its data into fdtab flags.

### haproxy-140 — Expose memory-level parallelism: preload, prefetch both candidates, separate load and store passes
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: In pointer-chasing loops, issue the loads (or prefetches) for every address you might need next as soon as it is known, choose between them with a conditional select, and in bulk operations do all the reads in one pass and the dependent writes in another.
Why it is faster: When the next address depends on a late computation, the CPU waits out each memory latency in turn. Prefetching both children of a tree node overlaps the two possible misses with the current comparison, and a ternary lets the predictor speculate on one of two known addresses. Doing more work up front is faster because it runs in parallel with the miss. Interleaving atomic stores with loads also defeats hardware prefetching, so a load-only pass followed by a store pass streams better.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: B-tree, trie and radix lookups that prefetch children (as in Masstree and ART). • Native / systems: branchless binary search that prefetches both next midpoints; GC mark loops that prefetch ahead. • Web / backend: batch lookups in a hash table: compute all bucket addresses first, then probe.
Watch out: prefetching wastes bandwidth when data is already in cache or when the guess is usually wrong. Small trees that fit in L1 gain nothing, so benchmark at realistic sizes.
Seen in HAProxy: 6af17d491f 2025-06-07 ebtree lookups prefetch both child pointers, load key, bit and branches early, precompute `1<<bit` and select the child with a ternary: eb32 with 100k keys 6.64 to 8.00 M lookups/s (+20.5%), eb64 with 1k keys 27.51 to 34.40 (+25%). • 3dda813d54 2025-06-13 Insertion with prefetch and a ternary root choice: +50% insertion rate on random keys. 61654c07bd ebmb lookup 10.07 to 12.25 M/s (+21.6%). • 52c6dd773d 2025-09-17 String-tree prefetch: lookup 276 to 199 ns (-28%), insert -4.9%. • cabe945876 2024-03-15 Ring writes copy all queued messages first with plain loads, then release the waiters in a second pass: +33% on 24C/48T EPYC.

### haproxy-141 — Place threads by CPU capacity and shared-cache domain
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: By default, run one thread per usable CPU, prefer the high-capacity cores on hybrid CPUs, and group threads by shared L3 so connection balancing and shared data stay within one cache domain.
Why it is faster: Slow cores in a hybrid CPU drag down shared work, and threads that share data across L3 domains pay interconnect latency on every exchange. Placement chosen from the topology avoids both without any code change in the hot paths.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: thread pinning in DPDK or Seastar; GOMAXPROCS or JVM thread counts per NUMA node. • Embedded: big.LITTLE-aware placement of latency-critical threads on mobile and embedded SoCs. • Data / databases: pin query workers by socket alongside their memory.
Watch out: containers and VMs may hide or misreport topology, and pinning fights the OS scheduler when other workloads share the machine. Provide an override.
Seen in HAProxy: 149ab779cc 2019-01-26 Starts one thread per bound CPU by default. • 6c88e27cf4 2025-05-13 CPU clusters sorted by per-CPU capacity, evicting those below 80% of the previous; 158da59c34 groups by CCX. • b74336984d 2025-06-26 The default `cpu-policy` became `performance`; 8aef5bec1e and c524b181a2 later added per-CCX and per-thread affinity.

### haproxy-142 — Place memory with NUMA first-touch from pinned threads
Lens: concurrency · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Allocate large per-group tables with lazily faulted `mmap`, pad each group's slice to a page, and make the first write to each slice from a thread bound to that group's CPUs, so the kernel places the pages on the local node.
Why it is faster: Linux places a page on the node of the CPU that first writes it. Zeroing a big table from the boot thread puts all of it on node 0, so every other node pays remote-memory latency forever. `mmap` returns untouched pages that are already zero, which also skips an eager zeroing pass. Page padding prevents two groups' slices from sharing a page.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: per-NUMA buffer pools and arenas allocated and first touched from already-pinned worker threads. • Native / systems: first-touch initialisation inside OpenMP parallel loops in HPC code; avoid `calloc` on huge arrays. • Web / backend: per-worker caches allocated by the worker itself after pinning.
Watch out: transparent huge pages, memory policies or a later migration can undo the placement. Verify with `numastat` on the target machine.
Seen in HAProxy: 4312f13a2d 2026-07-30 The fdtab moved from a zeroed aligned allocation to `mmap(MAP_ANONYMOUS)` with a page-rounded per-group stride; the boot thread binds to each group's CPUs before writing that group's slice. • 840d0ea428 2026-10-20 The same for `polled_mask`.

### haproxy-143 — Attempt the I/O first; register for readiness only after it would block
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O, libuv, Seastar
Principle: Most reads and writes on a busy non-blocking socket succeed immediately, so perform them right away and fall back to the event notifier only when the call reports it would block.
Why it is faster: The classic reactor asks the poller "is it ready?" and then performs the operation, so every operation costs at least one registration call and one wait round. Trying first removes both in the common case and also cuts latency, since the data leaves in the same loop pass that produced it. Once every layer behaves this way, intermediate "speculative event" caches become dead weight and can be deleted. Sending inline from the producer also recycles buffers while they are still hot instead of letting every stream fill one before anything is flushed.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: in Node/Netty/Tokio-style servers, write to the socket immediately and queue only the unwritten remainder; arm `EPOLLOUT` only on a partial write. • Native / systems: any epoll/kqueue/io_uring reactor: issue the non-blocking call, and subscribe on EAGAIN instead of polling first. • Data / databases: client drivers that attempt a non-blocking read of the reply before parking the connection on the poller. • Embedded: push into the UART/DMA FIFO directly while it has room; enable the TX-empty interrupt only when it fills.
Watch out: speculation pays only when success is likely; trying a read that is almost certain to fail (just after connect, before the request was sent) is a wasted syscall, see the next entries. Changing when events are processed is subtle: an attempt to fuse speculative and polled events (2f877304ef, +1.6% TCP) was reverted (3ef5af3dcc) because it broke SSL.
Seen in HAProxy: de99e99ecf 2007-04-16 "sepoll": speculative epoll, try the syscall and poll only after EAGAIN. About +10%. • cb651251f9 2008-08-29 Newly accepted FDs are tried once speculatively: `process_session` calls per session 6 to 5, `epoll_ctl` calls -10%, overall about +3%. • 573fd806ed 2009-03-22 Re-check only newly added FDs rather than the whole speculative list: up to +6%. • 037d2c1f8f 2012-11-06 Fully event-driven update list in which opposite updates cancel out; e9f49e78fe makes sepoll the default epoll poller. • f817e9f473 2014-01-10 A 3-bit per-direction FD state (active/ready/polled) remembers EAGAIN across enable/disable; 5be2f35231 calls all I/O callbacks from the FD cache. • 305d5ab469 2019-07-24 Once upper layers always try first and subscribe only on EAGAIN, the whole FD cache is removed (-292 lines, "a small performance boost"); 5bee3e2f47 drops the now-redundant `FD_EV_POLLED` bit. • 0eae6323bf 2019-12-20 DNS queries sent synchronously and built once for all resolvers: over 10 s, `epoll_ctl` 577 to 7, `recvfrom` 1533 to 582, `sendto` 369 to 162. • 2c71fe58f0 2022-02-09 The QUIC mux calls the transport's send directly instead of waking another tasklet. • e16762f8a8 2023-11-04 `h2_snd_buf()` calls `h2_send()` inline: 64 conns × 100 streams on one thread went 4.4 to 5.9 Gbps and 10,400 to 22-24 buffers (160 MB saved) at the same syscall count. • 57ec32fb99 2017-04-11 Related ordering: send before receive in the connection handler, so the send buffer is freed and reused sooner (less memory, small speed gain). • Also seen in H2O (h2o-116): c44e43ac2c 2014-12-30 Founding `do_write` calls `writev` at once. A full write goes straight to the pending-callback list, and only the leftover is copied and scheduled for writability. • 81aab86b73 2022-04-07 A non-blocking `connect()` that succeeds immediately is marked `IS_CONNECTING_CONNECTED` and queued directly. That skips the epoll wait and the `getsockopt(SO_ERROR)` check. • 574262b1a3 2023-02-21 After submitting an io_uring command, the completion queue is peeked at once "in hope that we might be able to complete the current one synchronously (as doing so improves locality)". A zero-delay timer is armed only if work remains. • 8268b41d8a 2023-11-01 The neverbleed client writes pending requests directly from the read callback instead of deferring through a timer. • Also seen in libuv (libuv-081): 9539443905 2011-04-26 First Unix buffered-write code: "First try to do the write inline". • 9b49e4c359 2011-06-22 `uv_write` calls `uv__write` at once if the queue is empty; the write watcher starts only if data remains. • b5e7798a89 2013-12-06 / 17d60e3f94 `uv_try_write()`: a synchronous attempt with an on-stack request and no POLLOUT left armed. • 0d43992c3b 2014-06-30 `uv_udp_try_send()`; 41891222bc `uv__udp_send` sends inline when the send queue was empty, instead of starting POLLOUT. • 9b8cef4428 2015-01-11 Windows `uv__tcp_try_write`: a non-overlapped `WSASend` when no writes are pending; c33fe9874a the same for UDP with `WSASendTo`. • 0cbad54f18 2017-04-17 Fix for the fallback: after the inline send, POLLOUT is armed only if the queue is still non-empty, so the EAGAIN path actually gets a notification. • 6999994def 2022-07-07 `uv_shutdown` with an empty write queue feeds the watcher for this iteration instead of arming POLLOUT, "without needing a lot of syscalls and then waiting for the kernel". • Also seen in Seastar (seastar-118): ccb052a418 2014-08-18 "Speculate epoll results": try the operation before waiting in epoll. • 3765bec155 2014-10-14 Speculates EPOLLIN/EPOLLOUT around `sendmsg`/`sendto`/`recvmsg`: UDP memcache memaslap score +30%. • 3f88e9068b 2019-07-25 After a successful `accept()`, speculate EPOLLIN on the listen fd and retry; new `try_accept()` returns `nullopt` on EAGAIN without an exception. Without it a connect storm drains at one accept per task quota. • 5d8d5ae625 2022-10-07 Network read/write moved onto io_uring: httpd/wrk 85,407 → 95,266 req/s, latency 1.49 → 1.35 ms. • fe8035cb7a a9caaf0fb1 2022-10-19 Sockets opened without O_NONBLOCK and read with `recv(2)`, so io_uring retries internally instead of returning EAGAIN.

### haproxy-144 — Keep readiness subscriptions stable: defer, coalesce and cancel interest changes
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy · Also: memcached, libuv, Seastar
Principle: Changing what the kernel watches costs a syscall per change, so avoid toggling interest that will be needed again soon, and compute the net change late so opposite updates cancel.
Why it is faster: Request/response traffic naturally flips read and write interest on every message. Issued eagerly, each flip is an `epoll_ctl`. Leaving interest enabled and filtering spurious events in user space, deferring a disable until the FD actually reports ready while inactive, subscribing to both directions in one update, or using edge-triggered mode turns cost proportional to requests into cost proportional to connections. The trade-off is occasional spurious wakeups, which are cheaper than the syscalls they replace.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: batch kqueue changelists or epoll updates per loop iteration; prefer edge-triggered mode when every handler drains until EAGAIN. • Web / backend: in custom event loops (libuv-style), avoid stop/start reading per message; pause reading only for real backpressure. • Embedded: leave an interrupt enabled and discard spurious events in software instead of masking and unmasking it per transfer. • Data / databases: keep pooled DB connections registered for readability across queries.
Watch out: edge-triggered and lazily disabled interest are only correct if every consumer retries until EAGAIN; a handler that stops early will never be woken again. Leaving receive interest on when the run queue is saturated just collects events you cannot process (see backpressure in the networking section).
Seen in HAProxy: 11f49408f2 2010-11-11 Don't clear already-cleared FDs (about 2 calls per session saved). • dbcd47ea35 2012-05-13 Apply `epoll_ctl` changes, then `epoll_wait`, then speculative events: ends the ADD/DEL/ADD ping-pong. • 3988d9342f 2013-12-27 Keep client-side read polling in keep-alive: 158k to 184k req/s (+16%), and zero `epoll_ctl` during `ab -k` combined with another change. • 796c5b7997 2016-11-29 Don't disable polling on DONT_READ for polled FDs (it caused up to 4 `epoll_ctl` per request): Atom 12,243 to 13,354 req/s at 0 B and 9,640 to 10,992 at 4 kB, "as high as 30%". • eaf05be0ee 2019-12-27 Lazy deregistration: `fd_stop_*` no longer updates polling; an update is queued only if the FD is still inactive after its callback. 100k requests over 200 connections: `epoll_ctl` 44,643 to 620. • 5d7dcc2a8e 2019-12-26 When an update is needed anyway, also enable the direction whose readiness is unknown: 10k keep-alive requests, `epoll_ctl` 20,078 to 66; 53055055c5 tracks per-thread poll state per direction to skip redundant updates. • bc52bec163 2020-06-18 `tune.fd.edge-triggered`: connections registered once both ways with `EPOLLET`, +14% H2-to-H1 request rate, `epoll_ctl` from 2 per request to 2 per connection. • 0d023774bf 2022-07-09 A migrated FD gets a deferred update instead of a synchronous `epoll_ctl(DEL)`: 25-30% fewer `epoll_ctl` with 8 threads and connection reuse. • bca5e14235 2024-09-27 Don't report "more data may follow" after end-of-stream, which made upper layers re-subscribe: `epoll_ctl` back from over 20k to about 3.2k in h2load runs. • Also seen in memcached (memcached-116): 32f382b605 2003-05-27 `update_event()`: `if (c->ev_flags == new_flags) return;` before `event_del`/`event_add`. • 833a7234bb 2023-02-21 Proxy backends split into a persistent `main_event` for reads, a transient `write_event` and an fd-less `timeout_event`, checked with `event_pending`, "to avoid constantly calling epoll_ctl syscalls". • 65e1ac8bfb 2023-06-28 Lazy deregistration: connections waiting on async IO stay registered. `event_del` runs only if the fd wakes early, and the re-add happens only in that case. • Also seen in libuv (libuv-083): 1282d64868 2012-08-23 `uv__io_start` only ORs bits into `w->pevents` and links the watcher onto `loop->watcher_queue`; `uv__io_poll` walks the queue once. On kqueue the changes are passed as the changelist of the blocking `kevent()` call itself, with an early flush only when the 1024-entry array fills. • 6e073ef5da 2023-05-01 Linux: a second 256-entry, non-SQPOLL io_uring holds `IORING_OP_EPOLL_CTL` entries; one `io_uring_enter(n, n, GETEVENTS)` flushes the whole batch. `user_data` packs op, slot and fd so a failed ADD (-EEXIST) is retried as MOD from a saved copy. • 69bad8201b 2024-12-06 The ctl-ring batching was made unconditional, even when the SQPOLL file ring is disabled: "works fine, is a nice optimization". • 5dcef22c62 2024-11-20 `uv__io_check_fd` sends EV_ADD and EV_DELETE in one two-entry `kevent()` call instead of two. • Also seen in Seastar (seastar-130): 96b813fbfc 2014-08-18 "Lazy epoll management" with `events_requested`, `events_epoll` and `events_known`. • 1b6892abf8 2014-09-01 `read()` instead of `recv()` on the epoll path. • 25e2487da5 2023-08-14 `EPOLL_CTL_DEL` once HUP/ERR arrives and no events are requested, stopping a 100% CPU spin.

### haproxy-145 — Treat a short read as "drained" and stop before the call that would fail
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O, memcached, Tokio
Principle: When a non-blocking read returns less than requested, the kernel buffer is almost certainly empty; stop reading instead of issuing a call that will return EAGAIN.
Why it is faster: A naive "read until EAGAIN" loop pays one guaranteed-useless syscall per wakeup, which can be a third of all receive calls. The return size is free information. For messages known to be small (a request), read once and stop; after a large read, consider the batch complete for now. The opposite case matters too: record-oriented transports such as TLS return at most one record per call, so there a short return does not mean drained.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Go/Rust/C network loops: break out on a short read when using level-triggered polling; loop on `SSL_read` while `SSL_pending()`. • Embedded: UART/USB drivers that stop on FIFO underflow instead of polling one more time. • Web / backend: message-queue consumers: a partial batch means "queue empty now", so sleep instead of re-polling.
Watch out: with edge-triggered notification you must read until EAGAIN or you lose the edge. The heuristic is per transport: TCP short read = drained, TLS short read = end of record.
Seen in HAProxy: 9641e8f6ee 2007-03-23 Stop reading after a read under 1460 bytes (one MSS). • ab3e1d313c 2007-06-03 Compare the `recv`/`send` return with the requested size: about 23% fewer syscalls. • af78d0fdb6 2009-01-08 Don't poll after EAGAIN if enough was already read; 6f4a82c7af stop after a large read: all splice EAGAINs gone. • 1b194fe03e 2009-03-21 `BF_READ_DONTWAIT` single read: about +10% on redirect/monitor workloads. • 5fddab0a56 2012-11-09 Disable reading after the first `recv` of a request: EAGAIN removed on 30% of recvs; d81ca04051 set DONTWAIT at connect, so no failed `recvfrom` in keep-alive. • 6c11bd2f89 2014-01-24 Mark the FD drained after a short read when polled: keep-alive +5%, and the 1.6% of failing `accept()` calls disappear. • b22fc30aaa 2015-12-14 Exposes the threshold as `tune.recv_enough` (default 10136 = 7 × 1448). • 4d7a884827 2019-07-31 Stop the H2 receive loop after getting data: 50% of H2 `recvfrom` calls had returned EAGAIN. • 6e59cb5db1 2020-02-20 `CO_RFL_READ_ONCE` while waiting for the first H1 message: `recvfrom` per request 5.0 to 4.0 (-20%). • 7784f1739c 2017-07-11 Counter-case: TLS reads no longer stop on a short return (a record boundary), which had woken the H2 parser separately for 9-byte frame headers and 16 kB payloads; 8de70bcb54 the same on send, which stopped early after 16 kB with 64 kB buffers. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-117): d04d495531 2014-10-15 `write_core` no longer retries `writev` after a partial write. • 0fd21a0f83 2014-11-13 `read(2)` is called again only if the previous read filled the whole buffer (`if (buf.len != rret) break;`). • 4331c4f95a 2021-07-16 The QUIC receive path loops again only if the last `recvmmsg` batch came back full. • 5f9bc0f62c 2022-04-25 The MSG_ZEROCOPY error queue is drained with a 2-slot `recvmmsg` instead of a looping `recvmsg`. A partial result proves the queue is empty. • Also seen in memcached (memcached-115): 2a733a1de7 2008-02-11 "Break out of the read-loop when read returns with available space": `if (res == avail) continue; else break;` • Also seen in Tokio (tokio-094): 28ce4eeab2 2022-07-26 `PollEvented::poll_read`: `if n > 0 && n < len { clear_readiness }`, which "seems to significantly improve performance under certain conditions". • c2612b446f 2022-09-02 Applied the same rule to partial writes. • b2e4c5fc9e 2024-07-02 Restricted it to an explicit list of epoll/kqueue platforms, because level-triggered selectors break the assumption. • 8307a06b5a 2026-11-08 Scoped it per resource: sockets keep the heuristic, while `unix::pipe::Receiver` and `ChildStdout` use `poll_read_pipe` with `short_read_means_drained = false`, because packet-mode (O_DIRECT) pipes return one packet per read.

### haproxy-146 — Use protocol state to skip I/O attempts and wakeups that cannot succeed
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy · Also: Netty
Principle: Before calling into the transport, check cheap state that already predicts the outcome: still connecting, nothing sent yet, a complete message just read, nothing queued, already subscribed.
Why it is faster: Each predictably useless attempt costs a syscall, a scheduler round trip, or both. A "subscribe" primitive that schedules the callback immediately when the resource is already ready, and arms polling otherwise, lets callers stop waking themselves "just in case". This removes failed reads and duplicate callback invocations in one move.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: HTTP clients that don't read before the request is written; promise APIs that resolve synchronously when the value is ready. • Data / databases: async drivers that skip arming a read until a query is in flight. • Embedded: skip the RX check when the FIFO-empty flag is set; don't kick a DMA channel with an empty descriptor list.
Watch out: a wrong prediction means a missed event and a stuck connection; an OPTIM that tightened H2 wake conditions was reverted for that reason (9fbc01710a reverted by 1eb31d30fe). Make the "skip" condition conservative.
Seen in HAProxy: 0f8d3ab362 2018-10-25 `process_stream` no longer tries `send()` first, since pending data was almost certainly not unblocked since the last poll; 31f04e4416 skip send/recv if already subscribed or the output is empty. • 908d26fd03 2018-10-25, 33a09a5f2a, 0dfccb20f5 Remove redundant sends and tasklet wakeups. • 47e9a1ad4e 2018-11-07 No receive attempt until the connection is established; 8dbd1a2e09 `fd_cant_recv()` on connect removed 100k failed `recvfrom` out of 300k for 100k forwarded connections. • dedd30610b 2019-07-26 Skip the wakeup and subscription after reading a complete request. • 7e59c0a5e1 2020-02-28 `conn_subscribe()` wakes immediately when already ready: in close mode, `epoll_ctl` 399,464 to 205,739 and failed `recvfrom` 100,000 to 2,651. • 493d9dc6ba 2020-02-28, 2c1f37d353 (OPTIM) H1 mux converts blind `tasklet_wakeup()` calls to subscribe: wakeups per request 3 to 1, `raw_sock_to_buf` 4 to 2, `si_cs_process` 3 to 1. • 2a761dcf0d 2018-02-26 H2 calls the data layer once per demux pass instead of once per frame: POST 58k to 60k req/s, half the `si_cs_recv_cb()` calls. • c09ef0c5fc 2022-08-08 Skip QUIC sending when no frames are queued; d1b573059a avoid `h1_send()` when no error message was produced. • 34b31d85cb 2024-04-10 `qel_need_sending()` gates `qc_send()`; c6e3d60fc1 fully-sent encryption levels leave the send list. • Also seen in Netty (netty-148): ea25b511f3 2024-12-11 io_uring `IORING_RECVSEND_POLL_FIRST` when the last read drained the socket; 1b19e4f7d8 `ACCEPT_POLL_FIRST` for accepts. • b0323b83bb 2024-12-23 uses `CQE_F_SOCK_NONEMPTY` and `ACCEPT_DONTWAIT` to know whether more data or connections are waiting.

### haproxy-147 — Use what the kernel already told you instead of asking again
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy · Also: libuv
Principle: Read the extra information carried by events and flag-combined calls (hang-up bits, byte counts, addresses from accept, atomic socket flags) and skip the follow-up calls that would only rediscover it.
Why it is faster: Many syscalls exist only to learn a fact the kernel had at hand a moment earlier: a `read()` that returns 0 after a peer close, an `fcntl` to set non-blocking, `getpeername` after `accept`. Each saved call is a full kernel round trip, typically paid once per connection or per request. The same idea applies to costly lookups: try the cheap local interpretation before the expensive remote one.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `SOCK_NONBLOCK|SOCK_CLOEXEC` on `socket`/`accept4`; `EPOLLRDHUP`; `d_type` from `readdir` instead of a `stat` per entry. • Web / backend: check whether a string is a numeric address before calling `getaddrinfo`; read peer info from the accept result. • Data / databases: use the row count returned by a write instead of a follow-up `SELECT COUNT`.
Watch out: some hints are kernel-version or platform dependent (the splice fallback had to stay for buggy kernels); gate them on detection rather than assuming.
Seen in HAProxy: 2c7ace07ad 2010-03-16 Health checks keep reading until EOF in the same wakeup, detecting close without another poll round; 7340ca5a54 no `shutdown()` on a socket already in error. • 82a04566ec 2011-12-11, b6daedd46c Stop the `recv()` fallback after splice EAGAIN on fixed kernels: +1%. • 1bc4aab290 2012-10-08 `accept4(SOCK_NONBLOCK)` saves an `fcntl` per connection: about +1%. • 1c07b0755d 2013-01-07 `EPOLLRDHUP` saves the `read()==0` on server close; baf2a500a1 uses it to avoid `splice()==0`. • 38d5892634 2013-11-16 The source address returned by `accept()` is marked known, removing `getpeername()`. • 2d351b68c2 2013-12-05, 06559ac1fb No double `SO_LINGER`, no useless `recv` on TCP checks; 7bb21532f4 no second `connect()` probe on unix sockets. • 3c8a89642d 2017-03-13 (OPTIM) `POLLRDHUP` with `poll()` avoids "a useless recv() after short responses on keep-alive connections"; fa0617660a kqueue sets readable only if `kev.data != 0`; 19c4ab97c1 maps `EV_EOF` to HUP/ERR. • 34a3eb4c42 2021-11-05 The client's destination address is fetched only when needed, usually saving a `getsockname()`. • 8b950f40fa 2024-02-20 Connected UDP sockets use `sendmsg()` with a NULL address, avoiding a kernel address lookup. • 35560e3ad3 2026-07-22 IP pattern parsing tries the literal IPv6 parsers before DNS: a 20k-entry IPv6 ACL check went from 14 s to about 1 s (two failed DNS round trips per entry). • Also seen in libuv (libuv-091): 5816f2d21c 2011-10-07 `uv__read` returns when `nread < buflen` instead of reading again into EAGAIN. • 442d11d618 2013-05-25 With POLLHUP, a partial last read and reading active, `uv__stream_io` reports EOF directly ("avoid extra read, short-circuit on POLLHUP"). • 2765509430 2011-07-18 Windows pipes ask `PeekNamedPipe` for available bytes and call `alloc_cb(handle, avail)`; previously the pipe was switched NOWAIT/WAIT around every read with `SetNamedPipeHandleState`, two extra syscalls and spurious 0-byte reads.

### haproxy-148 — Move more data per syscall: scatter-gather, segmentation offload, batched receives
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When per-call overhead dominates, hand the kernel several buffers or many packets in one call instead of one call per piece.
Why it is faster: Syscall cost is mostly fixed per call, so small messages are syscall-bound. Scatter-gather avoids both a copy into a contiguous buffer and a second call. UDP GSO lets the kernel or NIC split one large buffer into MTU-sized datagrams. Receiving several datagrams or frames per wakeup amortizes scheduler and cache-refill costs. Cap the batch so latency stays bounded.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `writev` header + body; Node `socket.cork()`/`uncork()`; Netty `maxMessagesPerRead`. • Native / systems: `sendmmsg`/`recvmmsg` and `UDP_SEGMENT` for DNS/QUIC servers; `close_range()` in process spawners. • Embedded: DMA scatter lists; TSO/LRO offload in network drivers. • Data / databases: vectored WAL writes; batched network round trips for result sets.
Watch out: offloads are not available everywhere; detect failure at runtime and fall back per destination. Bigger batches trade latency for throughput, so measure tail latency when choosing the cap.
Seen in HAProxy: 609ac2ab6c 2015-09-16 Log sending moves from `sendto` to `sendmsg` with an iovec; 59cee973cd header and message as two vectors; 5b78d9b437 the trailing newline as its own iovec so the message is never modified. • 9188ac60eb 2019-02-21 `my_closefrom()` asks `poll()` which of 1024 FDs are valid (POLLNVAL) and closes only those: about 2x faster than blind `close()`. • 1b0707f3e7 2022-06-30 The QUIC datagram handler loops on `recvfrom` up to `maxpollevents` datagrams per wakeup; 649b3fd9aa RX buffer raised to 64 kB to hold them. • 35470d5185 2024-05-30, af22792a43 UDP GSO: QUIC datagrams encoded back-to-back and sent with one GSO size; d0ea173e35 runtime fallback (EIO) remembered per listener as `LI_F_UDP_GSO_NOTSUPP`. • 04ce6536e1 2024-10-12 H2 receives and demuxes up to 12 extra rounds before waking stream handlers: 549 to 660 MB/s (+20%), `h2_io_cb` calls 2.6M to 300k, instructions -17%, L2 misses -31%. 16 rounds was slightly faster but raised peak latency 33%.

### haproxy-149 — Forward bulk payloads without copying them through intermediate buffers
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy
Principle: For data that is only relayed, move it kernel-to-kernel (splice, tee, sendfile) or let the producer write straight into the consumer's output buffer, and keep the generic buffered path only for small or inspected data.
Why it is faster: A relay that reads into user space and writes back pays two copies and pollutes caches with bytes it never looks at. Kernel splicing keeps them in kernel pages; direct producer-to-consumer writes ("fast-forwarding") skip the middle buffer and its wakeups. Pipes and setup have fixed costs, so apply size thresholds, pool the pipes, and drain them promptly to bound in-flight kernel memory.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `sendfile` for static files; `stream.pipeline` instead of buffering whole bodies; Java `FileChannel.transferTo`. • Native / systems: `splice`/`vmsplice`/`tee` in proxies; io_uring buffers handed straight to the egress socket. • Data / databases: zero-copy fetch paths (Kafka-style) from page cache to socket. • Embedded: DMA directly from a peripheral into the network TX descriptor.
Watch out: below a few kilobytes the pipe setup costs more than the copy; data that must be inspected or transformed (compression, filters) cannot take this path, and pinned kernel pipe memory needs a cap.
Seen in HAProxy: 982b6e37e4 2009-01-25, 3eba98aa57 Pre-created pipe pools for splicing; 686ac828fa maxpipes defaults to maxconn/4; bd9a0a7781 `tune.pipesize`. • 14acc7072e 2011-05-11 No splice below 4 kB: syscalls 6826 to 5793 on chunked data. • eb9fd5178e 2011-12-11, 56efc4896b Send spliced data immediately (fewer pipes in use) and don't retry when the output is full; fa8e2bc68c splice the last small block if a pipe is already in use. • 160287b676 2020-01-29 Per-thread pipe cache (capped at maxpipes/nbthreads) removes a 5-6% locking cost at high bitrates. • 242c6f0ded 2023-08-03 Mux-to-mux fast-forwarding callbacks (`init_fastfwd`/`fastfwd`/`done_fastfwd`), implemented for H2 11c05c516a, H1 2d80eb5b7a, PT ec22d3102d and QUIC 1bcc0f8892; 4be0c7c655 loops until a full buffer is forwarded ("significant improvement" with H2 clients). • 39b6f5b04c 2024-01-23 Applets produce directly into the peer mux; the cache (c061ba30f7) and stats (868205943c) are served zero-copy; 91b77c1632 chunked output of unknown size reserves room and patches the chunk size afterwards. • ecf36f2ca8 2026-04-02, f349d0b113 haterm: zero-copy forwarding plus `tee()` from a pre-filled master pipe for repeated payloads.

### haproxy-150 — Size each read to what the next layer will consume in one step
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Ask the kernel for exactly the amount the consumer can take at once, so the buffer ends empty (swappable, releasable) instead of leaving a remainder that forces extra small reads and copies.
Why it is faster: Filling an input buffer to raw capacity can force the next layer to split its transfer in two, with an unaligned copy and an extra syscall for the leftover. Under-reporting free space by the downstream overhead, or capping the first read at what the parser will accept, keeps transfers one-shot. When very little data is buffered, moving it to the front before reading is cheaper than two reads.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: wire protocols with a length header: read the header, then exactly the body into one allocation. • Embedded: size UART/DMA receive transfers to frame boundaries. • Native / systems: cap reads at MTU minus header when each read maps to one outgoing packet.
Watch out: under-sized reads on a bulk stream mean more syscalls; this pays off when the boundary is known and the remainder would otherwise cost a copy.
Seen in HAProxy: 3906e22f6f 2018-12-05 `buf_room_for_htx_data()` reports room minus the HTX header and two block headers; 45f2b89156 using it for the H1 demux buffer: up to +33% on large objects "due to less recv() calls and unaligned copies". • 29a22bc0a7 2018-12-04 Realign a nearly empty input buffer before `rcv_buf`, "cheaper … than to do 2 calls to recv()". • 88718955f4 2023-03-17 `h1_recv()` limits the first header read to `bufsize - maxrewrite`: strace shows 3 reads (16320+16+182) replaced by 2 (15360+1158), and the first buffer can be released.

### haproxy-151 — Make frequent kernel-provided values cheap: read the clock once, use the cheapest entry path
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy · Also: Aeron, curl
Principle: Calls made on every loop or request (clock reads, hot syscalls) should go through the cheapest available path and be taken once per scheduling pass, not once per use.
Why it is faster: Time reads may be real syscalls depending on clock source and platform, and repeated reads in one task also give inconsistent values. Taking one timestamp per pass, using the monotonic clock directly instead of maintaining a corrected wall clock, and using vsyscall/vDSO paths instead of slow trap entries removes per-use kernel cost.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: capture request start time once in middleware; cache `Date.now()` per event-loop tick for HTTP Date headers and logs. • Native / systems: `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_COARSE` or vDSO clocks for timeouts; one timestamp per epoll iteration. • Embedded: read the RTC once per tick and derive the rest from a systick counter.
Watch out: coarse clocks have millisecond-level resolution; anything measuring short intervals (pacing, latency profiling) needs a precise read, ideally taken once and shared.
Seen in HAProxy: 48d84c10b5 2010-11-14 `USE_VSYSCALLS` bypasses libc's `int 0x80` path for hot syscalls on i386: about +10%. • 5b88da269c 2012-06-18 Kernel-Mode-Linux support: syscall overhead divided by 4 on a Geode LX, about +1% overall. • 07cbd8e074 2022-11-25 `now_mono_time_fast()` on `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_COARSE`; df188f145b the costly CPU-time read is refreshed only when the coarse clock moves. • 499e057644 2024-09-08 Use `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` directly: saves "one gettimeofday() call per loop and two timeval comparisons" (with 4150851ce5, 24496803d1). • c5052bad8a 2024-11-19 `TASK_F_WANTS_TIME`: the scheduler takes one timestamp before running tasks that ask for it; 22bd92a87f QUIC pacing uses it instead of `now_cpu_time()`. • Also seen in Aeron (aeron-130): c07ca64713 2019-06-11 Linux nano clock switched from `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` to `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`. • 45e1ae719a 2019-06-12 Epoch clock uses `CLOCK_REALTIME_COARSE` where available. • Also seen in curl (curl-129): 61022f3817 2007-04-20 `Curl_socket_ready`, `Curl_poll` and `Curl_select` skip `gettimeofday()` when the timeout is 0 or infinite. 3616912d22 (2007-04-22) removed another call when custom timeouts are used. • 51d4885ca0 2011-12-20 `Curl_pgrsTime` stores one `Curl_tvnow()` in a local, replacing up to six calls. • 2f1e94eaed 2026-06-25 The reuse-match and reaper contexts carry `now`, so the clock is read once per connection search. • 3ddbd522b9 2026-09-10 "pass more `struct curltime` pointers around", including the DNS cache prune. fd33638fe2 (2026-09-30) replaced a multi-level cached `now` with explicit reads at the points that need them.

### haproxy-152 — One event-notification instance per thread
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Give each worker thread its own poller set containing only the descriptors it owns, instead of one shared set that reports every event to every thread.
Why it is faster: With a shared epoll set, threads wake for events they will only discard (thundering herd) and contend on the same kernel structures. Per-thread sets with per-FD "polled by" bookkeeping deliver each event once, to the thread that will handle it.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: one epoll/io_uring ring per core with `SO_REUSEPORT` listeners. • Web / backend: one selector per thread in Java NIO servers; one event loop per worker in Node cluster mode.
Watch out: descriptors that migrate between threads need explicit hand-over bookkeeping, otherwise events are lost or delivered twice.
Seen in HAProxy: d9e7e36c6e 2018-01-18 `epoll_fd[tid]` driven by a per-FD `polled_mask`: a listener bound to one of 4 threads went from 22,000 cps at 380% CPU to 43,000 cps at 100% CPU, and `poll_skip` from tens of millions to 0. The before/after numbers came from the activity counters added in d80cb4ee13.

### haproxy-153 — Optional busy polling to trade CPU for wake-up latency
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Instead of sleeping in the poller, spin on a zero-timeout poll so the core never enters deep idle states or gets descheduled, and make that spin loop as cheap as possible.
Why it is faster: Waking from a deep C-state, or from a hypervisor deschedule, adds tens to hundreds of microseconds. Spinning removes that latency at the cost of a fully used core. The spin loop itself must avoid shared writes, or the busy threads saturate a shared cache line.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `SO_BUSY_POLL`, DPDK poll-mode drivers for trading or packet processing. • Embedded: polling-mode drivers when interrupt latency exceeds the deadline.
