Watch out: burns a full core per thread and power; only for latency-critical deployments with dedicated CPUs. Never enable it on oversubscribed machines.
Seen in HAProxy: beb859abce 2018-11-22 `busy-polling` (epoll and kqueue): single-connection forwarding 4640 to 8540 cps (about 98 µs less latency per connection), redirects 12,500 to 21,250. • 58b73f9fa8 2022-09-21 Inside the busy-poll loop only the thread-local date is updated, the global one on exit (a700420671): 53M to 352M loops/s; perf had shown 67% of time in `clock_update_global_date()`.

### haproxy-154 — Pre-grow kernel-side tables at startup
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy
Principle: If the kernel grows a per-process structure on demand while holding a lock, force it to its final size during initialization.
Why it is faster: Linux doubles the FD table as descriptors are allocated, and the resize can pause all threads. Touching the highest descriptor once at boot moves that cost out of the traffic path.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `mlock`/prefault memory in real-time services; preallocate files with `fallocate`. • Data / databases: preallocate WAL segments and data files before accepting writes.
Watch out: costs kernel memory for the maximum size even if it is never used.
Seen in HAProxy: 425d7ad89d 2023-05-23 `prealloc-fd`: `dup2(0, rlim_cur-1)` then `close()` in `main()`, avoiding pauses of up to 47 ms during multi-threaded FD table expansion.

### haproxy-155 — Tell the OS your access pattern for sequential file processing
Lens: io · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Declare that a file will be read sequentially and in full so the kernel enlarges read-ahead.
Why it is faster: Default read-ahead is tuned for mixed access; with a cold page cache, larger read-ahead turns many small device reads into few large ones.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: full-scan and ETL readers; `madvise(MADV_SEQUENTIAL)` on mmapped files. • Native / systems: log processors and backup tools.
Watch out: no gain on a warm cache, and the wrong hint (sequential on random access) wastes I/O bandwidth.
Seen in HAProxy: f8c95d2a25 2012-06-12 halog calls `posix_fadvise(SEQUENTIAL)`: about +20% on a cold cache (SSD), no effect when hot.

### haproxy-156 — Reuse established connections, and manage the idle pool by demand and recency
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Keep idle upstream connections and hand them to later requests, sizing and evicting the idle pool from measured demand and recency rather than fixed rules.
Why it is faster: A new connection costs a TCP handshake round trip, often a TLS handshake (more round trips plus asymmetric crypto), slow-start ramp-up, and allocation. Reuse removes all of it. Pool policy then decides the hit rate: purge the least recently used connections, keep about as many as recent peak demand needs, kill excess gradually, and when threads may borrow from each other, allow it only above a low-water mark so borrowing does not become a storm.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: HTTP keep-alive agents (`keepAlive: true` in Node), gRPC channel reuse, LRU idle reaping. • Data / databases: connection pools with min-idle from recent peak, LIFO reuse and FIFO reaping (HikariCP, PgBouncer). • Embedded: gateways that cache upstream sockets and evict the coldest under memory pressure.
Watch out: reuse across clients must respect request semantics (a non-idempotent first request on a reused connection may fail on a server-side close), and cross-thread borrowing moves cache lines and kernel FD locks; let operators keep pools thread-local.
Seen in HAProxy: c12b5e663d 2015-08-04 Skip reallocation when a valid connection is reused; 449d74a906 "aggressive" reuse promotes a connection to the safe list after a second request; efb90f9dd3 swap connections between streams to raise reuse odds. • 00cf70f28b 2018-11-30 A session keeps up to 5 outgoing connections (for H2 multiplexing); 0c18a6fe34 idle timeout keeps unused ones for reuse; 86006a561e `http-reuse safe` becomes the default; a2dbeb22fc, c685d700fd smarter idle accounting and swapping. • 566df309c6 2020-03-06 Threads may take idle connections from other threads; 079cb9af22 kill half of the excess, not half of all idle connections; bdb86bdaab keep an estimate of needed connections (takeover rate roughly halved). • 2f3f4d3441 2020-07-01 `pool-low-conn`: steal only above a low-water mark. 10k connections, 16 threads, 210 servers: 314k to 352k req/s (2.1.7 had 185k), takeovers 300k/s to 13k/s. • 5afcb686b9 2023-08-21 A per-thread recency list; periodic purge removes from the least recently used end, which especially helps SNI-differentiated pools. • a826250659 2025-02-25 When pool sharing is disabled, don't kill other threads' idle connections: a 9% CPU trylock disappears, +12% on 128 threads; 8de8ed4f48 cross-group takeover is off by default.

### haproxy-157 — Tell the transport more data follows so writes coalesce into full segments
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When a write is known to be followed by another, mark it "more coming" so the kernel merges them into full-size segments instead of pushing each one out.
Why it is faster: Each partial write sent immediately becomes its own packet, with its own header, interrupt, ACK and, under TLS, an odd-sized record. A "more" hint on every chunk except the last lets the stack fill MSS-sized segments, cutting packets, ACKs and per-packet CPU on both ends. The hint must be propagated through every layer (mux, TLS, raw socket) or it is lost.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `TCP_CORK` or `MSG_MORE` when writing headers then body; Node `cork()`/`uncork()`. • Data / databases: client pipelines (Redis, Postgres extended protocol) that cork batched commands. • Embedded: user-space Nagle-like buffering in lightweight TCP stacks.
Watch out: forgetting to clear the hint on the last chunk leaves it waiting in the kernel; for interactive traffic, latency matters more than packet count.
Seen in HAProxy: face839296 2010-01-03 `MSG_MORE` on a response when a pipelined request is pending, so many 304s leave together. • 57f5c12c04 2010-12-02 No separate packet per chunk in chunked transfers, which also cuts client ACKs; 22be90b8db don't fast-forward partial buffers (avoids doubled `send()`); 0729303fb0 wait for more data until the last chunk in non-interactive mode. • c89a42feba 2020-06-19 H1 sets `MSG_MORE` whenever it knows data remains, not only when the upper layer said so; 8945bb6c05 fixes the flag being lost in forwarding. • 464fa06e9a 2023-03-17 The H2 mux sets it on every ring buffer except the last; ac78c4fd9d TLS carries it down to the raw socket: H2-over-TLS bandwidth +5-10% with multiple streams.

### haproxy-158 — Send optimistically instead of waiting for the peer, and piggyback setup on real traffic
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Do not hold the first request until the peer's handshake or settings arrive unless a feature truly depends on them; bundle preface, settings and first request into one flight.
Why it is faster: Each "wait for the peer" step costs a full round trip plus a wakeup and a separate segment. Sending optimistically also keeps 0-RTT and TCP Fast Open useful. Extra control frames that are sent "just in case" at setup cost bytes on every connection, including those that never need them; deferring them onto the first frame that must go out anyway is free.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: HTTP/2 and gRPC clients that coalesce the preface with the first HEADERS; TLS False Start and 0-RTT for idempotent requests. • Data / databases: pipelining the startup/auth exchange with the first query. • Embedded: MQTT/CoAP clients sending CONNECT and the first PUBLISH back to back.
Watch out: only safe where the protocol defines defaults the peer must accept before its settings arrive; features negotiated by the peer (extended CONNECT) still require waiting, and 0-RTT data must be replay-safe.
Seen in HAProxy: d8b2bb5c05 2014-01-28 The TLS handshake output buffer grows from 4 kB to 16 kB, so long certificate chains go in fewer round trips: handshake time cut by 3x with initcwnd 10. • 97aaa67658 2018-12-23 No early H2 WINDOW_UPDATE at connection setup: the large window is announced with the first update actually needed, saving bytes on connections that never upload. • b0e8edaef2 2025-10-29 The H2 backend may send before receiving the server's SETTINGS (waiting only for extended CONNECT/upgrade): one fewer tasklet wakeup per connection, about +5% connection rate. • a1f26ca307 2025-10-29 `h2_snd_buf()` emits the preface itself, so preface, settings and request share one segment: -15% context switches, `process_stream` calls per connection 3 to 2, +9.5% connection rate; +15.7% cumulative, and 0-RTT now works.

### haproxy-159 — Size flow-control windows and receive buffers to the bandwidth-delay product
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O, NATS, curl
Principle: A window smaller than bandwidth × RTT caps throughput at window/RTT regardless of link speed, so size it from the path, and share receive buffers elastically among the streams that actually need them.
Why it is faster: Protocol defaults (64 kB for HTTP/2) are tuned for memory, not for long fat paths. A fixed per-stream buffer stalls uploads on high-RTT links; a connection-level pool of buffers shared among active streams gives a single fast stream many buffers and still divides them fairly when many streams compete. Window values that match common frame sizes avoid trailing one-byte frames.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: gRPC/HTTP2 BDP-based window autotuning; raise `initialWindowSize` for bulk uploads. • Data / databases: replication streams and fetch sizes sized to RTT × bandwidth. • Native / systems: TCP receive autotuning and window scaling; credit pools in RDMA.
Watch out: large windows let one peer pin a lot of memory; round per-stream shares down on the backend side for fairness and bound the total per connection.
Seen in HAProxy: dc572364c6 2018-12-12 H2 connection window raised from 65,535 to about 2 GB after reports it "seriously limits the upload bitrate". • 0012f899dd 2022-06-04 (OPTIM) Default stream window 65,535 to 65,536 so curl uploads use only full 16 kB DATA frames. • 4eb3ff1d3b 2024-10-02 H2 streams use connection-owned buffers; 3816c38601 several per stream (iocb wakeups 208k to 128k); e018d9a0cf shared smartly: one stream reached 121 Mbps at 103 ms RTT instead of about 5.1 Mbps, and a fast client's download-time stddev fell from 5.81 s to 0.336 s; 0fd66703c2 advertises a small 16 kB base window. • a4f31ffeeb 2025-02-24, 60f64449fb QUIC streams get many receive buffers (in a tree, aligned on bufsize); dc7913d814 max-stream-data factor 90; 14a3fb679f MAX_STREAM_DATA sent per consumed buffer, so clients send fewer STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frames. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-136): 840e00ed29 2015-11-08 HTTP/2 `SETTINGS_INITIAL_WINDOW_SIZE` raised from 256 KB to 16 MB. • c9367936e3 2017-12-21 The connection-level window matches the advertised 16 MB. fb2f00acb8 2017-12-21 raises a stream's window from 65535 to 16 MB once its body is streamed, and 0f46059949 2017-12-22 makes that configurable. • f810eb5a00 2020-08-24 HTTP/3 request streams start at 32 KB and are promoted to `active_stream_window_size` when unblocked ("limit POST concurrency"). • 1db6ab578b 2021-06-17 CONNECT tunnel streams get the full active window. • 9f3f6ff689 2023-12-01 Per-connection `max_frame_payload_size` and `h2o_http3_calc_min_flow_control_size()` replace fixed constants. 7ac4d47a2d 2026-10-16 sizes client uni-stream credit to that minimum instead of quicly's 1 MiB default. • Also seen in NATS (nats-180): 0a206b4c64 2020-05-29 Stream snapshot download: 16 MB window, ack reply carries chunk size, buffered writer with gzip `BestSpeed`; 8e407f8db4 2020-05-30 moves the checksum pass to its own goroutine. • 0f548edcc6 2021-03-21 Consumer flow-control max pending 4 MB to 1 MB. • eb16c35016 2022-03-30 1 MB to 32 MB with a larger slow-start fraction: ordered consumers "very conservative with slow start", improving long-RTT throughput; 656a9534a5 2022-04-18 restores 32 MB after a dip to 16 MB. • 52200ab8bb 2022-03-31 Snapshot chunks 256 KB to 128 KB and window 32 MB to 8 MB where memory mattered more. • a2c1bdb137 2024-02-29 Snapshot ack channel sized to window divided by chunk size, clamped to [8, 8192], so inserts never block. • Also seen in curl (curl-145): a4d888857e 2016-11-16 HTTP/2 stream and connection windows set to 1 GB (`1 << 30`), also on the Upgrade path, which had sent no settings before. • 15f51474c8 2020-02-27 Window set to 32 MB. 0690b3330c (2019-08-28) sized the QUIC stream window to the buffer, and 7627a2dd9d extends it as data is consumed. • f15beb6f30 2022-04-10 QUIC send size raised from 20 KiB to 256 KiB, which had been "too small for the high latency environment". • 1886eef7fa 2023-05-20 HTTP/2 stream window raised from 512 KiB to 10 MiB. de4b0164f2 (2023-04-27) raised the connection window to 100× the stream window, which "fixes stalled connections". • eb0a366b70 2024-08-01 Starts at a 64 KB stream window and raises it to 10 MB on the first server reply, unless a rate limit applies. b34b757c2e (2024-10-24) set the ngtcp2 maximum window to 10× its initial value. • 0f7b182f2f 2025-12-19 After a redesign's windows were "crippling transfer rates", ngtcp2 auto-tuning was disabled and the window opened to the maximum at once when there is no rate limit.

### haproxy-160 — Keep about one bandwidth-delay product in flight on the send side
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O
Principle: Allow enough unacknowledged data to fill the path, but no more: tie send buffering to the congestion window and cap unsent kernel data near it.
Why it is faster: Too little in flight caps throughput at buffer/RTT; too much wastes memory and adds queueing latency for multiplexed streams that share the connection. Multi-buffer send queues and send-until-blocked loops keep the pipe full; cwnd-based allocation and `TCP_NOTSENT_LOWAT` bound the excess. Small frames go into small buffers so they don't consume a full slot of the budget.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: HTTP/2 servers setting `TCP_NOTSENT_LOWAT` (as nginx/h2o do) so prioritization still works; pipelining in HTTP/Redis clients. • Native / systems: QUIC stacks sizing send queues from cwnd; adaptive socket send buffers. • Data / databases: Kafka producer linger/batch sizes tuned to the link.
Watch out: cwnd changes quickly; allocation tied to it must shrink as well as grow, and low-water marks too close to zero cause extra wakeups.
Seen in HAProxy: a456920491 2022-04-15 A list of send buffers per QUIC stream, to "transfer more data without having to wait ACKs"; dc07751ed7 `qc_send_app_pkts()` loops until no room is left. • aeb8c1ddc3 2024-06-13 QUIC Tx buffers allocated against the congestion window (`buf_in_flight`); d0d8e57d47, 885e4c5cf8 a small-buffer pool; b355e89bf9 H3 HEADERS frames use small buffers. • ab37b86921 2024-06-05 Packets fill the whole Tx buffer down to header room instead of stopping below one MTU. • 2cdb3cb91e 2025-04-29 `TCP_NOTSENT_LOWAT` = `tune.bufsize`: on a 100 ms network, 10x less socket-buffer memory with no throughput loss. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-126): 66a01cc4fb 2016-05-16 "latency-optimized write mode for long latency connections": `obtain_tcp_info` (Linux `TCP_INFO`, macOS `TCP_CONNECTION_INFO`), `adjust_notsent_lowat`, `prepare_for_latency_optimized_write`. d7a94f82e7 2016-05-16 caps the HTTP/2 frame buffer to that size. • 563323026d 2016-05-16 Poll-then-write: `on_write_complete` waits for writability before generating the next frames, so they are built from fresh priority state. 691a68e477 2016-05-23 makes this unconditional on the evloop backend whenever data is buffered. • e4f89d4dd7 2016-05-16 Read-before-write in `run_socket`: WINDOW_UPDATE, PRIORITY or RST_STREAM that arrive in the same iteration affect what gets written. • e98dfb8a83 2016-06-16 Slack raised from +1 to +2 packets. 67699071ce 2016-06-15 resets the low-water mark once cwnd grows past the threshold. • c534b87155 2016-05-31 An 8-slot sliding average of loop time, and 693d4d9759 2016-06-08 disables the mode when `loop_time / RTT` is too high. • 3a83fba801 2016-09-13 Minimum RTT of 50 ms by default, and d225eefa39 2016-09-13 turns the feature on by default.

### haproxy-161 — Backpressure with hysteresis: stop producers early, resume them late
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When the output side is congested, stop reading and stop waking producers, and resume only after the backlog has drained well below the limit, not at the first free byte.
Why it is faster: Without backpressure, input accumulates in memory and every free byte triggers a stampede of wakeups that each copy a few bytes. A high/low watermark gap turns many tiny transfers into a few large ones; capping buffered data at what the active streams can use keeps memory and cache footprint proportional to real demand.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Node stream `highWaterMark`/`drain` with a real low mark; Reactive Streams `request(n)`. • Data / databases: pause Kafka partitions under load; resume consumers at 50% queue depth. • Embedded: XON/XOFF or RTS/CTS with separate assert and release thresholds.
Watch out: a gap that is too wide adds latency and can starve slow streams; blocking reads on a connection must never block the response that would unblock it (deadlock).
Seen in HAProxy: d846c267d5 2018-10-19 H2 `sending_list`: woken streams are put back to wait as soon as the mux buffer fills. • cb55f485da 2018-12-10 `H1C_F_IN_BUSY` blocks reads while a request is processed, fixing a CPU-burning loop on pipelined requests. • f210191dcd 2018-07-19 Cap conn_streams per H2 connection: "slightly faster now … probably due to the smoother memory usage". • 6f95f6e111 2020-03-04 (OPTIM) Stop receive polling only when the run queue exceeds 16 × `runqueue-depth`; full performance even with maxpollevents=20k. • 93c5511af8 2023-03-16 H2 marks itself decongested only when the ring is back to one buffer: wakeups had grown 5-50x and large-object throughput dropped 2-2.5x without it. • 0a7ab7067f 2023-11-09 Block H2 producers when buffers exceed the number of streams: 10k single-stream connections went from about 180k to 20k buffers with better bandwidth; d656ac7e13 same cap for zero-copy (16 streams use half the memory).

### haproxy-162 — Piggyback and coalesce acknowledgements and control frames
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy · Also: Seastar
Principle: Let an acknowledgement ride on data that is about to be sent, and merge many small window or ACK updates into one, re-enabling immediate ACKs only where the peer would otherwise stall.
Why it is faster: A standalone ACK or window update is a full packet or frame with headers, syscall and wakeups on both sides. When a response follows within milliseconds, delaying the ACK saves one packet per transaction. Accumulating consumed bytes and sending one update before switching streams halves control-frame counts.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `TCP_QUICKACK` management in RPC servers; deferred-accept listeners. • Data / databases: batched acks in brokers (AMQP prefetch, Kafka commits). • Embedded: piggybacked acks in custom UDP or radio protocols.
Watch out: delayed ACK interacts badly with Nagle and with peers waiting for an ACK before sending more (the 40-200 ms stall); switch quick-ack back on when the peer's next move depends on it.
Seen in HAProxy: 9ea05a790f 2009-06-14 `tcp-smart-accept`: one packet saved per connection. • 5e205524ad 2011-12-17 Quick-ack re-enabled for incomplete requests, avoiding 40-200 ms stalls; 93548be149 not enabled on empty buffers. • fbac6638c1 2012-09-03 Quick-ack disabled on SSL listeners: +2-2.5% transaction rate on Atom. • 24db47e0cc 2012-11-23 TCP health checks answer the SYN-ACK with an RST through delayed ACK, so the application is never woken. • 617592c9eb 2022-06-08 H2 stream WINDOW_UPDATEs deferred and merged: halved on a 100 MB upload. • Also seen in Seastar (seastar-140): 89827a3d92 2014-09-15 Delayed ACKs: "worth 25-30% on the http benchmark, since we no longer ACK the HTTP GET request, instead coalescing it with the response". • fb0123ec61 2014-10-24 ACK data and FIN in a single packet; ff674d3e avoids a duplicate ACK; cbc5e939 sends an ACK only with data present. • 2a3ce92b19 2014-11-25 Delay capped at 200 ms.

### haproxy-163 — Parse streamed protocols incrementally, with a fast path for complete input
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy · Also: NATS, Netty
Principle: Parse bytes as they arrive with a resumable state machine that saves its position when data runs out, process partial frames instead of waiting for whole ones, and add a tight path for the common case where a complete message is contiguous.
Why it is faster: Restarting from the beginning on each packet makes parsing quadratic in the number of fragments; waiting for complete frames forces whole-frame buffering and can deadlock when frames exceed the buffer. A resumable FSM parses each byte once. Generic code that handles wrap-around and partial input for every item is slow for many tiny items, so a specialised "everything is here" loop recovers speed.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: streaming HTTP/WebSocket/Redis parsers in Node or Go; streaming JSON for large bodies. • Embedded: protocol decoders on microcontrollers receiving bytes in fragments from a UART. • Data / databases: wire-protocol decoders with a fast path when the whole message is already buffered.
Watch out: two code paths must stay semantically identical; fuzz the fast path against the generic one. Some frames (compressed headers) genuinely need to be complete before decoding.
Seen in HAProxy: 58f10d7478 2006-12-04 An RFC2616 state-machine parser replaces the old one; headers are parsed once. • 8d5d7f20b9 2007-01-21 "Huge rework" with `EAT_AND_JUMP_OR_RETURN`: advance and jump, or save the state and return when out of data. About 2 µs per request at 1 GHz. 976f1ee561 places the most frequent states first. • d655ffe863 2013-04-02 Response forwarding if/else chain becomes a `switch`: -20% worst case with 1-byte chunks. • 2a761dcf0d 2018-02-26 H2 demux hands data upward once per pass instead of per frame. • bdcefe58b7 2021-05-21 `h1_parse_full_contig_chunks()` for many small chunks (2700 one-byte chunks fit in 16 kB); f7c2044f8f wrapping input copied in one call instead of two parser runs. • b5454d42df 2022-05-12 The H3 demuxer no longer waits for complete frames (except HEADERS), consuming less memory; 3df8ca0a4d QUIC partially copies STREAM frames larger than the remaining space. • Also seen in NATS (nats-165): fd4612fab0 2012-11-20 `parseState{state, as, drop, pa, argBuf, msgBuf, scratch}` with states like `OP_PUB`, `PUB_ARG`, `MSG_PAYLOAD`; arguments are `buf[c.as : i-c.drop]`; verbs matched case-insensitively per state. • 07ef71ff98 2020-06-01 `parseSize` rejects lengths over 9 digits before the digit loop. • 1d7c4712a5 2020-12-04 MQTT: one reusable publish struct per connection and the payload sliced from the read buffer. ### haproxy-164 — Adapt TLS record size to the traffic pattern
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O
Principle: Use small records at the start of a transfer or after idle so the receiver can decrypt early bytes within the first congestion window, then switch to full-size records for bulk streams.
Why it is faster: The receiver can only decrypt a record once it has all of it; a 16 kB record spread over several packets delays the first byte. For bulk transfers, small records multiply per-record crypto overhead and syscalls. Detect bulk mode from the traffic (a streaming flag, a congested output ring) and reset it after idle.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: dynamic record sizing in Go/Java TLS servers and CDN edges. • Native / systems: QUIC packetization and streaming chunk sizes that start small and grow. • Embedded: lightweight TLS stacks: small records also cut receive-buffer RAM.
Watch out: a multiplexer must derive "bulk" from the aggregate output, not per stream, or it never switches to large records.
Seen in HAProxy: 7bed945be0 2014-02-02 Record size chosen from `CF_STREAMER` (for example 5 × 2885 then 16421-byte writes); c5890e66cd streamer flags reset after idle. • 396a186def 2014-11-13 OpenSSL small-buffer mode support: about 44 kB saved per SSL session. • 14ea98af73 2023-03-16 The H2 mux sets `CO_SFL_STREAMER` when its output ring holds more than one buffer (9824f8c890); before, H2 always sent 2 kB records and stayed slower than H1 when CPU-bound. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-143): 26044e470c 2014-11-21 TLS records capped at 1,400 bytes. 63e5a55326 2015-04-05 keeps calling `SSL_read` while `SSL_pending()` holds plaintext. • b409d60c31 2016-06-16 Small records for the first 200 KB (or in latency-optimized mode), 16 KB afterwards. 639b065ed9 2016-06-16 applies 2^14 to the payload, not the record. e0a38428da 2016-06-07 computes the overhead from the negotiated cipher. a36173c8bf 2016-06-07 keeps the suggested size across state changes. • 9e3441175c 2021-04-14 `calc_next_tls_record_size` decides per record. The commit notes that an OpenSSL receiver reads each record with two syscalls, which delays ACKs. • 05ec77b785 2021-04-14 Without latency optimization the payload size is `SIZE_MAX`, which "avoids the cost of invoking SSL_write / ptls_send every 16KB". • ec348a5c1f 2021-07-01 The small-record threshold is lowered from 200 KB to 64 KB.

### haproxy-165 — Make on-the-fly compression cheap: light codecs, measured knobs, batched flushes, shared history
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy · Also: H2O
Principle: Treat response compression as a CPU budget: pick a codec and parameters by measurement, compress whole batches rather than individual writes, keep a small dictionary for streams of similar tiny messages, and back off when CPU runs short.
Why it is faster: Library defaults spend memory and CPU for little ratio gain. Each flush closes a block and, in a stateless compressor, discards back-references, so per-chunk flushing both costs CPU and inflates output. Streams of tiny similar messages compress poorly in isolation but very well against a small history. A stateless deflate-compatible encoder keeps no large per-stream state at all.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: avoid flushing gzip per write in Express/Node middleware; prefer fast levels or LZ4/zstd-fast for dynamic content; WebSocket permessage-deflate context takeover. • Data / databases: zstd dictionaries for small JSON rows or messages. • Embedded: small-table codecs and hardware CRC on MCUs.
Watch out: batching flushes delays bytes for interactive streams (SSE needs each event delivered promptly); per-stream dictionaries cost memory per connection, so allocate them from a pool and only for streams that benefit.
Seen in HAProxy: 7e488d781c 2012-10-26 zlib memLevel 9 to 8: same ratio, +12% compression speed. • 072a2bf537 2012-11-20 `maxcompcpuusage` lowers the level or stops compressing when idle CPU gets low. • b77c5c2693 2015-12-07 Chunked responses compressed at forwarding time instead of per chunk; 15530d28a4 chunk sizes emitted without leading-zero padding. • ab2b7828e2 2021-04-22 SLZ, a stateless deflate-compatible compressor, imported to address zlib CPU and memory use; 9e274280a4 no CRC table when native CRC instructions exist; 027fdcb168 about 1 kB less L1 footprint, +0.5%. • fcee2555fc 2026-11-01 Queue all available chunks and flush once per batch: a 1 MB HTML response in 256-byte chunks shrank from 405,057 to 402,030 bytes at level 1. • aa39e1b101 2026-11-16 A 2 kB per-stream history ring for server-sent events: real SSE output from 101.75% to 4.06% of the original size. • Also seen in H2O (h2o-146): c4db6b2f76 2015-08-25 gzip limits the generator to 8,192-byte chunks. 2a5f793848 2015-08-25 adds a per-request `preferred_chunk_size`. • 5a913970fa 2016-02-26 brotli copies input across iovecs into the ring buffer and runs `WriteBrotliData` only when the block is full, plus one final flush ("encode as much as possible at once").

### haproxy-166 — Exploit the protocol's header compression fully, with a fast path for small values
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Use every static-table entry the protocol offers and encode the overwhelmingly common short lengths with a direct one-byte path.
Why it is faster: Each header name found in the static table becomes an index of one or two bytes instead of a literal. A compact, length-bucketed table keeps that search cheap. Most lengths fit the one-byte prefix, so testing that case first avoids the general varint encoder.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 encoders in custom servers or proxies; gRPC metadata. • Embedded: CoAP option deltas and compact length prefixes in custom radio protocols.
Watch out: dynamic-table insertion has memory and security trade-offs (compression side channels on secrets); the static table is the safe, cheap win.
Seen in HAProxy: 2c3139489c 2018-12-10 The HPACK encoder covers all 54 static names instead of 10, via a length-bucketed table: encoded requests 85 to 56 bytes, responses 197 to 170 bytes. • 19ed92b47d 2018-12-11 Fast path for lengths under 127: about +0.5% request rate; 30eb809fdb short indexed-header helper for method, scheme and status. • c2a10d4b4c 2018-12-07 Header names stored lowercase once, so the H2 encoder no longer lowercases them again.

### haproxy-167 — Give each flow its own socket, owned by the thread that handles it
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Instead of receiving every datagram on one shared socket and redispatching it, give each connection (or each initiating thread) its own connected socket registered with its owner's poller.
Why it is faster: A shared receive socket is a contention point and adds a handoff hop to the owning thread; replies arriving on random threads also fight over the owner's lock. Connected per-flow sockets let the kernel deliver data straight to the right thread.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: connected UDP sockets per peer in game servers, DNS resolvers and QUIC stacks; RSS/flow steering. • Web / backend: per-worker outbound sockets for DNS or metrics clients.
Watch out: one socket per flow multiplies FDs and kernel memory; needs a fallback to the shared socket when FD limits are reached.
Seen in HAProxy: 40909dfec5 2022-10-24 A socket per QUIC connection; 5b41486b7f read from it in the connection's I/O callback "by reducing contention on the receiver socket"; dc0dcb394b also used for emission. • d624aceaef 2025-09-10 DNS nameserver datagram sockets bound to the calling thread: with 10 nameservers, CPU 38% to about 10%.

### haproxy-168 — Close connections in one step when half-close or lingering buys nothing
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy
Principle: If no more data will be exchanged, close both directions at once or reset with no linger, instead of a graceful half-close sequence.
Why it is faster: Half-closing costs an extra syscall and keeps kernel state alive until the peer finishes; graceful closes from the active side accumulate TIME_WAIT entries. For health checks and short-lived internal flows this is pure overhead.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `SO_LINGER {1,0}` for health checkers and load generators. • Web / backend: connection-pool teardown on shutdown.
Watch out: a reset discards unsent data and can surface as an error to the peer; only use it when nothing is pending.
Seen in HAProxy: 4c283dce4b 2009-12-29 `SI_FL_NOLINGER`. • 7bb68abb9f 2012-05-13 `SI_FL_NOHALF`: +7.5% HTTP connection rate on empty objects. • fd29cc537b 2012-11-23 Health checks disable linger instead of calling shutdown, avoiding TIME_WAIT buildup.

### haproxy-169 — Shape load with protocol signals: spread mass closes, advertise less concurrency under pressure
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Use the protocol's own controls (close notices, GOAWAY, advertised stream limits) to smooth reconnect storms and to shed new work before the server saturates.
Why it is faster: Closing every idle connection at the same moment makes every client reconnect (and handshake) at once. Spreading closes randomly over a window flattens the peak. Advertising fewer concurrent streams when an internal load metric is high makes well-behaved clients slow down before queues explode.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: jittered drain on rolling restarts; HTTP/2 SETTINGS adjustments under load (Envoy-style adaptive concurrency). • Data / databases: staggered client reconnects after failover; admission limits derived from queue depth.
Watch out: clients that ignore the signals are not slowed; this complements, not replaces, hard limits.
Seen in HAProxy: b5d968d9b2 2022-04-08 `close-spread-time`: during soft-stop, idle-connection expiry gets a random factor and active H1/H2 connections decide randomly per request to send "Connection: close" or GOAWAY. • b3a84800b4 2026-03-19 A cheap peak-averaged run-queue length per thread; c238965b27 `tune.h2.fe.max-concurrent-streams … rq-load` shrinks advertised streams quadratically above a target: load during attacks dropped from about 100,000 to about 1,500; 932d77e287 adds a floor.

### haproxy-170 — Pace with a sleeping timer and burst credit instead of busy re-wakeups
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Implement send pacing as a token bucket that sleeps on a coarse timer and grants enough burst to cover the next scheduling delay.
Why it is faster: Re-waking a sender in a loop until the next packet is due burns CPU. A credit-based pacer sleeps; sizing the burst from the measured event-loop time keeps throughput intact despite coarse timer resolution.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: token-bucket rate limiters on coarse timers; media streaming senders. • Embedded: radio TX scheduling with sleep plus burst.
Watch out: too much burst defeats the purpose of pacing (bursts cause loss); too little caps throughput when the loop is busy.
Seen in HAProxy: 8098be1fdc 2025-01-22 A QUIC pacing task armed by `qcc_wakeup_pacing()` replaces the active loop, which "drastically" reduced CPU; cb91ccd8a8 maximum credit computed per client from the average loop time (at least 1.5 ms).

### haproxy-171 — Prune protocol bookkeeping as soon as the peer proves it is obsolete
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Trim acknowledgement and range-tracking state once the peer has confirmed it, merge adjacent ranges on insert, and bound what a peer can make you track.
Why it is faster: Range sets that only grow make every later scan slower and memory unbounded. Once our ACK up to packet X is itself acknowledged, ranges below X never need resending. Coalescing contiguous ranges keeps the structure small with unique keys.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: SACK scoreboard trimming in TCP stacks. • Data / databases: truncating replication logs below the follower's acked index; dedup windows in stream processors.
Watch out: pruning too early loses information needed for retransmission; prune only on positive proof from the peer.
Seen in HAProxy: 141982a4e1 2022-03-15 QUIC records the largest packet acknowledged in each ACK frame sent and purges the ACK-range tree below it once that packet is acknowledged; 843399fd45 removes a CPU-heavy loop in `qc_ackrng_pkts()` that could trigger the watchdog. • 0ed94032b2 2023-04-17 ACK ranges capped at 32. • ae3e768d32 2024-10-02 ACKed stream ranges merged on insert, with a unique-key tree; this enabled out-of-order ACK accounting (f6599cf5a6) and restored lost throughput.

### haproxy-172 — Make handshakes cheaper: the client's fastest cipher, one-shot crypto calls
Lens: networking · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Let clients without AES hardware use the cipher they run fastest, and call key-derivation primitives through native one-shot APIs rather than building a generic context per call.
Why it is faster: Mobile CPUs without AES hardware run ChaCha20-Poly1305 much faster than AES. Generic envelope APIs allocate and configure a context on every derivation, and a QUIC handshake performs many of them.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: TLS configs for mobile-heavy APIs; one-shot `HMAC()`/`EVP_Digest()` instead of context new/free. • Embedded: choose ChaCha on cores without AES acceleration.
Watch out: crypto library APIs differ in subtle ways; reusing contexts across calls is fragile, so prefer documented one-shot entry points.
Seen in HAProxy: 926594f606 2018-05-18 `SSL_OP_PRIORITIZE_CHACHA`: ChaCha is preferred only when the client ranks it first. • 20686bf5fd 2026-10-14 Native HKDF (AWS-LC/BoringSSL) or single-call `EVP_KDF` (OpenSSL 3): full H3 handshakes 486 to 540 per second (+11%) on OpenSSL 3.5.4, 903 to 950 (+4%) on AWS-LC. An earlier per-thread context reuse (4e0af590e8) was reverted (54633f078c) because it broke with AWS-LC.

### haproxy-173 — Normalise to calls per request: count syscalls, wakeups and function calls per unit of work
Lens: observability · Source: HAProxy · Also: curl, Seastar
Principle: Measure how many syscalls, wakeups, failed calls and hot-function invocations each request costs, and optimise those counts directly; they are stable, explainable, and predict throughput.
Why it is faster: Throughput numbers are noisy and hide the reason. A count such as "5.0 `recvfrom` per request, 20% of them EAGAIN" points at the exact waste and shows whether a fix removed it. Syscall tracing (strace counts) and call counters are cheap to collect, and hardware counters (instructions, cache misses) explain the rest.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: track DB queries, cache calls and allocations per request (N+1 detection); `strace -c` or eBPF syscall counts under load. • Data / databases: buffer reads and WAL writes per transaction; `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` row counts. • Embedded: interrupts and DMA transfers per frame; cycle counters per packet. • Native / systems: `perf stat` instructions and cache misses per operation before and after.
Watch out: strace itself slows the process massively; use it for counts, not timings. Lower counts must still be checked against throughput and latency, since a removed syscall can be replaced by a worse wakeup.
Seen in HAProxy: cb651251f9 2008-08-29 Speculative accept judged by `process_session` calls per session (6 to 5) and `epoll_ctl` count (-10%). • 14acc7072e 2011-05-11 Splice threshold justified by total syscalls on chunked data (6826 to 5793). • 3988d9342f 2013-12-27 Keep-alive polling fix verified by zero `epoll_ctl` during an `ab -k` run. • eaf05be0ee 2019-12-27 `epoll_ctl` per 100k requests (44,643 to 620): cost became proportional to connections, not requests. • 6e59cb5db1 2020-02-20 `recvfrom` per request 5.0 to 4.0; 2c1f37d353 per-request call counts for `tasklet_wakeup`, `raw_sock_to_buf`, `si_cs_process`. • d114f4a68f 2022-10-12 strace confirms the thundering-herd wakeups are gone, alongside p99.99 TTFB 32.96 ms to 4.9 ms. • 88718955f4 2023-03-17 strace shows read sizes (16320+16+182 to 15360+1158); 6e1fe253b7 strace shows about 29.7k `madvise` calls during soft-stop, which led to calling the pool GC only after real frees. • 04ce6536e1 2024-10-12 Hardware counters explain a +20% gain: instructions -17%, L2 misses -31%, L3 misses -39%, `h2_io_cb` calls 2.6M to 300k. • Also seen in curl (curl-160): 6420672879 2013-04-26 Small hashes for the implicit multi handle: test 40 went from 1142 mallocs to 138, and peak memory from 118,969 to 78,805 bytes. • e60fe20fdf 2017-04-03 Embedded list heads: 17 fewer tiny allocations (15%) in a plain `curl localhost`. cbae73e1dd (2017-04-20) intrusive list nodes went from 96 to 80 mallocs (−16.6%) per simple HTTP transfer. • 8144ba38c3 2019-05-23 A double `fopen` of the config file was found by reading the memdebug log. • 675eeb1c94 2020-09-23 Reusing a per-connection dynbuf: test 100 dropped from 132 to 125 allocations (140 in 7.72.0), and test 103 dropped by 9 to 130. • 186340c9cd 2022-02-10 Counted 24 `strlen` calls on empty strings "alone when doing a simple GET from https://curl.se". • Also seen in Seastar (seastar-152): ece4ae4772 2018-01-11 Microbenchmarks for `parallel_for_each` added together with its allocation-free fast path. • cddce93dce f462c83a61 2022-03-22 perf tests report allocations, tasks and instructions per iteration (merge f6be3898). • 67c7df07b6 2022-08-08 Instruction count of `start_measuring_time` fixed so it is not attributed to the test. • 8e9dfcb352 2023-04-21 Allocator micro-benchmarks.

### haproxy-174 — Build low-overhead profiling into the product, switchable at runtime
Lens: observability · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Keep per-task CPU time, latency, lock-wait and allocation counters in the production binary, enabled on demand, so hot spots and contention can be found on real traffic.
Why it is faster: External profilers show where CPU goes but not which request type or call chain waits on a lock, an allocator slow path or a stolen CPU. In-process attribution per task answers that directly, and being always present means problems are diagnosed where they occur. Fast paths that would cost more to time than to execute (thread-local cache hits) are deliberately left untimed, and bookkeeping is skipped entirely when profiling is off.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: event-loop lag metrics in Node; pprof labels per handler in Go; async-profiler lock mode in Java. • Data / databases: wait-event views (`pg_stat_activity`), per-query lock and I/O time. • Native / systems: steal-time monitoring in VMs; per-subsystem counters exposed over an admin socket. • Embedded: per-task cycle and max-latency counters in the RTOS, readable over a debug port.
Watch out: profiling hooks on hot paths must cost nothing when disabled (see next entry); the act of timing can distort tiny operations, so time only the slow paths.
Seen in HAProxy: d80cb4ee13 2018-01-20 Global activity counters; they supplied the before/after numbers for the per-thread poller change (d9e7e36c6e). • 9efd7456e0 2018-05-31 Per-task CPU time and latency under `profiling.tasks`, "less than 1% extra CPU". • ed72d82827 2018-10-17 Measures CPU time stolen by other threads or processes, counting only losses of 500 µs or more. • baba82fe70 2018-11-22 Average loop time in "show activity". • 64435aaa85 2022-09-07 Caller/callee activity hash with short linear probing for cheap attribution. • 33c461314c 2024-11-19 Stream latency time no longer updated when no wake date is set, i.e. when profiling is off. • 503084643f 2025-09-11, 98cc815e3e Lock-wait and lock-held averages per task; 9d8c2a888b allocation time per task (about 2% at 2M req/s, only while profiling); 8fb5ae5cc6 allocations under a lock. These drove the stick-table, peers and resolver fixes of the same month.

### haproxy-175 — Make disabled instrumentation cost nothing, including its arguments
Lens: observability · Source: HAProxy · Also: Aeron, H2O, jemalloc, Kafka, Varnish, memcached, Netty, PostgreSQL
Principle: Put the "is tracing enabled?" test outside the call so that argument evaluation, string length computation and the call itself are skipped when tracing is off.
Why it is faster: An inline trace function that returns immediately still evaluates all its arguments first, at every call site on every request. A macro that tests the enable flag first removes that work. Passing pre-measured (pointer, length) strings built at the call site removes runtime `strlen` calls from the remaining enabled path.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `if (log.isDebugEnabled())` or supplier lambdas in Java; lazy log formatting in Python (`logger.debug("%s", x)`). • Native / systems: Rust `log!`/`tracing` macros; static keys or jump labels for tracepoints. • Embedded: compile-time log levels so release builds carry no trace strings.
Watch out: macros evaluate arguments conditionally, so arguments with side effects behave differently when tracing is on or off.

`__trace()`/`__trace_enabled()` take an `ist` built at the call site, which "greatly reduced" `__trace_enabled()` in perf top; 06fa9f717f the fixed 3-character thread-id string uses a known length. • d142c7f421 2026-04-14 Tracing can also be compiled out entirely (`USE_TRACE`): 6-10% smaller binary and faster build. • Also seen in Aeron (aeron-146): 477c433791 2014-08-20 "Defer string formatting ### haproxy-176 — Read the profile at the instruction level: a hot trivial load means cache-line sharing
Lens: observability · Source: HAProxy
Principle: When a simple load, test or fence shows a large share of samples in an annotated profile, the cost is a cache miss or coherence stall, not computation; look at what shares that cache line.
Why it is faster: Function-level profiles blame the function; instruction-level annotation shows which memory access stalls. A read-only config field that costs 8-13% of samples is almost always sitting next to a field other cores write. Fixing the layout (not the code) then gives large gains, sometimes more than doubling throughput.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `perf annotate`, `perf c2c` for false sharing; `pahole` to see which fields share a line. • Web / backend: JVM async-profiler in cpu/itimer mode with assembly view; padding hot counters with `@Contended` after confirmation. • Data / databases: latch and buffer-descriptor layouts verified with hardware-counter sampling.
Watch out: sample skid attributes stalls to the instruction after the culprit; confirm with a layout change and a benchmark rather than trusting a single annotation.
Seen in HAProxy: 90e9b8c8b6 2021-03-03 perf showed "a huge access cost on global.mode in process_stream()" caused by neighbouring atomic counters; `struct global` reordered. • a1090a5b61 2021-04-10 `fdtab` appeared in perf top because `ha_used_fds` shared its line; moved to a read-mostly section. • cc2672f48b 2021-03-03 A counter re-read right after its atomic increment showed in perf; fetch-and-add variants were introduced. • 4dd33d9c32 2022-12-20 Profiling on 48 threads showed `pool_free()` stalling on `pool->limit`; splitting hot fields took a QUIC test from 42 to 96 Gbps. • fd012b6c59 2025-11-03 12.87% of samples on a load of `server_id_hdr_name`; moving counters behind an alignment boundary gave +1% on ARM and x86. • a9df6947b4 2026-01-28 8.26% on a single `test %ecx` load in `may_dequeue_tasks()`: +3.6% RPS after separating the fields. • a79a67b52f 2026-01-28 27% of a function's samples on one `mfence`; replaced by atomic loads, +0.5-3%.

### haproxy-177 — Measure lock contention directly and make it visible at its source
Lens: observability · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Count lock acquisitions and time spent waiting per lock, and when back-off or spinning hides the culprit, switch it off for diagnosis so the contending caller appears in the profile.
Why it is faster: Contention shows up in CPU profiles as time in generic wait or back-off functions, detached from whoever caused it. Per-lock counters (acquisitions, wait seconds) identify the lock; disabling back-off moves samples back onto the contended function; "stolen time" separates contention from oversubscription.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Go mutex and block profiles; Java JFR monitor-wait events. • Data / databases: latch wait statistics per latch type (InnoDB, Postgres LWLock waits). • Native / systems: `perf lock`, lock-stat in the kernel, instrumented builds with per-lock counters.
Watch out: lock instrumentation adds overhead to the locks it measures; use a separate debug build or runtime switch, and compare the uninstrumented build for final numbers.
Seen in HAProxy: 8d38805d3d 2017-11-05 Scheduler run-queue lookups measured as skips (430M skips for 200k lookups) and spin-lock time (85.2 s to 0.46 s), alongside 15.9k to 447k req/s. • b20aa9eef3 2018-10-15 Time in the wait-queue lock on 12 threads: 4.7 s to 11 ms. • 1e928c074b 2019-05-28 Wait-queue write locks per run: 1,125,496 to 173, contention about 4.3x lower. • d48ed6643b 2020-10-16 About 5% of runtime waiting on the task wait-queue lock; with a seek lock, `wake_expired_tasks()` CPU 2.15% to 1.04%. • b13044cc1a 2022-10-11 `PLOCK_DISABLE_EBO` switches exponential back-off off so contended functions show in perf top instead of the wait functions.

### haproxy-178 — Sweep tuning parameters, choose the knee, and re-derive when the architecture changes
Lens: observability · Source: HAProxy · Also: Kafka, zstd
Principle: Pick batch sizes, shard counts, back-off curves and similar constants by sweeping them on representative hardware, judging throughput and tail latency together, and redo the sweep when the design or target CPU changes.
Why it is faster: Constants chosen by intuition are often off by an order of magnitude, and the optimum moves with thread count, cache size and CPU vendor. A sweep exposes the knee where returns diminish and where latency starts to suffer. A heuristic tuned for one design (a shared run queue) can become harmful after a redesign (per-thread queues).
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: thread-pool sizes, connection-pool sizes and batch sizes swept under load with p99 recorded. • Data / databases: group-commit size, buffer-pool partitions and compaction throttles per hardware class. • Native / systems: per-ISA defaults (x86 vs ARM) for spin and back-off constants. • Embedded: DMA burst and FIFO thresholds tuned on the target board, not the simulator.
Watch out: a sweep on one machine overfits; check at least two CPU families and thread counts, keep the knob configurable, and record why the default was chosen so it can be revisited.
Seen in HAProxy: 4827fd2a7e 2011-07-22 maxaccept 100 to 32 so the batch fits in cache: 67 to 94 kcps on a Core i5 with 4 MB L3. • c633607c06 2020-01-31 Scheduler class ratios from measurement, re-tuned in 1dfc9bbdc6 and 3ef7a190b0, reported as both connection rate and CLI response time. • 4327d0ac00 2021-02-19 runqueue-depth 200 to 40: up to +18% at 16 threads and lower RTT; 060a761248 generalised to 280/√threads; 66161326fd MAX_ACCEPT 64 to 4, the best accept rate at every thread count. • 7bf829ace1 2023-07-24 Pool free-list buckets swept: 368k RPS unsharded, then 3.5M/4M/4.7M/5.5M with 8/16/32/64 buckets. • f0d188f6ed 2023-08-12 About 7.5% of 32-bit primes evaluated over 128 arrangements of 40k real pointers to pick the hash multiplier. • 1a088da7c2 2024-03-04 Stick-table shard count: 16 gave 675k, 32 gave 980k, 64 gave 1008k, 128 gave 1040k RPS; 6c1b29d06f ring wait queues: 6 was best on EPYC, Xeon and Ampere. • 04ce6536e1 2024-10-12 Extra receive rounds capped at 12, because 16 was slightly faster but raised peak latency 33%. • 253fba01a7 2025-02-07 About 14 back-off variants measured: growth 1.25^N capped at 128k cycles cut the maximum write-lock wait 4x; a727c6eaa5 an x86-only trick kept off ARM, where it lost about 1%. • d8c2cb4bf6 2026-11-12 After the scheduler became per-thread, 280/√threads left only 14 tasks per batch at 256 threads; default returned to a flat 280. • Also seen in Kafka (kafka-178): e2d16b504c 2019-08-16 "ConcurrentSkipListMap shows performance regression": Streams stores revert to a synchronized `TreeMap` after measurement. • ca375d8004 2022-02-08 Scala compiler backend threads capped at `min(8, nproc)` after measuring a 6-10 thread sweet spot (60 s to 51 s). • 6bcc497c36 2023-05-05 Each varint encode/decode variant chosen empirically with `ByteUtilsBenchmark` on JDK 17. • 12ce9c7f98 2024-02-01 The ZK-to-KRaft migration batch minimum retuned from 1000 to 200. • cd08129f3d 2024-12-19 Group coordinator append linger default tuned from 10 ms to 5 ms. • d067c6c040 2025-09-19 Snapshot commit interval during coordinator loading set to 16384 offsets using a new loading benchmark: smaller slows simple loads, larger slows transactional loads. • Also seen in zstd (zstd-148): fd9d6b8e2f 2015-10-26 paramgrill automates the search over `{windowLog, chainLog, hashLog, searchLog, ...}`. 0dbf2874ee is one of many retuning commits ("faster level 1 at 256 KB"). • adbe74a8ac 2017-06-28 and 813535105b Levels adjusted for a monotonically increasing memory budget, plus a debug checker. • 3cbfac1cdb 2017-12-14 Levels 15 to 20 retuned to use the faster btopt. 706b5430bd selects btlazy2 for levels 13 to 15 after the delayed-update speedup and moves the monotonic-memory check into `ZSTD_estimateCCtxSize()`.

### haproxy-179 — Microbenchmark primitives in isolation, in units that transfer
Lens: observability · Source: HAProxy
Principle: Measure hot primitives (scanners, lookups, generators, hashes) alone with realistic inputs, and report cycles per byte, nanoseconds per operation or misses per lookup, so results compare across machines and versions.
Why it is faster: A 1% end-to-end change is hard to see in noise, but a 20% change in a primitive's ns/op is obvious. Units like bytes per cycle reveal whether a rewrite reached the hardware limit, and a dedicated test or generator keeps the primitive correct as it evolves.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Google Benchmark / Criterion with cycles/byte and counters; generator tools for tables checked into the repo. • Web / backend: JMH or Benchmark.js for parsers and serializers on captured production payloads. • Data / databases: index lookup ns/op and probe counts by key distribution.
Watch out: microbenchmarks run hot in cache and with perfect branch history; confirm every microbench win end to end before keeping complex code.
Seen in HAProxy: 8f3ce06f14 2019-11-22 SWAR control-character search measured at about 0.43 bytes/cycle. • c7a8a3c7bd 2022-06-21 `one_among_mask()` at about 4 ns per lookup at 3.8 GHz, about 2x faster than the rank function; unit test in 798d6fc0a7. • 3e31e2927c 2024-01-10 A `dev/phash` tool generates and checks the perfect hash for HTTP status codes. • ebab479cdf 2025-05-12 Authority character check measured at 1.2 ns/char at 3 GHz, preferred over a faster but compiler-fragile bitmap version. • 6af17d491f 2025-06-07 Tree lookups reported in million lookups/s per key count (eb32 100k keys 6.64 to 8.00). • fb7e5e1696 2026-03-11 Hash probing judged by retries per lookup (1.17 to 0.6). • 34b9b528e8 2026-07-23 Time-conversion cache: 38.4M lookups with 12k misses versus 59M misses for a naive scan order. • 4a9ec66fd8 2026-05-25 PRNG change measured as UUIDs per second (13.5M to 125M).
